Advanced Integrated Circuits on SOI


Ravikumar, V.K.1, Wampler, R.2, Ho, M.Y.1, Christensen, J.2, Phoa, S.L.1


Advanced Micro Devices Singapore Pte Ltd1, 508 Chai Chee Lane, Singapore 469032,  Advanced Micro Devices, Inc.2, 7171 Southwest Pkwy, Austin TX 78735, USA


Phone: (65) 67969888 Fax: (65) 62339080 Email: venkat-krishnan.ravikumar@amd.com


Abstract - Laser voltage probing is the newest generation of  tools that perform timing analysis for electrical fault  isolation in advanced failure analysis facilities. This paper  uses failure analysis case studies on SOI to showcase the  implementation of laser voltage probing in the failure  analysis flow and highlight its significance in root-cause  identification.


I. INTRODUCTION


Failure analysis (FA) at advanced technology nodes has  become increasing difficult because of smaller geometries and  increased sensitivity to subtle defects. This increases the need  for successful electrical fault isolation (EFI).


EFI utilizes laser-intrusive or emission-based techniques that  exploit anomalous signals coming from the defect. The device  under test (DUT) may be biased statically using simple source  meters for techniques like thermal-induced voltage alteration (TIVA) [1] or photo/thermal-emission microscopy (EM). DUTs  with defects that do not have an electric path between the static  supplies go through dynamic testing using automated test  equipment (ATE) by running patterns that exercise the defective  circuitry. Techniques like soft defect localization (SDL) [1] or  dynamic EM are then employed, depending on the failing  symptoms.


Often, as in some of the FA case studies discussed in this  paper, EFI is inadequate with the techniques already described, and there is a need to study the actual behavior of transistors  while they are toggled during the tests. This is called timing  analysis or waveform analysis. The common technique used in  the last decade worked by collecting photons released during the  toggling events and statistically accumulating them into a  waveform [2].


The technique has been extremely useful in the past, but with  voltage scaling and shrinking transistor sizes, the number of  photons being released has decreased dramatically. In addition, signals between two transistors in the collection window of a  few microns are indistinguishable, leading to cross-talk.


Laser voltage probing (LVP) is the latest generation of  timing tools that perform well against voltage scaling while


accommodating a much smaller collection window. The  technique employs the Franz-Keldysh and free carrier effects [3, 5] (i.e., decoding the optical modulation caused by changes in  the electrical field in a transistor’s space-charge regions). Both  continuous wave (CW) and femtosecond pulsed near-infra-red (NIR) lasers have been used to implement equipment using this  effect. Though LVP has existed for a long while, the current  implementations make the technique a lot more usable


This effect is also used to perform frequency mapping (FM) or laser voltage imaging (LVI) [4]. This technique has been  shown to isolate broken scan fails successfully [4]. In this paper, we provide a brief introduction to LVP and focus on interesting  FA case studies. In the cases we present, LVP and FM have  made the difference between failing to find the cause and  successful root-cause identification.


II. THE MECHANICS


As mentioned in the Introduction, LVP works on the  principles of the Franz-Keldysh and free carrier effects on the  space-charge region of a MOSFET [3, 5] (Figure 1). Also  known as the depletion region, the space-charge region refers to  the junctions between the source, drain, or channel of the  transistor and its body.


Figure 1: Physics of Franz-Keldysh and free carrier effects: Reflected laser beam is amplitude- and phase-modulated by the  electric field in the space-charge regions.


Two optical properties of this region and of interest to us  vary with the applied electric field: the absorption coefficient  and the refractive index.


When a laser of uniform amplitude and phase is incident  onto the space-charge region, some of the photons are absorbed (function of the absorption coefficient) and the rest are reflected. The reflected photons are, hence, “amplitude-modulated” by  changes to the absorption coefficient, which means changes in  the electric field due to toggling of the logic state of the  transistor are mapped onto the amplitude-modulated reflected  laser.


While changes to the absorption coefficient cause amplitude  modulation of the reflected laser, changes to the refractive index  cause a “phase modulation” that changes the phase of the  reflected laser. On the CW LVP implementation, detection is  based on changes to the absorption coefficient only. On the  pulsed LVP implementation, detection is based on both  absorption coefficient modulation and phase changes.


The modulated reflected laser is collected by a photodetector for translation from an optical signal into an electrical  signal. This resultant electrical signal is then amplified and  finally fed to an oscilloscope to reassemble the original  transitions on the transistor.


The sample is exercised continuously using the pattern of  interest on an ATE, and a trigger pulse is generated at a precise  vector, which is then fed into the LVP system. This pulse  triggers the oscilloscope at the window of interest and the  resultant waveform is averaged millions of times to reproduce  the logic states of the transistor in that interval.


The output of the amplifier electronics can also be sent into a  spectrum analyzer to identify frequency. The tool can then be  made to search for this frequency over an area; this technique is  called frequency mapping (FM).


Pulsed LVP is implemented in a different way but utilizes  the same basic principles of the Franz-Keldysh effect. Here, a  femtosecond laser pulse is shot at the transistor at precise  intervals from the trigger, recollected into bins, and accumulated  into a waveform. This implementation, however, does not  accommodate FM.


Though there are several applications for LVP and FM, our  focus for this paper is to isolate defects on SOI-based advanced  integrated circuits. LVP is particularly effective when used with  structural tests like scan or JTAG because we can predict the  behavior from the transistors of interest.


III. CASE STUDIES


The following case studies validate the use of LVP technique  on SOI. We present cases of successful fault isolation using both  forms of LVP, the continuous wave and pulsed laser voltage  probing. The cases include failures at the transistor’s active and  interconnecting metal layers.


CW LVP


This case discusses SOI qualification reject that failed scan  logic tests. The failure is gross at different temperature, frequency, and voltage conditions. No anomalous signals were  observed with other fault isolation techniques, including TIVA  and static or dynamic EM (Figure 2), and the area is too big for  direct physical analysis.


FM is performed on the unit. The scan test is looped  continuously using the ATE with a 50-MHz clock frequency. The area of interest is frequency-mapped. Unlike in a chain test, in a scan logic test the output of scan flops usually do not toggle  at the same frequency as the supplied clocks, though they could  be a harmonic.


Figure 3 is an overlay of the FM with the aligned CAD  layout. Strong and unexpected FM signals are observed at one  of the call-out circuitries. The other red sites belong to the clock  drivers and should be ideally present.


Figure 2: Dynamic photo emission at the area of interest  does not indicate any abnormality.


Figure 3: Overlay of FM with CAD. FM shows strong signals at  one of the instances indicated by diagnosis.


Figure 4 shows the CW LVP waveforms collected from the  circuitries of interest. The horizontal axis is in units of time and  the vertical axis, though arbitrary, is in fact an indication of the  state of the transistor. For practical purposes, we can assume that  a high on the waveforms indicates that the transistor is turned

The first pair of waveforms indicates clock transitions and in  this case toggles at 100 MHz. We observe two clock pulses in  the window of interest. Signals are collected from a reference  circuitry, which is expected to have toggles similar to the  circuitry of interest and are shown as the second pair of  waveforms. This  signal indicates an abnormal amplitude modulation of laser and  is probably due to an anomaly in the active regions of the  transistor.


Case 2. The second real product application case is a package  with a trace cracking after extensive reliability testing. The  SQUID-to-sample distance was also about 1 mm due to the  presence of the die. A fast-step scanning was done to locate  the approximate failure location and followed by a finer step


scan for improved location accuracy as shown by Fig. 9. The  current image at the left clearly follows the trace of interest  until the die edge, where the current density significantly  weakens which suggests the failure is around the die edge. Physical failure analysis found a trace crack open at the  vicinity current termination point as shown by the image on  the right. The fact that the current does not disappear  immediately after the termination point might be caused by the  crowded turns of the trace and die edge effects, which are  currently being investigated by software algorithm  improvement.  In this case, a normal MFI scan isolates the  trace open within ~150m.


Figure 9: SDR scanning of a trace open case with normal scanning  parameters. The left image shows the current density distribution  with open location highlighted. The optical image at the right shows  the actual trace and its failure location. In this case, the SDR scanning  location accuracy is about 150 m.


Case 3. The third case is a test vehicle package with multiple  C4 bump non-wet opens on a long daisy chain. The daisy  chain goes up and down between the die and package, and is a  good example of demonstrating tool applicability to long  traces inside Si. The SQUID-to-sample distance was again  about 1 mm due to the presence of the die. The 200MHz RF  probing signal at 1mW of power was fed into the sample from  the two ends of the daisy chain. The current density images  and cross section images confirming non-wet C4 joints are  shown in Fig. 10. The left hand side image is the first scan  with RF signal fed from the left side of the chain. The current  flow is not continuous because one segment of the trace was  covered by a solid metal plate on the top layer. The right hand  side image is the second scan with RF signal fed from the  right side of the chain. The RF current flow clearly follows the  daisy chain structure while its density decreases linearly  toward the open C4 bump. Simple observation on the current  density identifies that the open location is about 2 bumps away  from the actual failure location. Failure isolation using linear  extrapolation based on Eq. (3) was then applied to this case  and successfully pinpointed the open bump. This implies that  a localization accuracy of better than 70 m was achieved for  this case even with 1 mm probe-to-sample distance.


Figure 10: SDR scanning of daisy chain of multiple C4 non-wet  opens with normal scanning parameters. The left image shows the  current density distribution with open location at the first bump of the  left side chain. The right side image shows the current density  distribution with open location with inner bumps of the right side of  the daisy chain. Cross section confirmed the open location with  localization accuracy down to 70m using the linear extrapolation  algorithm.


Discussion and Conclusion


All six samples discussed in this paper are summarized in the  Table 1 below. This shows that the SDR scanning works well  for different package open failure modes with a resolution  down to 50 um.

SDR is a new non-contact and nondestructive technique that  has been demonstrated to localize open defects in package  substrates and interconnections.  It provides a 2D failure  isolation capability and complements TDR technique for more  efficient failure analysis. With this 2D current image and (x, y) location, SDR eliminates the need to calculate z location  with the analysis of circuit schematics. SDR has been shown  to be able to localize open defects to within 50 m for some  ideal cases and around 200m for real failure application. SDR can scan long and high impedance trace by adjusting  scanning frequency.  As this technique is further developed to  improve signal-to-noise ratio and better user experience, we  expect to be able to apply it to more defect types with reduced  throughput time.


Right hand bump open


Below we discuss three areas where SDR can improve to  establish its unique capability as the open detection tool. The  first area is to improve tool accuracy to locate the failure point  more precisely and consistently. The effort includes  developing an algorithm using current density linear  extrapolation method, and extending it to accommodate for  turns and short traces when current density is impacted by  geometry discontinuity. The second area is to increase signal  travel distance to account for failure detection of long traces  particularly inside Si where impedance is high. As shown  below in Figure 11 of signal travel distance vs signal  frequency, a higher frequency signal will have more loss and  travel a shorter distance on a given trace. Higher frequency  scanning, however, provides better location accuracy. This  imposes a challenge to optimize the tool for longer travel  distance and higher resolution without increasing signal power  for maximum tool applications.  The third area is how to direct  signal to a specific circuit branch with actual failure when  there are multiple circuit branches connected to the interested  chain. The tool development team has been actively looking  for solutions for these challenges with progress including  wider tool frequency range and better software algorithm. With its current capability on short and leakage detection, SDR is adding a unique application with increased accuracy, flexibility and capability to fill the electrical open isolation  gap that the industry is facing. This will make it a versatile  tool that provides solutions to a range of failure modes.

Localization of Dead Open in a Solder Bump by Space Domain Reflectometry


David P. Vallett and Daniel A. Bader


IBM Technology Group, Essex Junction, VT, USA dvallett@us.ibm.com


Vladimir V. Talanov, Jan Gaudestad, Nicolas Gagliolo, and Antonio Orozco


Neocera, LLC, 10000 Virginia Manor Road, Beltsville, MD, 20705, USA  talanov@neocera.com, gaudestad@neocera.com, orozco@neocera.com


Abstract


Space  Domain  Reflectometry  (SDR)  is  a  newly  developed  non-destructive failure analysis (FA) technique for localizing  open defects in both packages and dies through mapping in  space  domain the  magnetic  field  produced  by  a  radio  frequency (RF) current induced in the sample, herein the name  Space  Domain  Reflectometry.  The  technique  employs  a  scanning  superconducting  quantum  interference  device (SQUID)  RF  microscope  operating  over  a  frequency  range  from 60 to 200 MHz. In this paper we demonstrate that SDR is  capable  of  locating  defective  micro  bumps  in  a  flip-chip  device.


Introduction


Short  and open failures  are  commonly observed in  a  wafer  foundry  as  well  as  in  package  manufacturing,  especially  as  integrated  circuits  (ICs)  become  smaller  and  devices  are  stacked in a 3D formation. In the past, most failure analysis (FA) tools have been devoted to detect shorts and leakages in  the  packages  or  dies  using  techniques  such  as  photon  emission, liquid crystal  analysis,  laser scanning microscopy, infrared  thermal  detection,  and  Magnetic  Current  Imaging (MCI).   For  detecting  opens  Time  Domain  Reflectometry (TDR)  has  been  the  only  option  available  to  the  FA  community [1].  TDR uses a pulse generator and high-speed  oscilloscope to record reflection of the pulse from the open  defect in real time and can provide resolution down to 1 mm  making it mostly suitable for high-pin-count packages, such as  quad flat package and ball grid array, but less convenient for  smaller packages (less than 1 cm2). An extension of TDR uses  a Vector Network Analyzer (VNA TDR) to receive the device  response in frequency domain by applying a continuous wave (CW) and then measuring a reflected wave. The time domain  signal  is  obtained via inverse  Fourier-Transformation of  the  frequency data [3].


Recently,  Electro  Optical  Terahertz  Pulse  Reflectometry (EOTPR), an innovative TDR system, has been successfully  developed [4].  This is the first  system that  applies terahertz  technology  to  time  domain  reflectometry.  The  system  has


achieved  10  µm  resolution,  5.7  ps  rise  time,  94  dB  signal/noise ratio and 150 mm signal propagation range.


Magnetic  Current  Imaging  (MCI)  [5]  has  been  for  years  applied to non-destructively locate shorts and leakages as well  as high resistance defects caused by cracks, solder bump voids  and  delaminations  in  packages  and  dies.  The  technique  employs two types of  sensors:  a  Superconducting Quantum  Interference Device (SQUID) sensor for low current and large  working  distances  and  a  Giant  Magnetoresistance  (GMR) sensor for sub-micron resolution current imaging at wafer/die  level. Both sensors allow for probing signals below 100 kHz, due to limitations of the electronics and sensor wiring, making  it impossible to provide “flowing current” [6] through a trace  interrupted by  an  open  defect.  The  low-frequency  MCI for  packaged devices  is  accomplished using a  high-temperature  superconductor  (YBa2Cu3O7,  Tc=90K)  SQUID  to  measure  vertical  components  of  magnetic  fields  surrounding  conductors  carrying  current  to,  from,  and  through  short  circuits. An AC bias is utilized on the sample to enable lock-in  amplification  for  noise  reduction.  Magnetic  field  data  are  transformed  into  current  density  images,   which  typically  show higher gradients found in localized narrow current paths  or  dissolution  of  current  density  where  narrower  paths  dissipate to much broader conductors or power planes at shortcircuited sites. Current direction is also easily determined as  the fields surrounding active conductors are bipolar.


In this paper we investigate the ability of this newly developed  technique, Space Domain Reflectometry (SDR), for localizing  an open failure in a C4 solder bump in a flip chip device. This  particular case is especially important to FA community since  a cracked or non-wet C4 bump is one of the most common  open  failure  modes  in  flip-chip  technology. The  SDR  was  implemented  via  upgrading  a  conventional  low-frequency  MCI  system  Magma-C30  with  newly  developed  SQUID  electronics able to operate at frequencies up to 200 MHz [7].


Principle of Space Domain Reflectometry


DC and low frequency signals, normally used in electrical  probing of ICs, cannot pass through the open trace because of  the open defect. However, a CW RF signal injected into the  open trace from one end propagates all the way to the open  boundary  and  reflects  back,  similarly  to  VNA TDR.  SDR  injects a  CW radio-frequency (RF) signal  into the defective  trace and acquires a 2D image of the induced RF magnetic  field.  Because  at  RF  frequencies  the  open  impedance  Zopen (typically  in  MΩ range)  is  much  greater  than  the  trace  characteristic  impedance  Z0 (~50  Ω),  the  open  boundary  reflects back nearly all the incident power, while no RF power  is transmitted past the open (see Fig. 1). A superposition of the  incident and reflected signals creates a standing wave [2] in “front”  of  the  open,  where  the  zeros  (nodes)  and  extrema (antinodes) remain at the same positions.


RF prober


package


Figure 1: Sketch of the current standing-wave (red) formed in   a transmission line due to superposition of the signals incident


Z-stage-BGA


onto (blue) and reflected from (green) the open boundary. N   and  AN  indicate  the  standing  wave  nodes  and  antinodes,


XY-stage


respectively.


Since package traces have negligible losses at RF, the standing  wave amplitude varies as 2I0sin(γx) [2], where I0=√P/Z0 is the  amplitude  of  an  incident  wave  with  P the  power  of  the  incident probing wave, γ=2π/λ is the wavenumber with λ the  wavelength of a probing signal, and x is the position along a  trace.  Because  an  IC  is  much  smaller  than  λ~1m,  the  RF  probing signal has no phase variation along the trace, making  sin(γx)≈γx due to the fact that  γx<<1. Thus, in the vicinity of  the open the standing wave current amplitude decays linearly  as follows:


I ~ √P F x


(1)


where  F is the frequency of the RF probing signal, and the  open is located at  x=0. Imaging the standing-wave magnetic  field near an open defect with RF MCI system and inverting  this field into a current density image allows one to recover  the  standing  wave  profile  and  locate  the  open,  which  according to Fig. 1 coincides with the rightmost node of the  standing wave.


In  TDR,  the  spatial  resolution  is  defined  by  the  electrical  width  of  the  pulse  and  accuracy  depends  on  jitter  and  the  velocity  factor  of  the  conductive  path  and  surrounding  materials..  Due to  the  near-field nature  of scanning SQUID


microscopy the SDR spatial resolution is determined by the  SQUID sensor size and/or SQUID-sample separation, rather  than  the  wavelength  or  other  sample-dependent  factors.  An  estimate  shows that  for  representative  1  mW of  RF power  injected at 100 MHz into an open-circuited 50 Ω transmission  line, the standing wave current amplitude drops down to the  SQUID noise  level  only  20  µm away from the  open.  This  allows RF MCI to follow the flow of the standing wave RF  current nearly all the way to the open.


Experimental and Results


For  this  study,  a  standard  MCI  system was  upgraded  with  newly  developed  high  frequency  electronics  capable  of  pumping RF CW signal into the sample under test (open trace) and operating the SQUID sensor at 60 to 200 MHz (Fig. 2).

Case Study 1


To  create  a  standing  wave,  SDR  relies  on  impedance  mismatch between the trace characteristic impedance (Z0) and  the open lumped element impedance (Zopen), that is  Z0<<Zopen, which is the case for virtually all electrical and physical opens  associated with the package or die, including C4 bumps. At  the same time, it is very important to verify that substantial  change  in  interconnect  geometry  inherently  occurring  in  a  good C4 bump at  the  package-die  interface  is  not going to  create a “false” standing wave.


We  examined  two  flip-chip  samples  based  on  the  same  structure: a good sample and defective sample having a dead  open due to cracking in a C4 bump. The RF signal was fed  into the structure via the package solder ball using a single RF  prober. The prober signal wire (center conductor of coax) was  connected to the solder ball with a probing needle, while the  ground conductor was left floating.  Both samples were raster  scanned with RF MCI microscope and the resulting

From left to right: optical image of the   samples, magnetic field at 190 MHz, RF current density, the same current density in rainbow color scheme, and current profile along   the yellow line in optical image.


magnetic  field  images  were  converted  into  current  images  using standard inversion technique. Fig. 3 shows a comparison  of the results obtained on the good sample (top images) and  the  failing  sample  (bottom  images).  Leftmost  images (black/white) are optical images of the scan area and are used  to correlate features in the magnetic/current density images to  their physical location on the sample.  Red/blue images are  false color images of the measured RF magnetic field spatial  distribution while the images to their right are current density  images  obtained  directly  from  the  magnetic  field  data. Rightmost images show the current density amplitude profile  along the trace conducing to the C4 bump (thin blue line in the  current density images).


One can clearly  see  a  difference  between the  two samples. While in both cases the current disappears at the C4 bump, the  behavior is different. In a good sample, the current amplitude  is constant right up to the C4 bump, confirming an absence of  impedance  mismatch  between  the  package  and  die.  Such  behavior is expected since SDR operates at frequencies native  to IC, at which the impedance mismatch is minimal by design. In the defective sample, however,  an open C4 bump causes  gradual  reduction  in  the  current  toward  the  open,  where  it  finally vanishes due to the linear decay of the standing wave  amplitude.


Case Study 2


For the second study we utilized another flip-chip device also  with known open in the C4 bump near the die edge. The RF  signal  at  190  MHz  was  injected  into  the  sample  via  conventional  RF  probe  connected  to  the  corresponding  package solder ball in the bottom part of the trace (blue arrow  in  Fig.  4).  The  sample  was  raster  scanned  using  RF  MCI  microscope. The obtained magnetic field image was converted  into the current image using the standard inversion technique  and overlaid on top of a CAD image as shown in Fig. 4. One  can clearly see that once again the current gradually decays  toward the open, where it finally vanishes (green arrow).


Conclusions


We have shown that a Space Domain Reflectometry technique  implemented on a Magnetic Current Imaging system outfitted  with  a  high  frequency  SQUID  sensor  is  capable  of  nondestructively  locating   open  failure  in  packaged  devices. Unlike TDR and EOTPR with 20 and 200 GHz bandwidth, respectively, SDR operates at 100s MHz frequencies that are  native to modern chips. The obtained results demonstrate that  SDR can distinguish between a defective and good C4 bumps  in a flip-chip device and accurately locate the failure location  in a spatial 2D image, making it for an easier interpretation of  the results as compared to TDR's one-dimensional time-like  nature.

Advanced Fault Isolation Technique using Electro-optical Terahertz  Pulse Reflectometry (EOTPR) for 2D and 2.5D Flip-chip Package


Lihong Cao, Manasa Venkata, Meng Yeow Tay*, Wen Qiu*


Advanced Micro Devices, Austin, Texas, USA


*Advanced Micro Devices (Singapore), Singapore


J. Alton, P. Taday, M. Igarashi

Electro-optical terahertz pulse reflectometry (EOTPR) was


introduced last year to isolate faults in advanced IC packages.


The EOTPR system provides 10µm accuracy that can be used


to non-destructively localize a package-level failure. In this


paper, an EOTPR system is used for non-destructive fault


isolation and identification for both 2D and 2.5D with TSV


structure of flip-chip packages. The experimental results


demonstrate higher accuracy of the EOTPR system in


determining the distance to defect compared to the traditional


time-domain reflectometry (TDR) systems.


The time-domain reflectometry (TDR) technique has been


used for fault isolation [1] in the semiconductor industry for


more than 10 years. It becomes a useful tool to detect the


majority of the dead-open and resistive-open failures [2] at the


package level. However, with the increasing complexity of


package design, especially for new 3D-stacked packaging, the


capability to isolate an exact fault location has become critical


for root-cause analysis. Hence, a next-generation isolation tool


is required to improve the resolution compared to the


conventional TDR, which typically is able to obtain only a


500-µm distance-to-defect accuracy.


Recently introduced electro-optical terahertz pulse


reflectometry (EOTPR) system [3] using photoconductive


terahertz pulse generation and detection technology provides


high measurement bandwidth, low time base jitter and high


time base resolution. In this paper, studies were conducted on


the EOTPR system for non-destructive fault isolation and


identification for both 2D and 2.5D with TSV structure of flipchip packages. The comparison with the conventional TDR


and case studies were also performed.

Following EFI, the sample is deprocessed layer by layer  looking for anomalies at each metal and via level. No physical  defect was observed until M1. Nanoprobing performed  subsequently on the flagged instance (Figure 5) indicates a short  between the drain and gate of the NMOS transistor.


The NMOS is then cross-sectioned, and the scanning  transmission electron microscope (STEM) analysis shown on  Figure 6 exposed a silicide short between the gate and the drain, which explains the root cause for the failure.

Pulsed LVP


This analysis is also performed on a scan failure that fails  gross at different temperatures, voltage, and frequency. Referring to Figure 7, the schematic of the failing circuits, the  inverter driving the signal launch is flagged as defective by  diagnostics. This signal fans out to multiple multiplexers (Mux1, Mux2, Mux3).


The CAD layout is shown in Figure 8 and the signal  direction is marked in yellow. Mux1 and Mux2 are closer to  signal launch and Mux3 is further downstream. No anomaly is  observed through static FI techniques.

IV. CONCLUSION


This paper has summarized the theory behind LVP as well as  advantages of LVP compared to conventional tools. Using FA  case studies, it highlighted the usefulness of CW LVP, pulsed  LVP, and FM in advanced integrated circuits. This is the first  report of the technique with this implementation being  successfully applied to SOI.


The technique has contributed significantly to the success  rates of FA, improving root-cause identification and resulting in  earlier process fixes, therefore improving yield and reliability. It  also gives us an opportunity to understand test results and  correlate the defect to the failing symptoms.

Closer to the Theoretical Limit: Spherical Corrections to Aplanatic Solid  Immersion Imaging with Adaptive Optics


Y. Lua*, E. Ramsayb, C.R. Stockbridgea, A. Yurtc, F. H. Köklüd


T. G. Bifanoa,e, M. S. Ünlüd,e and B. B. Goldbergb,e

*Corresponding author. luyang@bu.edu Tel: (857) 919-4174.


Aplanatic solid immersion lens (SIL) microscopy is required  to achieve the highest possible resolution for next generation  silicon IC backside inspection and failure analysis. However, aplanatic SILs are susceptible to spherical aberration  introduced by substrate thickness mismatch. We have  developed a wavefront precompensation technique using a  MEMS deformable mirror and demonstrated an increase in  substrate thickness tolerance in aplanatic SIL imaging. Good  agreement between theory and experiment is achieved and spot  intensity increases by at least a factor of two to three are  demonstrated for thicknesses deviating several percent from  ideal. This technique is also capable of fixing aberrations due  to SIL fabrication, off-axis imaging and refractive index  mismatch.


The solid immersion lens (SIL) is now a critical tool in the  semiconductor industry to improve signal collection and  resolution in backside infrared optical inspection and failure  analysis [1]. The challenge of small feature sizes in integrated  circuits (ICs) combined with the requirement of a long  wavelength to penetrate the silicon substrate requires the use  of the highest possible numerical apertures (NAs), which can  only be achieved using SILs [2].


SIL imaging is implemented in a plano-convex geometry with  the convex surface of the SIL facing the backing objective of  the microscope and the planar surface in optical contact with  the substrate of the device under test (DUT). Two types of SIL  geometries create aberration-free imaging in a perfect, axial  system (Fig. 1).  The first type (Fig. 1, left), is the central SIL (cSIL) configuration, where light rays follows the radii to  focus at the center of the lens. The second is the aplanatic SIL (aSIL) configuration, where light refracts at the curved surface, focusing to the aplanatic point, a distance R/n below the center  of the immersion lens (Fig. 1, right), where R is the radius and  n the index. The aSIL increases the NA of the imaging system  by a factor of n (to a maximum value of n when the sine of the  cone angle equals unity, n=3.5 for silicon), and is currently the  only method for non-destructive sub-100 nm device function  evaluations.


Figure 1:  SIL geometries. Left: Central SIL. Right: Aplanatic  SIL. Both geometries are theoretically aberration-free, but the  aSIL has larger NA, allowing higher resolution. The figures  accurately show the NA of the SILs with the cSIL at a NA of


2.6 and the aSIL at a NA of 3.5.


There are several sources of aberration that can lead to  deviations from perfect imaging in the use of SILs in imaging  real DUTs. Even small mismatches between the actual  thickness of the substrate and that for which the SIL was  designed will lead to significant aberration, as aberration-free  imaging is only possible at the center or aplanatic point of the  SIL. In the case of a central SIL, the tolerance to mismatch is  relatively high, whereas for the aplanatic configuration the  tolerance to mismatch is low (Fig. 2). In this work, we study  and correct for the aberration due to substrate mismatch, currently considered one of the major impediments to industry  adoption of aSIL technology.


Figure 2:  Simulation on Strehl ratio vs. substrate thickness  using aSIL and cSIL with optimal substrate thickness of  100µm . Diffraction-limited performance is defined as a Strehl  ratio in excess of 0.8. Lower Strehl ratios imply higher  aberrations


Experiment


We explore chip thickness mismatch by using a deformable  mirror (DM) to compensate spherical aberrations caused by a  deliberate 96 to 112 µm variation across a test chip [3-6]. Light from the fiber-coupled laser was directed onto a MEMS  deformable mirror (DM, from Boston Micromachines Corp.) that has an active aperture size of 4 mm and 140 actuators (Fig. 3). The DM plane was conjugated to the back pupil plane  of the objective (Mitutoyo 10× 0.26NA NIR infinitycorrected). Rays from the objective were focused through an  aSIL with a radius of 2.350 ± 0.001 mm and height of 2.922 ±


0.001 mm. The reflected focused spot from the chip was  imaged onto an InGaAs camera (C1 in Fig. 3). Since openloop DM shapes were used for aberration compensation, it is  critical to make sure the DM active aperture is well aligned  with the objective back pupil aperture [7]; any de-centering of  the DM from the pupil aperture will add off-axis aberrations to  the system. Therefore, we implemented a flip mirror (M1 in  Fig. 3) to create interferometric patterns to ensure DM  centering by adjusting the symmetrical interferometric pattern  in the objective back pupil aperture. This was observed by a  second InGaAs camera (C2 in Fig. 3) that was conjugated to  the DM and back pupil plane (Fig. 4).


Figure 3: Aplanatic SIL imaging geometries. LS: 1340 nm  fiber-coupled laser. DM: deformable mirror. L1: fiber  collimation lens. L2-7: lens. M1: flip mirror. M2: fold mirror. OBJ: objective lens. ASIL: aplanatic SIL. S: substrate. ST: 3-axis stage. C1-2: InGaAs camera.


Figure 4: Interferometric patterns on the InGaAs camera (C2  in Fig. 3) ensuring objective pupil and DM alignment. a). DM  flattened. b). Middle four actuators actuated. c). Defocus  shape on the DM. d). Spherical shape on the DM.


We modeled the system in a ray tracing software, ZEMAX (Radiant ZEMAX, LLC) to determine the extent of the  tolerance of the aplanatic configuration to thickness error of  the substrate. Light from an object was directed into a paraxial  lens, used to model a well-corrected microscope objective at a  design NA of 0.25, leading to an overall system NA of 3.05. The light was focused through an aplanatic SIL designed to be  used with a 107 µm thick substrate. Spherical aberration in the  axial direction was visualized by simulating the PSF  numerically as a function of focal point translation (axial  translation of the backing objective with respect to the SIL). The simulations were done on 96 µm (11 µm thinner than  ideal) and 107 µm (ideal) thick substrates with a focal  translation from -50 µm to +50 µm in steps of 1 µm (Fig. 5).  In our simulation we also varied the thickness of the substrate  from 95 µm to 113 µm in steps of 0.1 µm; at each thickness  the system was re-optimized by allowing refocusing, varying  the distance between the objective lens and the SIL. For each  thickness, we determined the Strehl ratio, plotted in Fig. 6. Our  modelling shows it is only possible to achieve the diffraction  limit if the substrate thickness is accurate to -3 / +2 µm before  spherical aberration begins to degrade performance (Fig. 6). Since typical polishing tolerances on silicon devices are of the  order of ± 3 -5 µm, correcting these aberrations is necessary to  localize and detect defects.

We landed the aSIL on 5 different locations where the  substrate thicknesses were 96 µm, 99 µm, 102 µm, 107 µm  and 112 µm (experimental configuration seen in Fig. 3) to  perform the correction. Since the focused spot intensity was


700


dependent on the contact condition between the SIL and


94 96 98 100 102 104 106 108 110 112 114


substrate for each landing, it was difficult to directly compare


Substrate thickness (um)


the spot intensity before and after aberration compensation. Instead, we determined the amount of spherical correction  needed to achieve highest spot intensity at each location. As  predicted, a greater thickness deviation from the ideal case  required more aberration correction. The optimum wavefront  RMS spherical corrections were +700 nm, +500 nm, +300 nm  and -600 nm for substrate thicknesses of 96 µm, 99 µm, 102 µm and 112 µm respectively. In each case, we were able to  increase peak intensity by at least a factor of two as a result of  spherical aberration compensation. Any DM wavefront  correction applied to the situation of the 107µm thick substrate  only made the spot intensity lower, indicating a close-to-ideal  substrate thickness and aSIL combination. By fitting the  experimental data with 3rd order polynomials (Fig. 7, red), we  noted a strong correlation between the form of the Strehl ratio  and amount of spherical compensation required for different  substrate thicknesses. We also confirmed our assumption that  the dominant aberration term in the system was spherical  aberration, because the experimental data matched well with  ZEMAX simulation (Fig. 6)


To demonstrate compensation of spherical aberration due to  substrate thickness deviation from ideal, we explored in detail  the situation of a substrate measuring 8 µm thinner than

optimal. The experimental apparatus (see Fig. 3) allows  adjustment of two parameters: focal translation and magnitude  of first order spherical aberration applied to the wavefront by  the DM. The axial section of the expected PSF was simulated  numerically using ZEMAX as a function of focal translation  with no spherical aberration compensation. As expected, the  PSF shows significant deviation from the diffraction limited  ideal at a nominal focal translation of 0 µm (Figs. 8a and 8c). Consistent with the simulations shown previously in Fig. 4, it  is possible to increase the peak intensity of the PSF by  offsetting the focal translation, but this does not compensate  the spherical aberrations (see Figs. 8b and 8d). However, at the  nominal focal translation of 0µm, applying a compensating  spherical aberration of the wavefront with the DM results in a  narrower PSF with significantly larger intensity (Figs. 8e, 8f, 8g). No combination of non-zero focal translation and  spherical compensation produces a PSF with comparable  intensity.


Discussion


In this paper, we considered only spherical aberration, an onaxis aberration due to the substrate thickness mismatch. In real  FA situations, confocal laser scanning systems will introduce  off-axis aberrations, such as astigmatism, coma and higher  order asymmetric terms. The larger the size of the field of  view, then the larger the scan angle, causing greater off-axis  aberrations in the system. Based on our simulations, off-axis  aberrations are almost negligible for a field of view size  smaller than 10×10 µm 2. In theory, a MEMS DM, which has  an update rate about 3~10 kHz, can be used for off-axis  aberration compensation, but at a cost of frame rate of the  scanning system.


We have performed open-loop spherical correction on an aSIL  configuration using a MEMS deformable mirror when the  substrate thickness deviates from designed thickness. We have  shown a spot intensity increase more than a factor of two and  spot size decrease following aberration compensation, demonstrating the DM is a good candidate for increasing  substrate thickness tolerance in aSIL imaging. We also show  excellent agreement between modelling and the experiment. This approach can be used for next generation silicon IC fault  analysis where higher NA aSIL is required.

Fault Isolation of Open Defects Using Space Domain Reflectometry


Mayue Xie, Zhiguo Qian, Mario Pacheco, Zhiyong Wang, Rajen Dias


Intel Corporation, Chandler, AZ, USA


Vladimir Talanov


Neocera, LLC, Beltsville, MD, USA


Recently, a new approach for isolation of open faults in  integrated circuits (ICs) was developed. It is based on  mapping the radio-frequency (RF) magnetic field produced by  the defective part fed with RF probing current, giving the  name to Space Domain Reflectometry (SDR). SDR is a noncontact and nondestructive technique to localize open defects  in package substrates, interconnections and semiconductor  devices. It provides 2D failure isolation capability with defect  localization resolution down to 50 microns. It is also capable  of scanning long traces in Si. This paper describes the  principles of the SDR and its application for the localization of  open and high resistance defects.  It then discusses some  analysis methods for application optimization, and gives  examples of test samples as well as case studies from actual  failures.


One particular challenge of fault isolation for electronic  packages is accurate localization of open defects.  Such  defects include cracked traces, delaminated vias, non-wet  bumps, bump fractures, and other package or interconnect  structures that result in device open failure.  The main  approach for localizing these defects today is time domain  reflectometry (TDR) or similar technology. TDR sends a short  electrical pulse into the device and monitors the reflections.  These reflections can correspond to shorts, opens, bends in a  wire, normal interfaces between devices, or high resistance  defects.  Ultimately, any feature associated with a change in  the trace characteristic impedance produces a TDR response.  Comparison of this response to that obtained on a good part  allows pinpointing the failure site. Recent development in  TDR technique has achieved very high signal-to-noise ratio  and much improved spatial resolution [1].


R R C


Z


2


For more than a decade, Magnetic Field Imaging (MFI) has  been used for shorts and leakages localization in the packages


[2] and dies [3]. The technique employs a Superconducting  Quantum Interference Device (SQUID) sensor operating at  kHz frequency. MFI for packaged devices is accomplished by  measuring the vertical component of magnetic field produced  by the current flowing via the short circuit. Magnetic field data  are transformed into current density images, which typically  show higher gradients at localized narrow current paths, or  dissolution of current density where narrower paths dissipate  to much broader conductors or power planes at short circuit


sites.  An AC bias is utilized on the sample to enable lock-in  amplification for noise reduction.


In this paper, we show that by increasing the bandwidth of the  SQUID electronics into the RF range [4], MFI can find open  defects in packages and dies, through imaging magnetic field  in the space domain, giving the name to Space Domain  Reflectometry (SDR). This paper describes the principle of the  SDR and its applications for localization of physical and  electrical open defects. It then discusses some analysis  methods for application optimization, and gives examples of  test samples as well as results of case studies from actual  failures.


Fundamentals and theory of SDR


DC and low frequency signals, normally used in electrical  probing of ICs, cannot induce a current flow in the  interconnect structure interrupted by the open. This changes, however, if the signal frequency is brought into the RF range, where the open-circuited trace can be treated as a transmission  line of characteristic impedance Z0 terminated with the open  characterized by the lumped element Zopen, as shown in Fig. 1.


Figure 1. Lumped element representation of the open failure. Z0 is  the characteristic impedance of transmission line formed by the trace. By modeling the open as a parallel circuit with resistance R  and capacitance C, we obtain for its impedance:

Figure 4. MFI system based on scanning SQUID RF microscope.


RF-MFI images the magnetic field of a standing wave  following the failed structure to the vicinity of the open, which  allows recovering the standing wave current profile and to find  its node, thereby locating the open. Unlike in TDR where the  failure location is identified by distance (1D) measured along  the failing trace, SDR yields the failure isolation in 2D. According to the principles of near-field microwave  microscopy (e.g., see [6]), SDR’s spatial resolution is not  limited by the frequency but rather the SQUID (probe) size  and the SQUID-to-sample separation. Case Studies


This section contains examples of successful application of the  SDR technique to various open failure modes of a flip chip  package.  The spatial resolution of SDR on real package level  failure is to be studied through these cases.


Case 1. The first real product application case is a test vehicle (TV) with a serpentine structure of finer trace-to-trace pitch. This TV failed open after extensive reliability testing. The  SDR scanning distance was about 1 mm limited by the  presence of the die above the package. As a result, the SDR  scanning parameters were not optimized and instead used the “normal” setups, and the resolution for this case is expected to  be much worse than the one shown at the previous session. The current image at the vicinity of the failure, its optical  picture and actual failure location and failure mode are shown  in Fig. 8. In this case, normal scan was able to isolate the  failure to within a single serpentine line. However, magnetic  field cancelation due to alternating flow of current in adjacent  serpentine lines prevents the technique from resolving the  current in a single line. Improvements using focused area scan  with higher frequency and smaller steps and reduced SQUIDwindow distance will enable resolution improvement.


Figure 8: A sample with a serpentine open failure at one of the traces  was scanned using SDR with normal scanning parameters. The topleft image shows the current density distribution with open location  highlighted. The optical image at the bottom-left shows design layout  of the trace structure. The right image shows the actual failure  location and mode. The SDR current image identifies the first  serpentine trace with crack, which is about 400um away from its  physical location.


1. Basic principle of EOTPR system


A schematic diagram of the EOTPR system is shown in Fig. 1 (a). EOTPR measures an impulse response rather than the step  response of conventional TDR. Therefore, for EOTPR, high  impedance discontinuities in a DUT result in positive peaks, as contrast, low impedance discontinuities result in negative  features in the reflected signal. Fig. 1 (b) shows the EOTPR  results from a resolution test structure. Here, a high-frequency  probe is moved in 10 µm increments along the test structure  and ten waveforms are recorded at each position. The 10 µm  shifting in position of the reflection from the end of the  structure can be resolved clearly, demonstrating the ability of  the EOTPR system to achieve a 10 µm distance-to-defect  accuracy in a specified test structure. Thus, the EOTPR system  is chosen to evaluate its distance-to-defect accuracy and fault  isolation capabilities on 2D and 2.5D flip-chip packages, and  this paper presents results and discussion.


2. EOTPR measurement procedure


All of the EOTPR measurements are performed by bringing a  110 GHz circuit probe into contact with the pins of interest on  the DUT. EOTPR pulses are then launched into the DUT and  portions of the pulse are reflected back as it encounters  changes in impedance within the integrated circuit such as  open or short circuit defects. These reflections are recorded as  a function of time and result in the EOTPR waveforms. A  comparative mode is used where a failed device impedance  profile is compared with two reference impedance profiles, a  golden device (or known good device) and bare substrate.

Results and Discussion:


Case study 1: To evaluate the accuracy of EOTPR for  distance-to-defect measurements, a focused ion beam (FIB) was used to generate defects at different known locations from  a C4 bump pad in three identical samples on a 2D flip-chip  package. SEM images of the area around the C4 bump pad  and FIB trench are shown in Fig. 2 for each of the samples. The physical distance of the FIB hole from the C4 bump pad  is also shown in the table in the bottom of Fig. 2.


Bad 2


Each sample was measured from the C4 bump side with an  EOTPR system and the results were displayed in Fig. 3. Here, the EOTPR waveforms consist of a strong negative feature  which is due to the reflection of the EOTPR waveform at the  FIB generated fault. The fact that the feature is negative  indicates that the FIB has generated short circuit faults in all of  the samples. In Fig. 3, the results matched very well between  the actual distance created by FIB and the distance measured  by EOTPR system, which indicated the high-resolution  detection capability of EOTPR.

Conventional TDR was also performed on the same sample  for comparison. A comparative method was applied both on  EOTPR and TDR by using the reference good sample, bare  substrates without silicon die and silicon TSV interposer.


Fig. 5 showed conventional TDR measurement result. It  indicated that the failed sample waveform overlapped with the  reference interposer sample, which suggested the open  location was between the silicon die and interposer. However, it cannot tell the exact open location. By contrast, EOTPR  measurement results are showed in Fig. 6. By applying a  velocity factor of 0.4 to account for the speed of pulse  propagation in the device, we obtained a distance-to-defect of  23 um away from the top of the interposer, which is only 5um  deviation from the real defect.


Case study 3: The DUT failed the continuity test with  automated test equipment (ATE). The fault was confirmed as  an open failure with curve-tracing on the reported failing I/O  pad. EOTPR was performed on the same DUT and the


measurement result is shown in Fig. 7.  It revealed that the  failed unit waveform showed a positive peak indicating an  open circuit prior to the bare substrate termination. This  indicates that the fault is inside the package substrate.


To obtain an accurate fault location, we measured the distance  from the open in the failed unit to the bare substrate C4  termination and applied a velocity factor of 0.5 to account for  the speed of pulse propagation in the package substrate. The  result showed a distance to defect of 9.63 mm from the C4  bump pad (Fig. 7). Comparing this to the package design  layout, EOTPR results indicated the fault was located at the  Metal 7 layer. This was confirmed by subsequently physical  failure analysis. A crack was found on the copper trace at  Metal 7 (Fig. 8 (a)). The actual distance-to-defect from the C4  bump pad was 9636.76 µm (Fig. 8 (b)), determined by using  the distances from design layout. The actual distance-to-defect  is in good agreement with that determined from EOTPR, which further highlights the accuracy of this technique.

Conclusion:


The experimental results clearly demonstrate the capability of  EOTPR in defect-to-distance determination. This highresolution technique can be used to localize the failure  location for 3D packages with more complex structures.

Novel plasma FIB/SEM for high speed failure analysis and real time imaging of  large volume removal


T Hrnčíř, F Lopour, M Zadražil


TESCAN a.s., Libušina třída 21, 623 00 Brno, Czech Republic   tomas.hrncir@tescan.cz


A Delobbe, O Salord, P Sudraud


Orsay Physics S.A., 95 avenue des Monts Auréliens, ZAC Saint Charles, F-13710 Fuveau, France


The standard Ga Focused Ion Beam (FIB) technology is facing  new challenges because of a request for large volume removal. This is mainly true in the field of failure analysis. This article  will present the first combined tool which can fulfill this new  requirement. This tool, called FERA from TESCAN a.s., offers the combination of a high resolution Scanning Electron  Microscope (SEM) and a high current focused ion beam with  Xe plasma ion source (i-FIB) [1] designed by Orsay  Physics S.A.


The Xe plasma ion source offers an extremely high milling and  material removal rate, which is important in many fields of  applications, such as failure analysis in the semiconductor  industry (e.g. TSV or flip chip cross sectioning) or MEMS  fabrication and analysis. The high resolution scanning electron  microscope column expands the intrinsic advantages of the Xe


Ga FIB


10000


plasma ion source. SEM allows observation of the sample


Plasma FIB


during the milling process by utilizing two separate specially  designed backscattered electron detectors and a precise end1000


point detection based on SEM or FIB image information. Of  course there is no need to change the stage position to check  the milling result. Moreover, SEM offers imaging of the


resulting cross section and EDX or EBSD analysis, all being


integrated in the same instrument. With powerful integrated


software, the creation of complex tasks can be automated (e.g. FI[nm]B image resolution 10 3D tomography).


The Gallium Liquid Metal Ion Source (Ga LMIS) is the most


commonly used ion source in FIB applications. It has a


reasonable resolution (up to 2.5 nm in current commercial


0.0010.010.1110100100010000


state of the art ion columns), but only medium sputtering rate, poor secondary ionization coefficient and it suffers from ion  implantation. There is a wide portfolio of LMIS based on other  metals or alloys. Most of them are capable of compensating for  some of the disadvantages, e.g. silicon has no dopant effect  and gold clusters have a higher sputtering rate [2]. In general, however, none of them is suitable for rapid milling because  they can only deliver probe current up to several tens of nA to  the sample (maximum current in commercial instrument  specifications varies from 40 to 65 nA for the most powerful  Ga FIB).


On the other hand, the Xe plasma ion source is able to deliver  probe current higher than 2 µA, which makes the milling of  large structures much more time effective. The sputtering rate  is also higher compared to Ga. For example, the sputtering rate  for silicon at a primary energy of 30 kV was measured to be


3.2 atoms per ion for Xe compared to 2.4 atoms per ion for  Ga. This means we can obtain typically 50 times faster milling  with the Xe beam than with the Ga beam. Moreover, FIB  image resolution at currents higher than several nA is better for  a Xe beam because Ga beam resolution at high probe currents  suffers from increasing spherical aberration (Figure 1).


This article will be focused on failure analysis examples. The  different steps of extra large cross sections will be detailed (deposition of protective layer, rough milling, polishing). The  goal of this presentation will be to show a new tool that could  change everyday life in the world of failure analysis by  reducing the milling time tremendously.


FIB probe current [nA]


Figure 1: Comparison of FIB image resolution depending on  the probe current, solid line is for Ga LMIS FIB (Orsay  Cobra), the dashed one is for Xe plasma ion source FIB (Orsay i-FIB).


Design of the machine


The SEM column is uses a standard Schottky filament, offering imaging resolution up to 1 nm at beam energy 30 kV. The i-FIB column is mounted at an angle 55° towards SEM  column axis and it has a dedicated pumping system to pump  Xe gas used for the plasma ion source. i-FIB pumping and  other controls are fully integrated into TESCAN vacuum unit  and control software. A picture of the machine (FERA3 GMH) is shown in Figure 2.


Figure 2: TESCAN FERA3 GMH, high resolution SEM  combined with plasma FIB column and automated load lock  for fast sample exchange.


The design of patented i-FIB plasma source is shown in  Figure 3.


High purity Xe gas is injected into the gun through a  motorized microleak valve. After starting the plasma in the  gun by using the low power microwave generator, the gun  pressure is kept at a stable value and the gun can operate for a  virtually unlimited time. This is a significant advantage against  Ga LMIS sources, which require regular heating treatment and  their maximum lifetime is limited by the consumption of Ga to ~1500 hours of the beam time only.


Ground


High voltage


Figure 3: i-FIB column schematics.


Currently it seems the only consumable parts of the i-FIB  column, in addition to the Xe bottle, are beam limiting  apertures. These are damaged approximately ten times more  quickly than apertures in Ga FIB columns and special  procedures must be used in the control software to reduce the  damage and to increase their lifetime.


i-FIB is able to deliver probe currents to the sample higher  than 2 µA for rough milling and less than 10 pA for high  resolution imaging. The imaging resolution is less than 30 nm, which makes it possible to make nice FIB SE images of many  cross sections, but Ga LMIS performance for imaging is much  better (see Figure 1). The main advantage of plasma FIB over


TESCAN


Ga LMIS is the great enhancement of the milling rate, because  probe currents are much higher. At probe currents higher than  several tens of nA, the resolution of plasma FIB exceeds that  of the Ga LMIS FIB, as the spot size increase does not depend  so strongly on the probe current like in a case of Ga LMIS FIB (Figure 1).


Due to the high sputtering rate, the plasma FIB/SEM system  must be designed to handle relatively high amounts of material  redeposited from the sample. The typical semiconductor  sample consists of a high amount of Si, combined with  conductive layers (e.g. Cu) and insulators (e.g. resin molding  compounds). This combination of different materials is  redeposited on all surfaces, which are reachable by a straight  path from the milling spot on the sample. In contrast to Ga  FIB, for plasma FIB the thickness of such redeposited layers  starts to be a problem for some detectors because at some  point the redeposited layer can block the electron signal  effectively.


FERA electron detectors are designed in a special way to be  resistant to this effect. The Everhart-Thornley Secondary  Electron (ET-SE) detector is protected in two ways: (1) protected from redeposition of sputtered material by  positioning the detector far from the milling spot, and (2) protected from extremely high FIB-generated SE current by  software protection.


A backscattered Electron (BSE) detector has to be protected  too. Tescan FIB instruments traditionally use the BSE detector  for live observation during the milling process. However the  standard BSE detector, using a YAG scintillator, is located too  close to the sample and it can be reached by the redeposited  material too easily. Therefore we have integrated  another BSE


I


detector, located inside SEM column and far away from the


Motorized

sample, where it is much less affected by the redeposition


(scialicy


AmpiWier


process. This detector, called in-beam BSE, can be used for


Gasinier


sample observation during the milling, even when milling at


HF cource


the highest FIB current (higher than 2 µA).


The standard BSE detector can still be used, its main

The geometry of both detectors is shown in Figure 4.


Figure 4: Geometry of two BSE detectors, which are specially  designed to be able to withstand the high amount of the  redeposited material.


Both SEM and plasma FIB columns are able to deliver beam  energy up to 30 kV. FIB energy was 30 kV for all ion milling  and deposition processes described in this text.


The SEM column offers the possibility of chemical analysis (energy-dispersive X-ray spectroscopy - EDX) and because of  the low magnetic field coming from the objective lens it is  possible to use it to collect non-distorted Electron  Backscattered Diffraction (EBSD) patterns.


Flip chip cross section


The typical C4 or flip chip cross section (cross section through  the solder ball, bond pad and underlying IC) is shown in  Figure 5. The diameter of the solder ball was 100 µm and it  took 15 minutes to prepare this cross section, first milling at  2 µA and then polishing at 300 nA. Note the polishing current  is much higher than the maximum current of all commercially


100.00μm


available Ga LMIS ion beams, but due to the better resolution  at high current for plasma ion beam (Figure 1) we can polish  the cross section at such high current, making the preparation  time much faster. Using a Ga beam from the beginning, it  would be necessary to work on the same cross section for more  than 10 hours. Such enhancement of the preparation rate  allows us to prepare more cross sections at the same time or to  increase cross section width and cutting more adjacent bumps  in a reasonable time.


Figure 5: Flip chip cross section, imaged at 10 kV beam  energy using BSE detector. Sample provided by courtesy of  IBM, Bromont, Canada.


Through Silicon Vias (TSV) analysis


Cross sectioning of TSV samples is another typical application  of the plasma FIB/SEM instrument. As in the case of the flip  chip cross section, here it also helps to reduce the preparation  time from many hours to several tens of minutes only. Unlike  cross sectioning through the solder ball, a typical TSV sample  usually requires a protection layer on the surface, prepared by  Pt or C ion beam induced deposition with the Gas Injection  System (GIS). This allows studying the cross section of a Cu  cap on the surface, without damaging it by the tail of the ion  beam during the rough milling.


Figure 6: Cross section of through silicon vias, with Pt  protection layer prepared by fast Xe ion beam induced  deposition before the milling. The sample was imaged at beam  energy 15 kV using BSE detector.


Preparation of protection layers by plasma FIB/GIS is very  fast compared to Ga LMIS ion beam induced deposition. A Xe  plasma FIB at 100 nA probe current is able to prepare a


BSE


Sample  In-beam


--E


50.00 μm


protection layer 300x30x30 μm in 40 minutes, so that the  deposition rate is higher than 100 μm3s-1. When using a Ga  beam for Pt deposition during e.g. TEM lamella preparation, the deposition rate is only around 0.15 μm3s-1 (although it is  prepared on much smaller area). So the faster deposition rate  of the plasma FIB accompanies the faster milling rate and this  allows preparation of protection layers or conductive/insulator  layers for IC edit much faster, compared to a Ga LMIS ion  beam.


Figure 6 shows a typical TSV cross section. The rough cut was  400 μm long, 100 μm wide and 50 μm deep. The total time  needed for this cut was 45 minutes at a Xe ion beam current of


30 μm


2 μA. For the final polishing, we have selected a row of 4 vias  only (100 μm long) and polished them with a beam current of  300 nA for 15 min to reveal the required row, then the final  polishing has been performed at 100 nA for 15 min. Deposition of the protection layer (100 μm long, 10 μm wide  and 10 μm high) took several minutes only.


MEMS sample analysis


Figures 7 (overview) and 8 (detail) show a cross section  through a MEMS sample, revealing the structure located more  than 200 μm below the sample surface. The milling time was  1 hour 20 minutes at a Xe beam current of 2 μA. As the bulk  material of the sample was Si, no protection layer was  necessary. The thick Si layer worked as a natural protection for  the structure of interest here, which was much thinner than the  Si layer above that, and it allowed us to finish the polishing of  the structure at the highest FIB current; no further polishing at  the reduced current was necessary.


Figure 7: Cross section of MEMS sample, revealing the site of  interest located more than 200 μm below the surface. Image  was taken at beam energy 10 kV using BSE detector.


Figure 8: Cross section of MEMS sample, detailed image of  the structure located more than 200 μm below the surface. The  image shows excellent quality of the polished surface, without  any visible damage or curtaining effect introduced by the ion  beam. Image was taken at beam energy 10 kV using BSE  detector.


Sample was provided by courtesy of STMicroelectronics, Cornaredo, Italy


Several applications of the novel Xe plasma FIB/SEM  instrument were shown with respect to the failure analysis. The  performance of the instrument was tested and discussed in   comparison to Ga LMIS FIB systems. The Xe plasma FIB  offers much higher milling rate, greatly reducing the time  necessary for many failure analysis tasks. The machine allows  live observation of the milling process using two specially  designed BSE detectors which allow precise end point  detection ability.

FemtoFarad/TeraOhm Endpoint Detection for Microsurgery of Integrated Circuit Devices


Jim Colvin


36217 Worthing Dr., Newark, CA 94560 USA


Interactive electrical endpoint detection when thinning  conductive and capacitive materials opens the door to  approaching a suspect site in an IC without relying on the  traditional iterative approach.  Controlled approach of  embedded conductors in insulators (packages) as well as  controlled die thinning with submicron control will be  shown, allowing safe approach to the desired feature  without overshoot.


Creating access points in an IC has traditionally required  iterative guesswork to reach the target.  Laser  decapsulation places the surface at risk due to ablation  damage caused by the glass beads.  Non-uniformity of  removal is problematic both chemically and with the  laser.  This paper will demonstrate a novel endpointing  technique with, currently, <1µm control using capacitive  detection through the package or insulator.  Thinning  with a resolution below 1 micron enables controlled and  repeatable removal of material, obviating the need to “stop and look”1,2.  Embedded defects can now be  quickly approached both horizontally and vertically, greatly reducing the chance of overshoot by monitoring  during the removal, the local capacitance and resistance  as the insulator is thinned.  Masking methods to expose  multiple areas will be discussed along with local  Capacitive/Resistive mapping of embedded features.  Resolution improvements using guard ring technology is  anticipated to improve resolution to <40nm for  electrostatic detection.  Resistive detection is limited by  tip geometry.


Background


Experimental setup:


A modified UltraTec ASAP-1 IPS digital polisher along  with an electrically isolated mount plate, shown in figure  1, was used3.


Figure 1:  Image of 208 PQFP mounted to test plate with  crystalbond wax.  At least one of the leads needs to be in  contact with the mounting plate to the die.  Note: the


water stays in the cavity due to surface tension, defining  the upper plate of the capacitor.


Figure 2 is a basic circuit diagram of the endpoint  detector system.  Since requirements are to sense  capacitively down to 10 fF and resistively up to 1 TΩ.  A  high impedance instrumentation amplifier was used at the  front end of the detection circuitry to monitor voltage  swing on the mounting plate.  Capacitance of the plate  was increased from 20 pF to 100 pF based on current  experimental results, but is adjustable to suit the need by  adding external capacitors (C1).  The charge on the plate  can drift with capacitance so it is necessary to add  approximately 1 TΩ of resistance (Bias at R2) tied to the  midpoint voltage to control drift.


Figure 2:  Circuit diagram of endpoint detector.


The upper and lower main plates were tied to ground to  minimize interference and parasitic capacitive influence  caused by head height changes.  The milling spindle was  configured to drive a coupled 3.3V pulse signal to the  milling/polishing tip.  See figure 3.  The mount plate was  isolated above the lower ground plate and tied to the  input of an instrumentation amplifier.


Figure 3:  Copper tape is added to form a ground shield  around the milling spindle.  This reduces AC interference  and crosstalk capacitance.  Tip electrostatic field focusing  can be improved with guard technology around the  milling spindle tip when used as a sensor.  This is  anticipated to improve sensitivity to <40nm and allow  improved local mapping.  Commercially available  sensors are reported to measure better than 12nm.4,5


Software/hardware was modified to do the following:


1. Differentially detect the capacitively coupled  charge transferred to the mount plate from the  spindle tip into the low fF range and high GΩ  range.


2. Sample and remove local AC interference.


3. Accumulate and average the data stream, displaying a running result during operation, and  control DC drift in purely capacitive  environments.


4. Provide force feedback control and closed loop  operation of the capacitive endpoint to auto stop  at a predetermined value.


Since distance is inversely proportional to capacitance for  a parallel plate capacitor, equation 1, the endpoint  reading accuracy drastically improves as the target  endpoint is approached.  A capacitive response yields a  square wave based on the ratio of charge transferred to  the lower sensor plate per equation 2 6,7.  The pulse rate is  differential at ¼ line frequency =7.5 Hz.  A resistive  component will show as an RC time constant based on  time to charge the 100 pF plate through the resistor, similar to figure 4.  The software samples and  discriminates capacitance (slope = 0) from resistance. Since DI water has resistivities of 20 MΩ-cm or less it  will appear as a short circuit in endpoint8.  Mineral oil  and crystal bond wax (hot wax) are covalent and  therefore non-conductive. Both can serve as masking  materials for exposed areas, opening the door for various  access techniques.


Any crack or breach in the insulator will show  conductively, depending on the chosen fluid, providing a  safe early halt to the process.  A crack causing early  endpoint detection can be masked with mineral oil or  crystalbond, then DI or distilled water reapplied to  continue the process until reaching bonds or any other  desired endpoint.

Resistive Endpoint Detection:


The part is attached to the plate using hot wax with one  or more leads in contact to form the lower plate.  The  desired area to open and tool are selected or loaded from  a previous recipe.  Tilt is corrected, and then a  preliminary pocket 100µm deep is cut.  The surface is  wiped clean and DI water is added only in the pocket.  Surface tension holds the water in the pocket; however, it  is of little concern since capacitance values are secondary  for this example.  The milling process proceeds until the


endpoint jumps with a reduced resistance value due to  bond wire contact with the DI water, typically through a  local crack or pocket in the plastic proximal to a bond  wire, typically a few minute operation.  Figures 5, 6 and  7 show the result.

Masking the Resistive Endpoint:


If an endpoint is obtained due to an unwanted area, such  as a lead finger, crack or exceptionally high wire loop, being exposed, the pocket can be rinsed out, dried and  coated with a thin layer of mineral oil to isolate the  endpoint signal.  The area is retargeted and DI water  added.  The disturbed areas from milling will wet with all  other areas remaining isolated.  Endpoint will occur on  approach of next feature related to milling area.

Locating a resistive leakage zone:


If an endpoint read has occurred, close inspection may  not reveal the local region responsible.  If the location  needs to be identified, such as a suspected crack, a bead  of water is suspended from the tip and the tip is moved  slowly over the cavity hovering above the surface.  A  positive reading shows the proximity of the leakage.  The  water trail evaporates behind the tip allowing the area to  be triangulated.  This method was used to identify the  highest exposed bond wire in figure 7.  Local passivation  removal with endpoint using colloidal silica or other  ionic polishing compounds is accomplished using the  same principles outlined above.


Potential Issues:


1. Dry milling resistive endpoint requires the tool  to physically contact the wire resulting in  excessive damage.  Additionally tribo-charging  of the material during removal causes wild DC  swings on the endpoint.  Use DI or similar  solution.


2. False early endpoint can occur if package leads  or surface caps couple to the cavity due to  residue or splashing.  Masking methods can be  used if needed.


3. Die tilt needs to be checked on approach at  around 60µm and corrected using tip scan  methods to map the local capacitance variation  over the die.


4. Capacitance data can be erroneous due to die  coat, tilt or delamination issues.  A resistive  endpoint is typical for delamination or cracks to


prevent die damage since the liquid will enter  and wet a bond underneath.


5. Lateral parasitic capacitance was largely  ignored, as was the issue of varying dielectric  constants in the material.  Special cases may  require characterization.  Generally the parasitic  from the lead dress and lead frame can be  ignored since the primary signal will dominate  on approach to endpoint.


Resistive Endpoint Thinning of Silicon


The resistive endpoint method can be used to thin silicon  substrates on a variety of packages.  The setup differs  since polarity now matters and the substrate leakages will  be well below the GΩ range, actually into the 100kΩ  range or less for tip scan measurements.  Several  modifications and observations for the setup are required:


1. Capacitance plate measurement method will not  work in most cases due to the conductive nature  of the substrate.


2. Resistive substrate method covering the pocket  area using an ionic liquid will result in low  impedance readings making local thinning 2D  readings problematic.  The tip will need to be  locally conductive (tip core insert or tip  containing one or more soft alloys of Cu, Al, C, Pb, Sn, Sb, Au, In) and covalent compounds will  need to be used, such as oil.


3. Need to sweep through positive and negative  measurements to determine the IV curve and  resulting breakdown.  Resistance alone can be  used but threshold is independent of resistance  so it represents the best metric for thickness.


4. Conductive tip design and interface becomes  important since the tip cannot rely on the fluid  to provide conductivity but must maintain  intimate contact with the silicon as it is scanned  and removed.


Characterization and Calibration of Silicon Endpoint:


The part can be mounted while on the board or  individually as shown in figures 14 and 15.  Since we are  not concerned with capacitance endpoint and the  impedances are in the KΩ range the large board poses no  problem.  The die surface is tilt corrected and normally  heated to relax the stress as described in another paper in  this proceedings10.  For this experiment, die curvature  will be deliberately left in order to characterize and  validate the endpoint method.  The surface was polished  to 50µm globally, then pocket polished further with a  2mm diameter Xylem tool with the traditional iterative  method.  Once an open area was observed the process  was halted.  Figure 16 is the result. A scan line at 0,-690  to -1000,-690 µm was chosen, based on a 0,0 origin in  the center of the pocket.
