Substrate thinning of epitaxial or degenerate substrates is


accomplished with similar methods except the substrate  leakage will change from low ohmic to a diode behavior  as the active area below the doped substrate is exposed.  Characterization is recommended on a sacrificial unit to  identify the expected electrical characteristic versus  thickness for a given process technology.


It should be clear from this work that the Ultra Tec  ASAP-1 IPS system has been reconfigured as a scanning  probe microscope (SPM) meeting the three basic  definitions of an SPM12:


1. A scanning tip.


2. X, Y, Z  dimension scan control.


3. Force feedback.


Current can be injected or an electrostatic field formed or  measured from the scanning tip. Local 2D/3D  capacitance/magnetic/conductive SPM mapping will be  added to the software. Mapping, in combination with  milling and polishing capabilities, eliminates the need to  go between different tools – offering a clear step towards  the universal tool that FA Engineers are keen on  finding13. Moreover, the combination approach opens the  door to in-situ characterization, including local injection  of current into an active substrate for latchup or similar  sensitive node localization with a scanning tip.  Mapping  and feedback control of local conductive, electrostatic, Si Thickness vs.Vt


and magnetic fields interactive with material removal

Controlled microsurgery has been demonstrated with  capacitive and resistive feedback control.  This opens the  door for improved resolution with SQUID Microscopy, InSb thermography (Lock-In), Thermal Laser Stimulus  and similar techniques without fully exposing the die  topside or stopping at the desired critical target thickness  on silicon from the backside.  Multiple pockets can be  created with each prior pocket masked enabling complex  multiple access points to an IC or multi chip module.  Dendritic leakage issues can be monitored and  approached allowing the remaining 5µm to 10µm of  plastic to be ion milled for surface science analyses.  Plastic removal issues with copper wires, especially fine  pitch wires damaged with acid and/or Laser techniques, can also be alternatively exposed using slower plasma  methods due to much thinner plastic profiles.  Capacitance and resistive mapping of local features by


scanning/milling with the tip opens up a world of new  possibilities.

Cross Section Analysis of Cu Filled TSVs Based On High Throughput PlasmaFIB Milling


Frank Altmann, Jens Beyersdorfer, Jan Schischka, Michael Krause


Fraunhofer Institute for Mechanics of Materials, Halle, Germany  Frank.Altmann@iwmh.fraunhofer.de, Tel: +49-345-5589-139


German Franz, Laurens Kwakman


FEI Europe B.V., Eindhoven, The Netherlands


In this paper the new Vion™ Plasma-FIB system, developed  by FEI, is evaluated for cross sectioning of Cu filled Through  Silicon Via (TSV) interconnects. The aim of the study  presented in this paper is to evaluate and optimise different  Plasma-FIB (P-FIB) milling strategies in terms of performance  and cross section surface quality. The sufficient preservation  of microstructures within cross sections is crucial for  subsequent Electron Backscatter Diffraction (EBSD) grain  structure analyses and a high resolution interface  characterisation by TEM.


In order to (failure) analyse and (process) characterise newly  developed high density interconnect technologies based on  TSVs within vertically integrated devices, adapted target  preparation and analysis work flows are needed. These  frequently combine highly efficient site specific cross  sectioning and subsequent grain structure and interface  characterisation by Scanning and Transmission Electron  Microscopy (SEM, TEM) [1,2].


Nowadays, combined FIB/SEM systems are widely used for  physical failure analysis of CMOS devices as the focused ion  beam allows site specific specimen preparation with nanometre  precision. Standard FIB systems based on liquid metal ion  sources (LMIS) can maintain a properly focused Ga ion beam  up to a current of ~65 nA and achieve volume removal rates in  the range of a few μm3/s. These removal rates are well adapted  for cross section preparation in e.g. CMOS devices, however, for TSV interconnect analyses the relative slow milling  process becomes a crucial time limiting factor. Given the large  dimensions of the TSVs, ~100 x 100 μm2 cross sections have  to be prepared and for this purpose ~105 – 106 μm3 of material  has to be removed requiring multitudinous time of FIB milling. The new Vion Plasma-FIB system can produce focussed ion  beams with a much higher current and as such offers an  attractive alternative to the classical FIB systems for TSV  interconnect analyses. Hence the specimen preparation is still  site-specific but can be done 20 up to 100 times faster.


However, due to the existing surface topography and the  presence of different materials and microstructures, the P-FIB  milling rates do vary locally and massive “curtaining” may  occur, particularly at large area cross sections. These milling  artefacts can make subsequent SEM analyses or the  preparation of large area electron beam transparent specimens  for TEM analyses difficult or even impossible. To overcome  this problem, new milling strategies based on an automatized  specimen rocking technique were introduced recently [3].


In this study the surface quality resulting from these new  milling strategies is analysed in detail and evaluated for further  EBSD grain structure analyses.


TSV test specimen


The P-FIB milling and EBSD grain structure analyses were  primarily evaluated using blind TSVs of 50 μm in diameter  and 150 μm depth, fabricated in 300 µm thick Si wafers [4] (see Fig. 1). Etching of the TSVs was done by deep reactive  ion etching, using patterned photoresist as the etch mask. The  TSV dielectric layers consisted of 1 μm thermal oxide  followed by 0.5 μm of low stress LPCVD silicon nitride.


Figure 1: Silicon interposer substrate with arrays of Cu filled  TSVs


Highly conformal copper seed layers were deposited by  MOCVD, in preparation for TSV plating. Copper CMP was  used to remove the Cu plating overburden and to planarize the  TSVs. The specimens were analysed as fabricated and after  annealing at 400 °C for 3 h in a N2 atmosphere to reveal  changes in the Cu microstructure induced by thermal cycling.


Plasma-FIB System


We used a commercial available FEI Vion™ Plasma-FIB  system to evaluate milling performance, surface quality and its  overall applicability for large area cross sectioning of TSV  interconnects. The system is equipped with an ICP source  providing a focused Xe beam in a wide beam current range  from several pA up to 1.3 μA. The P-FIB image resolution at  low beam currents is <25 nm and the achievable spot size at  high beam currents is <5 μm. The system is equipped with gas  injection systems for Pt-deposition and a specific XeF2 gas  chemistry using a coaxial nozzle design applicable for fast Si  trenching. The new iFAST software allows fully automatized  milling operations applying different ion beam currents,


PFIB polished TSV


specimen moving and tilting including an automatized drift  correction. In order to demonstrate the performance of the  P-FIB a cross section of a Cu filled TSV of 50 μm diameter  and 150 μm depth was prepared. First a Pt protective layer  with dimensions of 80 μm x 10 μm x 3 μm was deposited on  top of the TSV using a 7 nA Xe-ion beam (see Fig. 2 left).

Figure 2: Plasma-FIB preparation on an embedded TSV

Single TSVs after grinding


structure - left: top view after deposition of the Pt protective  layer; right: TSV cross section after P-FIB polishing/rocking


Then coarse milling of a 140 μm x 60 μm box was done at


1.1 μA beam current for 20 min to reach a depth of about  200 μm. A subsequent pre-polishing step was performed by  box milling at 470 nA for 15 min. Final polishing was done at  59 nA tilting the specimen plus or minus 8° to reduce  curtaining. This specimen rocking procedure was repeated 20  times within a processing time of 25 min. The overall  processing for TSV cross sectioning takes about 1 h and can  be fully automatized using the iFAST software. The copper  grain structure of the TSV was imaged at 74 pA using the  continuous dynode electron multiplier detector (see Fig. 2  right).


Evaluation of Milling Regimes


To achieve exploitable cross sections of Cu filled TSV  structures for subsequent EBSD grain structure analyses, different P-FIB milling strategies were used. The results of  those strategies were analysed and compared in order to  optimise the milling performance and the cross section surface  quality and to investigate potential occurring effects caused by  high rate ion milling on the microstructure as well.


TSV cross sectioning


The first cross section was prepared by cleaving the Si  specimen along an array of TSVs and a following mechanical  polishing to approach the edge of several TSVs. This enables  an optimal access for the P-FIB milling and the possibility to  compare different milling regimes at the same Si specimen (see Fig. 3).


Figure 3: Cross section made by cleaving and further


mechanically polishing to approach the TSV edge, P-FIB  milling was performed to cross section the TSV centre


Prior to the P-FIB milling a protective Pt layer was deposited  in the Vion system on top of each TSV to minimise curtaining  artefacts caused by the surface topography of the TSV. Then, a  first P-FIB milling was performed to cross section through the  centre of the TSVs using beam currents of 570 nA for 10 min (step 1) and 230 nA for 7 min (step 2).


After this coarse milling a final P-FIB polishing step was  additionally performed to improve the surface quality, using  different milling parameters on each TSV. These parameters  were the beam current (27 nA or 74 nA), the milling pattern (box or line by line) and if the specimen was either static or in  automated “rocking-mode”.


In the “rocking-mode” the specimen is alternating tilted (plus  and minus 8°) laterally in-between the polishing steps. Thus a  further reduction of the curtaining artefacts is ensured (see Fig. 4 and 5).

Surface roughness and slope profile


The TSV specimens, prepared with these different P-FIB  parameters, were analysed using AFM in order to characterise  the surface roughness and the slope profile of the cross  sections (see Fig. 6).


Figure 6: AFM measurements of surface roughness and slope  profile of P-FIB cross sections


AFM topography maps of the surfaces after the final P-FIB  polishing with and without specimen rocking are presented in  Fig. 7. The reduced surface roughness after specimen  rocking/polishing can be clearly seen. A groove at the  SiO2/Si3N4 dielectric layer is observed due to a higher sputter  rate, which was not fully removed by the final polishing step,


but an additional FIB polishing should also remove this  artefact easily.

As a result optimal conditions for TSV cross sectioning and  further EBSD grain structure analysis requiring a low surface  roughness could be achieved by 27 nA final polishing applying  the rocking/polishing technique.

EBSD analysis of Cu filling


In order to optimise the TSV processing steps in terms of  reliability detailed investigations of the reliability limiting


inverse pole figure


failure mechanisms are essential. In particular the relationship  between microstructure, e.g. the local texture and the  reliability properties under temperature cycling have to be  investigated. EBSD is an efficient and practical technique for  grain structure analyses due to its excellent spatial resolution


[5] and its ease of use. The Electron Backscatter Kikuchi  Patterns are generated on a phosphor screen by  back-diffraction of a stationary beam of high-energy electrons  from an almost perfect volume of a crystal and are recorded by  a high-sensitivity CCD camera. The geometry of a Kikuchi


然sd -2nd0


pattern is unique for a particular crystal structure and crystal  lattice orientation, thus allowing the identification of the  particular lattice type and orientation from the EBSD  measurements by appropriate software tools and data bases. The specimens are investigated by scanning the electron beam  across a selected surface area. Due to the high surface


20μm


sensitivity of EBSD the quality of the obtainable results is  directly related to the quality of the prepared specimen surface


[6]. In the present study, the geometrical requirements for an  EBSD analysis could be fulfilled by specific removal of  material along the direction of the diffracted beam, enabling an  artefact free texture analysis with spatial resolution in the  range of nanometres. Figures 10 and 11 show quantitative  EBSD orientation maps of Cu filled TSVs acquired from  surfaces prepared using a 74 nA Xe-ion beam. Again, rocking  the specimen with respect to the specimen surface had been  found to be the optimum preparation regime.


Comparing TSVs as fabricated with additionally annealed  TSVs (400 °C anneal for 3 h), the change of grain size and  grain distribution due to the temperature storage is obvious. In  fact, the mean grain diameter was about 0.5 μm in the case of  not annealed TSVs whereas after annealing, a mean grain  diameter of 1.2 μm was determined. The results obtained so  far could not reveal any texture during recrystallization for the  analysed annealing procedure.

Summary and Conclusions


It is demonstrated that the novel Plasma-FIB system enables  time efficient characterisation and failure analysis of TSV  interconnects and offers significant advantages compared to  common mechanical or Ga-FIB based preparations, as the  relative large regions of interests can be prepared rapidly. Furthermore, P-FIB polishing provides a fast and reproducible  method, which allows cross sections without significant  damage or a modification of the microstructure. This is  essential for EBSD based process characterisation. It makes it  possible to study Cu protrusion or other microstructural  changes which are related to annealing of TSVs and cause  reliability issues.


Part of this work has been performed within the framework of  the project ESiP, in which the Fraunhofer IWM was funded by  the German “Bundesministerium für Bildung und Forschung (BMBF)” under contract 13N10972 and the ENIAC Joint  Undertaking. Another part of the work has been performed in  the project JEMSiP-3D, which is funded by the Public  Authorities in France, Germany, Hungary, the Netherlands, Norway and Sweden, as well as by the ENIAC Joint  Undertaking. The authors gratefully acknowledge  RTI International for providing the TSV specimens.

Microstructural Considerations on the Reliability of 3D Packaging


Zhiheng Huang*, Zhiyong Wu, Hua Xiong, and Yucheng Ma


School of Physics and Engineering, Sun Yat-sen University, Guangzhou 510275, China  *Corresponding author, email: hzh29@mail.sysu.edu.cn, tel./fax: +86 (0)20 8411 0530


Microstructure and its effect on mechanical behavior of  ultrafine interconnects have been studied in this paper using a  modeling approach.  The microstructure from the processes of  solidification, spinodal decomposition, and grain growth in  ultrafine interconnects has highlighted its importance.  The  size, geometry and composition of interconnects as well as the  elastic energy can influence microstructure and thus the  mechanical behavior.  Quantification of microstructure in  ultrafine interconnects is a necessary step to establish the  linkage between microstructure and reliability.


Microelectronics packaging technologies have been developed  at a much faster pace than the research on materials issues  related to the technologies, e.g. microstructure and  microstructural evolution of materials, and their correlation  with the reliability of electronic packaging.  3D integration is  skyrocketing at the component and system level today where  dimensions of the materials shrink to a few tens of microns or  even less.  At such a scale, microstructural features become  appreciable and are expected to play an important role in  determining the behavior of 3D packaging.


Although not an unfamiliar term, microstructure has not  commonly been integrated into failure analysis models for  microelectronics packaging.  Considering the dimensions of  current 3D packaging technologies, e.g. the Cu TSV  structures, the timeliness to study microstructure and its  relationship with reliability is justified.  In addition, microstructural modeling techniques, e.g. the phase field  method, have reached maturity and can provide a wealthy of  useful information with respect to the microstructure of  materials under different manufacturing processes.  The  computing facilities today can afford a “through-process” modeling and simulation for the real materials used in 3D  packaging.


Integration of microstructure and related information into the  current reliability and failure analysis platforms still presents  challenges.  Although the experimental and modeling  techniques can nowadays provide digitalized microstructure, or even 3D microstructure in some cases, of the interconnects, statistical analyses on the microstructure have to be carried out  in order to retrieve quantified microstructural characteristics  that can be used to establish a linkage with reliability.  In  addition, a majority of the phase field microstructure modeling  and simulations have been conducted on regular geometries.


In 3D packaging and integration, however, the sizes and  geometries of interconnects, which can influence  microstructural formation and hence reliability, are additional  design parameters that should be taken into consideration.  Furthermore, directly coupling microstructural evolution and  failure analysis presents additional challenges.


This paper aims to address some of the above mentioned  challenges in the integration of microstructure into reliability  modeling for 3D packaging.  The structure of the paper is  organized as following.  The modeling methodology section is  introduced after the introduction section, and the results and  discussions section is presented following the modeling  methodology section.  In the results and discussion section, microstructures during solidification in ultrafine interconnects  are presented first.  The size and geometry effects on  microstructural formation are discussed.  With microstructures  available, a mechanical modeling based on elastic mechanics  is conducted on the joints to obtain von Mises stress  distributions.  In the second section, microstructures at solid  state in small intercoonects under an external strain are  presented.  The influences of size, geometry, composition, and  stress on microstructure are discussed.  In the third section, the  influences of crystallographic orientation, size and distribution  of copper grains on thermo-mechanical stresses of a 3D CuTSV structure are presented.  Finally, a method for  quantification of microstructural features in ultrafine  interconnects is discussed.


Modeling Methodology


A microstructure informed multi-physics modeling framework


[1] based on the finite element method [2] is adopted in this  work.  The finite element based framework enables the  incorporation of the size and geometry of interconnects into  the model.  The microstructure in ultrafine interconnects is  modeled using phase field methods [3-5], which include  modeling of microstructure during solidification, spinodal  decomposition during solid state aging, and grain growth.  The  Vonoroi algorithm [6] is adopted to generate the copper grains  in Cu-TSVs.  Two methods are used for microstructure  informed mechanical modeling based on elastic mechanics.  The first method is naturally built into the phase field models  by considering of the elastic energy.  For this method, the  model developed by Müller et al. [7] is used.  The boundary  conditions for the mechanical modeling are set as following: the bottom boundary of the joint is fixed and the top boundary  is subjected to a prescribed shear displacement of 10 nm to the  right.  The left and right curved boundaries are free to deform.  The second method is to conduct mechanical modeling  directly on the microstructure from phase field modeling using


the structural mechanics module in COMSOL Multiphysics


[8].  In this method, the boundary conditions for the  mechanical modeling have the same settings as in the first  method except that a prescribed shear displacement of 16 nm  is used.


Results and Discussion


Solidification Microstructure and von Mises Stress


The left column of Fig. 1 presents microstructures at 2.510-7 s  during solidification in joints with different geometries and a  standoff height of 16 m.  Three nuclei are initially placed at  the bottom boundary of the joints.  It is clearly demonstrated  that the geometries of the joints can influence the morphology  of the dendritic structures.  In particular, the dendrite grown  from the nucleus near the right boundary, hereinafter referred  to as dendrite no. 3, exhibits a significantly different  morphology in three geometries.  Dendrite no. 3 in the  hourglass shaped joint cannot grow much due to the constraint  from the concave boundary.  In contrast, dendrite no. 3 in the  rectangle shaped joint grows as a regular dendrite without any  obvious influence from the vertical boundary.  Dendrite no. 3  in the barrel shaped joint shows an asymmetric structure along  the vertical primary dendritic arm, i.e. the secondary dendritic  arms on the right hand side of the primary arm grow as long as  the convex boundary can accommodate.


Due to the difference in microstructure, it is reasonable to  expect that the mechanical behavior of the joint will be  different because different phases in the solidified  microstructure have different mechanical properties, e.g. an  intermetallic compound possesses a higher elastic modulus  than the -Sn phase.  Although the microstructure shown in the  left column of Fig. 1 is a simplified version of real  microstructure, the importance of microstructure and the way  that microstructure exerts influence on mechanical behavior of  ultrafine interconnects is highlighted in the right column of  Fig. 1.  In the mechanical modeling, we assume that the blue  and red phases in the solidified microstructure possess the  properties of the Cu6Sn5 and -Sn phases respectively.  The  morphology of microstructure is observable in the plots of von  Mises stress distribution.  It seems that the microstructure  effect on von Mises distribution is more obvious in the  hourglass shaped joint among the three geometries.  This is  due to the fact that the hourglass shaped joint exhibits the  lowest maximum von Mises stress under the mechanical  boundary conditions used in this study.  The geometric effect  is obvious since the locations of the maximum stress and the  patterns of stress distribution intrinsic to the three geometries  keep consistent with the results on mechanical analyses of  joints without microstructure.


Solid State Microstructure and von Mises Stress


Microstructures at solid state, i.e. from a spinodal  decomposition process, in ultrafine Sn-37Pb eutectic joints  subjected to a shear strain to the right with different  geometries are shown in the left column of Fig. 2.  Similar to  the microstructure from solidification, the morphology of the


phases formed near the boundaries of joints as small as 1.5 m  tend to conform to the geometry of the boundaries.  The  heterogeneous von Mises distributions in the joints as shown  in the right column of Fig. 2 indicate a strong effect of the  microstructure on mechanical behavior of ultrafine  interconnects.

Fig. 3 presents microstructures and von Mises stress  distributions in joints with 8 m pad sizes.  Compared to the

However, the microstructure effect on the von  Mises distribution is clearly visible.  In addition, the  phenomenon that a layered structure with certain thickness  conforms to the geometry of the boundaries is more  pronounced in the 8 m pad size joints under an external shear  strain.  It appears that the layered structure is not continuous  along the boundaries and it breaks up in the regions where the  joint exhibits lower stresses.

Microstructure and von Mises Stress in the 3D Cu-TSV


Fig. 5 presents the effect of microstructure of Cu on the  thermal-mechanical stress in a 3D Cu-TSV structure.  Detailed  description of this model is referred to previous studies [9-10].  We introduce copper grains with different crystallographic  orientations into the model.  The effect of anisotropy of the  elastic modulus of Cu on the thermal-mechanical stress in the  TSV structure is investigated with results shown in Figs. 5a-b.  Fig. 5a shows the temperature distribution in the TSV, which  suggests that there is a small temperature gradient from top to


bottom in the Cu via.  However, the difference in elastic  modulus of Cu along different crystallographic directions can  be significant.  As a result, Fig. 5b suggests that there is rather  heterogeneous thermal stress distribution in the TSV.  It is  noted that some grains in the Cu via exhibit a stress level  which is close to the maximum stress in the structure.  This  result is quite different from the point of view of a traditional  model where the microstructure of Cu is ignored.


In addition to crystallographic orientation, the effect of grain  size distribution on the thermal stress in the TSV is further  studied.  According to the Hall-Petch relation [11], a small

Quantification of Microstructural Characteristics


We have discussed the microstructure and its influence on the  mechanical behavior of ultrafine interconnects in the previous  sections.  In fact, we can obtain the microstructure in ultrafine  interconnects by either an experimental or a modeling  approach.  However, the microstructure in interconnects has to  be quantified with the salient characteristics retrieved in order  to establish a linkage between microstructure and reliability.  Towards this end, we propose a method based on singular  value decomposition (SVD) [14] to quantify the  microstructural features in ultrafine interconnects, which will  be detailed elsewhere [15].  As an example, Fig. 6 shows the  difference in the quantified microstructure between an  interconnect with a certain pad size and geometry and the  benchmark, which is the averaged microstructure of the  available samples, as a function of the pad size.   The curve  shows that the difference in microstructure between an  interconnect and the benchmark decreases as the pad size  increases.  This is a reasonable result since the microstructure  in smaller interconnects is more heterogeneous and therefore is  more amenable to change.  In addition, the difference in  microstructure between an hourglass shaped interconnect and  the benchmark is larger compared to a barrel shaped  interconnect with the same pad size, which indicates that the


microstructure in an hourglass shaped interconnect is more  heterogeneous and amenable to change.

Open TSV Technology


The TSVs in the technological approach of the ams AG  connect a bottom and a top wafer electrically as depicted in  Fig. 1. Thus e.g. a sensor application on the bottom wafer can  send signals on the shortest possible way through the top wafer  substrate to a processing circuitry on the top wafer. The large  diameter enables deep TSVs with a moderate aspect ratio of


2.5. The thick top wafer is of advantage for handling issues. Though the TSV has a depth of 250μm a contact resistance of  only 0.35  is achieved.


The bottom and top wafer are bonded by Si/SiO2 direct  bonding. Both wafers are fabricated with a modified 0.35 μm  CMOS process flow. The TSVs are processed after finishing  the CMOS devices. A deep reactive ion etch process is used  for etching the TSVs into the substrate (Bosch process). A  spacer module provides the isolation towards the substrate and  opens the TSV bottom down to the landing pad (metal on  bottom wafer). The metallisation is done with a conformal  CVD deposition of W inside the TSV and a sputtered AlCu  layer, which covers mainly the top wafer surface. A large  overlap of W and AlCu ensures a good electrical contact from  the TSV to the top metal layer. Finally the passivation  consisting of a combined Si Oxide and Si Nitride layer is  deposited.


Figure 1: Schematic build-up of a TSV


Failure localisation using optical, thermal and  SEM based techniques


For test specimen containing TSV chains, electrical shorts  between the TSV metallisation and the silicon substrate were  investigated. Optical and thermal imaging techniques can be  used to localise the origin of such shorts due to their light  emission and local resistive heating. Photoemission  Microscopy (PEM) has been proven to be a useful method to  determine the defect position in a lateral projection [2]. Defocussing series [3] produced by Lock-in Thermography  enables a three-dimensional localisation of the sidewall short  within the open TSV. A precise and high sensitive localisation  is provided by a new approach based on Electron Beam  Absorbed Current (EBAC) imaging within a SEM

3D-Localisation by LIT Defocussing


In addition to the previously described method, the localisation  of the short position inside open TSV structures can be  determined by a new defocusing method based on the Lock-in  Thermography (LIT) technique. Here, the shorted circuitry is  operated by a periodically pulsed supply voltage with  adjustable frequencies and the device is imaged from the  surface side by a high sensitive IR camera system. Two  channel images processing in correlation to the electrical  stimulation is applied to improve the signal-to-noise-ratio  significantly. As a result, LIT provides a high spatial  resolution down to 1 μm and a high sensitivity in μW range. In  consequence, the detection and investigation of thermal  emissions and the distribution to the surrounding area is  feasible. In terms of open TSV structures, LIT can be used to  investigate defect-related heat distributions in relation to the  axial focus position. By shifting the focus position with m  accuracy, thermal imaging can be focused down into the TSV  structure, which is demonstrated in Fig. 4.


12-Emission microscope objective lens


Figure 4: Schematic built-up of the LIT defocussing on a TSV


13-Probe needles


Figure 5: Local electrical probing of the investigated TSV  structure and imaging of the resulting thermal emission at  various focus positions


Therefore, precise 3D-defect localisation is provided. Figure 5  depicts the application of this method on a defective TSV  structure. By using local electrical probing, the device is  stimulated and the resulting heat propagation at the surface is  imaged by the LIT system. The focus position is shifted  towards the bottom in 25 μm steps. With further defocusing, a  decrease of the spot size can be observed. A minimum spot  size is reached for a focus distance of 200 μm to the surface. An analysis of the relationship between thermal amplitude and  lateral position for different focus positions is shown in Fig. 6. With an increasing focus depth relative to the surface a  decrease of the absolute amplitude as well as a decrease of the  lateral extension is visible.


Figure 6: Comparison of the lateral temperature distribution  in relation to the focus position


In order to correlate the measured thermal emissions to a spot  size, Gaussian fitting functions in combination with the pixel  size and optical magnification factor were applied. The results  are shown in Fig. 7.


Distance to Surface [μm]


Figure 7: Determined spot size in relation to the focus  position


Starting from the investigations on the TSV surface, a strong  increase of the spot size is observed for the -25 μm and -50 μm defocus. Due to reflections of the generated heat  radiation on the TSV side walls, the detected temperature  distribution is shifting with increasing defocus position. With  closer distance to the real defect position these influences are  more and more negligible. For focus positions deeper than the  defect position, an increase of the spot size can be observed.

Enhanced Target Preparation Techniques


Typically FIB techniques are used to provide a high precision  site specific cross sectioning. The access for FIB preparation  and SEM observation of sidewall shorts in open TSVs is very  limited and only defects near the surface can be analysed in  this standard procedure.


In order to get access to deeper located defects for FIB/SEM  analyses and also TEM preparation, high volume cross  sectioning is required. In a first step precise site specific  mechanical grinding was used for a cross section through the  middle of the TSV structure (see Fig. 9).


Figure 9: Light-optical image - tilted view of cross sectioned  TSV specimens directly after target preparation by  mechanical grinding


Further approaches based on fast and precise laser ablation LILs have been considered for a reliable and repeatable preparation.


An ultra-short pulse Laser system was evaluated for accurate


micromachining. Crucial parameters for a fast, secure and


reliable preparation of open TSV structures are the


wavelength, the average pulse power and the repetition rate. In


this study, a fibre-based picosecond Laser working at 532 nm


wavelength was used for specimen preparation. During the


ablation process an averaged pulse power between 0,15 μJ and


0,25 μJ at 200kHz repetition rate was used.


Figure 10: SEM image of open TSVs prepared by optimised  laser processing


By developing smart laser milling strategies even wafer  bonded substrates with a thickness of ~1mm could be cross  sectioned with a sufficient quality, which enables further  specimen preparation by FIB or a direct access for localisation  techniques (see Fig. 10).


Failure Analysis Case Study


The newly adapted techniques for precise and three  dimensional defect localisation and site specific defect  preparation were applied at an electrically shorted TSV chain  to demonstrate a full and effective failure analysis flow for  determining the physical root cause of the shorts.


The case study was performed at high-ohmic shorted TSVs  respectively with a leakage current of μA range @10 V. As a  first method Lock-in Thermography was used to localise  several defective TSVs within the chain. Then local probing of  a single defective TSV was performed to measure the IV  characteristic of the short. It was revealed that the electrical  behaviour was like a Schottky diode (see Fig. 11).


Figure 11: LIT image with two thermally active TSVs (left); curve tracing of a single TSV shows characteristic of a  Schottky diode (right)


By further LIT investigations, using the explained defocussing  procedure, the defect could be localised at the TSV sidewall  near the bottom of the TSV at about 200 μm depth (see  Fig. 12).


Figure 12: Result of Lock-in Thermography defocussing series  localising the high-ohmic short at TSV sidewall


Thermal hot spot


TSV cross sections


The localisation and characterisation of the defect by using  electrical (see Fig. 11) and LIT investigations was confirmed  by an additional EBAC analysis.


For that purpose the specimen was tilted and rotated to get full  access to the TSV failing position. The increased sensitivity of  EBAC permits highly precise defect localisation for target  preparation by SEM/FIB. Even though no external voltage is  biased during the EBAC investigation, the charging by  electron beam may alter the defect and need to be minimised.


Several EBAC spots were detected at the sidewall near the  bottom of the TSV (see Fig. 13).


Figure 13: Overlay of SE- and EBAC-signal - EBAC analysis  on a complete TSV structure to localise relevant defect  positions (red spots) as leaking path


After the localisation the specimen was cross sectioned  through the middle of the TSV by precise mechanical grinding  and polishing steps. Thus the localised defect was preserved  and so FIB/SEM cross sectioning had been performable at the  remaining TSV structure. The specimen was tilted in two  directions to get access for the FIB milling and SEM  observation at the defect site using a pre-tilted specimen  holder (see Fig. 14).


Figure 14: SEM image of 45° tilted specimen with cross  section at the localised defect position


Afterwards FIB milling and simultaneous SEM observation  were performed to screen the defect area. A rough  inhomogeneous sidewall can be seen at the TSV bottom with  abnormal thickness deviations of the isolation oxide layer  between the substrate and the sidewall metallisation. Additional embedded voids were observed at the interface  with three dimensional Si artefacts. Such defects are caused by  an insufficient Si etch process at TSV bottom.


position after FIB polishing (overlay of two cross section  images)


A three dimensional formed Si residue was detected within the  EBAC active area (see Fig. 15). It shorts the substrate with the  TSV metallisation at the bottom sidewall and thus generates a  high-ohmic leakage path. Due to the three-dimensional shape  the complete leakage path was only observable by reviewing  several cross sections. This kind of failure can occur when the  last Si etch steps were processed insufficiently and thus  significant layer thickness variations were caused. The  described case study shows the feasibility of the explained  localisation and preparation methods to characterise complex  three-dimensional defects at interconnect structures.


LIT and EBAC imaging techniques have been adapted for  three dimensional and precise short localisation within open  TSV structures. The defect sites can be easily navigated within  the FIB/SEM system for further cross sectional analysis. In  order to get access to the defect sites for destructive physical  analysis full cross sectioning of the devices exactly through the  defective TSV structure is necessary. In addition to precise  mechanically grinding also ps-laser milling was demonstrated  to speed up the preparation process. As an outlook combined  laser milling and FIB cross sectioning within one device could  be a very effective way for specimen preparation at TSV  interconnects. The feasibility of these techniques was  demonstrated on a device with shorted open TSVs.

A new Technique for non-invasive Short-Localisation in thin dielectric Layers by  Electron Beam Absorbed Current (EBAC) Imaging


Michél Simon-Najasek, Jörg Jatzkowski, Christian Große, Frank Altmann


Fraunhofer Institute for Mechanics of Materials, Halle, Germany


Michel.Simon-Najasek@iwmh.fraunhofer.de, Tel: +49-345-5589-138


In this paper a novel approach for precise localisation of thin  oxide breakdowns in transistor or capacitor structures by  electron beam absorbed current (EBAC) imaging based on  Scanning Electron Microscopy will be presented. The  technique significantly improves sensitivity and lateral  resolution of short localisation in comparison to standard  techniques, e.g. Photoemission Microscopy, and provides  direct defect navigation within a combined FIB/SEM system


PE-beam


for further cross section analysis. The oxide short is minimal  affected by electrical stimulation preserving its original defect


Needle


structure for further physical root cause analysis. The  feasibility of this new technique is demonstrated on a gate


Injected


oxide (GOX) and two capacitor oxide (COX) breakdown


charge


failures.


One of the major factors limiting the lifetime of integrated


Metal line


circuits is the occurrence of dielectric breakdowns in one of  the circuit’s field-effect transistors or capacitors. Process  related weaknesses in thin dielectric layers or overvoltage  stress caused by externally induced discharging (ESD) can  lead to early failures in the gate or capacitor oxides. Physical  analysis of such (early) failures caused by oxide breakdowns  can help to distinguish between process related and electrical  root causes by analysing the corresponding defect signature. Because of the small dimensions of dielectric breakdowns  there is a high risk of modifying the original defect signature  during localisation procedure, if the defect structure is heated  by forcing a current or by Optical Beam Induced Resistance  Change (OBIRCH). Other methods like Photoemission  Microscopy (PEM) or Lock-in Thermography (LIT) typically  require a dissipation power in the μW range. In this paper a  new approach based on Electron Beam Absorbed Current (EBAC) imaging within a Scanning Electron Microscope (SEM) will be introduced providing defect localisation at a  dissipation power in the lower nW range, thus preserving the


10m


original defect structure of thin dielectric breakdown failures. Additionally EBAC can localize defect sites with sub  micrometre accuracy overcoming alignment failures between  optical defect navigation by PEM and further SEM/TEM cross  section analyses based on Focused Ion Beam target  preparation.


EBAC principle


The primary electron beam of the SEM can be utilised as a  non-contact local current source. The penetration depth of the  injected electrons can be aligned by choosing the acceleration  voltage from some 100 nm up to several μm depending on the  penetrated material. The injected electron current can be  measured by local probing of metal lines or poly silicon  structures, Fig. 1.


Figure 1: EBAC principle


The absorbed beam current in the IC structure is detected by a  probe needle and amplified by a sensitive EBIC amplifier. Then a current image is reconstructed from the amplified  current signal and can be superposed to the SEM image.


Figure 2: EBAC localisation of an open defect in a metal line


If the metal network is locally interrupted, only the probed  part of the net gives an EBAC signal and the position of metal  line opens can be exactly located by a change of contrast in  the EBAC image, Fig, 2 [1], [2]. Furthermore shorts in


metallisation networks can be detected by comparative EBAC  imaging; thereto the superposed image is compared to the IC  layout or to a reference device.


Localisation of thin dielectric shorts by EBAC  imaging


A new approach for thin film short localisation based on


EBAC imaging has been developed. In the first step, layers  above the defective device structure are removed by CMP (chemical mechanical polishing) using an Allied MultiPrep  system or locally selective etching by FIB. Particular care  must be taken that the IC structure under investigation is not  damaged by polishing and FIB. After the specimen  preparation, it is fixed on a standard SEM stub and loaded into  the SEM chamber in-between the probing stages. Imaging the


i1


corresponding IC structure is conducted by a probe needle at


Poly Si plate+GOX


one of the two electrodes next to the defective thin oxide si


layer. For example at a single transistor structure this could be  the gate electrode or the Si substrate underneath. The substrate  is grounded by a second needle at the chip edge, so that the IC  structure and the current amplifier are on the same voltage  level. Then the transistor or capacitor structure with the planar  oxide lying in-between the two electrodes is stimulated by the  primary electron beam perpendicular to the surface. Using the  scan engine of the SEM an EBAC image containing the  EBAC current value as grey level is produced and can be  overlaid on the SEM topography image for navigation. The  current is divided exactly at the vertical thin oxide short and  flows into both electrodes resulting in a lower EBAC current


acquired by needle probe. The energy of the primary electrons  has to be aligned in order to allow the electron interaction  volume reaching the oxide so that one part of the current is  flowing into the substrate resulting in a locally darker EBAC  signal, Fig. 3.


Figure 3: EBAC scheme for top down short localisation in  thin film oxides


The lateral resolution of EBAC short localisation can be  further improved by a stepwise lowering of the primary  electron energy and thus reducing the lateral dimension of the  electron injection volume, Fig. 4. Depending on the remaining  thickness above the oxide, which can be minimized further by  partial delayering of the IC structure, sub-micron localisation  of oxide shorts can be achieved utilising this method.


Figure 4: EBAC imaging of a shorted gate structure at different primary electron energies to optimise the lateral resolution of defect  localisation with corresponding Monte Carlo simulation (Casino Version 2.42)

Failure analysis case studies utilizing EBAC  short localisation


GOX Short in a Poly-Si gate structure


A first case study was done at a shorted gate structure of a  MOSFET transistor. The GOX short could be localised by  PEM but could not precisely enough for further TEM cross  section analysis, because of the large size of the gate electrode (10 μm x 20 μm), the thin oxide and the imprecise defect  navigation with an accuracy of only several μm, Fig. 5 left. Thus the defect was further localised by EBAC imaging. Beforehand the oxide and nitride passivation and oxide  isolation layers above the gate plate were removed by local  FIB milling to get an optimal access for the electron beam. Additionally, the gate signal line was disconnected from the  entire circuit and the contacts to the poly Si gate were made  accessible for electrical probing. By contacting one via by a  probe needle the absorbed electron current at each position  could be measured and visualised by EBAC imaging. At the  defect side the EBAC signal is decreased so that this can be  seen as a dark spot in the EBAC image superposed to the SEM  image, Fig. 5 right. It is notable that the accuracy of the EBAC  localisation is significantly higher than the PEM localisation, so that a TEM lamella could be prepared directly at the  localised defect position.


Figure 6: TEM investigation of the short signature proves a  typical signature of an ESD damage


The TEM investigation revealed a GOX defect with a typical  signature of an ESD event, Fig. 6. Structural defects which  indicating a process related root cause could not be found.


COX Short in a large area modular capacitor structure


The next case study was performed at a large area capacitor  consisting of 20 modules with 13 columns and 38 rows each  showing a leakage current during final testing. Altogether this  capacitor type consists of 9880 single capacitors.


In the first step all single capacitors had to be separated. The  upper plates of all single capacitors are connected over a  metallisation network above the capacitor structure. The  metallisation network was removed over the whole capacitor  area by parallel polishing, Fig. 7.


Figure 7: Delayering to separate each CAP single structure  for PVC and EBAC imaging


Mechanical delayering into the contact structure  to disconnect the capacitors from each other


8 keV


3-Figure 8: PE optimization for passive voltage and EBAC imaging with corresponding Monte Carlo simulation


After disconnecting each single capacitor the whole structure  was investigated by passive voltage contrast imaging in the  SEM [3]. Although FIB voltage contrast has a better intensity  than SEM voltage contrast it was not chosen in order to avoid  any surface charging and thus ESDFOS damages on further  single capacitors. In the SEM the optimum acceleration  voltage has to be worked out in order to improve the voltage  contrast. At the optimum acceleration voltage faultless  capacitors appear bright while shorted capacitors appear dark. After delayering and by using a primary electron energy of  2 keV the aberrant voltage contrast can only be seen on the  poly contacts, with 7 keV the aberrant voltage contrast could  be seen over the whole poly plate which is shown in Fig. 8. Acceleration voltages above 7 keV reveal first a decreasing  and then no different passive voltage contrast.


Afterwards the free standing contacts of the localised poly  plate of the shorted capacitor were connected by a probe  needle and then scanned by the electron beam to find the  defect position in the single capacitor oxide. During the


scanning the absorbed current was measured, amplified and  superposed on the SEM image. At the defect side a dark spot  could be found in the EBAC image indicating the COX short, Fig. 9 left. The short in the capacitor oxide could be localised  with sub- m precision. To clarify the defect nature a TEM  lamella was prepared by FIB in-situ lift-out technique directly  at the defect position. At the end it was assumed that an oxide  weakness at the capacitor edge was causing the COX  breakdown but this could not be proven due to the large defect  volume shown in Fig. 9, right.


The reason for the large defect volume was the high capacity  of the overall capacitor structure. If only one of the capacitor  oxides fails, the total electrical charge of all 9880 single  capacitors is flowing over this defect. Due to the high current  density at the defect the material around the short is heating up  to melting point and completely changes the original defect  structure so that the original failure root cause can no longer  be investigated.

Pad Size, μm


Figure 6: Quantification of microstructures in ultrafine  interconnects with different sizes and geometries.  The  ordinate represents the difference in microstructure between  an interconnect with a certain pad size and geometry and the  benchmark, which is the averaged microstructure of the  available samples.


This paper studies the microstructure and its effect on  mechanical behavior of ultrafine interconnects.  The following  conclusions can be drawn:


(1) Microstructure is a parameter that can influence the  mechanical behavior of ultrafine interconnects;


(2) The size, geometry and composition of the ultrafine  interconnects as well as the elastic energy can influence  the microstructure present inside the interconnect and thus  the mechanical behavior;


(3) Quantification of microstructure in ultrafine interconnects  is a necessary step in order to establish the linkage  between microstructure and reliability.

High-frequency TSV Failure Detection Method with Z parameter


Joohee Kim, Daniel H. Jung, Jonghyun Cho, Jun So Pak and Joungho Kim


Korea Advanced Institute of Science and Technology (KAIST), Deajeon, Republic of Korea  E-mail: joohee@eeinfo.kaist.ac.kr


Jong Min Yook and Jun Chul Kim


Korea Electronic Technology Institute (KETI), Seongman-si, Gyeonggi-do, Republic of Korea


As the TSV count increases, chip yield can be severely  degraded due to failures during the TSV or die-stacking  processes. This paper will present and discuss on the usage of  failure masks designed to detect and differentiate failure types  such as connection failure and insulator failure based on


Output signalCoupled signal


frequency-domain one point probing measurement. The  failure masks are proposed on the basis of the frequencydomain analysis of TSV failures with Z11 magnitudes.


Through-Silicon Via


Insulator failure


Connection


Recently, many of mobile and graphic application systems


failure


Stacked-chip


require higher system bandwidth as well as lower power


Input signal


with TSVs


consumption. In order to meet highly increasing system  bandwidth requirement, the I/O count and the channel  bandwidth have been increased [1]. However, the amount of  the power consumption increases as the I/O count and the  operating frequency which is determined by the channel  bandwidth increase. Thus, TSV technology becomes a key  solution allowing to realize both of the system performance  requirements simultaneously. Since the height and the  diameter of most commonly implemented TSV are under 100 μm and 10 μm, respectively, those not only enable high-speed  signaling but also lower power consumption [2-3]. As a result, a TSV-based 3D IC is emerging as a powerful system  integration solution which enables high-speed and low-power  communication based on vertical stacking of dies with TSVs.


However, the bottleneck of the further improvement and the  mass production of 3D ICs is the exponentially degrading chip  yield as the TSV count and the number of stacked die increase


[4]. As the required system bandwidth increases, the I/O count  of a system, which directly affects to the TSV count, needs to  be increased. In addition, 3-dimensional integration needs diestacking processes which add mechanical and thermal loads  during wafer thinning and die bonding. If not controlled well, resulting TSV failures may severely degrade the final 3D IC  yield.


The main challenge in establishing TSV stacking  technologies in mass production is ensuring sufficient  reliability and yield. Already identified defect mechanisms  are: 1) via drilling and side wall isolation, 2) copper plating  process, and 3) die stacking and alignment. Resulting defects  from those processes are 1) insulator defect due to the  impurities or the insulator breakdown [5], 2) voids or dimples


[6], and 3) disconnection or die misalignment. Due to those


various types of TSV failures, the TSV-based chip yield is  degraded which directly affects to the cost. To improve the  reliability of the TSV-based 3D IC, it is necessary to develop  novel non-destructive diagnostic and localization methods to  help to provide rapid feedback of failure types and locations  to the fabricators.


Figure 1: Due to the connection failure and the insulator  failure in a stacked-chip with TSVs, signal propagation has  failed which results in the closed eye of the output signal and  the signal is coupled to other TSVs through the silicon  substrate.


In this work, novel failure masks are proposed to detect and  differentiate TSV failures on the basis of the high-frequency  Z-parameter measurement and analysis. Among various TSV  failures, the most critical failure is the connection failure  which cannot transfer the signal as shown in Fig. 1. If the  TSV channel is disconnected, the signal cannot be transmitted  resulting in the closed eye of the output signal as shown in  Fig. 1. Partial connection can occur when the dies are  misaligned resulting in the increased insertion loss of the TSV  channel. And the second one is the insulator failure which  causes leakage current through the silicon substrate. It  degrades noise margin of the system since it causes noise  coupling between TSVs or TSV and active circuits. Based on  Z parameter analysis, novel failure masks will be presented  enabling to detect and differentiate between these TSV related  failures.

Depending on the  frequency regions A, B and C, the dominant electrical  parameters which determine the Z11 magnitude of the TSV  channel are characterized [9].


In order to electrically detect and differentiate TSV failures  along the TSV-based channel in a stacked-chip, electrical  characteristics of TSV failures have to be analyzed. On the  basis of the electrical model and analysis of a normally  fabricated TSV and a failed TSV, key electrical characteristics  for the failure detection are extracted from the Z-parameter  analysis and measurement. Since the electrical length of the  TSV channel is short, electrical characteristics are difficult to  analyze with the transiently reflected wave using TDR  measurements: rise time of the TDR waveform must be  shorter than the time delay of the channel to diagnose TSV  failures along the stacked TSVs. Thus, frequency-domain  analysis is necessary to electrically detect TSV failures. Zparameter measurements focusing especially on the Z11  magnitude, has been selected as the failure-detecting

Figure 3: (a) The equivalent circuit model of the stacked TSVs  with disconnection failure with Cdisconnect and (b) the extracted  Z11 magnitudes from Z11 magnitudes of the normal stacked  TSV channel and the disconnected TSV channels as varying  Cdisconnect


For a full disconnection failure, the disconnected part is  modeled as a series capacitance along the TSV channel as  shown in Fig. 3(a). In Fig. 3(a), Zeq,TSV ( ) represents the  equivalent impedance of a TSV which is dependent on  frequencies. Due to the series capacitance, Z11 magnitude of  the failed case has higher values than that of the normal case. The Z11 magnitude is extracted by subtraction of the Z11  magnitude of the defected TSV channel from that of the  normal one. As a result, the values of the extracted Z11  magnitudes are all positive in all frequencies up to 20 GHz as  shown in Fig. 3(b). In addition, Z11 magnitudes of the  defective channel become smaller as frequency increases, because the impedance of the series capacitance due to the  disconnection gets smaller as the frequency increases. As a  result, Z11 magnitude in region C closes to zero which  means that Z11 magnitude of the defected case closes to that


of the normal case. Therefore, Z11 magnitude is constant in  region A and decreases in region B, when the TSV channel  has a disconnection failure.


In case of a partial connection due to the misalignment  between the stacked dies, the series resistance of a TSV-based  channel increases since the resistance of the partially  connected part is added to that of the normal one. As a result, Rcontact is added to the equivalent circuit model of the normal  TSV channel as shown in Fig. 4(a). And the resulting Z11  magnitude of the defective case has higher values than the  normal one due to the increased series resistance. Consequently, Z11 magnitudes are all positive in all  frequencies up to 20 GHz as shown in Fig. 4(b). Therefore, Z11 magnitude increases as frequency increases in region A,

With an insulator failure, the equivalent circuit model is  proposed as shown in Fig. 5(a). If there are impurities or  cracks inside the insulation layer or break downs occur, they  can be represented with Gox,defect in the equivalent circuit  model. The values of Gox,defect change depending on the  properties  of the defect. . In Fig. 5(b), Z11 magnitudes are  plotted as Gox,defect varies. Since the capacitance from the


insulation layer is the most dominant one among the parasitic  capacitances of the TSV channel, it dominantly determines the  electrical behavior of the Z11 magnitude in low frequencies, in region A, as shown in Fig. 2 [8-9]. Cox is about hundreds of  fF order and other parasitic capacitances such as CSi, CIMD, and  CBump are several to tens of fF order. Thus, the Z11 magnitude  of the TSV channel with an insulator defect differs from the  normal one only in region A. As a result, Z11 magnitudes  are only shown in region A with the insulator failure. However, Z11 magnitude with an insulator defect is smaller  than that of the normal one due to the insertion loss from the  Gox,defect. As a result, Z11 magnitudes are all negative in  region A when there is an insulator failure.

Proposal of TSV Failure Masks


To detect TSV failures on the basis of the frequencydomain measurement results, the failure detection criteria  must be clearly defined. From the analysis results of TSV  channels with or without defects, it can be noticed that the  electrical characteristics of TSV failures  appear differently  depending on the failure types and frequency regions. Thus, they can be differentiated with Z11 magnitude which can be  calculated from the Z11 magnitude of the normal and the  defected TSV channels. With the Z11 magnitude differences,


failure masks for the failure type and location detection are  newly designed as shown in Fig. 7.


Failure masks are proposed based on the Z11 magnitude  difference between that of the normal TSV channel and that of  the failed TSV channel. Since Z11 magnitude of the  connection failure case is larger than that of the normal TSV

Because this method can be applied by using only one-port  RF measurement on top of a die or wafer as shown in Fig. 6, failures can be easily detected, regardless of TSV process  types and also whether or not the dies are stacked, by using  the proposed failure masks. In order to use this method, it is


needed to design probing pad for test on the top die. In  addition, Vector Network Analyzer (VNA) and the GS or  GSG-type microprobe tip is needed for the frequency-domain  Z parameter measurement.

Figure 8: (a) Z11 magnitudes from Z11 magnitudes of the  normal stacked TSV channel with TSV diameter variation, (b) insulator thickness variation and (c) TSV height variation


For the sensitivity analysis of the proposed failure mask  with process variations, the failure mask requires maximum 1


dB margin in Z11 magnitude when there are 10 % variation of  TSV design parameters such as TSV diameter, insulation layer  thickness and TSV height as shown in Fig. 8. The plotted  Z11 magnitudes are extracted by comparing Z11 magnitudes  with or without process variations. For example due to   unstable via etching processes, fabricated TSV diameter (dTSV) may differ from the original design value. A dTSV of 1 μm  corresponds to the margin of TSV diameter fabrication error  of maximum 10 % when dTSV is originally designed as 10 μm. With dTSV of 1 μm, Z11 magnitude varies by at most 0.4 dB  as shown in Fig. 8 (a). Due to unstable insulation layer  deposition processes, TSV oxide thickness (tox) can differ  from the originally designed value as shown in Fig. 8 (b). tox  of 0.1 μm corresponds to the margin of error of maximum  20 % when tox is assumed as 0.5 μm. With tox of 0.1 μm, Z11  magnitude differs by 1 dB. In addition, TSV height (hTSV) may  differ from the originally designed value because unstable  wafer thinning processes result in non-uniform substrate  thickness across a wafer. Consequently, the thickness of the  center of the wafer and the edge of the wafer can be different. ΔhTSV of 5 μm is the margin of TSV height error of maximum  10 % when hTSV is assumed as 50 μm. Even though there are  Z11 magnitude variations depending on process variations, the  proposed failure masks are still valid since those process  variations bring parallel level shift of Z11 magnitudes due to  the capacitance difference depending on process variations. As a result, those process variation effects do not require redesign of the proposed failure masks but the possible failure  detecting range may be reduced depending on the amount of  the process variation.


In this paper, novel TSV failure mask designs are  presented, allowing to detect and to differentiate TSV failures  such as insulator and connection failures. The proposed  analysis flow consists of frequency-domain measurements, extracting Z11 parameter up to 20 GHz on good (reference) and failing TSV channel. Depending on the failure types and  frequency ranges, the measured and simulated Z11  magnitudes differ between normal and failed in a  characteristic way. Thus, this technique can be utilized to  detect and differentiat TSV failures.

Enhanced failure analysis on open TSV interconnects


F. Altmann, C. Schmidt, J. Beyersdorfer, M. Simon-Najasek, C. Große

In this paper different methods and novel tools for failure  localisation and high resolution material analysis for open  TSV interconnects will be discussed. The paper shows the  application of enhanced methods for the localisation of  sidewall shorts in open TSV structures by adapted  Photoemission Microscopy (PEM), Lock-in Thermography (LIT) and Electron Beam Absorbed Imaging (EBAC). In  addition, a new highly efficient target preparation technique is  presented, which allows the combination of Laser and FIB  milling, in order to access TSV sidewall defects. Finally the  use of this technique is demonstrated in a failure analysis case  study.


In recent years, technological solutions for 3D integration of  semiconductor devices have increasingly attracted attention. For that reason the ams AG has developed new technologies  based on open TSV interconnects. Deep reactive ion etching  of Silicon and other standard Silicon processing steps are used  to form TSV sidewall isolations and metallisation with Ti/TiN, W and Al. Finally, the TSVs are capped by a SiO2 film and  SiN layers. A schematic cross section of the final TSV  structure is depicted in Fig. 1.

Conclusions and Outlook


Different failure analysis case studies utilizing EBAC imaging  for precise and sensitive short localisation were demonstrated  on shorted transistor gates and large area capacitors. FIB cross  sectioning and further SEM/TEM physical analyses were  performed to analyse the defect signature and to derive the  root causes of the failures. It was shown that EBAC can not  only be used for localising shorts and opens in metal networks, it can also provide precise defect localisation on thin IC oxides  with extremely low dissipation power preserving the original  defect structure and providing optimal conditions for defect  navigation within a FIB/SEM system.


In the future, the sensitivity of the whole probing and  amplifying system should be increased so that it will be  possible to localise even smallest oxide defects before they  completely break through. This technique can be used to  analyse defects without changing the physical structure by  current overload coming from the electrical stimulation  necessary for PEM or OBIRCH.


We would like to thank Reinhard Portius and Ekkehart  Brandstädt of XFAB Germany and Volkmar Gottschalk of  ELMOS Germany for kindly supplying specimen as well as  PEM and OBIRCH localisations.

Precise localization of 28nm via chain resistive defect using EBAC and  nanoprobing


P. Larréa, H. Tupina, C. Charlesa, R.H. Newtonb, A. Reverdyc


a STMicroelectronics, R&D Laboratory, 850 rue Jean Monnet, 38926 Crolles, France


b DCG Systems nanoInstruments Division, 1321 North Plano Road, Richardson, Texas 75081 c Sector technology, 2 avenue de Vignate, 38610 Gières, France


As technology nodes continue to shrink, resistive opens have become increasingly difficult to detect using  conventional methods such as AVC and PVC. The failure isolation method, Electron Beam Absorbed


Current(EBAC)Imaging has recently become the preferred method in failure analysis labs for fast and highly  accurate detection of resistive opens and shorts on a number of structures.  This paper presents a case study using a


two nanoprobe EBAC technique on a 28nm node test structure.  This technique pinpointed the fail and allowed direct  TEM lamella.


1. Introduction


As integrated circuit devices evolve to smaller feature  sizes and higher densities, the task of performing  successful failure analysis (FA) is becoming increasingly  difficult.  More and more often physical defects need TEM  imaging to be physically observed, and this requires  extremely high precision defect localization. Since the  thickness of a TEM lamella is around 100nm, the failure  localization must be at least as precise as the TEM lamella  is thin.  Moreover, any technique which can perform with  such precision must also be fast enough to keep up with  the throughput demands of the modern FA lab.


The resolution required to localize a failure on this kind  of structure makes the conventional fault localization  techniques inefficient, e.g., Emission Microscopy (EMMI), Light Induced Voltage Alteration (LIVA), and Thermallaser Induced Voltage Alteration(TIVA).  Generally  speaking, the current systems available using an optical  lens, even a SIL (Solid Immersion Lens), lack the high  resolution required for direct TEM lamella post fault  localization.


Two common SEM based techniques, Passive Voltage  Contrast(PVC) and Active Voltage Contrast(AVC) have  been used in the past to localize fails with better precision  than the aforementioned techniques.  However, even these  techniques have their limitations. First, both PVC and  AVC require an SEM with excellent image quality at low  accelerating voltages(less than 1kV) to obtain any sort of  resistive contrast. Secondly, both hydrocarbon  contamination and surface charging effects are detrimental  to the resolution of these techniques, and therefore make  meaningful failure isolation difficult [1]  As technology  nodes continue to shrink below 32nm, both techniques


have proven insufficient in many cases for pinpoint  localization of defects.


Recently, a technique called EBAC( Electron Beam  Absorbed Current) Imaging has reappeared in FA labs  after many years of dormancy.  This technique enables fast  isolation of resistive opens and shorts to the exact problem  contact, and makes direct TEM lamella possible.  EBAC  based FA is quickly becoming the “go to” method for open  and short detection in the most advanced FA labs in the  world, for its ability to quickly and directly isolate defects.


Unlike the previously mentioned techniques PVC and  AVC, the ability to obtain resistive contrast with EBAC is  not significantly affected by surface charging or  hydrocarbon contamination.  This is because EBAC does  not rely on secondary electrons emitted from the sample  surface, and thus surface effects such as those previously  mentioned do not significantly affect the absorbed current. While hydrocarbon contamination does not significantly  limit the resolution of the EBAC technique as it does with  PVC and AVC, it can limit the ability to obtain low  resistance ohmic contact between the nanoprobe and metal  line, and thus measures must be taken to inhibit the  formation.  The nanoprober used for this case study  includes an RF plasma downstream asher which is used  prior to SEM imaging and if used properly inhibits any  buildup of hydrocarbon contamination.


In this paper we present a case study involving the use  of a two nanoprobe EBAC technique commonly referred  to as RCI(Resistive Contrast Imaging). This technique  enables the isolation of lower resistance defects than the  commonly used single probe EBAC technique and makes  direct TEM lamella possible.

2. Experimental Setup


The following experiment was performed using a DCG  Systems dProber system with Electron Beam  Characterization(EBC) option. A high gain current to  voltage amplifier capable of 10^11V/A amplification


was used for all EBAC images(See Figure 2).  Imaging  is provided by a Zeiss Supra 55 FESEM and is capable of  excellent image quality at accelerating voltages <500V.


This experiment was performed on a 28nm CMOS test  structure. Figure 3 shows the vertical design of this  structure as a chain from M1 through contact to active.  This structure repeats and forms a chain of low resistance  contacts.  In Figure 5 the top layer M1 level is shown  across the entire test structure.  The sample is completely  planar at M1 level across the entire die. Additionally, the  M1 layer is completely exposed with no oxide inhibiting  low resistance ohmic  contact with any point on the  structure.   While Figure 3 shows the M1 and active layers  in the same direction, in reality they are at 90 degrees to  one another in the x y plane as shown in Figure 4.  Also as  shown in Figure 4, these chains are linked together to form  a 3.7um by 3.7um resistive path.  This same structure is  repeated three times and each one is connected to the next  at the active level as shown in Figure 5.


The goal of this study was to exactly isolate a resistive  defect which showed up in wafer level electrical test  results, but which needed further isolation to allow TEM  inspection.  In the end, a two probe EBAC technique was  chosen as the method to isolate the fail and the results are  given in the next section.

3. Steps and Results


3.1. Electrical characterization of the structure


DUPC


The first step in the analysis process was to confirm the  existence of a resistive fail.   To test, we performed a two  nanoprobe resistance measurement on each chain of  resistors for both a reference structure and the fail  structure.  The data obtained is shown in Table 1 below.


Table 1 Electrical Test Results


|Structure |1 |2 |3 |
|---|---|---|---|

|Reference |70KΩ |70KΩ |70KΩ |

|Fail |70KΩ |70KΩ |2000KΩ |

Since we know that each chain of resistors is composed  of 340 contacts, we can evaluate the resistance of a single  reference contact as approximately R~206Ω. In the same  way we can evaluate the fail contact as Rfail~1.930MΩ.  From our measurements we see that structure 3 shows a  very high level of resistance. At this point a dichotomy  technique sometimes colloquially referred to as “Divide  and Conquer” could be performed to isolate the fail [2]. The drawback of this technique is that it requires a  minimum of 8 to 9 resistance measurements to isolate the  fail on a structure with 340 contacts. Each measurement  requires repositioning and landing of two probes which  adds a significant amount of time to the failure isolation  when compare to a technique such as EBAC. Another  drawback of “Divide and Conquer” is that for the last few  measurements the probes are landed very close to the  defect, and this can cause the defect to change or disappear  completely.  All things considered, it is more secure and  quick to use techniques such as charge contrast, or EBAC.

3.2. Passive Voltage Contrast

Passive Voltage Contrast was quickly performed on


both reference and fail devices using an accelerating 1263voltage of 0.5kV.  As shown in Figure 5, no resistive  contrast is obtained on R3 despite the presence of an


approximately 2MΩ resistive defect.  PVC was deemed


insufficient in this case for defect isolation.


3.3. Single probe EBAC on fail structure


Single probe EBAC was performed next on the  structure also using an accelerating voltage of 0.5kV.  As  shown in Figure 6, no resistive contrast was obtained using  this technique.


3.3.1. Explanation of the result:


In Figure 7 we see a diagram of the device where R1


corresponds to the resistance of structure 1, R2  corresponds to the resistance of structure 2, and R3  corresponds to the resistance of structure 3. The left part of  R1 is connected to the EBAC amplifier via the signal  probe tip. We will assume many electrons are absorbed


into the line between R3 and another resistance R4 which  is connected to ground.  In reality, R4 corresponds to  lowest resistance path the absorbed electrons can take to  ground other than through the EBC amplifier.  In many  cases this path happens to be through the nanoprober stage.


This resistance is in general, very high, and has a profound  effect on the usefulness of single probe EBAC.  If


R1+R2+R3<<R4, then the vast majority of the electrons


1771


will travel to ground through the EBC amplifier and create

a signal on the EBAC image. This is what is happening in


our single probe EBAC analysis of the fail.   The  resistance of the fail is still much lower than the resistance  of the only other path to ground (the path through R4).  Thus we see the entire device “light up” as if there is no  fail.  Single probe EBAC did not isolate the fail in this


case, but in cases where the resistance of the fail is >~1GΩ, resistive contrast will be seen in the EBAC image  at the fail site[3].

3.4. Dual probe EBAC (RCI) on fail structure.


Since single probe EBAC was not able to isolate the  fail, we decide to land a ground probe on a line connected  to the right part of structure 3 to perform RCI.  The EBAC


signal probe is still landed on a line connected to the left  part of structure 1.  An SEM view of the setup is shown in  Figure 8. In Figure 9 we can clearly see an abrupt change  in contrast in structure 3.  This exactly isolates the defect  and in Figure 12 we see a zoomed in EBAC and SEM


overlay image of the defect which allows for easy  preparation of a TEM lamella.


3.4.1. Explanation of the result:


In Figure 10 and 11, Ra corresponds to the resistance  of structure 3 before the fail contact. Rb corresponds to the  resistance of the fail contacts. Rc corresponds to the  resistance of structure 3 after the fail contact. When the  incident beam arrives before the highly resistive contact,


nearly all of the electrons go through the amplifier. This is  because Ra<<Rb+Rc.


Figure 10: EBAC interaction before fail


When the incident beam arrives after the high resistive  contact, nearly all of the electrons go through the grounded  probe tip. In this case, we have Rc<<Rb+Ra so the  electrons have a low resistance path to ground to the right  of the fail contacts. The path to ground through the EBC


amplifier is no longer the lowest resistance path.  This is


Figure 11: EBAC interaction after fail


Figure 12: Composite EBAC SEM image of fail


why past the fail we do not see the EBAC image “light  up”.


3.5. TEM view of the defect


In Figure 13 we see the TEM image of the defect as a  resistive open interface between M1 and contact. This  defect is an interface of only 2nm, and creates a high  resistance between M1 and contact.


Figure 13: TEM view of defect


4. Conclusion:


This dual probe EBAC or RCI technique gives the  highly precise defect localization needed for direct TEM  lamella. Two contacts were suspected following this


analysis and were localized down to an area of 40nm x  200nm. This high level of localization has decreased the  turnaround time from initial failure identification to the  final reporting of a root cause. The success of this  experiment required the implementation of the dProber,


TEM, and the EBAC technique to create a direct path  between localization and TEM lamella.


Dual probe EBAC(RCI) has now become one of the   dominant techniques for IC failure analysis around the  world.  This dual probe EBAC(RCI) technique will prove  to be even more indispensible for future technologies such  as the coming 18nm node.
