In FAB 300mm Wafer Level Atomic Force Probe Characterization


Terence Kane


IBM Systems Technology Group/Microelectronics Division, Hopewell Junction, New York, USA


A 300mm  wafer atomic force prober (AFP) has been  installed into IBM’s manufacturing line to enable rapid, nondestructive electrical identification of defects.  Prior to this  tool many of these defects could not detected until weeks or  months later.


Moving failure analysis to the FAB  provides a means of  complementing existing FAB inspection and defect review  tools as well as providing independent, non-destructive  electrical measurements at an early point in the manufacturing  cycle [1] Once the wafer sites are non destructively AFP  characterized, the wafer is returned to its front opening unified  pod (FOUP) carrier and may be reintroduced into the  manufacturing line without disruption for further inspection or  processing.


Whole wafer atomic force probe electrical  characterization has been applied to 32nm, 28nm, 20nm and  14nm node technologies.  In this paper we explore the cost  benefits of performing non-destructive AFP  measurements on whole wafers. We have found the  methodology of employing a whole wafer AFP tool  complements existing in-line manufacturing monitoring  tools such as brightfield/dark field optical inspection, SEM in-line inspection and in-line E-beam voltage  contrast inspection (EBI).


Integrated Device Manufacturers working at advanced  technology nodes have been using nanoprobing to support the  needs in Failure Analysis and Fault Isolation as other methods  have faded in efficacy as technology nodes move into the  nanoscale.[1].  As nodes have scaled below 32nm, a great  majority of our device failure analysis must be electrically  characterized by AFP nanoprober in order to successfully  achieve root cause analysis.  Inside of the FAB however, nanoprobing has only been available by breaking wafers and  sending them to our FA lab.  The time required for analysis in  the FA lab creates an unacceptable barrier to providing such  measurements.


We have therefore acquired a 300mm wafer atomic force  nanoprober  in order to make critical nanoprobing  measurements readily available to the FAB.  Our strategy of  moving FA to the FAB is helping streamline new process  development and enhance yield.  Our intended uses for the  tool are to:


1) IMPROVE SUCCESS RATE OF CURRENT TOOLSET:


(TEM, FIB, EBI ETC.).


2) ACCELERATE EARLY PROCESS YIELD LEARNING


3) PERFORM NON-DESTRUCTIVE AFP IMAGING MODE


INSPECTIONS AT EARLY FEOL LEVELS


In order to fulfill its function for IBM’s FAB, we decided that   the tool should required to do the following:


1) Allow nanoprobing of 300mm substrates without  requiring fracturing of wafers, be FOUP compatible, communicate to Factory Automation software, leverage automation already available in wafer  probers and allow remote access to data.


2) Does not add contamination to wafer during  measurement so that wafer processing is still possible  without un do yield detractors or subsequent tool  contamination risk.


3) Must be sufficiently hands free so that some uses  such as routine inspections can be carried out without  intervention.  More complex circuit probing with user  command is allowed.


4) Provide full utility of nanoprobing suite of  applications so that the 300mm tool is not capability  limited relative to lab tools.


5) Throughput 10X greater than Lab nanoprobing.


Non-destructive detection of defects on 300mm wafers  within the FAB is described using these techniques and then  returning the wafers for subsequent manufacturing processing. This approach enables a closer to real time insight into yield  issues that would not be detected until subsequent BEOL  metallization processing and testing weeks to months later.


Whole wafer atomic force probe electrical  characterization has been applied to 32nm, 28nm, 20nm and  14nm node technologies.  In addition to the cost benefits of  performing non-destructive AFP measurements on whole  wafers, the AFP tool complements existing in-line  manufacturing monitoring tools such as brightfield/dark field  optical inspection, SEM in-line inspection and in-line SEM  passive voltage contrast inspection.


An example of the complementary role of in-line  SEM passive voltage contrast inspection followed by AFP  characterization will be detailed.  300mm AFP  characterization is applicable both to bulk silicon wafers as  well as SOI wafers.


The measurements provided by AFP disclose the true  nature of anomalies identified by in-line SEM inspection  and  eliminates subsequent  time consuming conventional  technology product limited yield (TPLY) analysis. TPLY  analysis is time consuming and involves destructively  fracturing the wafer, and iterative sample polishing and SEM  inspections.


To meet the very high throughput demands of a  manufacturing enviornment up to 15 sites per wafer per hour  would be tested with this 300mm AFP tool  to provide real


time feedback on front end of the line defects as well as timely  process information on FEOL process splits.


Tool Hardware and Use Description


The 300mm  wafer atomic force probing tool is based on  a conventional wafer probing platform common to in-line  testing and available with single or dual FOUP wafer  handling. The loading/unloading and alignment of wafers is  identical to the conventional prober operation with an onboard  upwards and downwards facing cameras performing optical  pattern recognition on the wafer and AFP probes.  This step  course aligns the wafer to the probes within a minute or so. Wafer defect site coordinates are loaded into the software  from the factory automation software when a wafer is loaded  into the prober.  When a site is manually or programmatically  selected, the wafer is positioned using the prober stage which  is good to about +2um (prober mfg specs).  Figure 1 shows a  wafer map with mapped defect sites shown in the  software.


Figure 1 :Wafer map of defect files allowing either  programmatical or point and click navigation.

The  picocurrent image at the bottom of the figure also includes a  second class of defect not detected by EBI.  The second class  of defect was related to the p-type devices and was of opposite  polarity.


Figure 3; AFP Picocurrent sensitivity range of femto  amps versus micro amps of sensitivity for EBI tool. (Reprinted from ISTFA 2010 ref 3)


In order to achieve the required noise immunity in a FAB  environment, it was necessary to mount the prober onto an  active isolation system.  Furthermore, the prober itself is a


noisy tool and custom isolation was installed in the mating  structure for the AFP that replaces a standard top plate.  The  mating structure and the frame of the prober are modified with  stiffening and alignment structures to improve the accuracy  and stability of the prober to enable it to become a nanoprober.  A single FOUP wafer prober with the AFP head was mated to  where a tester would ordinarily be placed.  In place of a tester, measurement instruments are used such as parametric  analyzers or capacitance, LCR meters etc.


Five AFP prober heads are integrated into the black


housing encompassing these heads. This is shown in Figure 4.  We required that the heads use IR laser force feedback so that  any stray beam on the sample did not induce OBIC currents


that might shift characterization results.  The performance is


similar to our FA lab tools although the instrument operation


is more user friendly because it is hands off and all


adjustments and positioning is controlled by software.


Figure 4: 300mm atomic force probing head. The five head  AFP design includes IR lasers, pico current imaging, NCVS  imaging.topography imaging, dC/dV measurements and AFP  IV electrical probing capabilities and hands off operation.


The tool as configured performs AFP pico current imaging, nanoprobe capacitance voltage spectroscopy (NCVS), atomic  force topography images as well as atomic force probe  electrical characterization as have been shown in previous  literature  [3,4,5,6,7,8,9 & 10].


Convenient and Early Electrical Characterization


A simple use of the tool was carried out by measuring  resistance values for a resistor structure.  Navigation was  accomplished using CAD coordinates and driving the stage  manually to the pattern of interest.  Figure 5 shows an image  of the metal 1 resistor structure. It was not designed with  probing pads separating the individual segments and was not  possible without a nanoprober.  This measurement highlights a  use of the tool to debug and control process parameters even  where no pre-defined in line test structure exists.  Graph 1 is  an example of the results.


Figure 5:AFP Image of 32nm M1 to Silicide Resistor  Structure


Graph 1: In-line process control example of AFP


measurement of actual vs. predicted value of a resistor


structure.


This example is a simple depiction of the tool’s utility in being  able to perform precise electrical inspections although no  design provision is made to allow it at in-line test. Arbitrary  electrical tests can be performed at silicide up to first  metallization levels.


Inspection and EFA of Defect Maps


An example of the tool’s role as a complimentary function of  improving the existing toolset’s success rate is shown below in  Figure 6.  As described above, a wafer that has been  previously inspected in an EBI tool was loaded into the  prober.  The wafer map of EBI defect locations is displayed  from a defect map shown in Figure 1.  A subset of the defect  sites are re-acquired using pico-current imaging.  It is only  necessary to review a small portion of the defects for several  reasons.  First, most of the defects will turn out to be the same


defect repeated many, many times over a wafer. Second, as is  shown in the result, the tiny scan includes both the EBI  mapped defect and several examples of other defects  undetected or undefined by the Ebeam tool.  The image in  Figure 6 shows the image captured by the E beam tool  highlighting the isolated defect (black contact in center of  image).


e I


Figure 6: in-line eBeam inspection image of defect mapped in  defect map shown in Figure 1 and AFP Imaged and probed  and then again optically in Figure 2


The motivation as to why the defect should be re-acquired and  imaged and probed by AFP is important to understand.  The eBeam tool is fast and capable of locating many such defects  but in many cases, it cannot provide much definitive  information about the defect.  For instance a simple e-beam “dark” could be a missing contact or one that has an open  electrical connection.  In the case above, the contact mapped  was in fact a missing contact.  It is not clear if it was a void or  poorly adhered and pulled out during CMP.  What is clear is  that follow on physical analysis would have been required to  define the type of failure.  With the AFP, confirmation comes  far more quickly than a slow and destructive physical analysis.  In the case of more complicated leakages highlighted by the  EBI tool, at sub 30nm geometries, the TEM is often the only  tool that can be used to locate the failure.  There is an  enormous overhead associated with obtaining TEM results but  in order for a successful analysis; the nanoprobing result is  required before the physical analysis.


Figure 7 shows an example where an EBI tool defect  map did not match the marking tool which used the same set  of coordinates.  The wafer is required to return to a defect  review tool to image the error so that subsequent lamella pluck  can proceed.  This iterative inspection routine is both costly  and time consuming and the success rate suffers.  The AFP


analysis of a reacquired defect often makes subsequent  physical FA unnecessary as the type of defect is already  electrically defined via nanoprobing.


Figure 7: Errors in subsequent FIB cuts and sample lift out


can be addressed with AFP Pico Current Imaging and


Marking


In addition to complimenting the existing TPLY and  inspection tool suite, the wafer AFP tool adds value and saves  time because wafers may be analyzed in the AFP for defect  review and then be subsequently processed either directly or  with a mild cleaning step without going for TPLY.  This has  already taken place in our FAB and initial results appear to  indicate that nanoprobing with the tool does not contaminate  the wafer or interfere with subsequent processing.  This  advantage of AFP over current TPLY is under study and will  be the subject of a follow-on paper


AFP supplements In-Line Testing


A yield issue was investigated involving an analog  sensor embedded into a logic chip.  These types of analog  sensors, in this case a thermal sensor, tend to require tight  control of threshold voltage in both the NFET and PFET  transistors.  Furthermore, the sub 30nm gate length process  will not inherently yield threshold values appropriate for many  analog sensing circuits and therefore the layout is heavily  modified to produce the correct drive currents and Vth and  each of these must balance in the NFET and PFET  transistors  for the circuit to function.


Previous to this work, this structure had been  investigated in our lab using the AFP.  Because some of the  measurements from that work showed a Vth mismatch, a test  structure was added to the scribe line and an inline monitor of  this transistor design was added to the standard testing.  Despite the inline monitoring of the process, the yield issue  did not resolve itself sufficiently and a wafer was directed to  the AFP 300 for a more thorough investigation.  A small  portion of that study is included here because it demonstrates  the utility of the wafer level approach; and particularly the  high throughput relative to the lab AFP tool.


The sensor device consists of multiple fingers and is


duplicated at various positions on the die and repeated across Drain/Body the wafer.


The inline monitor results shown in green on the


wafer map in Figure 8 measured mild Vth variations depending


upon the die and wafer location.  The AFP wafer tool was


Source


used to interrogate the product transistors and attempt to


correlate the e-test monitor values to the product sites.  While Gateit is conceivable to attempt this with the FA lab AFP tool, it  was estimated that it would have consumed about 3 months of


full lab time and therefore was not feasible..  The full probing


results were obtained in 3 days with the FAB tool.

Because the entire wafer is at probing level, it is


02

unnecessary to delayer individual samples as in the lab and  therefore far easier to probe subsequent die.   Furthermore, there is no possibility that the die position can be lost as the  wafer is intact and with probing data is stored with navigation  information.  The propensity to confuse the probing data die  location when performing dozens of measurements makes this  type of study impractical on anything but a full wafer tool.  The ability to probe the product site devices easily over the  entire wafer addressed issues of center, middle or edge wafer  locations and possible lithography dependencies.

Comparison with In Line Tester


We were asked to compare the transistor parametric obtained  in the functional product array to the inline tester results to  verify our results.  The AFP nanoprober is quite capable of  measuring these structures by placing its probes onto the  probing pads.  The result of this comparison was very  surprising.  75% of the devices that had been measured by the  tester registered a severe gate to well leakage as is shown in  Figure 11.  25% of the devices previously measured appeared  normal.  The result was solidified by measuring devices that  had not been tested.  100% of those devices were undamaged.  In either the working tested or untested devices, the Vth  measurements were in good agreement with our product array  results.  Example data is shown in Figure 11.


Figure 11: Top: 75% of Temperature Sense Transistor  measured by in-line tester showed a gate short when measured  by the AFP. Bottom: 25% of same devices were not damaged  by tester.  Finding that testers damaged e-test monitors was an  unexpected result of this circuit debugging.


Summary


The methodology of 300mm AFP characterization both as a  stand alone tool as well as a complementary tool at contact  level to existing in-line SEM passive voltage contrast tools  without destructively  damaging wafers is clearly  demonstrated.


This methodology will provide a real time insight into yield  issues that would not be detected until subsequent BEOL  metallization processing and testing.

"Going One Step Beyond" sometimes involves bringing the  past back into the present, to allow the Failure Analysis  community to continue their contribution to present and future  technologies.  Circuit Level probing is testing, measuring or  characterizing the operation of an electronic circuit.  It has  been around as long as there have been circuits.  It is Circuit  Level probing which reveals the true operation of a circuit, and takes the next step beyond electrical engineering design  and circuit simulation.  This paper presents the return of  Circuit Level probing in nanoelectronics by means of SEM  based nanoprobing.


Though Circuit Level probing is not a new concept, it has not  been possible with today's nanoelectronics.  This has been  primarily due to the mismatch between the spatial  characteristics of hand-held or bench probing capabilities and  ever smaller ground rule dimensions of the samples.  Prior to  integrated components (ICs), discrete components in a chassis  or on a printed circuit board could be probed with hand-held  test probes.  With integrated circuits, the test probes were  placed with X-Y-Z micrometer positioners within bench  probing systems [1].  Eventually, as technology nodes became  smaller, the accuracy and precision of micrometer positioners

became problematic.  It was in this phase of microelectronics

integrated into the bench probing process [2].  The interaction


220


between FIBs and today’s nanoelectronic materials, plus

the continuum between fully on and fully off.  The vast  majority of Failure Analysis tools and techniques for Circuit  Level analysis are based in or optimized for Digital Circuits.

This includes the charge based technique of Voltage Contrast (VC) [3].  It also includes optical / electrical analysis  techniques such as Photon Emission Microscopy (PEM) [4], Laser Assisted Device Alteration (LADA) [5], Soft Defect  Localization (SDL) [6], Laser Voltage Probe (LVP) [7], and  Laser Voltage Imaging (LVI).


Background of Failure


Today’s nanoelectronics, though dominated by Digital  Circuits, also contain Analog Circuits.  A Digital Thermal  Sensor (DTS) is one example of an Analog Circuit.  Its  purpose is to measure the temperature of the semiconductor by  means of a diode.  Further information about the DTS circuit  will be presented in the next section.


Associated with the Functional Test performed on the product, each DTS circuit is calibrated to ensure its output is accurate.  This calibration routine is incorporated within the circuit  design; however, the range of temperature readings can be  compromised if excessive adjustment is made during the  calibration.  Characterization of the amount of adjustment  required for each DTS circuit, across the individual product  die, indicated specific DTS circuits were systematically offset  with respect to other DTS circuits and design targets (Fig. 1).  Across wafer characterization of the across product die  signature found the edge of the wafer had more offset than the  wafer center (Fig. 2).


Figure 1.  Digital Thermal Sensor (DTS) circuit response  across a component.  Blue arrows point to typical circuit  response.  Purple arrows point to the offset circuit response.


Figure 2.  Across Wafer Variation of DTS circuit response  indicating the edge is worse than the center.  Worse response  is seen by the amount and intensity of red.


Digital Thermal Sensor Circuit


Though the name of the DTS circuit contains the word “digital”, it is indeed an Analog Circuit.  The digital aspects of  the circuit pertain to the calibration adjustment and how the  circuit is incorporated into the product’s digital nature.  The  analog nature of the DTS circuit includes the resistors, diodes, Operational Amplifier (Op Amp), and Current Mirrors (Fig. 3).


Key to the proper operation of the DTS circuit, and the basis  for the failure analysis, is the need for the following Analog  Circuit characteristics to function properly.  The resistors must  have their ratio matched to a tighter tolerance than their  absolute values.  The forward bias, current versus voltage (IV), characteristic needs to be matched for the multiple diodes.  The Input Offset Voltage characteristic of the Op Amp should  be small.  The Transconductance, gate voltage versus drain  current (Vg-Id), and Output, drain voltage versus drain current (Vd-Id), characteristics of the Current Mirror devices should  be matched.


As indicated previously, a systematic across product die offset  was observed.  During this analysis two anomalous DTS  circuits were analyzed, and the results were compared to a  typical DTS circuit.  This allowed the consistency of any  abnormalities to be observed, plus their findings were  compared to design information and a known typical DTS  circuit.  The two anomalous DTS circuits are designated  Instance #2 (Inst 2) and Instance #3 (Inst 3), with the typical  circuit designated Instance #1 (Inst 1).

Circuit Probing – Resistance Matching


The DTS circuit contains two resistors, one located in the  feedback loop of the Op Amp and the other in the output  portion of the circuit.  Di2res is the Op Amp resistor and is  associated with diode Di2.  Rlad is the output resistor which  contains multiple “unit resistors” in a binary ladder and is  associated with the calibration adjustment.  There is an 18 to 7  design value for Rlad to Di2res, with actual values of 13,500  ohms and 5,200 ohms, respectively.


The values of the two resistors were obtained by Kelvin  probing at Back End of Line (BEOL) nodes.  Forced current  values were chosen to ensure both precision in the voltage  values and no self heating of the resistors.  The 18 to 7 design  value, or 2.57 ratio, was found to be between 2.54 and 2.55 for  the three DTS circuits.  These represented an error of 0.7% to


1.1%, with the one anomalous and the typical DTS circuit  having the larger value (Fig 4).  The absolute value of each  resistor is also within design tolerances, indicating the  resistors were not responsible for the anomalous DTS circuit  response.

Circuit Probing – Diode Matching


The DTS circuit contains multiple diodes, and they are the key  circuit elements which respond to temperature.  Two diodes  are used as inputs to the Op Amp, and one diode is used as a  forward voltage monitor.  All the diodes are forward biased in  the DTS circuit operation, where they convert circuit current  into voltage.  The forward bias voltage matching across the


diodes is important.


The diode forward voltage was measured as the circuit power  supply, Vdrc, was swept from zero to the circuit maximum


88918088919893653383839188849


(Vmax).  The diodes connect directly to circuit ground, thus  the diode voltage was measured directly at the DTS circuit.  Also measured was the Op Amp output node, Mirror.  Simultaneously measurements of these multiple circuit nodes  provided the data needed to calculate the gate to source  voltage (Vgs), of the Current Mirror devices.  The Vgs of the  Current Mirror devices is referenced as PFET Vckt - Vdrc  when it is calculated with respect to the Vdrc voltage, and  indicates when the operation of the DTS circuit is in a stable  state with a consistent output current.


Two significant observations were obtained from the diode  matching Circuit Probing.  The diode forward voltage was  measured, which is component level information.  Also  obtained, and necessary for proper interpretation of the diode  voltage, was circuit level information on when it was  operating in a stable state.  For all three DTS circuit instances, stable operation occurred before the minimum power supply  voltage (Vmin).  The diode forward voltage values for each  DTS circuit instance are well matched, and at the expected  voltage, when the circuit power supply was between Vmin and  Vmax (Figs. 5-6).

Circuit Probing – Op Amp Offset Voltage


The DTS circuit uses an Op Amp to convert the temperature  dependant response of diodes into a signal which the circuit  then uses to generate its output.  The basic operation of the Op  Amp is to provide a proper output current signal, based on the  values at its inputs.  The Input Offset Voltage, the difference  between the positive and negative inputs, indicates whether  the Op Amp output is at the proper value.  This Op Amp  Offset voltage should be small.


The Op Amp Offset voltage was measured using the same  approach as the diode matching characterization, where the  power supply voltage was swept from zero though Vmax.  In a  similar fashion, simultaneous voltage measurements were  taken on the two Op Amp inputs (Di1 and Di2), plus other key  circuit nodes.


The Op Amp Offset characterization reconfirmed the DTS  circuit operation was stable between Vmin and Vmax.  Also  observed, the calculated Op Amp Offset Voltage was similar  between DTS circuit instances, and at an expected value  ranging between 1 mV and 3 mV (Fig. 8 and 10).  Both Op  Amp input voltages have a similar response as the diodes (Fig. 7 and 9.)

Circuit Probing – Mirrored Current


The DTS circuit uses multiple Current Mirror devices within  the Op Amp, and to provide DTS circuit outputs based upon  the Op Amp signal.  The Current Mirror devices should be  matched in their Transconductance (Vg-Id) and Output (VdId) characteristics.  The current in the Op Amp feedback loop, which includes resistor Di2res, is the more significant current.  It is this current which should be mirrored in the other Current  Mirror devices.


The Mirrored Current, or current in the Op Amp feedback


三会38员男的安员5on∞吕台988管


loop, was characterized using the same approach as the other  Circuit Probing characterization, where the power supply  voltage was swept from zero to Vmax.  Simultaneous voltage  measurements were taken at Di2 and Di2res, plus other key  circuit nodes.


Mirrored Current was calculated using the measured voltages  and the previously measured resistance.  Again, the circuit  operation was confirmed to be stable between Vmin & Vmax.  The Op Amp feedback loop characteristic for Di2res was the  same as other diodes, with Di2 having a constant voltage  offset from Di2res (Fig. 11 and 13).  The calculated Mirrored  Current between DTS circuit instances was matched, and at  expected values near 13 uA (Fig. 12 and 14).

Circuit Probing – Device Characterization


The previous Circuit Probing characterization indicates the  Resistance matching, Diode matching, Op Amp Offset  matching, and Mirrored Current values are as expected with  no significant differences between the typical DTS circuit and  the anomalous DTS circuits.  These were implemented as the  measurements would not affect the DTS circuit operation and  they, or calculations based on the measurements, were easily  interpreted.  Unfortunately, the cause of the systematic offset  in the anomalous DTS circuits has not yet been identified.


882288884882889889 88888888868888666666*********


More in-depth Circuit Probing characterization was needed to  identify the nature of the anomalous DTS circuits.  These tests  were developed to query additional circuit information; however, they had to be defined to ensure they would not  influence the operation of the DTS circuit, plus the DTS  circuit operation would not alter the validity of the test results. The primary interest of this in-dept Circuit Probing test was to  characterize the other Current Mirror devices, and more  specifically the matching between them.  The test definition  used a somewhat similar approach to the previous Circuit  Probing characterization; however, it was modified to obtain  the device Output characteristics (Vd-Id).  The power supply  voltage was stepped between Vmin and Vmax, while other  circuit voltages were swept across a range within the stable  operation of the DTS circuit.  In essence this resembled family  of curves device testing, but was implemented at the circuit  level.  This test was applied to other Current Mirror devices, referenced as J2 and J4.


The raw data obtained from this circuit level characterization  of the discrete devices is not easily interpreted from direct  observation.  However, all the data was available to calculate  the device’s gate to source voltage (Vgs) and drain to source  voltage (Vds).  These, along with the direct measurement of  the drain current (Id), allowed the Output characteristics to be  plotted within the stable operating range of the DTS circuit.  These same calculations were applied to the Mirrored Current  test data, and the device data for all three devices was overlaid  for each DTS circuit instance (Figs. 15-16).  The data obtained  from J2 and J4 is consistent with Output characteristics of  devices with Vgs steps.  Also observed is how the DTS circuit  actually transitions across the device Output characteristics as  the circuit enters stable operation and moves within the Vmin  to Vmax range.


The typical DTS circuit instance #1 had J2 and J4 current  mismatching (with respect to Mirrored Current) of 4.5% and


1.5%, plus absolute Id values of 13.9 uA to 13.3 uA, respectively.  The anomalous DTS circuit instance #2 had a J2


88888888888888888888444444444


and J4 current mismatching (with respect to Mirrored Current) of 8.8% and 0.7%, plus absolute Id values of 12.4 uA and 13.7  uA, respectively.  The cause of the anomalous DTS circuit  performance was now identified to be lower drive current on  the J2 leg of the circuit.  This was also directionally correct  with the required adjustment during the DTS circuit  calibration.

Circuit Probing – Measurement Repeatability


As the Failure Analysis of the DTS circuit progressed, with  the identification of low drive current on the J2 leg of the  Current Mirrors as the circuit anomaly, a significant amount of  test data was collected.  The depth and breadth of this data  allowed various aspects of measurement and circuit  repeatability to be analyzed.  These would include circuit  operation within the SEM vacuum environment, electron  beam contamination of the sample affecting the electrical  probing, repeatability, accuracy and precision of Circuit  Probing test data obtained on nanoelectronic components, plus  sample charging, materials alteration, and device degradation

Enhanced Comparison of Lock-in Thermography and Magnetic Microscopy for  3D defect localization of System in Packages


Christian Schmidt, Frank Altmann


Fraunhofer-Institute for Mechanics of Materials IWM Halle, Germany


David P. Vallett


IBM Systems & Technology Group, Essex Junction, Vermont, USA


Lock-in thermography and magnetic current imaging are  emerging as the two image-based fault isolation methods most  capable of meeting the challenges of short and open defect  localization in thick, opaque assemblies. Such devices are  rapidly becoming prevalent as 3D integration begins to ramp  up production. This paper expands on previously published  work with a qualitative comparison of the techniques on single  chip and stacked die packages with known designed-in or FIBcreated defects.


Facing the challenge of 3D defect localization at fully  packaged devices and complex system in packages, Lock-in  thermography (LIT) and magnetic current imaging (MCI) are  seen as promising solutions to fulfill the necessary  requirements. Both techniques are especially well-suited for  imaging through the relatively thick and opaque layers used in  IC packaging and will become critical as 3D packaging  technologies emerge. On one side, LIT is especially useful for  the fault isolation concerning local resistance changes by  detecting the resulting temperature change with high spatial  resolution and sensitivity. Due to the fact that heat propagation  through opaque material layers is time-depending, the method  can be further used for internal heat transfer investigations, which consequently enables both lateral and depth-resolved  defect localization [1]. On the other hand, MCI is used for  imaging magnetic field distributions as a result of the applied  electrical current. Here, 3D localization can be obtained by  analyzing the peak-to-peak distance which is a function of the  defect depth [2]. An additional advantage to MCI is the  possibility not only to detect local shorts but also open circuits. A direct comparison of both techniques with respect to single  IC and localization through transparent material layer has been  published [3]. This paper extends this comparison with new  work for the FA on stacked-die devices.  LIT and MCI are  applied on samples with predetermined defects having known  configurations and known depths in both single and stacked  chip formats. It will also report on the newly developed  capability of MFI to image and localize ‘dead opens’ or


completely open circuits. Finally, for LIT an experimental  approach is presented for systematic investigations on different  defect geometries as a possible approach to further improve  3D LIT application. Here, a laser setup with different focus  positions is applied on a reference material layer and the  resulting spot is correlated to the determined phase shift  values.


Sample description


General information


For comparing the abilities and weaknesses of both methods, fully packaged test devices including single die and two-die  systems are used. Both used types of these designed-for-defect  localization samples are shown in Figure 1. For single die  samples, an IC with a thickness of 180μm and lateral  dimensions of 7.2mm by 7.2mm is encapsulated in a mould  compound (MC) resign. For two-die devices, a second die, with a thickness of 180μm and lateral dimensions of 5.26mm x


5.26mm, is stacked on top of the first by using die-attach tape (DAT). Consequently, defects in two different depths to the  device surface can be investigated:


• z1: 655μm


• z2: 480μm


Figure 1: Schematic buildup of used single (top) and two-diedevices (bottom).


Both dies are fully processed and internally electrical  connected using bond-wiring processes and contain several  different and design-related defect scenarios.


Designed defect structures


Both types of device contain ICs with defined test structures  for simulation of different defect scenarios. An overview is  given in Figure 2, naming shorts close to the bonding area (1), electrical opens (2, only for MCI), corner cracks sensors as  short-circuits (3) and chip-to-chip-chains (4). In addition, crack sensors and meander-shaped line structures (250 x 250 μm² with 3μm line space) are designed. Electrical connection  is provided internally by using bond-wires and externally  solder balls on the devices backside. Each single defect

Operational principle of applied FA methods


Standard and 3D Lock-in Thermography (LIT)

For investigations using LIT, electrical active defects are


Die 2


stimulated using a pulsed excitation signal. The resulting local  temperature increase is captured by a sensitive IR camera and


Die 1


internally correlated using a set of sin/cos-functions. As a  result, amplitude and phase signal of the propagating thermal  wave can be determined with high spatial resolution (μm) and  high sensitivity (μW). The operational principle is described in  detail in [4, 5].  An overview over the resulting measurement  images is shown in  Figure 3.


For 3D defect localization, a depth estimation is enabled by  quantitative analysis of the phase signal. Due to the timedependency of thermal propagation through opaque material  layers, a time delay (phase shift) can be detected between  stimulation and thermal response signal. The total amount of  phase shift is related to the present layer thickness of the  investigated material and its thermal properties (heat  conductivity, heat capacity). Using this behaviour, a  calibration model of the theoretical phase shift can be  generated if the internal device build up is known, Figure 4.

Depending on the defect depth, the resulting thermal wave has  to propagate different numbers of material layers resulting in a “fingerprint”-phase shift value. Comparing both, theoretical  and experimental determined phase shift behaviour, a defect  depth resolution is realised, Figure 5.  The accuracy of this  approach can be further increased if several instead of a single


number of lock-in frequencies are applied.


Magnetic microscopy and 3D Magnetic Current Imaging (MCI)


Magnetic current imaging for packaged devices is  accomplished using a high-temperature (72K) superconducting  quantum interference device (SQUID) to measure the vertical  components of the magnetic fields surrounding conductors  carrying current through short circuits or current arising from  standing wave reflections due to opens. For imaging shorts a  small AC bias is utilized on the sample to enable lock-in  amplification for noise reduction. For opens, an RF waveform (60-200 MHz) is applied to generate a reflected standing wave  current – a method known as space domain reflectometry (SDR) [6]. Magnetic field images are then transformed into  current density images which typically show higher gradients  inherent in localized narrow current paths or dissolution of  current density where narrower paths dissipate to much

broader conductors or power planes at short circuit sites. For  open circuits the current density rapidly decreases toward the  open approaching zero at the actual end of the conductive  path. The operational concepts of both methods are shown in  Figure 6.


For 3D MCI applications, depth estimations are possible by  analysing the relationship between magnetic field strength, current and geometrical distance between peaks in the zcomponent of the magnetic field intensity normal to the  surface of the sample (current direction is also easily  determined as the fields surrounding active conductors are  bipolar). In this manner it is possible to estimate both absolute  current depth and relative distance between two or more  conductors. Figure 7 shows a schematic demonstration of the  magnetic field image where a sufficiently straight and isolated  current path exists, producing two distinct peaks in the zdirection.

Daisy-chain structure


A daisy-chain structure also modified by FIB with an open  circuit was analyzed with MCI/SDR. Figure 8C shows the  resulting current density images indicating the location of the  open circuit in the region where the signal decreases to zero. An overlay with the device x-ray image in Figure 8D shows  the standing wave signal ending at the open circuit just at the  die edge where the wire bond terminates and the open daisy  chain is located.


Short investigations using LIT and MCI


Corner crack sensor - LIT


Starting the short investigations on die level, a corner crack  sensor is stimulated with an electrical power of ~ 5mW. For an  optimal spatial resolution a lock-in frequency of 10Hz and a  measurement time of ~30min have been chosen. Figure 9  shows the obtained thermal emission. Furthermore, from the  shape of the hot spot the edges of the die area can be  estimated.

Investigations on stacked-die devices


Corner crack sensor on different die levels - LIT


Following the investigations on a single die device, LIT is  used for 3D defect localization on different die levels. Hereby, corner crack sensors on both die levels are electrical  stimulated and the resulting phase shift is determined. Due to  the pyramid structure of the device, both areas are covered  solely by Mould Compound. The resulting phase shift vs. frequency relationship is shown in Figure 13.


Both phase shift gradient are in good agreement to the  theoretical behavior. The results show a clear and easy


separation of both die levels which consequently enables 3D


defect localization. Using an electrical power of ~5mW, the  measurement time can be limited to ~ 20 – 30 min in total.

Influences of the defect geometry to the resulting  phase shift for 3D LIT investigations


Theoretical consideration and experimental setup


Besides the influences of geometrical thickness and thermal


properties of the propagated material, also the defect geometry 1,2mm


1,2mm


can influence the resulting phase shift. Figure 15 shows three  different types of geometries including point, line and  extended heat sources and the resulting temperature


distributions. Known from theory, those differences can  influence the resulting phase shift determinations [4].

In order to investigate these influences, an experimental setup 1,2mm


1,2 mm


is applied using a fiber laser at 1064 nm for heat stimulation of  a well-known material layer, Figure 16. The laser is focused in  a close distance to the sample backside and triggered by the


LIT system.


Stepwise defocusing of the laser beam from the sample  backside leads to a constant increase of the beam diameter. Starting from a point-like heat source, with increasing distance  an area heat source is realized and the resulting phase shift is  measured. For comparable results, a gaussian laser beam  profile is used which remains constant for all investigations.


Experimental results


In Figure 17, the determined spot size at lock-in frequency of  25 Hz for different distances to the sample back side are


visible. As assumed, with increasing distance an increase of  the spot size can be regarded.


λ=1064nm


In order to determine the absolute spot size, line scan are  applied and fitted by Gaussian functions, Figure 18 (top). The  comparison of the different spot profiles show a constantly  decrease of the absolute amplitude in combination with an  increasing spot size.


Laser


Knowing the size of a single detector pixel and the optical  magnification of the system, a spot size can be calculated. As a  result, the measured phase shift vs. frequency relationship is  shown in Figure 19 with respect to the spot size.


The investigations show that with increasing spot size a phase  shift offset occurs. For frequencies higher than 5Hz, this offset  can be regarded as constant. Only for low frequencies, where  heat can propagate deep and 3-dimensional into the material  the resulting offset is related to the applied frequency. With  respect to depth determination in microelectronic devices, this  influence can be disregarded due to low thermal properties of  capsulation and die attach materials.

For defect localizations using LIT the ability of short detection  has been presented for both single and stacked die devices. Here, LIT shows the advantage of buried defect localization  even under several hundred micrometer of capsulation  material. Due to its averaging nature, the sensitivity and in  consequence the precision of 3D defect localization can be  adapted by optimizing both measurement time and applied  lock-in-frequency. Influences of defect variables (e.g. defect  geometry, lateral defect position) also have to be regarded and  a first approach concerning different defect geometries has  been presented.


MCI capability for imaging current paths due to shorts in both  single and stacked die has also been shown. As with LIT it is  possible to image buried defects with high sensitivity as very  strong signals were detected with uW of applied power even in  the stacked die configuration. The additional MCI method of  SDR has also been presented showing the ability to also image  opens thereby significantly broadening the application of MCI  for 3D packages.

Non destructive failure analysis of 3D electronic packages using both Electro  Optical Terahertz Pulse Reflectometry and 3D X-ray Computed Tomography


Yan Li, Yongming Cai, Mario Pacheco, Rajen C Dias, and Deepak Goyal


Intel Corporation, Chandler, Arizona, U.S.A  yan.a.li@intel.com


The 3D package configuration presents challenges to  conventional Fault Isolation (FI) and Failure Analysis (FA) methods. This paper illustrates that with correct Electro  Optical Terahertz Pulse Reflectometry (EOTPR) data  processing, interpretation and additional reference spectra, the  combination of EOTPR to isolate the open/high resistance  failure location and 3D X-ray Computed Tomography (CT) to  image the failure is very effective for System in a Package (SIP) FI/FA.


Semiconductor industry demand for higher levels of  integration, lower costs, and a growing need for a complete  system configuration has resulted in a continued drive towards  3D electronic package solutions.  A System in a Package (SIP) is a type of 3D package, which is characterized by the  inclusion of more than one type of active electronic  component in a single package, and usually has a package on  package configuration [1]. Figure 1 illustrates the schematic of  a typical SIP, where a field-programmable gate array (FPGA),

in the form of a ball grid array (BGA) package, was mounted


in tandem with a flip chip CPU die onto a common package  substrate.  The resultant joint between the FPGA package and  the substrate was termed mid level interconnect (MLI). While  the solder joints between the CPU to the substrate and the

Figure 1: Schematics of a typical SIP.


Due to the increased complexity in 3D packages, multiple  failures may exist in different locations of one package after  the reliability test, thus non destructive fault isolation (FI) and  failure analysis (FA) techniques with shorter throughput time (TPT), higher success rate, and capability of analyze multiple  failures in one unit, are highly desirable.


Time-domain reflectrometry (TDR) has been used as a very  effective non destructive FI technique to isolate both open and  short failures in flip chip packages. It injects a step electrical  pulse into the packaging interconnects, and collects the  reflections from the package. By comparing the reflected  waves from the failed unit with those from the good reference  unit, and the bare substrate, the location of the failure can be  isolated to be either in the die or the package substrate. However it is difficult to precisely tell the exact location of a  failure due to the limited resolution of ~500μm when 35-40 ps  rise time of the pulse is used [2].


The next generation of TDR, which is called Electro Optical  Terahertz Pulse Reflectometry (EOTPR), was then developed  to further improve this non destructive technique [2]. As  shown in Fig. 2, EOTPR uses an ultrafast laser pulse striking  onto a biased photoconductive switch to produce a very fast  electrical pulse, which is then injected into the package. The  reflected signal is picked up by another photoconductive  switch.  With a very short rise time of 5.7ps, and high  resolution of 10μm, the failure location could be precisely  identified using EOTPR [2].

As illustrated in Fig. 3a, 3D X-ray computed tomography (CT) relies on a basic operation principle of using an x-ray source to  radiate the object at different tilt angles, with a rotating stage  providing such angular displacement in equally spaced angles, and a detector collecting the 2D x-ray images at each angle[3]. All 2D images are mathematically superimposed and  processed to obtain a three dimensional map of the sample, as  shown schematically in Fig. 3b. Since the processed data  contain the volumetric information of the sample under test, the analyst can manipulate it to display virtual cross-sections  or slice views at any given location of the three dimensional  data set. This provides the capability of removing interfering


features in the field of view that may mask out the signal  produced by the defect in single 2D x-ray images, The  outstanding property of 3D x-ray CT as analytical method  comes from the fact that subtle information of the defect  contained in each 2D image becomes important when it is  added to signal contributions at a specific xyz location in  every single of whole set of images collected at equally spaced  angles. Due to its high resolution of 1.5-2μm, and much  shorter TPT compared with physical FA, 3D X-ray CT has  become a very important non destructive FA technique to  image package failures such as trace cracks, solder joint  defects, solder joint bridging, substrate via cracks etc.


Figure 3: (a) Basic schematic of the 3D x-ray CT setup  consisting of x-ray source, rotating stage and detector. (b) The  projections at each angle of the intensity of collected 2D x-ray  images are superimposed and mathematically processed to  generate the three dimensional image.


This paper describes how a combination of EOTPR and 3D Xray can be used to fault isolate and image the defect none  destructively in a 3D package.


Experimental results and Discussion


A FLI daisy Chain (DC) in the test vehicle (TV) of FCPGA  package failed open after the reliability test. After the open  failure was confirmed using hand probing, the unit was ready  for further failure analysis.


Conventional TDR results


A set of conventional TDR data was obtained first to see if it  could isolate the failure. As shown in Fig. 4, the orange  spectrum, which is from a bare SIP substrate, could be used to  determine the location of the MLI. To identify the location of  the FLI (inside the FPGA package), the spectrum from a  reference unit without the FPGA die is acquired and shown in  blue. It overlaps with the red waveform taken from the failed


unit, suggesting that the failure is close to the FLI joint in the  FPGA package. However it is difficult to distinguish if the  failure is in the FPGA substrate or in the FPGA die. To further  isolate the failure, EOTPR analysis is needed.


There are four main aspects to effective EOTPR interpretation  of 3D packages with multiple levels of interconnects: understanding of the EOTPR incident waveform, selection of  correct ground pin, acquiring of additional reference spectra, and indentifying of the exact physical failure location in the  package.


Figure 4: Conventional TDR spectra from the failed unit, the  golden unit, a bare SIP substrate, and a SIP package without  FPGA die.


Understand the EOTPR incident waveform


Unlike a step electrical pulse used in conventional TDR, EOTPR uses a pulse with a sharp peak. As illustrated in Fig. 5, the incident impulse in reality carries some noise that rises  from internal reflections due to GaAs boundary in the  photoconductive switch, electrode capacitance and waveguide  within the transceiver box before reaching the probe tip. Those  reflections show up as “ringing” highlighted in the red box of  Fig. 5. When collecting reflected waveform from the failed  pin, this ringing will superimpose onto the return signal and  add unwanted peaks which sometimes can lead to confusion  during data interpretation. The ringing could be minimized by  optimizing the design and manufacture of the transceiver  circuitry.


Correct ground pin selection


Similar with the conventional TDR technique, the ground  contact must be provided when using EOTPR to have a good  signal return path. Correct ground pin selection is critical to  obtain meaningful EOTPR data. Wrong ground pin selection  could lead to wrong FI results. The EOTPR spectra shown in  Fig. 6 taken from a failed structure in another package  illustrate that for the same failed pin, different ground pin  selection could lead to opposite FI results. Spectra in Fig.6 (a) obtained using a good ground pin indicates that the failure is


Phase 2: Bit Voltage stays at the corresponding Vdd and WL  Voltages (1.4V) while BitBar Voltage sweeps from  corresponding Vdd and WL Voltages (1.4V) to 0V.  Phase 2  writes logic 1 in the cell as seen by the reversal in Bit and  BitBar currents. If the current flips at Bit Voltage near Vdd or  Vss, the cell has write-stability and write-ability problems  respectively. If the current does not reverse, the cell is unable  to write.


Phase 3: Bit Voltage stays at the corresponding Vdd and WL  Voltages (1.4V) while BitBar Voltage sweeps from 0V to  corresponding Vdd and WL Voltages (1.4V). Phase 3 should  contain logic high during the read operation.


Phase 4: Bit Voltage sweeps from the corresponding Vdd and  WL Voltages (1.4V) to 0V while BitBar Voltage stays at  corresponding Vdd and WL Voltages (1.4V). Phase 4 is  similar to Phase 2 and should write logic 0 into the cell.

Figure 3: SRAM bitcell stability tests at different power supply  voltages conducted at M1 level. (a) Vdd and WL at 1.4V, (b) Vdd and WL at 1.2V, (c) Vdd and WL at 1.0V and (d) Vdd  and WL at 0.8V. (e) Change in maximum Bit current at Vdd  and WL of 1.4V, 1.2V, 1.0V and 0.8V at each hour interval. Bit current and Bitbar currents obtained over 4 hours of  e-beam exposure (500eV) showed that the response is  repeatable at each hour interval, indicating no degradation.


(ii)


Static Noise Margin Test


In a standard 6 transistor SRAM cell (shown earlier in Figure  1) the core of the cell is formed by two CMOS inverters, where the output potential of each inverter is fed as input into  the other. This feedback loop stabilizes the inverters to their  respective state.


The access transistors and the word and bit lines, WL and BL, are used to read and write from or to the cell. In standby mode  the word line is low, turning the access transistors off. In this  state the inverters are in complementary state when the  p-channel of inverter one and n-channel of inverter two are on.


To write information the data is imposed on the Bitline and the  inverse data on the BitlineBar, the access transistors are turned  on by setting the word line to high. As the driver of the bit


lines is much stronger it can assert the inverter transistors. As  soon as the information is stored in the inverters, the access  transistors can be turned off and the information in the inverter  is preserved.   For reading the word line is turned on to  activate the access transistors while the information is sensed  at the bit lines.


A key figure of merit for an SRAM cell is its SNM. It can be  extracted by nesting the largest possible square in the two  Voltage Transfer Curves (VTC) of the involved CMOS  inverters in Figure 4. The SNM is defined as the side-length of  the square, given in volts. When an external DC noise is larger  than the SNM, the state of the SRAM cell can change and data  is lost.


In the SNM test, Vdd and bit line (BL) Voltages of each  inverter are varied from 1.4V to 0.4V, in steps of 0.2V. Voltage data at both “Read” and “Hold” conditions (when WL  is biased at Vdd for “Read” and 0V for “Hold”) are then  collected.  CH voltage is then swept from 0V to the respective  Vdd voltage and CL voltage is then collected.  The butterfly  curves are shown in Figure 4. The butterfly curves were  obtained by inverting the voltage transfer curve (VTC) of  inverter 1 and plotting it together with that of inverter 2. SNM  is given by the length of the side of a square between the two  VTCs.

in Si, while spectra in Fig.6 (b) using a wrong ground path  suggest that the failure is in the substrate.


Power or ground planes in packages are ideal for the return  path. However in package TVs, these planes were not  designed.  In this case, the failed net and all the nets around it  need to be studied in the package design file in order to find a  good return path. The selected net should be longer than the  failed net and most of its part is next to and parallel routed  with the failed net.


Figure 5: incident electrical pulse in EOTPR


Figure 6(a),(b): EOTPR spectra using a good ground pin, and  a wrong ground pin, respectively.


Additional reference spectra


After a good ground pin was selected, the EOTPR spectra  from the failed unit and a golden/passing unit were obtained  and were shown in Fig. 7. The reflected signal from the failed  unit is the same as the golden one until it reaches the failure  location highlighted with a red arrow in Fig.7. A positive peak  showed up at that location indicating an open or high  resistance failure. The physical location of the failure in the  package cannot be determined without the spectra from  additional reference units.


EOTPR spectrum was obtained from the same package pins of  a bare SIP substrate (without FPGA package) to identify the  location of the MLI in EOTPR spectra. The green waveform


in Fig. 8 is from the bare SIP substrate. As there is no MLI  connection to FPGA package for a bare substrate, a big  positive peak showing open appears at the location of the  MLI, which is indicated by a green arrow in Fig. 8. For units  having MLI connection, a capacitive dip showing up as a  negative peak is found around the MLI location. By  comparing the failure location to the MLI location in Fig. 8, it  can be determined that the open failure is in the FPGA  package, not in the SIP substrate.


Figure 7: EOTPR spectra from the failed unit and the golden  unit.


Figure 8: EOTPR spectra from the failed unit, the golden unit, and a bare SIP substrate.


To further isolate the failure, EOTPR spectrum from a  reference unit without the FPGA die is needed to determine  the location of the FLI (inside the FPGA package) in EOTPR  spectra. After the FPGA die was lapped off from another  golden/passing SIP package, EOTPR waveform was obtained  from the same packages pins and was shown in Fig. 9 in blue. As the FPGA die was removed in the additional reference unit, there is a positive peak showing open at the FLI. Interestingly, there is no capacitive dip in the golden unit around the FLI, probably due to the overlapping of the two capacitive dips at  both the MLI and the FLI. Thus the additional reference


spectrum is critical to identify the FLI location in EOTPR  spectra. In the zoom-in image of Fig. 9, it looks like the open  failure is right before the FLI joint and is in the FPGA  substrate.


Identify the exact physical failure location in the package


The raw data of EOTPR waveform is time-based data. To get  the exact failure location using EOTPR, the time-based data  needs to be converted into distance data by calculating the  phase velocity, νp, of the electromagnetic wave. It can be  expressed as [4]:


Where, νp, is the phase velocity, C is velocity of light in free  space, and εeff is the effective dielectric constant of the  package. εeff is different for different packages and can be  estimated using known package structure locations in EOTPR  waveform.


Figure 9: EOTPR spectra from the failed unit, the golden unit, a bare SIP substrate, and a SIP package without FPGA die.


Figure 10: The failure location can be estimated using known  package structure locations in EOTPR spectra.


The distance between the MLI and the FLI can be calculated  using the package design file. As shown in Fig. 10, the εeff can  be estimated based on the MLI and FLI locations in the  EOTPR spectra and the actual distance between them, which  is around 1.6 mm. Using the estimated εeff, the distance


between the failure location and the FLI can be estimated to be  about 100 μm.


2D X-ray image


2D X-ray imaging was performed around the failure location  to see if the open failure can be detected, the result is shown in  Fig.11. As 2D X-ray can only provide one projective image of  the sample from top to the bottom, the much stronger X-ray  contrast from SLI, MLI and FLI solder joints in the SIP  package shadowed the relatively weaker contrast from the top  substrate layer inside the FPGA package. The top layer traces  inside the FPGA package cannot be imaged without physically  remove the SLI solder joints, SIP substrate, MLI solder joints, and most part of the FPGA substrate.


Figure 11: 2D X-ray image around the failure location


3D X-ray CT


The 3D X-ray CT images were non-destructively obtained  using the x-ray CT microscope, model MicroXCT-200  manufactured by Xradia. One of the key components of this  system is an x-ray detector with proprietary x-ray optics and  optical design that provides significant reduction of the  dependence that magnification has with respect to the sourceto-sample distance [3]. The design achieves resolutions in the  range of 1.5 to 2 microns without the need of placing the  sample close to the source, thus allowing full sample rotation  required for 3D x-ray imaging without sample-source  collision. This is an important capability because it allowed  scanning intact SIP samples without the need of trimming  them, regardless of sample size. The energy level used was  150kV to allow effective x-ray penetration across solder joints  that were in the field of view of the isolated defect locations. Figure 12 shows the 3D view, virtual X-sectional view and  virtual planar view of the 3D X-ray CT images, where a trace  crack is identified in top FPGA substrate layer very close to  the FLI connecting to the failed pin. The distance between the  trace crack and the center of the FLI is about 100 μm, very  close to the failure location predicted by the EOTPR.


The case study presented above demonstrate that EOTPR and  3D X-ray can be used to non- destructively identify and image


failures in a complicated 3D package. Other than the trace  crack in the top layer of the FPGA package, failures like MLI  or FLI solder joint crack, MLI or FLI solder non wet, trace  cracks inside the SIP substrate, were also successfully  analyzed using this technique. The application of EOTPR and  3D X-ray CT on other failed structures can be found in  reference 2 and 3.


Figure 12: (a) 3D view, (b) virtual X-sectional view, and (c) virtual planar view of the 3D X-ray CT showing a top FPGA  substrate layer trace crack close to the FLI.


A combination of advanced non-destructive methods such as  EOTPR and 3D X-ray has been shown to be very effective in  failure analysis of complex defects in SIP.

[2] Cai, Y., Wang, Z., Dias, R. C., and Goyal, D., “Electro  Optical Terahertz Pulse Reflectometry- an Innovative  Fault Isolation Tool”, Proc 2010 Electronic Components  and Technology Conference, pp. 1309-1315.


[3] Pacheco, M., and Goyal, D., “Detection and  Characterization of Defects in Microelectronic Packages  and Boards by Means of High-Resolution X-Ray  Computed Tomography (CT), Proc 2011 Electronic  Components and Technology Conference, pp. 1263-1268.
