Failure analysis using scanning acoustic microscopy for diagnostics of  electronic devices and 3D system integration technologies


Peter Czurratis, Peter Hoffrogge


PVA Tepla Analytical Systems GmbH, Deutschordenstrasse 38, 73463 Westhausen, Germany


Sebastian Brand, Frank Altmann, Matthias Petzold


Faunhofer Institute for Mechanics of Materials IWM, Walter-Hülse-Strasse 1, 06120 Halle (Saale), Germany


New semiconductor chip technologies and technologies for 3D  integration require information’s of packaging and interface  defects in 3 dimensions, that means the lateral dimension of  the defect and the location inside the device or package must  be defined. In this paper, new methodical approaches for non  destructive failure analysis on 3D integrated TSV samples are  introduced. The concepts combine improved scanning acoustic  microscopy (SAM) imaging hardware with unique software  solutions for defect identification and quantitative analysis of  mechanical properties using scanning acoustic investigations. In case of MEMS 3D integration, e.g. based on direct bonding, related interface defects must be investigated by SAM. With  respect to 3D integration applications, the potential of recent  SAM improvements applying specifically adapted hardware  and custom-made signal processing algorithms will be  discussed. Examples of SAM-based failure detection  techniques for the application in 3D integration are  demonstrated. New technologies are shown to improve the  through put of fully wafer scanning using scanning acoustic  microscopy. To improve the defect resolution, a new  transducer design was developed to increase defect resolution  and signal to noise for interface characterisation.


3 D integration technology for bonded wafers and integration  technologies for flip chips combined with solder bump  interconnections are applied widely in electronic device  manufacturing. For ensuring the connective reliability, appropriate failure analysis methods for non-destructively  inspecting the electrical contacts are needed. Due to power  dissipation thermally induced mechanical stress is a major  issue for the reliability of bonded and soldered interfaces [2],


[4], [5]. Several techniques for inspecting interconnections in  electronic circuits including optical systems, X-ray  microscopy, scanning electron microscopy, strength testing  and microsection analysis are available. Scanning acoustic  microscopy however, is of increasing interest due to its unique  ability to illuminate optically opaque materials, providing  depth-specific information and to locally evaluate mechanical


properties. Moreover, acoustic microscopy is working nondestructively, which allows the application of several test  procedures and cycles at a single device [1].


The goal of the current work was the development of an  automated working application for a non-destructive  estimation of quality related parameters of flip-chip  interconnections in electronic devices. Time resolved scanning  acoustic microcopy in the frequency range between 110 – 230  MHz was applied. From recorded unprocessed ultrasound rfsignals appropriate signal parameters were derived and related  to the contacts condition. Connective defects were detected  automatically using specific parameter ranges of the  backscatter amplitude integral (BAI) [3], pulse separation  characteristics and the distribution of the wavelet coefficients, estimated from the rf-signals.


1. Material and Methods


1.1 Scanning Acoustic Microscopy


Scanning acoustic microscopy is a powerful tool for nondestructively deriving depth-specific information of optically  non-transparent samples combined with a lateral resolution in  the µm-range. Employed was a fast scanning acoustic  microscope (SAM 300, PVA TePla Analytical Systems GmbH  and AUTO WAFER 300, Westhausen, Germany) equipped  with transducers in the range of 110- 230 MHz frequency  range. The focal length of the transducer was 3,2- 8 mm, required for placing the focus inside bonded interfaces of  wafers and the chip at the interface die/flip-chip-connectors. Scanning was performed with an increment of 4 µm in the  lateral x- and y-directions, while the transducer tip and the  sample were immersed in distilled water at room temperature. In acoustic inspection a coupling medium between the  transducer and the specimen is always required for impedance  matching and thus, for coupling the acoustic waves into the  material under investigation. Echoes were digitized at a  sampling rate of 500-1000 MS/s with a resolution of 8 bit and  then stored on the microscopes internal hard drive. Data files  containing unprocessed rf-signals were transferred to a desktop  computer and off-line analysis was performed.


Samples inspected were bonded Si wafers and CPU-devices  manufactured in flip-chip technology containing solder bumps


(80 µm – lateral diameter) for connecting the silicon chip to  the die-attach of the CPU. The silicon layer had a thickness of  approx. 750 µm with a surface area of 20 mm x 30 mm.


1.2 Signal Analysis for automated bump detection


Signal analysis of the rf-echoes was performed using  custommade MATLAB (The Mathworks, Natick, USA) software. The lateral locations of the bumps were estimated by  differentiating between the interfaces silicon/connector and  silicon/ underfill using characteristic reflective features of  these interfaces as illustrated in Fig. 1. Figure 1-right shows  the Hilbert transformed acoustic cross section of the sample. Echoes occurring at the silicon/connector interface were  considerably longer than echoes from the silicon/underfill  interface. Parameter values of the backscatter amplitude  integral (BAI) were computed within the time range between  the exit echo of the silicon and the signal peak at the bumpconnector/die-attach interface as indicated by the red rectangle  in Fig. 1. Further analysis was applied to signals of the bumpinterconnections exclusively for characterization of the  connector condition. These processing steps included wavelet  analysis employing a Gaussian mother wavelet of the 4th order  for deriving wavelet coefficients versus scaling factors and  time. Wavelet analysis is a 2-D redundant signal  transformation which overcomes the main weaknesses of the  short-time-Fourier-transformation (STFT). The application of  a static window length as used in STFT results in a constant  time and frequency resolution of the transformed signal. In  wavelet analysis, the analyzing wavelet is expanded and  compressed in addition to sliding over the signal analyzed. This adaptation results in an improved temporal and spectral  resolution, which cannot be obtained by a Fourier  transformation [6]. During scaling of the analyzing wavelet, the number of oscillations of the wavelet remains constant, resulting in a variation of the spectral content. Wavelet  analysis of a signal computes coefficients at different scaling  factors describing the correspondence between the analyzing  wavelet and the signal. For illustration, a wavelet coefficient  corresponds to the area below the result of the multiplication  of the analysis wavelet and the signal at a particular wavelet  scaling. Although the STFT provides a complex coefficient for  one parameter (frequency), the continuous wavelet transform (CWT) computes one coefficient for each combination of the  scaling factor and the temporal position. The wavelet  transformation performed in the current study used the  MATLAB functions for the CWT. A Gaussian wavelet of the  fourth order was chosen because of its similarity to the  ultrasonic pulses used in the experiments. Wavelet analysis  was applied to the signals obtained at positions which were  detected to be assessed at a flip-chip connector. The  distribution (mean and standard deviation) of the wavelet  coefficients were computed versus scaling factors and as a  function of the temporal position.


In addition to wavelet coefficients the backscatter amplitude  integral (BAI), the relative peak positions, the peak amplitudes  and the corresponding pulse widths at the silicon-bump


interface were computed. For estimating the pulse width, the  peak position and the peak amplitudes time signals were first  transformed into the Hilbert domain and the pulse width was  obtained in ns at -6 dB of the peak amplitude. The BAI can be  computed according to [3] as the integral of the Hilbert  transformed absolute values of the time signal prior to low  pass filtering. Due to integrating over time multiple echoes can  be included in the BAI computation resulting in an improved  contrast.


Finally, the parameters of the peak position, the BAI and the  wavelet distribution were scaled, thresholded and combined in  a classifier for deriving a binary void-intact decision of the  flip-chip connectors.


To provide an easy-to-handle visual indication the resulting  decision matrix was color coded and superimposed to the BAIparametric image.


Fig. 1: Sample description. The scheme on the left illustrates  the 750 µm silicon layer with the flip-chip contacts


underneath. On the right a cross-section image through the  CPU-device is shown. Boundaries and corresponding echoes  are indicated by red lines. The red rectangle shows the time  range for estimating BAI values.


1.2.1 Complementary Defect Assessment


During the method development locations of defective bump  contacts were estimated using X-ray microscopy (XD7600, Dage). From these known void and known intact positions  ultrasonic data were acquired and signal parameters were  computed for comparison to parameter values obtained at  known intact connectors. Characteristic parameter ranges of  the peak position, pulse length, BAI and the distributions of  the wavelet coefficients versus time and temporal positions  were estimated from the acoustic data and implemented in the  analysis algorithm. Thresholds for the classification were  defined for each of the mentioned parameters using half of the  Euclidic distance between the mean values of the two  categories.


1.2.2 Verification by SEM and X-Ray Microscopy


For verification of the methods accuracy, the success rate of  the acoustic defect estimation was evaluated using “unknown” samples. Void positions were estimated from the ultrasonic  data and the binary decision matrix was computed. The results


flip-chip contacts


silicon


flip-chip  connector


were handed to an experienced technician for verifying the  defects using X-ray microscopy. Finally, the two rows  containing the highest number of defective contacts were  cross-sectioned, polished and connectors were imaged using  scanning electron microscopy (TM1000, Hitachi).


1.3 Delamination analysis in stacked die devices


For axially localizing delaminated spots in stacked devices an  approach of analyzing differences in acoustic reflection signals  was pursued. Silicon dies commonly integrated in stacked die  devices are extremely thin showing a time of flight much  shorter than the duration of the applied acoustic excitation  pulse. This issue was addressed by using a numerical  deconvolution technique described elsewhere (Hornarvar et al. 2001). According to linear system theory the transfer function  of a linear time independent system can be computed by  deconvolving the input signal x(t) from the output signal y(t). In practice a reference pulse recorded from a plain reflector  can be used for deconvolution and will provide the systems  acoustic transfer function which is characterized by the  interfaces of the inspected device. Prior to analyzing the  differences between the signals recorded from an unknown and  a reference sample acoustic echo signals were subjected to  either a Hilbert transform or a numerical deconvolution  algorithm. Resulting signals were then subtracted and  differences caused by the variation in the acoustic properties (due to a delamination or a void) of the samples were used for  visualization. Evidence for a delamination is obtained if the  acoustic reflection signal at an interface shows a higher  amplitude in the unknown inspection sample compared to a  known reference. To increase the reliability the reflectivity of  the following interface was also analyzed. If the reflection  amplitude in the following interface shows a higher value than  in the reference sample the delamination has been localized  axially.  In the current case-study a set-up containing a stack of  4 dies has been used for investigating the applicability of the  described Method. A schematic of the sample is provided in  figure 2.


Figure 2: Schematic of the sample structure with expected


signals reflected at the individual interfaces. A change in


signal due to a delamination is shown on the left vs. the signal  on the right.


2. Results and Discussion


2.1 Acoustic Analysis: connector localization


According to the acoustic cross-selection in the image in Fig. 1  interconnect positions were located using the pulse length of  the echo obtained at the silicon/connector interface. In  presence of a flip-chip contact the peak amplitude of the echo  at the silicon-connector interface was 200% higher compared  to the signals measured at the interface silicon/underfill. The  pulse width obtained from a connector was 30 ns while the  echo at the silicon/underfill interface was only 10 ns long. Figure 3 shows two Hilbert transformed signals recorded at the  location of a flip-chip connector (black) and in between two  connectors (silicon/underfill). The differences in the peak  amplitudes and pulse widths are evident from the signals  plotted in Fig. 3.


2.1.1 Acoustic Analysis: defect classification


Wavelet analysis was applied to the echo signals recorded at  the silicon/interconnect interface. Significant differences in the  distributions of the wavelet coefficients were observed  between intact and defect flip-chip connectors.


Fig. 3: Hilbert transformed and low pass filtered signals. The  black and red curves were computed from signals recorded in  presence and absence of a connector, respectively.


Wavelet coefficients estimated from a void showed a broader  distribution versus scaling factors and wavelet positions  compared to values obtained from an intact contact. In case of  a void wavelet coefficients of equal amplitude occur with a  much broader distribution over all wavelet positions. The  average distribution width over the scaling factors (sf) was


1.7sf for intact connectors and 2.5sf for the voids. Figure 4  contains the results obtained by wavelet analysis. The wavelet  coefficients computed from an intact flip-chip connector are  plotted in the image in Fig. 4, while the right-hand-side image  illustrates the coefficients obtained from a defective connector.


at fc-contact


between fc-contacts


Fig. 4: Wavelet coefficients estimated at the silicon/connector  interfaces of an intact (left) and at a defect connector (right).


Distributions were estimated at 70% of the maximum values. The second parameter included in the classification was the  relative position of the signal peak at the silicon/connector  interface. Pulse shifts of up to 20 ns between intact and defect  contacts were observed. Both parameters were combined in a  classifier for increasing the success rate and to optimize for  false- positive classifications. Figure 5 shows the results  estimated by the classifier. Green indicated spots in Fig. 5 -left  specify positions of intact bump-connectors. Voids and falsepositive contact positions are marked red. The settings and  parameter ranges applied for computing the results in Fig. 5-left were optimized for classification of the flip chipconnectors at the outer rim of the device. The application of  adapted settings will be required in order to classify the inner  interconnects.


The graph in Fig. 5 illustrates the parameter values at  connector locations. Parameter values were thresholded, scaled  to 255 (8bit) and plotted color specific in this graph. At  locations where all parameters reached the threshold defined  for an intact connector the pixel in the image is white, since  the values of each color channel is 255. In locations were an  intact connector has not been identified (not all parameters  reached the level of 255), the pixel values deviate from white. Green indicated are all pixels that have not been identified as a  flip-chip connector.

2.1.2 Verification by SEM and X-Ray microscopy


For validation of the defect classification based on the acoustic  microscopy data both, scanning electron and X-ray microscopy  were applied to the same CPU device. The decision map (Fig. 5-left) generated by the analysis algorithm developed here was  given to a technical assistant experienced in void detection  using the X-ray microscope. In Fig. 5-middle all voids  detected based on the acoustic microscopy data are indicated  by a yellow mark. Red indication in Fig. 5-middle corresponds  to voids that could be confirmed by X-ray microscopy. It can  be seen that 8 out of 31 voids estimated from the acoustic data  could not be confirmed by X-ray, although they have been  pointed out explicitly by the acoustically based decision map. This leads to the assumption of 25.8% false-positive  classification. However, in addition to X-ray microscopy  scanning electron microscopy was applied to the two rows of  connectors with the largest number of voids detected  acoustically. For acquiring electron micrographs the CPU  device was sequentially polished to the middle of each  connector row and then further prepared for SEM-imaging. Voids revealed by SEM-imaging are indicated by a white  circle in Fig. 5-middle. It can be seen that thirteen voids  detected by analyzing the ultrasonic echo signals could be  verified by SEM imaging. However, it also shows that two  false-positive and one false-negative detections occurred, which corresponds to an overall success rate of 90.3% including both kinds of false detection. Figure 6 contains  electron micrographs of the connectors that could not be  verified by X-ray microscopy, but were revealed when  analyzing ultrasonic signals obtained by scanning acoustic  microscopy. It can be seen that all of the flip-chip-connectors  are disconnected from the substrate. The numbering in Fig.4-middle corresponds to the numbering in Fig. 6 (top-right  corner of each micrograph). The electron micrograph in Fig.-.6-1 reveals that this particular connector is only partially  disconnected and also shows a noticeable thinner gap between  the connector and its socket. This kind of defect will show an  electrical connection during electronic testing. However, in a  practical application this connector will definitely fail due to

thermal processes caused by the applied electrical current. It  was somewhat surprising to see that this kind of void could be  detected from the ultrasonic signals. The micrographs 1 – 6  displayed in Fig. 6 clearly show the connectors to be defective.

graph contains the signals that are stronger in the reference. The C-scan image shows the location of the B-scans (redmarker).


In the next step two dimensional maps shown in Fig. 10 were  computed from the amplitudes of the numerically  deconvoluted acoustic signals at the expected arrival times. From the signum of the differences of those signals the images  were separated into a “Positive” (where the amplitude in the  inspection sample was higher compared to the references  sample) and a “Negative” (vice versa) image. From those  results it can be concluded that a delamination appeared in the  second interface at the location between the mold compound  and the die.


Figure 10: C-scan images recorded at the expected arrival  times from the first and second interface from the numerically  deconvoluted acoustic reflection signals. The graphs named  positive show the signals that were stronger in the inspection  sample while negative corresponds to signals that have higher  amplitude in the reference sample. If the reflectivity at an  interface is higher in the inspection sample and the reflectivity  is higher at the following interface in the reference a  delamination is proven.


The acoustically based method developed here showed good  performance in classifying flip-chip contacts in a fast, cost  effective and automated way. It seemed superior to  complementary X-ray inspection by a trained person. Further  experiments will be performed in the near future for  optimizing the methods reliability.


Using an extended signal processing algorithm the acoustic  transfer function can be computed by deconvoluting the  excitation pulse from the echo signals recorded from a stacked  die device. By analyzing the differences between a known  reference sample and an unknown inspection sample a  delamination was located and assigns to its axial position  between mold compound and die.


The authors also thank Natalie Yeo for technical assistance  with the defect evaluation by X-ray microscopy and the SEM  imaging.

Analysis of an Anomalous Transistor Exhibiting Dual-Vt Characteristics and Its  Cause in a 90nm Node CMOS Technology


Yuk L. Tsang1*, Xiang D. Wang1, Reyhan Ricklefs2, Jason Goertz1


1Freescale Semiconductor Inc., Austin, TX


* ra0149@freescale.com


2Now with Intel, TX


In this paper, we report a transistor model that has successfully  led to the identification of a non visual defect. This model was  based on detailed electrical characterization of a MOS NFET  exhibiting a threshold voltage (Vt) of just about 40mv lower  than normal. This small Vt delta was based on standard  graphical extrapolation method in the usual linear Id-Vg plots. We observed, using a semilog plot, two slopes in the Id-Vg  curves with Vt delta magnified significantly in the  subthreshold region. The two slopes were attributed to two  transistors in parallel with different Vts. We further found that  one of the parallel transistors had short channel effect due to a  punch-through mechanism.   It was proposed and ultimately  confirmed the cause was due to a dopant defect using scanning  capacitance microscopy (SCM) technique.


With ever shrinking device dimensions of CMOS integrated  circuit technology, the defect critical size causing circuit  failure becomes even smaller. This size reduction trend and


process complexity had made failure analysis extremely  difficult.  Dopant related failures, in particular, in a single  device remains a challenging non-visual defect (NVD) for  even ultra high resolution microscopy techniques such as  SCM [1, 2], SMM [3], TEM holography [4, 5], and SSRM [6,


7]. Complicated sample preparation and imaging optimization  are some of the obstacles, although a handful of such  successful analyses have been reported on both single  crystalline silicon and poly silicon [8, 9, 10, 11]. Once a  precise fault isolation is achieved, a good knowledge of what


to investigate next before deploying any of these advanced  techniques is essential.  To meet this requirement and to  increase the success rate of any physical analysis, particularly  the subtle NVD types, a detailed electrical analysis to pinpoint  exactly where and what to look for in the sample is critical. As


a result of an accurate model based on this electrical analysis, and with its guidance, appropriate methods can be effectively  utilized to find the defect. This quick turnaround feedback is  important for wafer fabrication lines to make corrective  actions to address critical yield issues. In this work, we discuss  the detailed electrical nanoprobing analysis that led to an  accurate physical model. This model had guided the selection  of dopant mapping technique to bring out the NVD.


Experiments


(1) Fail site isolation


The unit being analyzed had a tri-state leakage failure of 2uA  on the failing I/O pin.  Optical Beam Induced Resistance  Change (OBIRCH) analysis was used to identify a specific  site within the failing pin circuitry (Figure 1). The OBIRCH  site was in the NFET buffer transistors (Figure 2).


Figure 1  OBIRCH site in the NFET buffer indicated by the  red dot


OBIRCH site


Figure 2  CAD layout showing OBIRCH site in the transistor arrays (a), and transistor finger IDs’ (b). The drain in Finger#4


exhibited the most leakage as measured by atomic force probing.  Legend: D=Drain, and S=Source


(2) Electrical characterization and model


The finger transistors were then characterized using atomic


force probing (AFP) and compared. Finger transistor #4 was


found to exhibit higher channel conduction as shown in the


Id-Vg plot in Figures 3 and 4, where Id is the drain current


and Vg the gate bias. This leaky transistor lined up with the


OBIRCH site. The linear extrapolated Vt for the leaky


transistor #4 was about 40mv lower than the neighboring


finger transistors (Figure 3). The Id-Vg curve for transistor


#4 did not indicate obvious anomalous characteristics other


than a small Vt shift. However, the subthreshold


characteristics in the semi-log plot (Figure 4) for the same IdVg curves revealed an abnormal curve shape in transistor #4


as compared to the reference transistor. At 10nA, the Vt was


significantly lower by about 230mv in transistor #4. The IdVg curve shape appeared to suggest there were two


transistors in parallel resulting in two slopes. One of the  transistors had the normal Vt while the other exhibited the  lower Vt as shown in the insert in Figure 4.


To assess this model proposal, two transistors with


significantly different Vts known to be due to design W/L  geometry effects were measured by AFP.  These two sets of  Id-Vg curves were then added together and the sum was  plotted and compared with the two individual curves (Figure  5).  The shape of the resultant curve resembles that of the


leaky finger transistor #4, indicating the parallel transistor  model is highly plausible to account for the leakier transistor #4 characteristics.


Figure 3  Transistor Id-Vg linear curve plots for leakier transistor #4 and neighboring reference normal transistor. Transistor #4 Vt


was about 40mv lower by linear extrapolation method.

The  semi-log plot revealed a larger subthreshold Vt delta of about 230mv at 10 nA and a curve shape with two slopes Sa and Sb. Slope Sc is that of the reference transistor. Gate 3 and Gate 4 represent finger transistors #3 and #4 respectively. The model  schematic for transistor #4 is shown in the insert.

The formation or cause of the two-sloped transistor was next  investigated in more detail. The drain current was evaluated in  transistor #4 and a reference transistor by varying the drain


bias (Vd) at two different gate bias (Vg) with the substrate and  source at 0v. It was found that in the off state at Vg=0v, the  drain current in the finger transistor #4 increases according to  a power law governed by Id~Vdn, while that in the reference  transistor showed normal insignificant current in the pA range


(Figure 6a). Curve fitting for transistor #4 had extracted a  power index n~2.82 in line with space charge limited current [12, 13]. In the on state at Vg=0.5v, the failing transistor drain  current increased at a much faster rate in the saturation region (Figure 6b).


Another mechanism appeared to exist in the leakier transistor #4.  It is proposed that there were two mechanisms occurring  in transistor #4 simultaneously. One mechanism is that of the


normal transistor component in parallel with slope Sc.  The  second mechanism is the punch-through conduction due to  space charge transport [12] when the Source and Drain  junction depletion regions merged as in a short channel  transistor.  The surface diffusion current component due to


surface band-bending and DIBL effects will also play a  significant role in the short channel transistor. The punchthrough phenomenon has been extensively investigated and  reported [13, 14]. This abnormal short channel transistor with  subthreshold slope Sa is the other transistor component in


parallel with the first component having slope Sc. Slope Sb is  the transition slope as a result of combining the short channel  transistor and the normal transistor. In this case, the  predominant component is the short channel transistor as  indicated by the pronounced increase in channel conduction


(Figure 6). There are numerous possible causes for the short  channel transistor component, such as poly linewidth, dopant  distribution, etc.. The search for the root cause is discussed in  the next section.


Figure 6  Id-Vd curve plot at Vg=0v (a) and Vg=0.5v (b). The normal transistor exhibited insignificant conduction  while the failing transistor conduction increases with  increasing Drain bias in the off state. In the on state the  failing transistor conduction increases at a much faster rate. In all cases, the substrate and source were at 0v.


(3) Physical verification


A Scanning Capacitance Microscopy (SCM) dopant mapping  technique was attempted. Planar view SCM imaging after  unlayering the sample to bare silicon was performed. Topography image showed normal structure appearance  across the transistor (Figure 7a). The poly patterning defect, but not the poly grain structure, was therefore excluded as the  cause.  However, in the dC/dV dopant mapping image, a dark  contrast anomaly showed up in the channel region of finger  transistor #4 (Figure 7b). The contrast was found to be


sample voltage bias dependent suggesting that it was a  dopant related defect and not a physical defect. The contrast  indicated the p-type channel dopant under the gate oxide was  partially missing resulting in a significantly reduced pchannel region. The very small remaining p-region will result  in a transistor with severe short channel effect causing a  punch-through mechanism consistent with the electrical  behavior observed in section (2).

Figure 7  The topographic image (a) is processed to bring out the contrast of the channel region, which does not show abnormal  topographic features at the abnormal SCM site (b). The dopant image contrast indicated a missing p-type dopant. In the dopant  mapping image, bright contrast represents p-type and dark contrast n-type.


Process fix


Two approaches were attempted and results proved to be  effective in solving the failure mechanism. One approach was


to reduce the poly implant energy to minimize the dopant  channeling penetration. While this approach clearly verified a  dopant penetration into the channel issue had occurred, it was  unable to solve a coexisting problem with large poly grain  that had caused a Vt issue in PFET [15]. Another approach


that had prevented both dopant penetration and poly grain  issues was a low temperature and short time Dt poly process. This lower thermal budget had resulted in more uniformly  distributed poly grains minimizing implantation channeling  and Boron depletion in the PFET poly [15]. This low Dt


approach was successfully implemented in the wafer  fabrication line.


Conclusion


A MOSFET exhibiting a two slope characteristic in the Id-Vg  plot was successfully modeled as a transistor formed by two


transistors with two different Vts in parallel. One of the two  transistors was attributed to possess a low Vt while the other  a normal transistor.  Id-Vd transistor characterization results  had suggested the existence of a punch-through mechanism


due to short channel effect in the low Vt transistor. Hypothesis of channel dopant defect as one of the two most  likely causes based on the electrical characterization data was  verified by SCM dopant mapping technique that had clearly  revealed the P-type channel dopant was partially missing.


This missing dopant defect had resulted in an abnormal  transistor with a very short channel. A follow-up problem fix  using a low Dt poly process was implemented.

Study of Static Noise Margin, Cell Stability and Influence of Electron Beam on  sub-30nm SRAM Using SEM-based Nanoprobing with 8 Nanoprobes


T.H. Ng, M.K. Dawood, P.K. Tan, H. Tan, C.K. Oh, J. C. Lam and Z.H. Mai


GLOBALFOUNDIRES Singapore Pte. Ltd. 60 Woodlands Industrial Park D Street 2  Singapore 738406


E-mail: tsuhau.ng@globalfoundries.com


SEM-based nanoprobing has proven vital in identifying nonvisual failures through electrical characterization in current FA  metrology for fault identification.[1,2] A majority of the work  reported with SEM-based nanoprobing utilize 3 to 4 probes to  obtain standard transistor family curves.[3] With 8 probes used  concurrently, the system could have the ability to obtain other  important information such as cell stability as well as the Static  Noise Margin (SNM).[4] The SNM is an important metric in  determining the stability of 6T SRAM designs to noise and  their dependency on other factors such as supply voltage. The  use of 8 probes allows a direct method to study the cell  function during its actual operation. However, device  performance is known to be affected by e-beam induced  artifacts in an SEM, especially at high kV which can  complicate the analysis of the electrical measurements.  The  suitability of such measurements in a SEM-based environment  has not been fully studied. In addition, ultra low-k (ULK) dielectric is known to degrade upon exposure to electron beam  at high kV.  In this work, the cell stability and SNM at  different biasing conditions at low electron beam energy (500eV) of a sub-30 nm technology node SRAM device have  been characterized and it has been shown that no significant  degradation has been introduced during the data acquisition  and imaging processes in the SEM.


Experimental Setup:


Two  6T SRAM samples were prepared by delayering them to  Metal 1 and Metal 2 levels.  Cell stability, SNM (butterfly  curves) and current leakage measurements were then carried  out on these samples using up to 8 nanoprobes in a DCG  nProber system. To minimize e-beam induced damages, the  investigation was carried out at a low acceleration voltage of  500eV while still maintaining good Passive Voltage Contrast (PVC). Bit cell stability, static noise margin test as well as  leakage study between 2 adjacent floating wordines (WL) were  then performed on the SRAM samples.


(i)


Bit Cell Stability Test


In the bit cell stability study, the Vdd and WL voltages are  varied from 1.4V to 0.2V, in steps of 0.2V. The Bit and BitBar


currents are collected with the Bit and BitBar voltages  programmed in the following 4 phases:


Phase 1: Bit Voltage sweeps from 0V to the corresponding  Vdd and WL voltages (1.4V) while BitBar Voltages stays at  the corresponding Vdd and WL Voltage (1.4V). Phase 1  initializes the cell to logic 0 state. If the cell flips during this  phase, (reversal in bit and bit bar currents) the cell is unstable.

0.416V

(iii) Leakage between 2 adjacent floating WL at Metal  1


In the study of leakage current between 2 adjacent floating  word lines (WL) at Metal 1, WL2 voltage is swept from -1.4V  to 1.4V and WL1 is biased at 0V. The device is subjected to  exposure under electron beam for 4 hours and the curves are  taken at each hour.  As seen from the characteristic curves in  Figure 5, no additional leakage is introduced into the 2  adjacent WLs after exposure to the e-beam.

In this work, SEM-based nanoprobing has been shown to be a  viable method to characterize cell stability, static noise margin  and leakage for sub-30nm device.  Good resolution imaging  with PVC can be achieved with low electron voltage (500eV) throughout the nanoprobing process.  No significant change in  leakage current between the adjacent WLs was observed after  the ultra low-k (ULK) samples were exposed to the e-beam for  4 hours.   Bit cell stability and static noise margin at different  power supply voltages have been studied on a sub-30nm  SRAM device.

Leaky Device Channel Anomaly Identification and Case Study by Nano-probing  Technique, Curve Fitting, and Model Analysis


Wei-Chih Wang, Ching-Shan Sung, Ya-Hsiu Lin, Haw-Shan Chen, and Jian-Shing Luo. Physical Failure Analysis Department


Inotera Memories, Inc. Taoyuan, Taiwan, Republic of China


E-mail: waynewang@inotera.com TEL: 886-966-689-728.


This paper demonstrated the use of curve fitting method on  device transfer characteristic curve for device carrier mobility  analysis and failure mechanism verification. In the content, a  systematic device characterization was performed to identify  device failure mode and failure site. Based on physical  observations and electrical results, a device gate oxide boron  penetration failure mechanism and an unexpected subtle p-type  dopant at p-MOS device channel area was conjectured. However, this unexpected p-type dopant was successfully  proved by subsequent carrier mobility analysis results, and the  gate oxide boron penetration failure mechanism was  accordingly verified.


Since technology node and device keep evolving, and scaling, device engineering has faced more and more challenges  regarding to severe short channel effects (SCE). Simultaneously, invisible defect and subtle anomaly  identifications on modern device become more challenging to  failure analysis (FA) experts. However, conventional fault  isolation methodologies are not sensitive enough for these  critical defect identifications [1]. Nevertheless, nano-probing  technique provides a solution.


In reference to prior nano-probing analysis papers, device  contact site invisible defect and minor failure identifications  have been widely studied and reported [2-3]. As to device  channel anomaly, most specialists combine nano-probing  electrical results with device simulation data for invisible  defect identification and process failure mechanism  verification [4-5]. Unfortunately, not every FA lab has device  simulation software. Hence, in this paper, we demonstrate the  use of curve fitting method and model equation analysis on  one yield impact case for device channel invisible defect  identification and failure mechanism verification.


Atomic Force Microscopy (AFM) based nano-probing system  equipped with an Agilent 4156C parameter analyzer was used for  following device characterization.


Case Background


A few lots had low yield issue in our FAB. By failing bit map  inspection, the failing bits distributed to both sides of a sense  amplifier (SA) circuitry, as shown in Figure 1.


Figure 1: Failing bits expended across both sides of a sense  amplifier circuitry.


In addition the fail signatures had strong dependency with the  SA circuit sensing delay. As the delay took longer, the fail  counts increased. According to this dependency, a device  leakage issue at the failing addresses was concluded. With  subsequent physical failure analysis (PFA) along the failing  bits, there was no physical defect finding. Hence nano-probing  technique was performed on the suspected devices for further  device level analysis.


Result and Discussion


Device Failure Mode Identification


Figure 2 shows the leaky p-MOS device that was successfully  identified by device transfer characteristic analysis. The leaky  p-MOS device had a higher device on-state current and a submicroampere off-state leakage current by comparing with its  neighboring reference and a reference p-MOS device on good  wafer.


Figure 2: The ID-VG curve illustrated that the failing p-MOS  device had a higher on-state current and a sub-microampere  off-state leaky current.


In addition, off-state ID-VD curve and current flow analysis  were subsequently performed to verify the device leakage  path. In Figures 3 and 4, the leaky p-MOS device had unusual  high current at low drain voltage region illustrated that the  leaky device channel barrier was abnormal low, and the  unusual high current was purely a source to drain current  through the device surface channel. Furthermore, the device  contact diode curve analysis in Figure 5 specified that there is  no electrical contact site difference between the failing device  and its reference. Hence it was considered that the device  anomaly is located in the channel area. Besides, we analyzed  the leaky p-MOS device‘s electrical behavior under positive  gate voltages condition. As shown in Figure 6, the leakage  current was gradually eliminated with the positive increasing  gate voltage and this electrical behavior led us to believe that  this channel anomaly was not a kind of physical defects, such  as source to drain crystalline defects. It’s an invisible defect. More specifically, it was an unexpected p-type dopant in the  device channel area. Thus, we were going to prove this  conjecture in the following analysis.


Figure 3: The Off-state ID-VD curve illustrated the leaky pMOS device had unusual higher drain current.


Figure 4: The Off-state curve current flow analysis  demonstrated the leaky current was a purely a source to drain  leaky path.


Figure 5: The p-MOS device contact diode I-V curves  illustrated that there is no electrical difference between the  failing and its neighbor reference p-MOS device.


Figure 6: Leaky p-MOS device ID-VG curve analysis at  positive gate voltage region. Note that, as gate voltage  became more positive voltage, the leaky current became  lesser.


Failure Mechanism Model


According to the process history, these yield impacted lots had  same abnormal process records, “longer gate poly deposition  time”, and that means those wafers stayed longer in furnace. And it was believed that those wafers had larger gate poly  grains. TEM dark-field gate poly grain image in Figure 7  proved it. The large gate poly grain extended from top to  bottom of the whole gate poly film. Due to these large gate  poly grains, the device gate oxide boron penetration  phenomenon could easily happen with the following thermal  process, and these related studies could refer to prior  literatures [6-7]. Thus the cause of the unexpected p-type  dopant at device channel could be entirely understood by this  boron penetration failure mechanism, and it was described in  Figure 8. The wafer had large gate poly grains and suffered  serious ion channeling effect and deeper boron doping level at  gate poly boron counter-doping process. During the post  implant thermal process, boron elements diffused along the  gate poly grain boundary, and it penetrated through device  gate oxide then resulted in a discontinuous and subtle p-type  dopant at device channel area. However, this discontinuous  and subtle dopant at device channel was very hard to  distinguish and identify by conventional wet stain skill and  doping profile methodologies; therefore we intended to  demonstrate it by device electrical behavior analysis.


Referred to device physics knowledge, we understood that  carrier mobility in silicon was inversely proportional to doping  concentration. Higher doping concentration at device channel, more severe scattering effect and worse device carrier mobility  degradation would happen as the device was under operation. Figure 9 gave the illustration of this phenomenon. Thus we  performed curve fitting method and model equation analysis  on device transfer character curve for device carrier mobility  degradation analysis.


Figure 7: The Dark-field TEM image illustrated the large gate  poly grain extended from top to bottom of the whole gate poly  film.


Figure 8: The device gate oxide boron penetration failure  mechanism illustration. (a) Normal p-MOS device after gate  poly deposition. (b) Normal p-MOS device at gate poly boron  counter-doped process. (c) Normal p-MOS device after post  thermal process treatment. (d) Failing p-MOS device had  large poly grain. (e) Failing p-MOS device at gate poly boron  counter-doped process. (f) Failing p-MOS device after post  thermal process treatment.


Figure 9: The scheme illustrated that the leaky p-MOS device  had unexpected p-type dopant at device channel suffered  severe scattering effect and worse carrier mobility  degradation as the channel was conduction.


Modified Ideal Equation


After some simplification of ideal equation, we had the Eq. (1), and this equation would be taken as the model equation for  the following device carrier mobility analysis.


VDS of Eq. (1) was considered to be a constant which was kept  at -50mV at the measurement. However, since there is no  dimensional difference between the failing and its reference  device, the device effective length (L), width (W) and gate  oxide capacitance (COX) of Eq.(1) were regarded as an  insignificant constant under device strong inversion condition (VGS > Vt). Therefore, curve fitting method and model  equation analysis were performed on transfer characteristic  curve region I and region II, and the curve fitting equation X  and Y denoted Eq. (1) VGS and IDS, respectively. And the  absolute value of curve fitting equation X coefficient  represented device carrier mobility value at channel area. Thus, Figure 10 and 11 showed curve fitting results and  equations, and these data was summarized in Table I.


Figure 10: This diagram illustrated the gate voltage range of  the region I and II on transfer characteristic curve.


Figure 11: Region I curve fitting result and trend line  equation. Note that, R2 represents the fitness of curve fitting  method.


Figure 12: Region II curves fitting result and trend line  equation. Note that, R2 represents the fitness of curve fitting  method.


Table I: A summary of curve fitting results and trend line  equations at transfer characteristic curve region I and II.


Curve Fitting Results and Failure Mechanism Verification


Table I shows a comparison with a neighbor reference. The  leaky p-MOS device had 33% lesser device carrier mobility  value at region I and suffered worse carrier mobility  degradation at region II. Referred to prior paper’s study [8], carrier mobility value in silicon is an inversely proportional  relationship to the carrier concentration, as shown in Figure


12. By this relationship and all above observations, the  additional p-type dopant at device channel area conjecture was  proven, and the device gate oxide boron penetration failure  mechanism was accordingly verified.


Figure 13: A diagram illustrated that hole (Boron) carrier  mobility was inversely proportional to carrier concentration  in Silicon [8].


Root Cause Fixed


The abnormal long gate poly deposition duration, it was  caused to the “typhoon visit” at that time period. Typhoon  provided a low atmosphere pressure environment and this  made the tool recipe interlock. Hence boat could not be pulled  out after gate poly deposition step. With some software  corrective actions, this issue was resolved, and yield was back  to the normal.


In this paper, systematic device characterization identified a  leaky p-MOS device at the failure site.


By subsequent device carrier mobility analysis, the unexpected  p-type dopant induced device carrier mobility degradation was  successfully proven, and the device gate oxide boron  penetration failure mechanism was accordingly verified.


As to perform the curve fitting method and model equation  analysis on device transfer characteristic curve for device  carrier mobility analysis, few suggestions were provided as  below:


(1). A suitable model equation; The R2 value of curve fitting  result could illustrate the suitability of the model equation.  (2). There must be no obvious device dimensional difference  between faulty and reference device.


(3). A suitable curve fitting region on transfer characteristic  curve. (4).The R2 value of curve fitting result must be close to  1; 0.99 < R2< 1 was acceptable.

Photon Emission Spectra of FETs as obtained by InGaAs detector


Arkadiusz Glowacki, Christian Boit


Berlin University of Technology, Institute of Radio Frequency and Semiconductor System Technologies  Section Semiconductor Devices


Einsteinufer 19, Sekretariat E2, D - 10587 Berlin, Germany


aglowa@mikro.ee.tu-berlin.de, Tel.: +49-30-314-24922


Yoshiyuki Yokoyama


Hamamatsu Photonics Germany, Herrsching, Germany


Philippe Perdu


French Space Agency, CNES  Toulouse, France


In this work we present spectrally resolved photon emission  microscopy (SPEM) measurements for short-channel FETs  acquired through the backside of the Si substrate using InGaAs  detector. Two spectrum resolution methods have been used: continuous using a prism and discrete using a set of  interference band-pass filters. The photon emission (PE) spectra have been corrected for the background / noise of the  detector; they have been calibrated with respect to the system  optical transmission function and corrected for the absorption  on free carriers in the remaining layer of Si substrate. We  discuss all the standardization aspects thoroughly as they are  crucial in order to obtain correct device-intrinsic PE spectral  information. Finally, we present the spectral results for FET  devices operated in various operating conditions.


Photon Emission Microscopy (PEM) is one of the most  commonly used non-destructive functional analysis and defect  localization failure analysis (FA) techniques. Technological  advancements of the microelectronic industry like multi-level  metallization and flip-chip packaging resulted in an increased  demand for FA techniques to be performed from the chip  backside, through the Si substrate. The detector typically used  for frontside PE applications, namely Si-CCD, could also be  used for the backside analysis but its applicability was strongly  limited by strong electron-hole related absorption of light in  the substrate material with the amount of photons absorbed  being dependent on the light wavelength, sample thickness and  doping concentration. Doping concentration increasing from  technology to technology further complicated the analysis as it  also lead to increased free-carrier-related absorption. Other  detectors, working in near-infrared (NIR) spectral regime (like  InGaAs and MCT) were more often applied using the  advantage of better transparency of Si to NIR wavelengths. But even these detectors suffered from increased free-carrier


absorption resulting from increased doping concentration  necessary to reduce short-channel effects. Thinning down Si  substrate material was the only solution for optical techniques  like PEM to remain applicable to backside analysis.


While integral mode of photon emission is sufficient for  the localization purposes, it delivers only limited information  about the device functionality. Knowledge of the spectrum of  the emitted light may provide additional information about  device operating condition, functional state and/or possible  defects. Spectral mode of PE microscopy (SPEM) is of interest  since the PE microscopes are in the market with the first  results being published more than two decades ago [1, 2]. Over  the years several approaches have been made to detect the PE  spectra from MOSFETs [3, 4] but the results that have been  published are very often inconsistent with each other and  usually are limited to narrow spectrum as obtained using single  detector – usually in visible spectral regime as obtained using  Si-CCDs.


In this study we would like to present the results of the  SPEM experiments performed on 120 nm process technology  devices acquired through the backside of the Si substrate using  modern InGaAs detector with the sensitivity in NIR regime  between 900 nm and 1550 nm (see also Fig.1).


Fig.1: Spectral sensitivity characteristics of InGaAs detector.


We also discuss background / noise aspects of the InGaAs  detectors and how to correct for it when processing the  spectral data. We describe the data standardization process  taking into account spectral sensitivity function of the system  and absorption in the remaining Si substrate.


Devices and Sample Preparation


The devices under test (DUT) used in this study were  manufactured by Infineon Technologies AG in 120 nm process  technology. The nominal voltage for n-type and p-type FETs  was ±1.2V. The FETs were surrounded by STI (shallow trench  isolation). In addition, STI and metal dummy tiling was  present. The thickness of gate oxide was 30 Å, n-well  thickness - 2 μm and n+ and p+ diffusions - 100 nm. Doping  levels of most important Si regions are as follows: substrate  doping concentration – NSUB = 5 x 1015 cm-3, well doping  concentration – NWELL = 2 x 1017 cm-3 and diffusion doping  concentration – NDIFF = 2 x 1020 cm-3.


In the first step, wafer was diced and test structures of  interest were glued and bonded in the 256-pins SBGA  packages. Next, the bonded samples were encapsulated   and packages were opened from the backside. Final step of  preparation was mechanical die thinning to obtain the substrate  desired thickness. For the experiments with the InGaAs  detector it was not necessary to thin down the samples to very  low thicknesses of remaining Si. In most cases, sample  thickness is in the order of 80 – 100 μm. Several transistors  were chosen for this investigation in which the channel width  was 10 μm in all cases while the length was ranging from 100  nm to 140 nm. A layout of the FET is shown in Fig.2.


Fig.2: Layout and exemplary photon emission image of a FET  operated in saturation.


Experimental Setups


The core of the setup is the well known Phemos-1000 PEM/LS  system manufactured by Hamamatsu Photonics. The detector  was -70°C cooled InGaAs FPA. The spectral sensitivity  characteristic of this detector is presented in Fig.1. It is  sensitive between 900 nm and 1600 nm. However, the spectral  response is further narrowed by the low-pass filter with the  cut-off wavelength of 1550 nm. It is used to decrease the noise  and possible interference coming from blackbody radiation. In  order to resolve the spectrum of the emitted light two methods  have been used. First method employed the prism to obtain the “continuous” spectrum with a very good wavelength resolution


(2 – 15 nm). The second method employed a set of  interference band-pass filters that enabled the “discrete” spectrum acquisition with the wavelength resolution of ~50  nm. The DUTs were activated using external semiconductor  characterization system. Experimental setup used in this study  is presented schematically in Fig.3.


Fig.3: Experimental setup for discrete and continuous PE  spectrum acquisition using InGaAs detector.


Spectral Data Acquisition & Standardization


The samples used to obtain the PE spectra using InGaAs  detector were typically ~80 – 100 μm thick. For each spectrum  acquisition method, the data standardization has been  performed in order to obtain device-intrinsic PE spectral  characteristics.


Discrete spectrum


In order to obtain a discrete spectrum a set of band-pass  interference filters has been used. The number of filters and  their bandwidth has both defined the wavelength resolution, spectrum averaging property and the overall spectrum  acquisition time. For a device operated in a desired electrical  condition a PE image has been acquired with each filter  inserted in the optical path of the system (see experimental  setup in Fig.2). In every image an area has been defined to  include the emission originating from the studied FET. Another area has been created in the region containing only  background signal. Average background signal has been  calculated and then subtracted from the integral FET emission  signal after being multiplied by number of pixels in the area  containing the FET signal. This signal has been later divided  by the exposure time used for the individual image  acquisitions in order to obtain the FET integral emission  intensity for one second of the exposure time. The signal  intensity vs. exposure time linearity of the detector has been  proven beforehand. After the spectrum data has been acquired, it was necessary to correct the experimental data for the  spectral transmission of the optical path and the quantum  efficiency (QE) of the detector in order to make another step


towards the device intrinsic PE spectrum. Two methods have  been used to obtain this correction data. One method employed  the calibrated light source. The other method relied on the  knowledge of the spectral transmission / sensitivity  characteristics of all the system components (see Fig.4). Both  methods enabled the calculation of correction coefficients for  individual filters. These coefficients included information  about both the transmission of the optical path and detector  spectral sensitivity. Both methods have been used in order to  increase the trustworthiness of the experimental data. All the  components of the optical path are shown in the Fig.4 together  with the convoluted spectral characteristics for individual  filters.


Fig.4: Components of the optical path and convoluted  characteristics of the transmission characteristics of  individual components for each of the employed  interference band-pass filters.


The individual correction factors are then calculated as field  under the convoluted characteristic and used to correct the  spectral data. The last step of the standardization is to correct  for the light absorption in the remaining Si thickness using  simulation results, as also presented before in Fig.5.


Continuous spectrum


The continuous spectrum resolution method employed a prism  as a spectrally resolving unit. The spectral dispersion range of  the used prism has been adjusted to the spectral regime of the  InGaAs detector. The prism could be conveniently inserted in  the optical path whenever desired. It was positioned in a way  to be parallel to one of the dimensions of the detector and  perpendicular to another. In such a configuration, when the  prism was inserted in the optical path, the light was dispersed  extending only in one dimension of the detector as presented  in Fig.5. Acquisition of just one image could deliver the full


spectral information as provided by the detector range. Short  acquisition time of the entire PE spectrum is one of the main  advantages of this method over the discrete spectrum  acquisition method. After the spectrum image has been taken, a background image needed to be acquired with the device in  off condition and subtracted from the on-state image.


Fig.5: InGaAs PE images acquired without and with prism  inserted in the optical path.


Proper background signal subtraction is a very important step  as it affects significantly the shape of the PE spectrum  especially in the regions of weak signal intensity. In case of  FET this means in the short wavelength regime (high photon  energy). After the background subtraction, from the resulting  image the spectral data could be read as the intensity versus  pixel number. First of all, the pixels needed to be translated  into wavelength. Reference measurements were performed  beforehand to deliver the data necessary for this  transformation. Once pixels were translated into wavelength, one needed to additionally correct for the illumination density  as the pixel-wavelength dependence was not linear. The next  step was the correction for the spectral response function of  the system which included the components of the optical path  and the detector. This data was also obtained through the  reference measurements. A calibrated light source of the  known spectral response has been used to obtain this data. After the correction for the system spectral function one had  the spectrum of the light that was emerging from the backside  of the sample. The last necessary correction was the one for  the absorption in the Si substrate layer remaining under the  device after backside sample preparation. The correction data  was obtained through simulations as already presented in [5]. This final correction for the absorption in Si delivered the  device intrinsic PE spectrum.


Experimental Results Presentation & Discussion


The discrete PE spectrum of FET operated in saturation (VD = VG = 1.2 V) obtained through the application of band-pass  filters is presented in Fig.6. The continuous PE spectrum is  presented in Fig.7. Both spectra are then also compared in  Fig.8 and they are consistent with each other. The intensity of  light increases exponentially with the wavelength, with only  slight distortion from the exponential trend for high  wavelengths (low energies). These results are in agreement  with the previous measurement data obtained using Si-CCD  detector sensitive in the shorter wavelength regime (450 nm –


In this work we have demonstrated spectrally resolved  measurements of PE from a 0.12 μm x 10 μm, n-type  MOSFET operated in various electrical conditions. InGaAs  detector has been used. System spectral sensitivity was 900 – 1550 nm. Two methods to acquire the device PE spectrum  have been presented. Standardization steps necessary to obtain  device-intrinsic PE spectrum have been described and spectral  data presented for both methods of PE spectrum acquisition. It  is very important to perform all the mentioned correction steps  properly, in order to obtain the correct device-characteristic  PE spectra.

Near-Infrared Photon Emission Spectroscopy Trends in Scaled SOI  Technologies


Ulrike Kindereit, Alan J. Weger, Franco Stellari, Peilin Song


IBM T.J. Watson Research Center, Yorktown Heights, NY, USA  ukinder@us.ibm.com, phone +1-914-945-1469


Hervé Deslandes, Ted Lundquist and Prasad Sabbineni


DCG Systems, Fremont, CA, USA


In this paper, near-infrared photon emission spectroscopy  measurements from ring oscillators in 45 nm and 32 nm SOI  process technology are compared. Employing a cryogenically  cooled camera, the measurements cover a broad spectral range  from 1200-2200 nm. Both leakage and switching emission, increase monotonically with the wavelength, suggesting  measurements should be made at longer wavelengths than has  historically been practiced. The paper discusses the optimum  cut-off wavelength for maximum signal-to-noise ratio and the  obvious importance of reduced ambient temperature for  performing measurements.


Recently, backside photon emission spectroscopy  measurements up to 2200 nm have shown increased intensity  at longer wavelengths for a ring oscillator (RO) built in IBM’s  45 nm SOI (Silicon On Insulator) process technology [1]. The  measurement setup employed different band-pass filters and a  cryogenically cooled MCT (Mercury Cadmium Telluride) camera. The measured switching emission was found to be  exponential to the reciprocal of the device voltage as expected, see [2] and [3], and it increased monotonically for longer  wavelengths as suggested by other works [4], [5], showing  only very faint signal levels for voltages below 0.9 V (nominal  voltage for this device is 1 V) and at wavelengths shorter than  1775 nm. Detectors, conventionally used for emission  microscopy, such as Si-CCDs (Silicon Charge Coupled  Devices) or InGaAs (Indium Gallium Arsenide) cameras have  much lower cut-off wavelengths than the camera used in [1] (see [6]) and hence would fail to detect a large portion of the  signal at longer wavelengths.


At longer wavelengths thermal emission tails are a significant  source of undesirable background, limiting the acquisition time  and increasing the noise. For this reason, it is important to  determine, if the increasing emission levels found in [1] are a  general trend. If that is the case thorough investigations should  be performed in order to determine which wavelength range is  best suited for emission measurements, since from some  wavelength on the thermal background of the detector will be  so high that signal acquisition will be difficult or even  impossible.


This paper compares the previously measured 45 nm results  with data of a comparable ring oscillator, manufactured in  32 nm SOI process technology, and discusses emission levels  at longer wavelengths, in order to find out if emission at those  longer wavelengths is a general trend or just a single incident. (Note that it is not the aim of this work to compare the  electrical capabilities of the two process technologies and that  development test chips were chosen.) Further switching and  leakage emission spectra have been investigated separately in  order to learn if there is an optimal wavelength range for  blocking unwanted signals. At longer wavelengths the  acquisition of the signal is influenced by the thermal  background which reduces exposure time, limiting ability to  detect faint signals. In order to show if the influence of  ambient temperature on the thermal background of the camera  and the leakage and switching emission can help improve  measurement capabilities – such as performing low voltage  measurements – a thorough investigation of the thermal  camera background and the measured signal-to-noise ratio  spectra are shown for different temperatures.


Ring Oscillators in 45 and 32 nm Technology


The Devices Under Test (DUTs) used for this publication are  ring oscillators, manufactured in IBM’s 45 nm and 32 nm SOI  process technologies. These devices are particularly suited for  photon emission measurements with a high magnification  objective: they both consist of 100 stages of inverters, folded  to form a rectangular shape. A NAND gate is inserted into the  RO loops to enable/disable oscillation. The output of the ROs  is divided by 1024 and made externally available to the chip  for monitoring the RO frequencies during different operating  conditions. The RO voltages (VRO) were varied between 0.7  and 1.6 V (nominal DUT voltage 1 V). The corresponding  output frequencies, switching and leakage currents can be  found in Figure 1-Figure 3. Note that due to the difference in  the gate dimensions between the two process technologies, the  RO built in 32 nm process technology has an active area that is  about 15 % smaller than the one built in 45 nm. Further note  that the electrical device data and the emission spectra may not  be representative of other devices fabricated in these process  technologies, since the DUTs used for this investigation are  process development test chips.

Spectral Photon Emission Measurements


For each measurement condition of the ROs, three types of  acquisitions have been performed, which are described in the  following paragraphs.


3.0


Background Emission (BE)


The thermal background emission of the setup has been  acquired by switching the DUT off entirely and simply  recording the emission levels without any device activity (note  that it has been proven that these levels do not differ  significantly from those recorded without any target in the  field of view, suggesting similar emissivity levels for DUT  surface and the dark box). Besides the thermal background, this measurement also includes camera noise and the  remaining ambient radiation.


Leakage Emission (LE)


When the RO supply voltage is on, but the NAND gate is set  to disable the oscillation, the RO is in leakage condition, i.e. there is no switching emission from the gates. In this DC  device state, 50 % of the NFETs and 50 % of the PFETs are  leaking all the time, thus emitting Leakage Emission caused by  the off-state leakage current. All other FETs are off.


Switching Emission (SE)


When the RO supply voltage is on and the NAND gate is set to  enable the ring oscillator, the transistors in the circuit are  switching and emit photons due to the hot carrier emission.  After the switching event, the transistors are in leakage  condition again. Since the switching transition is very short, it  can be assumed that the leakage emission in the switching case  is approximately the same as in the leakage emission case: since the RO is ringing, the states of the FETs will change  continuously: all of the NFETs and all of the PFETs are  leaking about 50 % of the time, with the exception of the  switching event. The measured emission in this condition is  then a combination of the Leakage Emission LE and the  Switching Emission SE, i.e. LE+SE. Henceforth the pure  Switching Emission SE can be obtained by acquiring an LE  and an LE+SE image and then subtracting the first from the  latter. Also note that the leakage emission image contains the  background, as well, so applying this method will result in the  subtraction of the background emission from the acquisition. The Switching Emission SE also consists of any self-heating  i.e. black body radiation that occurs in the RO while switching.  This portion has been investigated in [1] and was found to be  small in comparison to the hot carrier emission.


Data Acquisition and Processing


Note that the definition of the Switching Emission SE used  here differs from the one of the previous work [1]:

(II)


Here, the raw Leakage Emission rLE and the raw Switching  Emission rSE (which is obtained by subtracting the leakage


image LE form the switching plus leakage image LE+SE, see  above) are normalized to the exposure time tExp and the filter  transmission TF. The camera response is reasonable flat over  the wavelength range of the measurements (confidential data, not shown here) and hence the emission intensity is not  corrected for it in this case. The normalized Switching  Emission SE and Leakage Emission LE intensity are then  stated in counts per second nanometer. All other  normalizations will be annotated separately.


Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR)


The signal-to-noise ratio has been calculated for the SE  images. For this purpose, the mean of the signal area has been  divided by the standard deviation of the noise area (schematic  as in [1], Figure 4), which is of the same size as the defined  signal area but in a location far from the RO emission site.


Comparison of 45 nm and 32 nm Results


In order to separate the LE and SE spectra at different  temperatures, it was necessary to retake the data of the 45 nm  RO. The measurements here were taken on a different chip, such that the values are not exactly the same as in the previous  measurements [1] due to process variations and acquisition  conditions. However the repeatability across devices is about  10-20 % (depending on the filter used) for room temperature. Previously the results were showing a stronger SE signal  decrease for lower temperatures, which points to an even lower  black body emission portion in this case, in comparison to the  data presented in [1].


In Figure 5 the room temperature emission spectra of the  45 nm and the 32 nm ROs are compared. The graphs show the  Leakage Emission LE, the Switching Emission SE and the SEto-LE ratio for three different RO supply voltages: for an  elevated 1.3 V, for the nominal device voltage 1.0 V and for a  lower 0.7 V. The ratio of the Switching Emission SE to the  Leakage Emission LE can be used to determine, if there is a  wavelength range that is best suited for time-resolved  measurements (for more details see e.g. [7] or [8]), where the  Leakage Emission is an undesired signal source and should be  avoided (filtered out) in order to collect most of the useful  Switching Emission.


SE and LE Spectra


Note that the Leakage Emission of both devices is in the same  range for all voltages, with the 32 nm Leakage Emission being  a little higher than the one of the 45 nm technology, whereas  the Switching Emission levels of the 45 nm device are up to a  factor of four higher than those of the 32 nm device at the  same voltage. Since the emission levels are likely to scale with


 tTExpF snm 


the current levels of the devices, the Switching Emission data  has been normalized to the RO switching current ISW and the  Leakage Emission data to the RO leakage current IL. As an  example, the result for 1.3 V is shown in Figure 6. The  normalization of the Switching Emission to the switching  current shows the similarities of the two switching spectra for  wavelengths up to 1775 nm. For longer wavelengths, the  45 nm RO still has higher emission levels per mA current.


After normalizing the Leakage Emission spectra to the leakage  current, both leakage spectra are qualitatively similar in shape, but the fact that the Leakage Emission, here per mA leakage  current, is stronger for the 32 nm technology is more

Low Voltage Acquisitions


Cut-Off Wavelength for Optimal SNR


The emission data presented in this publication shows that, especially for low voltage measurements, it makes sense to do  emission measurements at longer wavelengths – possibly  beyond the spectral response of the InGaAs camera. The  switching emission levels increase at longer wavelengths for  all voltages. So particularly for low voltage measurements, where the weak signal levels could prevent doing emission  measurements, the stronger emission levels at longer  wavelengths are important. The comparison of leakage and  switching spectra at lower voltages, see graph for 0.7 V in  Figure 5, shows that the useful switching emission is  dominating the spectrum at longer wavelengths. Also the  investigation of the SNR at 0.7 V revealed maxima at  1764 nm.


Influence of Ambient Temperature on the Measurements  For very faint signals, improvement of the SNR is vital. Besides accumulating acquisitions in order to average out the  noise and hence improve the SNR1, the SNR also improves for  longer exposure times, as can be found in Figure 8. In order to  detect faint signal levels, it is necessary to use longer exposure  times – in the range of minutes – whereas strong signals can be

time) of the camera is plotted versus the filter wavelength for  room temperature RT, -5 °C and -38 °C. The exposure time for  this measurement was chosen to be 128 s. At room  temperature a measurement with the 2051 nm filter and this  exposure time is not possible, because the camera saturation  level would be reached (no data point at 2051 nm). At -5 °C  and at 1914 nm the background level decreased to about a half  of its original level, but at 2051 nm the camera would still be  saturated within 128 s. Only at -38 °C the camera could be  used for acquisitions of 128 s and at 2051 nm filter  wavelength. A comparison of the three data sets shows that the  thermal Background Emission BE basically is halved for a  temperature decrease of 30 °C with the according increase in  exposure time. The thermal Background Emission for the three  temperatures and for the 2051 nm filter versus exposure time  can be found in Figure 10. This graph shows nicely that the  possible exposure times nearly double for a drop of 30 °C in  ambient temperature. Note that the data has been treated by  using a code which catches the saturation level: if there was  only one pixel that reached the saturation level of the camera, the data has been discarded and the next lower exposure time  has been chosen for the measurement.

Temperature Dependency of LE and SE


Further the influence of the ambient temperature on the SE-toLE ratio has been investigated. Unfortunately for very low  temperatures, such as -38 °C, the ROs of both process  technologies did not work electrically (most likely due to  contact issues), i.e. there was no Switching Emission to be  measured. This shows that there is a limit to the use of low  ambient temperatures for improvement of the SNR. Figure 11  shows the SE-to-LE ratio for both process technologies at the  nominal device voltage for room temperature (compare to  Figure 5) and for -5 °C ambient temperature. At -5 °C, the SEto-LE ratio basically doubles for both process technologies. Both signals, the SE and the LE, decreased at lower  temperatures, but the decrease of the LE is much stronger than  the one of the SE. So particularly for time-resolved  measurements, where the DC leakage emission is undesired, low temperature measurements should be preferred. Note that  this is only an option, if the device works at that temperature  and for the case that the temperature can be decreased as  desired (might not work for high power applications).


Figure 11: Ratio of Switching Emission SE to Leakage  Emission LE for the nominal RO supply voltage 1.0 V for both  devices.


Temperature Dependency of SNR


Finally the influence of the DUT temperature on the SNR is  summarized in Table 1. For the 1764 nm filter it was possible  to choose long exposure times even at room temperature, which is the reason why they are the same at room temperature  and at -5 °C. For the 1914 nm and 2051 nm filter it was  possible to increase the exposure time for the lower  temperature measurements by a factor of two, as expected  from preceding investigations (see Figure 9 & Figure 10  above). This is one of the reasons for the higher SNR, as  shown in Figure 8. The largest SNR values were achieved with  the 1914 nm filter (45 nm process) and with the 1764 nm filter (32 nm process) at -5 °C. But the strongest increase of the  SNR was achieved with the 1764 nm filter: in the 45 nm  measurement the SNR improved by a factor of six due to  lowering the temperature.
