Words for the “Whys”


Richard J. Ross


Consultant, Essex Junction, Vermont 05452  rjrossvt@comcast.net


In an era where the complexity and cost of Failure Analysis  tools and techniques is rapidly expanding, it is easy sometimes  to lose sight of the basic tool and technique required for  successful root-cause analysis.  That technique is intellectual  curiosity and the tool is the human brain.  This paper will  describe a simple methodology to insure that this tool and  technique are properly engaged either concomitant with or in  the absence of state-of-the-art instrumentation and  computation.  Two simple case studies will be used to  illustrate where the Failure Analysis process can easily go  awry without proper attention to detail, and, conversely, from  too much attention to detail.


Failure Analysis shares any number of similarities with both  news reporting and detective work.  Introductory journalism  students and/or “cub” reporters are instructed in the “Four  W’s” of “Who, What, Where, When” and the “Big H” of “How”. Detectives share these key questions as anyone  familiar with the board game “Clue” ™ (or the movie based  on it) knows.  While the “What” (murder) and “When” (previous night) are always the same, the “Who”, “Where” and “How” are the objects to be resolved (e.g.: Colonel  Mustard in the Library with the Axe; Mrs. White in the  Kitchen with the Knife; etc.).   While Failure Analysis shares  the need for this information, it is often the input of our  activity as opposed to the desired result.  We are supplied with  the “Who” (the customer), “What” (the part), “Where” (location of the unit), “When” (power-on hours or stress  hours), and “How” (Fail indication).  Many young, and, as  will be shown in the following examples, even experienced  analysts can mistake the “How” or the “What” for the   true  root-cause or “Why” of the failure.  What we need to be able  to supply is forensic analysis (like “CSI” or “Bones”) that  determines the “Why” of the failure.  Not accepting the first  explanation or the seemingly obvious solution key to  successful analysis.


In any number of texts, articles, and classes on Quality  Management, reference is made to the concept of the “Five  Whys”.    The concept is straightforward; when confronted  with a problem, especially one which results in a failure of a n  organization, system, or product to perform to specification, the analyst should continue to ask “Why?” until the root cause  is revealed.  The number of five “Whys” was arbitrarily


selected based on some anecdotal evidence that it took, on  average, five repeated answers to the question in order to “unlayer the onion” down to that actual root cause, as opposed  to the first few answers which only dealt with the fail  signature. Toyota developed the practice of asking "why" five  times and answering it each time to uncover the root cause of  a problem.[1]   Mr. Ohno does not specify how the “Five  Whys” process was discovered, but, as a parent, I expect he  got it from his three-year-old child’s persistent questions (“Daddy, why is the sky blue?” comes to mind).  An example  of the process is shown in Figure 1, below.


Repeating "Why" Five Times]


1. Why did the machine stop?


There was an overload, and the fuse blew.


2. Why was there an overload?


The bearing was not sufficiently lubricated.


3. Why was it not lubricated sufficiently?


The lubrication pump was not pumping sufficiently.


4. Why was it not pumping sufficiently?


The shaft of the pump was worn and rattling.


5. Why was the shaft worn out?


There was no strainer attached, and metal scrap got in.


Figure 1: Five “Whys” example [2]


It would have been tempting to define the cause of fail as a  blown fuse, with a corrective action of replacing the fuse, or, even going further into the analysis to simply replace the  pump as “normal wear & tear”.  Continuing the analysis by  continuing to ask “Why” eventually disclosed that a strainer  or filter in the lubricant tank was not attached and that allowed  metal filings to get into the shaft of the lubrication pump and  caused the bearings to prematurely wear out.


This kind of analytical technique can be of use to the  Electronic Device Failure Analysis community – in the two  cases below, the use (and misuse) of the “Five Whys” technique will be examined.  These are actual cases  encountered over 39 years in the semiconductor industry.  One  shows that the simplest answer does not result in the discovery  of the root cause, even when the evidence seems to support  that conclusion; the second shows the over-analysis can lead  to paralysis and significant schedule delays.


Case 1: “Popcorn”, Anyone?


A number of years ago, a situation arose which quickly “exploded” (choice of words intentional) into a crisis.  At a


college recruiting fair, a company representative was setting  up a PC-based presentation when the machine had a boot  error.  After a few unsuccessful attempts to start the computer, he removed the cover to find one of the accessory cards had  loosened during transport.  He seated the card (with the power  connected) and, to his horror, heard a loud “crack” and saw a  blue-yellow flame shooting out from the back of the machine.  After attempting to blow out the flame (I am NOT making this  up!), he had the presence of mind to pull the power cord.  He  tried plugging the machine back in and rebooting, but, alas, the damage was done and he completed his work at the event  without the computer.  Fortunately, no students were present  while this occurred.  The card was returned to our lab for  analysis.  The fail signature was obvious – the plastic package  was severely discolored and a large chunk of it had been  forcibly ejected (hence, the crack sound).  Upon low  magnification inspection a piece of the gold wire could be  seen. X-ray analysis confirmed that several bonded wires had  been ripped apart and that at least one had been vaporized.  Package schematic analysis confirmed that the wire that had  evaporated was one of the five volt power supply lines. The  fail, because of the “crack” noise and the ejected plastic  became known as the “popcorn” problem.  Upon reading the  incident report and the initial inspection of the part, the  answer to “Why #1” (Why did the module cease to function?) became obvious – Electrical Overload (EOL), caused by “hotplugging” the card – case closed.


Well, not exactly.  Since this happened on a system which was  sold to the general public, it was determined that a task force (when in doubt, form a group of high-level people to report to  even higher-level people to confuse those who really have to  do the work – management 101) should be formed to assess  the potential liability to the general public.  Oddly enough, this actually proved to be a good idea.  The first step was to  try to re-enact the scenario.  At the assembly plant for the PC, dozens of machines were set up and the card in question was “hot-plugged” a number of times.  In about five percent of the  trials, there was  smoke and a cracking sound with subsequent  failure of the part – in a significant fraction of these cases, a  particle of plastic was forcibly ejected from the package – often with enough force to travel over twenty feet in the  assembly room!  This was deemed to be a potential major  safety issue for the general public.  While a short-term “fix” of  sending a cautionary note to existing owners and affixing a  sticker to the user guide for new purchases warning the  customer not to “hot plug” a card was implemented, we turned  attention to “Why #2:  (Why only this card and this part?)  None of the other cards experimented with had exhibited this  explosive fail, nor did any other part on the failing card  exhibit any damage.


Upon inspection of the card and its assembly drawing, we  noted that several of the gold-plated lands on the card were  indented.  This, of course, was Why #3. (Why were some of  the lands indented and did that matter to the fail mechanism?)  Meanwhile, for completeness, we depackaged several of the  die from the failed packages, cleaned them up and sent them  for test to determine what section of the die was destroyed by


the overload.  Visual inspection showed no damage and, to  our surprise, the die tested as all good!  Now we were faced  with Why #4:  (Why did a one micron thick AluminumCopper line withstand enough current -- in excess of one  Ampere, by calculation -- to vaporize a one mil gold wire?)


These answers took several days of analysis.  The question of  the card land indentation was fairly straightforward.  The card  chosen had the correct form factor and was a standard part in  inventory.  It had been used in the days of Bipolar EmitterCoupled Logic (ECL) for large system applications.  These  devices used multiple power supplies and had a specific  power-up sequence requirement.  The indents were deliberate  so as to insure that some power supplies were enabled before  others as the card was inserted.  After comparing the card  layout with the application in this case, we found that the five  volt supply to several parts, including the failing one, was  indented, while most signal lines were not and most ground  planes were not.  A quick check of the pin-out of the failing  module indicated that, if the card were “hot-plugged” the  signal lines would make connection before the VDD power  supply was enabled).  If the signal line was set at a logical “1”, the output pin of the driver circuit would be supplied a  positive five volts, the ground plane would be enabled, and  the VDD supply would not make contact for milliseconds.  A  circuit analysis showed that this was a necessary and sufficient  condition for the output driver to go into latch-up mode.  As  soon as the power supply line was enabled, a very low  resistance path from VDD to ground was established, permitting literally Amperes of current to flow.


As to Why #4, chemical, mechanical and thermal analysis of  the packaging material showed that the material had very poor  thermal conductivity, and had a composite structure such that  it could fracture along grain boundary lines under extreme  internal stress.  The former indicated that the wire supplying  the VDD heated up so rapidly that the wire evaporated before  the current density became high enough to damage the die (the  current was dispersed across several power supply wires on  the die, but concentrated (for that circuit) in the one package  wire.  The resulting heat and pressure from the vaporization  caused the package to fail at its weakest point, and, if that ran  along two or more grain boundaries, the result was a piece of  the material being ejected with great force and/or a crack in  the package with an accompanying blue-yellow flame. In  short, the gold wire acted as a fuse protecting the die!


Finally, we were left with Why #5. (Why was this particular  part so susceptible to latch-up?)  Several other modules on the  card had been exposed to this condition and had not failed.  Inspection of the physical design graphical files showed that, for unknown reasons, design-rule mandated guard rings were  either incomplete or contact to them were insufficiently biased  in the specific output driver macro used only on the failing  part.  This was immediately called to the designers’ attention  and a redesign and mask change was implemented on an  expedited basis.


The application of the “Five Whys” to the preceding case is  shown in Figure 2.


1. Why did the part cease to function?


Electrical Overload (EOL) due to card hot plug.


2. Why only this card?


Inappropriate use of a stock design card


3. Why was the card use inappropriate?


It was designed for a different family of products, which  led to a power-up sequence that caused the drive device


of the output driver to go into latch up mode, causing the  package to fail.


4. Why did the package fail and not the die?


The package material was of low thermal conductivity


causing the current to be absorbed by the package wiring  since the die was overly sensitive to the latch-up


conditions.


5. Why was this part so susceptible to latch-up?


Inadequate guard ring design and/or bias on the output


driver macro


Figure 2: Five “Whys” as applied to Case #1


As a result, the final corrective actions, in addition to the  warning letters/stickers, included the output driver macro  being redesigned to reduce latch-up sensitivity and the card  being redesigned to eliminate the indentations.  The fixes, both short and long-term effectively eliminated the problem.  Nonetheless, the incident proves the point that things are not  always so simple as they seem upon answering the first “Why?”  It would have been very ease to simply conclude that  EOL and “operator error” was the root-cause as well as the  proximate cause of the fail and other products using the same  card design and/or output driver macro design would have  been exposed in the future.


Case 2: It’s A Sticky Problem


The next case dates from an even earlier life as manager of a  Technology Reliability group, charged, in this case, with the  technology feasibility qualification of an organic inter-level  dielectric (ILD) made of a polyimide-based material, in a  bipolar SRAM application.  The desire to move to a polyimide (PI) ILD was not driven by the need to lower inter- or intralevel capacitive coupling, as is the case today, but was an  attempt to planarize the metal layers, allowing for smaller  interconnect vias and thus smaller die sizes.  This exercise was  a technology qualification stress and was critical to meeting  program schedules for a next-generation of large-system  memory.  Obviously, there was much attention being paid at  many levels of management across several functions and  divisions as to the success of this (at the time) radical  departure from accepted practice.


There were two level of the polyimide ILD, one between the  two metal levels and one on top of the second metal level  beneath the final flip-chip ball-level metallurgy.  The standard  temperature-bias (T/V) and temperature-humidity-bias


(T/H/V) reliability life stresses were required for passing the  qualification.  Samples were determined, and pass-fail criteria  established.  The first stress results were not encouraging.  Inside of 48 hours of stress, the T/H/V cell (85 degrees C, 80% Relative Humidity, 5.0 Volts) was exhibiting massive  fails at initial continuity test.  Why #1: What caused the parts  to fail continuity testing? Failure analysis confirmed metal  corrosion on both metal levels.  Why #2:  What caused the  corrosion? Physical analysis showed that the corrosion  product was Aluminum Chloride caused by Chlorine (Chlorine being present in a number of wet etch chemistry  steps) penetrating the polyimide edge seal accompanied by  massive delamination of one or both polyimide levels from the  underlying die surface.  All that was necessary for Chlorine  ion migration was the bias differential on the outside metal  line (designed for the stress) and the moisture inherent to the  T/H/V stress and the failure of the polyimide to oxide  interface.


As the failure of the stress created a major impact on the  ultimate product qualification and, effectively, the  announcement of a major system enhancement, there was, as  might be expected some consternation in the management  ranks.  An immediate repopulation of the stress (each  repopulation required the fabrication of three product lots, each lot separated by one manufacturing shift) was ordered, with extreme caution taken in handling the parts in the fab.  The results were, while not as disastrous as the original stress, still bad enough to fail the specification. A third repopulation  produced similar results.  We now had the full attention of  upper management, within and outside our site and division.  That, of course, meant that “help” was on the way, not to  mention daily status meetings and reports.


We now approached the Why #2 – Why was the seal failing?  The original design had the first level of polyimide contacting  the final silicon dioxide passivation layer and the second level  of polyimide etched to contact the first level.  A “rip-stop” (polyimide-free area) had been etched in the kerf cut area so  that the dicing of the wafer would not damage the polyimide.   The observation from Failure Analysis was that the seal was  failing due to delamination of the ILD from the underlying  oxide surface.


What then ensued was a range of experimentation.  Everyone  had a theory, including very senior levels of management, and  multiple experiments were designed.  Alternative polyimide  materials, alternative etchants, alternative edge designs (overlap vs. “underlap”), polyimide contact to oxide vs. bare  silicon were all evaluated in combination.  The variables are  shown in Table 1.


Etchant


Overlap Underlap Oxide Bare  Si


PI  1


PI  2


Table 1:  Experiment cells in Case 2


As can be seen from Table 1, this resulted in a multidimensional array of variables, which wound up comprising  26, or 64 different experiment cells.  Each cell required three  lots of test structures, each separated by one production shift, and, potentially a minimum of 168 hours of T/H/B stress. In  every case run, the stress produced inconsistencies lot-to-lot (and even wafer-to-wafer), but no cell passed the stress  conditions.  In actuality, only about half the potential cells  were run, but, given that manufacture from first metal to final  packaging took a minimum of one week, and the stresses ran  from 48 hours to 168 hours depending on the severity of the  fallout, over a calendar year of experimentation was  consumed. This delayed the product qualification by a like  amount.


As can be imagined, this attracted even more attention.  Resource from internal and external research and development  organizations were applied, PhD process engineers handcarried the wafer lots through the various sectors, high-level  manufacturing engineers and technicians ran the new process  steps, yet the results continued to be inconsistent, but always  unsatisfactory.  Some lots were fine, in others, individual  wafers were fine, but the overall results always failed the  requirements.


This brings us to Why #3 – Why was there no significant  difference between the various experimental cells?  Logically, there should have been some statistical variance across  material type, etchant, and edge design. Yet, whatever was  tried exhibited the exact same results.  Clearly, something  outside the variables chosen for experimentation was at the  core of the problem.  But WHAT??


One evening, as the high-level manufacturing engineer was  following an experimental lot through the line, he was waiting  for a standard step to be performed – the application of the  normal adhesion promoter prior to PI deposition. As the  operator took the bottle of material and squeezed it on to the  wafer for spin-on deposition, he agitated the bottle.  The  engineer happened to observe particulate matter swirling  around in the bottle and asked the operator if that always  happened.  The operator said: “Only if I shake the bottle”.  The engineer asked how often the in-line bottle was refilled.  The answer was that the bottle was refilled about once a shift.  The engineer then asked where the bulk material was stored.  He was shown the large bottle of material and, when he shook  it, a swirl of particulates could be seen.  He checked the  expiration date on the bottle and found that the material was  over a year past expiration date.  This brings us to Why #4: Why didn’t anyone suspect the adhesion promoter in the first  place as it was a common step in all the cells?  Everyone from  process engineers, to manufacturing engineers, to reliability  engineers, to failure analysis engineers, to management at  various levels were so laser-focused on what was new and  different, that they never considered what was old and  standard.


A rush order for new material was issued and fresh adhesion  promoter was implemented.  A few different experimental


cells were run, and, again the results across the cells were  similar, except that this time, all variations passed.  It mattered  very little which PI, etchant, or edge design was implemented, so long as the adhesion promoter was fresh and not  contaminated.  In the end, PI1, etchant 1, and the overlap  design was implemented as the process of record – there were  minute statistical differences that implied that this was the best  and most cost-effective conclusion.


Which brings us to Why #5: Why was the use of outdated  process chemicals allowed? A check was made and it was  found that all of the adhesion promoter material in stock was  outdated – it had been used previously only to the promotion  of adhesion of a protective overcoat at the end of production, the edge adhesion of which was not critical as it was only  needed to prevent mechanical damage during dicing and chip  mounting -- so little of the material was consumed on a dayto-day basis, that it was just used until consumed.  Since it had  never been a problem before, no one even considered that a  new application might be more sensitive.


The application of the “Five Whys” to the preceding case is  shown in Figure 3.


Corrosion due to failure of the edge seal


2. Why did the edge seal fail ?


Delamination of the polyimide from the substrate


3. Why did none of the various redesigns and/or material


changes work?


All of the experimental cells were subject to contaminated  adhesion promoter material.


4. Why didn’t anyone suspect the adhesion promoter in the  first place as it was a common step in all the cells?


All the analytical focus was placed on the new material


and steps.


5. Why was the use of outdated process chemicals allowed? It had never been a problem before (It had “Always been  done” this way)


Figure 3: Five “Whys” as applied to Case #2


The result of the debacle in this case was the loss of over one  year of qualification/production volume and the resulting  revenue, not to mention the material and labor cost of  performing all of the experiments and overtime involved.  The  lesson to be learned from this case is that sometimes, what’s  new isn’t the problem – focus on what’s common.


In both of the cases discussed, the Failure Analysis process  was flawed.  In the first case, the forest obscured the trees in  that, had the first observation (EOL from “hot-plug”) and the  warning notice been the final resolution, the problem would  have eventually percolated into every design in which the  flawed off-chip driver macro would be used.  In the second  case, the trees obscured the forest in that the entire working


group fixated on the changes and ignored the process step  common to all variations.  This resulted in a costly delay in  product qualification and shipment with accompanying  revenue implications.


In neither case were any innovative techniques nor  sophisticated instrumentation required to resolve the rootcause of the failures.  The application of simple techniques  like the “Five Whys” is often far more successful an approach  than blindly hitting the problem with high technology.  While  there are many new, innovative, and complex tools and  techniques discussed at ISTFA or which can be seen on the  exposition floor, often the best analysis technique is  intellectual curiosity and the finest failure analysis tool is the


1.5 kg of gray matter situated between your ears.  It is “Wise” to remember the “Whys”.


[1] Ohno, Taiichi, Toyota Production System: Beyond LargeScale Production, Productivity Press; Portland, OR, 1988.


[2] U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, Kaizen Rapid  Process, http://www.epa.gov/lean/thinking/kaizen.htm


EOS (Electrical Overstress) – the old, unknown phenomenon?


Peter Jacob


EMPA Duebendorf, Duebendorf ZH, Switzerland


Peter.jacob@empa.ch, Tel. +41-58-765-4288, Fax -4054


Frequently, Electrical Overstress (EOS) is understood in a  similar context like Electrostatic Discharge (ESD). However, when looking deeper, only 3-5% of EOS failure signatures are  caused by ESD. The dominant root causes can be found on  system level – often inaccessible for the device failure analyst. However, switching procedures, (sometimes hidden-) inductance loads, are the unconsidered and undiscovered  problem makers. This paper reviews and highlights these  failure mechanisms.


EOS-as F/A Conclusion: Challenge or Resignation?


Up to now, EOS is often associated to ESD; but an interesting  discussion at the German ESD Forum meeting in December  2011 pointed out that only 3-5% of EOS failures are really  related to ESD.


All failure analysts are familiar with the typical EOS failure  textures shown in figs 1 and 2, where dark pad connections or  even molten bond wires appear. The root cause of such  damage can be ESD- but in most cases the damages appeared  in operational conditions – with an extremely low risk of an insitu-ESD impact. One might argue, an ESD pre-damage  generates the leakage path (but as long as the device is not in


Fig. 1 (left): molten metal lines, close to the pad, a frequent  EOS failure texture in integrated circuits


Fig. 2 (right): High current EOS, molten bond wires


operation it would not cause burning) and later, when  operational conditions are applied – it would fail due to the  high current flow under operational conditions. Certainly, this  is correct – but only if the ESD happened before – which


hardly can be proven by F/A in case of molten or burned  structures. However, looking into statistics of next level F/A – on subsystem or system level – it turns out that a wide variety  of other – frequently unconsidered - problems may cause EOS. These major root causes are the subject this paper deals with. Furthermore, we have to keep in mind the definition of EOS. If  we look to fig. 3, taken by courtesy of Christoph Thienel from


[1], this definition becomes very clear. For every component, we have a specification. Within this specification, the function  is guaranteed. The upper limits of the specification are given  by the so-called “absolute maximum ratings”.  If the device is  operated above the specification (even if this happens only for


Fig. 3: Operation ranges of a microelectronic device; during  in-spec operation, the device may suffer from pulses out of  spec (EOS). Since most of the pulse amplitudes happen within  the (non-specified, above maximum ratings!) area, the device  survives this treatment for long time. If, however, a pulse  appears beyond the destruction limit (right tail of the  distribution) immediate destruction of the device happens. Courtesy of C. Thienel, Bosch Germany [1]


a short periods of time) we are in a region, which we call “robustness”. This means that the construction of the device  would allow it to survive at least a certain time also an  operation within a certain range above specifications without  suffering irreversible damage. However, no guarantee can be  given when operating in this region. If we leave the zone of  robustness towards further stressful operation conditions, we  reach an EOS level where the device must fail. In daily  practice, frequently devices are operated for short times “on  risk” already within the robustness zone. For instance, if the  DC supply includes ripple pulses at significantly higher  voltage, the device is operated beyond the absolute maximum


ratings but within the robustness zone. The distribution of  these ripple pulses can (for instance) be in a manner as shown  in fig. 3. As long as only some pulses appear within the robustness zone, the device usually might not fail. If, however, just  few very high-level-pulses of the right end of the distribution – which already leaves the robustness zone - appear at the Vdd  pin of the device, it will instantly be killed due to EOS.


Investigations on EOS failure root causes


Overview


Long-term failure analysis on device as well as on system level  worked out the following main root causes:


• Insufficiently protected inductive loads in  semiconductor circuitry


• Cross talk-effects in long wiring setups like light  railway vehicles or wind power plants


• Sudden power interrupts or power instability


• Return current effects in case of complex power  supply situations


• Electrical backlash at interfaces between power- and  microelectronic circuitry.


• Thunderstorm lightning


Further EOS root causes are listed in detail within an excellent  new publication [2] of K. Kaschani and R. Gaertner.


“Hidden” inductive loads


Many components include “unknown” inductive loads. Typical  examples are DC-DC converters, which are frequently used in  PCBs, but also PCB-layouts, transformers, R-C-circuitries, filters and on-board-relays act as inductive sources. Voltage  spike responses of inductive loads on any kind of current  change may generate ESD-like-looking damage. However, the  basic difference to nearly all kinds of standardized ESD testing  is that ESD tests are applied without device operational  voltages whilst inductive responses appear under regular  operational conditions.


Circuitry Deficiencies


Let’s have a look –based on the previous list- on the most  frequent EOS-causing circuit problems found on system level. At first we need to understand the function of a step-upchopper as shown in fig 4. This basic circuitry is a key element


Fig. 4: Setup chopper: When S opens, the coil L reacts with a  high voltage pulse, which passes the diode and charges C. If  this happens in repetition, Ua becomes significantly higher  than Ue. Image by Wikipedia


in modern power electronics – but often it is unintentionally a  part of the circuitry in microelectronic PCBs. A capacitor is


charged via an inductance and a serial diode. At the common  point of inductance and diode, a switching element (which is  usually a thyristor or transistor) draws the voltage potential  down to mass potential in short pulses. When this switch is onstate, a maximum of current is drawn through the coil. When  the switch is now opened, the inductance tries to keep its  magnetic condition and thus generates a high voltage response  pulse. This pulse passes the diode and thereafter charges the  capacitor towards a potential which by the time becomes  significantly higher than the input voltage. A discharge of the  capacitor via coil cannot happen due to the reverse blocking  characteristics of the diode. Unfortunately, many protective  circuitries, dedicated to block RF pulses from Vdd or other  pins, are designed in a rather equivalent manner (but other  intention). Let us look at fig 5, showing a typical RF blocking  circuitry of an integrated circuit: The Vdd voltage is guided  through a coil with a capacitor to ground at the end. The lowFig. 5: Typical Vdd protection of an IC; The LC-combination  acts as a low frequency pass filter, blocking spikes and highfrequency pulses to prevent IC damage.


pass-filter characteristic of this circuitry acts as a useful barrier  against RF-pulses getting into the device. If, however, the  components which are still missing for a step-up-chopper, follow device-internally, later EOS is already preprogrammed. Many devices strongly vary in power  consumption, for instance according to internal clock  frequencies or externally driven circuitry. These load (and thus  current-) variations may replace the boost switch by changing  the current through the coil. Finally, this scenario produces  chip-internally high voltage which never has been intended to  be there in the design phase and resulting in EOS.


Another example can be observed frequently in DC-DCconverters. Many circuit designers do not know that in these  converters also inductances are integrated but not expressively  described in the datasheets. A combination of internal  inductances with an external coil may also lift voltage pulses  towards critical levels. Fig. 6 shows such an example. However, in this case, the victim was neither an IC nor the  DC-DC-converter but the first in a parallel battery of  stabilizing tantalum capacitors at the voltage input. The  converter-internal regulation circuitry in combination with  variable output loads generated a high gradient of input current  variations. These resulted in generating voltage spikes in the  coils – and – as the designer should know – killed the tantalum  electrolytic capacitor, see fig. 7. Generally, tantalum electroly-


Fig. 6: Load variations in the DCDC converter (right) induced voltage pulses by the coil (top center), which  destroyed the first tantalum capacitor (yellow indicated)


Fig. 7: Burned Tantalum electrolytic capacitor due to an EOS


Fig. 8: output part (not complete drawing) of a DCDC  converter. Since the center GND was resistively connected to  the coil center (yellow), the GND potential varied, depending  from the load. Asymmetric load lifted the unloaded voltage  side towards higher voltage output and destroyed the  capacitor.


tic capacitors must not be used in applications with a high  pulse level due to construction issues [3]. Beyond this, the  example also shows the consequences of economic forces in  microelectronics: In former times, a useful derating of course  was self-evident. If today a voltage of 12V is applied, the  capacitor in use will not be a 40V-type but maybe in the best  case a 16V-type – which sometimes in reality makes not more  than 15V but has been sportively rated to 16V by competitive  and economic reasons.


Fig. 12: “Flying” setup of an induction coil (grey box), the  spark gap (top) and the coupling loop with the DUT (left).


With this setup, we succeeded to provoke failures on RFID  devices and we could heal failures which have healed  themselves by thermal treatment on PCBs of automotive  applications.


Similar like on ESD-failures with recovery behavior [6], activating and deactivating mechanisms have been observed in  case of EDD.


Using Marx- or Tesla generators, the test setup was exposed to  the nearby operation of such generators without any direct  coupling (fig. 13 and 14). Considering these indirect stressing  approaches, it  is  highly  recommended to  use simple lead


Fig. 13 (left): Marx voltage pulse generator


Fig. 14 (right): Tesla coil in use for EDD tests


accumulators as power source and to use simple, analog meters  for voltage/ current control and for signal monitoring –if  available- an old, valve-driven oscilloscope. Using highlysophisticated, sensitive peripheral electronics as used in  digitally regulated power supplies, digital multimeters etc. includes the risk that their electronics become part of the  circuitry under test and probably might suffer EOS damage, too.


Another, more reproducible approach is the inductive or  capacitive coupling of TLP pulses from a TLP tester into a  PCB by means of a coil (or capacitive) antenna which has been  adapted in size to critical PCB line layouts, which could act as  an antenna (fig. 15).


While the Ruhmkorff induction coil is useful for a general  robustness evaluation (this test, however, is unspecific and  very broad-banded), the TLP-based coupling is dedicated to  specifically supposed pulse coupling mechanisms, to which it  can be adapted.


Fig. 15: unprotected signal path on a PCB, mounted in  neighborhood to high-current-cables. Yellow lines are located  on the front side, blue lines on the back side of the PCB. The  wiring acted as a cross-talk antenna to receive destructive  pulses in case of current-gradient changes in the cables.


Experimental results of tests


At Empa, we made EDD experiments on several control units  and PCBs which have shown a significant amount of field  failures. With our experiments we could successfully  reproduce the observed field failures on PCB level and  partially heal failures on field return PCBs, too.


In the first step, a “conventional” failure analysis started on  PCB level. After the failed device on the PCB has been  identified, it has been decapsulated (whilst remaining on the  PCB). In order to avoid peripheral damage on the PCB by the  decapsulation acid, a silicone guard ring was deposited around


the integrated circuit. After decapsulation, the PCB was  operated under normal condition and internal failure  localization has been made by liquid crystal analysis and  emission microscopy (fig. 16). A comparison with new “golden” PCBs as references ensured, that the emission  microscopy signal really originated from the failure. The EDD  experiments were regarded as successful, when 1. The new  PCB behavior showed the same failure functions as the field  returns and 2. The same integrated circuit on the PCB failed  and 3. After decapsulation, the same signal has been observed


Fig. 16: Emission microscopy image of the damaged device. Exactly the same spot could be reproduced by EDD testing  using a Ruhmkorff coil on a healthy device


by liquid crystal thermography or emission microscopy. Due to  confidentiality reasons, the details of EDD experiment  conclusions cannot be published, however, we can summarize  these results into three groups of device level, PCB level and  system level: The main topics of device level improvements  are to strengthen latch up withstandability (implant/ geometry  design) and to increase the speed and energy absorbing  capability of pad protective structures. On PCB level the  highlights are to avoid tantalum capacitors to absorb voltage  peaks, to use ceramic capacitors only strongly accordingly to  PCB layout rules [7] and to embed critical signal lines into  grounded PCB neighborhood to shield them and to keep them  as short as possible [8].. Additional active and passive  protection circuitry might be needed in order to absorb spike  energy. Capacitors and other components should be usefully  derated in terms of duty cycles, voltage and current maximum  ratings. LEDs and optocoupling transmitters need specific  reverse bias protection like serial diodes. On system level, the  wiring needs to be revised in terms of cross coupling paths, electrical backlash, shielding, arcing and EMC interference. For failure analysis including all three levels, a sound  anamnesis of application, failure-history and -statistics and  fabrication-specific aspects become more and more a  mandatory part for a conclusive F/A on device level.


EOS failures are primarily caused by electrodynamic events  like EDD. In many cases these events result from hidden or  ignored inductances within the PCB or system circuitry. ESDinduced EOS exists, too, but, compared to EDD, it is only a  minor issue in the one-digit-percentile-order-of-magnitude. In  its first part, the paper discussed the variety of EOS failure  textures and the main root causes.


EOS-related stresses leave the absolute maximum ratings of a  device and, thus, are not useful for “pass” od “fail” tests. However, as a part of failure analysis beyond the scope of  device level, they serve to help identifying external root causes  by provoking identical EOS failure textures as observed in the  field. EDD-related stress tests and experimental results and  conclusions were summarized in a general manner considering  the levels “device”, “PCB” and “system”.


The author wants to acknowledge Horst Gieser and Henry  Wolf from Fraunhofer Institute EMFT Munich for their  valuable experimental support when combining EDD  experiments with TLP testing setups. Christoph Thienel from  Robert Bosch Reutlingen earns a big “Thank You” for various  valuable discussions and inputs on the EOS topic, fig. 3  originates from one of his presentations on EOS.


Last, not least, sincere thanks are given to my colleagues  Giovanni Nicoletti and Samuel Gloor from Empa for their  valuable analytical work and to Bobby (Albert) Kunz and  Willy Knecht for the technical support in many electrical  experimental setups.

[2] Kaschani K.T., Gärtner, R. „A Methodology to deal with  Electrical Overstress in the Design, Handling and  Application of Integrated Circuits“, pp. 67-74  Tagungsband 12. ESD Forum 2011 Munich, ISBN 978-3-9813357-1-5

[7] Vogel, Gert, Ausfallanalysen an Keramikkondensatoren. http://www.home.vrweb.de/gert.vogel/dokumente/ausfalla nalysen_an_keramikkondensatoren.pdf (visited June  2011, German language only)


[8] Todd Hubbing,  “PCB EMC  Design  Guidelines:  A Brief  Annotated List”, 0-7803-7835-WO31117.OO 0 2003


IEEE, see:

DRAM Cell Fault Localization using Passive Voltage Contrast


San Lin Liew, Yu Pei Wei, Bi Jen Chen, Hua Sheng Chen, Jian Shing Luo


Physical Failure Analysis Department, Inotera Memories Inc., Taoyuan, Taiwan, Republic of China  Email: slliew@inotera.com


This paper presents the memory cell level passive voltage  contrast (PVC) involving diode, capacitor and transistor  devices in a (dynamic random access) DRAM chip. More  particularly, we show that the voltage contrast sensitivity can  be improved significantly by the adjustment of scan location  and scan location sequence. Both leaky and resistive fault  localizations by PVC imaging are presented to illustrate our  point.


The memory on a DRAM chip consists of thousands of  homogenous rectangular memory arrays. In each array, wordlines and bitlines (also regarded as rows and columns, respectively) are arranged perpendicular to each other. A  memory cell, which is capable of storing 1 bit, either “zero” or “one”, is located at the intersection of each wordline and  bitline. The circuitry of a memory cell is rather simple, comprised of only one transistor and one capacitor per bit as  shown in Fig. 1. Driven by both competitive cost reduction  and performance factors, the production of DRAM has  continuously evolved toward the scaling down of feature size  in device dimension while at the same time offering increased  functionality and chip size. Nowadays, each DRAM memory  chip is typically neatly packed with billions of miniature  memory cells. Concomitantly, locating the exact fault site has  become an increasingly challenging task which is often  accompanied with lengthy root cause identification turnaround  time.


One of the most widely used failure localization techniques in  the semiconductor failure analysis community is scanning  electron microscope (SEM) based PVC imaging, which was  first published by Jim Colvin in 1990 [1]. This technique is  also regarded as a probe-less voltage contrast technique as the  device under test (DUT) is not supplied with any external  power supply or signal sources [2]. The principles and  applications of PVC are well documented, with most of the  works concentrated on contact (poly gate, n+ diffusion and p+ diffusion contacts) and metal line levels PVC [3-5]. In parallel, several works have progressed on the enhancement of voltage  contrast signal by the adjustment of parameters such as scan  direction and primary beam settings [6-10].


This paper focuses on the application of memory cell level  PVC on our DRAM product. Equally important to note, a new  voltage contrast improvement methodology involving scan  location is described. More specifically, we show that by  scanning a specific location on the sample prior to the actual  PVC scanning of the defects of interest, the voltage contrast  signals can be improved significantly. This methodology is  particularly useful when dealing with resistive cells. Complementarily, we show that the same specific location  should be avoided from the beam scan if leaky cells are of  interest.


To illustrate our point; firstly, localization of leaky memory  cells by conventional PVC is presented. Then, isolation of  resistive memory cells employing our new methodology is  presented and contrasted with the result obtained without  utilizing the new method. The physics behind the techniques  used is described. Lastly, best practice recommendations on  scan location sequence are given.


Figure 1: Circuit diagram of a single memory cell. The  capacitor stores charge, indicating a 0 or 1. The transistor  acts as a switch enabling charge to flow into or out of the  capacitor. The wordline provides voltage to turn on/off the  transistor. The bitline functions as a path for data to flow  in/out of the capacitor.


Experimental Details


A simplified physical structure of a memory cell is shown in  Fig. 2.  In our work, memory cell level PVC samples were  prepared by parallel lapping down to an arbitrary capacitor  level as indicated in Fig. 2, leaving a finished surface exposing  the neatly arranged capacitors. All the PVC images shown in  this paper were acquired using a Hitachi S-4800 operated at  1kV accelerating voltage and at zero degree tilt unless  specified. As the PVC term implied, no external bias of the  specimen other than the grounding of the samples through the  stage was applied.


Figure 2: A simplified memory cell structure comprised of a  capacitor and an NMOS transistor. In this work, samples were  parallel polished to an arbitrary capacitor level as indicated  by the dashed line.


Case 1: Isolation of leaky cells by directly scanning onto  the location of interest


Figure 3 shows part of a memory array which consists of  thousands of memory cells. A leaky cell was easily visualized  and shown as a bright contrast spot as indicated by the red  arrow. The physics involved is explained as follows. When the  capacitor cell level sample was impinged with a 1kV electron  beam, the number of secondary electrons emitted exceeded the  primary electrons incident on the surface of the sample, thereby creating a positive potential sample surface. As shown  in Fig. 2, underneath the capacitor cell was an n+ capacitor  contact, followed by an n+ diffusion in p-well. The n+ diffusion active region and the p-well formed a reverse biased  diode, which effectively inhibited the accumulated positive  charge in the capacitor drained off to the substrate. This  resulted in most secondary electrons being attracted back to  the sample, allowing very few electrons to reach the electron  detector. Therefore, the good memory cells exhibited a dark  contrast. On the contrary, leaky memory cells were able to  discharge through a leakage path to the substrate, consequently  allowing more secondary electrons to escape from the sample  surface and reach the electron detector. This explains why the  leaky memory cell shown in Fig. 3 has a bright capacitor  contrast. Note that this isolation of leaky cells is only feasible  if the periphery region is not scanned beforehand.


Figure 3: Top-down PVC image showing thousands of stacked  capacitors in a memory array. The leaky capacitor can be  easily identified as a bright contrast spot as indicated by the  red arrow.


Figure 4a shows an enlarged image of another single bit leaky  capacitor localized by PVC imaging in a real case application. Subsequent top-down mechanical polishing revealed the  presence of a capacitor contact to bitline short (Fig. 4b) caused  by an excessive etching.


Figure 4(a):  Enlarged top-down PVC image showing the  leaky capacitor in bright contrast.  (b) SEM image showing a  bitline to capacitor contact short as indicated by the red  arrow.


Case 2: Isolation of resistive cells by scanning the  periphery region prior to the location of interest


Under conventional PVC, a resistive memory cell will exhibit  a dark contrast undifferentiated from the good memory cell. Therefore, good and resistive memory cells are  indistinguishable.


As illustrated earlier, good memory cells exhibited a dark  contrast because the capacitors were charged up. However, this was only true when the transistors were in the off state. If  the transistors were turned on, the charge in the capacitors  would be “dumped” or drained to the periphery circuits or  more specifically, sense amplifiers through the bitlines. The  charge flowing path associated with a switched-on transistor is  illustrated in Fig. 5. Indeed, this is also a typical phenomenon


which occurred during the DRAM read operation. In this  work, we employed this understanding on our memory cell  device to localize resistive cells.


To turn on the transistors, a beam of electrons was scanned  onto the periphery region’s interlevel contacts which have  electrical connections to the underlying wordlines. Within  several seconds, the active memory cells (as opposed to the  dummy memory cells) on both sides of the scanned periphery  region (commonly called the wordline stitch region) exhibited  bright contrast, as shown in Fig. 6. The resistive memory cells  can now be easily distinguished from the good ones, as  evidenced in Figs. 7 and 8.


Figure 5: When the transistor is switched on, the charge in the  capacitor is able to drain off through the bitline to the sense  amplifier in the periphery area. The charge flowing path is  indicated by the green arrows.


Figure 6: SEM PVC image showing good memory cells in  bright contrast, a phenomenon observed after the contacts in  the wordline stitch region were rastered with electron beam.


Figure 7: PVC image with strong voltage contrast signals  showing the resistive memory cell in dark capacitor contrast.


Figure 8: PVC image showing capacitor contrast on two  memory arrays. The transistors on the left and right memory  arrays were in the off and on states, respectively. Notice that  the resistive memory cells at the bottom right can be easily  distinguished from the good ones with the transistors   switched on.


Interestingly, depending on the fault type (either a leaky or  resistive memory cell) and suspected fault location, the most  appropriate contrast formation or pattern can be generated to  best localize the fault. For instance, if only the odd/even row  memory cells are of interest, one can choose to switch on the  odd/even row transistors only, as shown in Fig. 9. This can be  done by careful navigation of the SEM electron beam to the  specific contacts in the wordline stitch region. On the other  hand, if only the odd/even column memory cells are of  interest, it is important not to raster scan the associated  capacitors of interest before the actual PVC image acquisition  takes place. In addition, prior to scanning the wordline stitch


region which would effectively turn on the transistors, the  sense amplifier area should be scanned beforehand. By doing  this, the scanned contacts in the sense amplifier will supply a  voltage to the bitlines. Later, when the transistors are switched  on (by scanning the wordline stitch region), charge in the  bitlines will flow past the transistor gate channel and reach the  capacitors, as illustrated in Fig. 10. Then, as the associated  capacitors of interest are scanned, one would be able to see  that only the “pre-charged” odd/even column capacitors show  a bright contrast, as illustrated in Fig. 11.


Figure 9: PVC images showing capacitor contrast pattern  obtained when only the even row transistors were switched on.


Figure 10: Schematic diagram showing the charge flowing  path from the bitline to the capacitor. This phenomenon  occurred if the sense amplifier was scanned prior to the  switching-on of the transistors.


Figure 11: PVC images showing capacitor contrast pattern  obtained when only the odd column transistors were switched  on.


Figure 12 shows a single bit resistive capacitor localized using  this alternate turn-on-transistor PVC technique in a real case  application. In another memory cell single bit case, an  anomaly at the capacitor contact bottom caused by insufficient  pre-deposition cleaning was revealed by wet chemical  deprocessing (Fig. 13) after the resistive memory cell was  isolated with this PVC technique.


Figure 12: Enlarged top-down PVC image showing the resistive  capacitor in dark contrast.


Figure 13: Tilted SEM image showing capacitor contacts after  removal of surrounding oxide by wet chemical processing. The toppling contact suggested the presence of an anomaly  below the problematic capacitor contact, which in turn  resulted in a high resistance issue.


It is worth noting that after the transistors were switched on, the transistors remained in the on state for days until the  electrical field in the transistors finally dissipated.    Consequently, when dealing with an unknown fault type, it is  best to treat the fault as the leaky type during the initial fault  localization process; in other words, avoid scanning the  wordline stitch region until one has finished the leaky fault  isolation process.  Figs. 14a and 14b are used to illustrate this: on the same sample, if the odd row transistors were switched  on, one would not be able to recognize the odd row single bit  leaky transistor since all the odd row capacitors will show  identical bright contrast.


Figure 14a: Top-down PVC image showing the odd row leaky  capacitor in bright contrast as indicated by the red arrow. Note that only the even row transistors were switched on.


Figure 14b: One is unable to distinguish this leaky capacitor  with both even and odd row transistors turned on.


We have shown that electron beam scanning on the periphery  region has a large influence on the memory cell level voltage  contrast signals. Careful consideration of the scan location and  scan location sequence can significantly improve the voltage  contrast signals. We recommend as a best practice, avoid  scanning the periphery region during the initial fault  localization. Being able to conveniently recognize the faulty  capacitor, which is typically sitting among thousands of similar  capacitors has proved to be a powerful technique for our  DRAM products. Although only the memory cell level PVC  was covered in this work, we believe that this methodology  can also be implemented in other semiconductor integrated  circuits having similar circuitry design.


[1] J. Clovin, “A New Technique to Rapidly Identify Low  Level Gate Oxide Leakage in Field Effect  Semiconductor using a Scanning Electron Microscope ,” Proceedings of ISTFA 1990, pp. 331-336.


[2] R. Rosenkranz, “Failure Localization with Active and  Passive Voltage Contrast in FIB and SEM”, J. Mater. Sci: Mater. Electron, vol. 22 (2011), pp. 1523-1535.


[3] Z. Gemmill, L. Durbha, S. Jacobson, G. Gao and K. Weaver, “SEM and FIB Passive Voltage Contrast”, Microelectronics Failure Analysis Desk Reference Fifth  Edition 2004, pp. 431-437.

3D tomography analysis of dark voltage


contrast failure in PCRAM device


Jangwon Oh, Jonghyeop Kim, Taekwon Lee, Seungjoon Jeon,  Won Kim, Hojoung Kim, Changreol Kim


Research and Development Division, SK Hynix Semiconductor Inc., San 136-1,  Ami-ri, Bubal-eub, Ichon-si, Kyoungki-do 467-701, Korea


The failure analysis using transmission electron microscopy (TEM) has been actively preceded in semiconductor industry. But due to the overlap issue and structural complexity of  devices, it has become harder and harder to perform failure  analysis using normal projected bright field (BF) and high  angle annular dark field (HAADF) TEM images. To overcome  these problems, 3-dimensional (3D) tomography technique has  been suggested. In this work, we clarify the root cause of dark  voltage contrast (DVC) failure at the bottom electrode contact  region in PCRAM by using 3D tomography analysis. The 3D  tomography samples were prepared in lamella shape by using  focused ion beam (FIB). The electron energy loss  spectroscopy (EELS) and 3D tomography analysis in scanning  transmission electron microscope (STEM) HAADF mode  were carried out. Through 3D tomography image  reconstruction by AMIRA program, we observed ‘open  contact fail’ between the BEC (Bottom Electrode Contact)-1  and the BEC (Bottom Electrode Contact)-2 at DVC region that  could not be shown in 2D image.


Keywords: FIB, TEM, 3D tomography, semiconductor


Semiconductor devices have become smaller and more  complex for the tech shrinkage and the development of low  voltage operating device. Additionally, 3D stack structure has  been developed for the above same reasons. In general, to  investigate the fail caused in semiconductor process  development or device characterization, FIB and TEM has  been a very useful tool. However, conventional TEM analysis  has its limitations. The overlapping issue of complex structure  in 2D cross section TEM image makes it difficult to reveal the  root cause. Besides, preparing thin lamella becomes more  challenging at smaller technology node.


Therefore, 3D tomography technique which reconstructs the  3D image using multiple TEM images taken from a wide range  of angles has been briskly studied [1]. The 3D tomography  technique could be classified as pillar and lamella type by  specimen preparation process, and both techniques have its  own pros and cons.


In pillar type, the specimen for 3D tomography is prepared  to pillar shape using FIB for the particular holder which could  rotate 360°.  Main merit of this technique is that it does not  have missing edge due to the direct observation using this  rotatable holder. However, the specimen preparation process is  much more difficult due to its particular specimen shape (pillar


shape) and the longer time is needed for securing data than the  lamellar type. On the other hand, in lamella type, the specimen  is prepared to lamellar shape using FIB, and the observed  images at each tilt angle in TEM are reconstructed to the 3D  image. Although tilt angle of observed TEM image is limited  from -80° to 80° due to the mechanical limitation of TEM  normal holder, it is faster to achieve the result and it has larger  observable region than the pillar type. Additionally, for the  lamellar shape, it is possible to correlate with a TEM analysis, directly. In this study, the DVC region at the bottom electrode  contact region of phase change random access memory (PCRAM) after chemical mechanical polishing (CMP) was  observed, and then the lamella type TEM specimen of this  region was prepared via FIB. At last, the DVC fail mechanism  was identified by 3D tomography, which was reconstructed by  tilted HAADF STEM images.


In this study, the fail position which showed the DVC – (Figure 1) was observed using electron beam inspection (EBI). After marking the fail position, the lamella type TEM  specimen was prepared by FIB (Helios 400S_FEI Company) in-situ method [2]. Because the dimension of cell was too  small, to avoid overlapping of the cell images, the specimen  was tilted from -40° to 40° with 0.5° interval, and then, about  160 STEM HAADF images were observed by TEM with  accelerated voltage at 300 kV (TITAN80-300_FEI Company). Lastly, the 3D tomography was reconstructed with these  observed images by inspect 3D and AMIRA program [3].


Figure 1. EBI SEM image showing dark voltage contrast  failure in PCRAM BEC area of EBI SEM image


In general, the DVC fail at a BEC region was occurred by an  open contact fail or interface resistive issues at the BEC  region. The STEM HAADF image analysis at the interface  between BEC1 and BEC2 region (Figure 2) shows that the  direct cause of the DVC fail, as mentioned above, was not  clear because the TEM lamella was too thick to reflect these  issues. Although, at the BEC of the fail region, the area of  relatively dark TiN contrast between the BEC1 and the BEC2 (abnormal region in Figure 2) was observed, the Ti line  intensity profiles of the EELS line scan data did not show the  TiN contents differently. Figure 3 is EELS line profile results  of fail area. But it did not show any difference because the  void is smaller than the sample.


Therefore, to clarify the DVC fail, STEM 3D tomography  analysis via AMIRA program was carried out using the  observed STEM HAADF images. In Figure 4 and 5, the  interface between the BEC1 and BEC2 was not show at the  normal region. However, at the fail region, the reconstructed  3D images clearly show the 3D open contact feature between  the BEC-1 and the BEC-2 region. It means that the main cause  of DVC area at the BEC was open contact fail, and, the fail  mechanism was believed to be the under-etched BEC-2  contact. It is noted that 3D tomography analysis is powerful  technique to reveal the fail mechanism.


Figure 2. Cross sectional STEM HAADF image of dark  voltage contrast failure area


Figure 3. EELS line profile results of fail region

Figure 4.  3D reconstructed images of fail and normal region  by STEM 3D tomography at the TiN BEC-1 (a) top, (b) middle, and (c) bottom

In this study, using 3D tomography technique, we  investigated the DVC fail which was not clearly revealed by  2D projection TEM image analysis. The lamella type TEM  specimen prepared by FIB was observed using TEM at various  tilt angles, and the 3D tomography were reconstructed with the  observed image by inspect 3D and AMIRA. As a result, it  showed that there was ‘open contact fail’ between BEC1 and  BEC2 at DVC region.


That is, overlap issues which were caused by shrinkage and  complexity of semiconductor devices can be overcome by 3D  tomography technique and it has great potential for   semiconductor failure analysis.

Dual-phase Interferometric Confocal Imaging for Electrical Signal Modulation  Mapping in ICs


A. Yurt, E. Ramsay, F. H. Köklü, C. R. Stockbridge, Y. Lu, M. S. Ünlü and B. B. Goldberg


Boston University, Boston, MA USA


In-situ monitoring and mapping of electrical activity of  devices play important roles in test and fault analysis of  integrated circuits (ICs). As front-side access for probing is  limited due to the increased metallization and packaging, back-side laser techniques have been widely used to  investigate transient changes of charge density in the vicinity  of the devices [1, 2]. The presence of free-carriers alters the  local refractive index and thus influences the intensity of the  laser beam reflected from the device. Modulation  Mapping/Laser Voltage Imaging is an established technique  for imaging the carrier activity at a given frequency [3, 4]. In  this method, the reflected beam intensity is detected by a fast  photodetector and then the signal is filtered at a specific  frequency using a spectrum analyzer or lock-in amplifier [5, 6]. A spatial modulation map is formed by recording the  amplitude of the filtered signal while the laser beam is  scanned in a region of interest. The map can then be  investigated to identify the location of any unexpected  activity.


Advancing chip manufacturing technology has necessitated  more sensitive and faster signal acquisition due to the increase  in the circuit complexity and small feature size of the devices. Several interferometric probing methods have been  demonstrated to address these issues [7, 8]. Here, we  demonstrate a differential dual-phase interferometric imaging  method in which the reflected probe beam modulated weakly  by the charge carriers is mixed separately with two reference  beams with a known phase shift in between. Performing a  balanced detection scheme on these two channels allows us to  significantly reduce the common-mode-noise associated with  strong DC background and its noise. We systematically  compare the performance of the interferometric technique with  the conventional mapping method through investigating  inverter chains on two test chips of 180nm bulk silicon  technology and 32nm SOI technology.


Experimental Setup


In Fig. 1, the schematic shows the detection section of the  optical setup. A linearly polarized infra-red CW-laser beam (λ = 1340nm) is focused by an aplanatic solid immersion lens (aSIL) objective with a nominal numerical aperture of 3.18  through the silicon substrate (Pprobe = 0.4 mW - 0.25mW) onto  a region of interest in the sample and scanned by  galvanometer mirrors to form a two-dimensional image. At  each point, the reflected beam is collected by the same aSIL  objective and divided into two beams by a polarizing beam  splitter (PBS) and each beam is then coupled into single mode


fibers (SMF) that feed the differential inputs of an AC-coupled  balanced InGaAs - PIN photodetector. The reference beam is  transformed into a circularly polarized beam with a polarizer  and a quarter-wave-plate (QWP) after it is reflected from the  reference mirror (RM); then the reference beam (Pref =


0.3mW) is similarly split into two with a phase difference of π/2 by the polarizing beam splitter (PBS) before being coupled  into the same single mode fibers (SMF). The photo-voltage  generated on each detector can be expressed as:

where Ir, Ip(t) and θrp(t) refer to the detected optical intensity  of reference and reflected probe beams; and the phase angle  between the two beams, respectively. The differential output  of the balanced detector, VRF(t), retains only the contribution  of the interferometric terms:


Note that the amplitude of the signal is amplified by the  reference beam of which intensity can be adjusted  independently. Also, the differential detection allows us to  eliminate significantly the common-mode noise associated  with the large background intensity (first two terms in Eq. (1)). The differential output of the balanced detector is fed into  a lock-in amplifier in order to perform heterodyning to


decouple the amplitude of the signal (


) from the  contribution of the phase component (θrp(t)) at the frequency  of a local oscillator signal (fLO). In our measurements, the local  oscillator signal is provided by the test chips in order to obtain  the electronic phase maps in addition to the amplitude maps. When the phase maps are not of interest, the internal signal  generator of the lock-in amplifier is used to provide the local  oscillator.


Although it may not be critical in most applications, the signal  to noise ratio (SNR) of the DC component of the reflected  probe beam intensity that provides the topographical  information about the DUT can also be improved through the  interferometric mixing. Assuming Ir >> Ip, the DC component  can be obtained using the monitor-outputs of the balanced  detector:


where Vref refers to the voltage read on the detector that  monitors the reference beam intensity.

Figure 2a and Fig. 2b show the images of a part of an inverter  chain in a 180nm technology bulk silicon test-chip (DUT-1) recorded through the two monitor-outputs of the balanced  detector.


Figure 2: (a) and (b) The raw images of an inverter device  recorded by the monitor-outputs of the balanced detector. (c) Reconstructed image to obtain the amplitude image through  decoupling the phase contribution using Eq. (3). The length of  the scale bar is 2 microns.


The topographical features of the devices are barely visible  due to strong intensity fluctuations over the whole image due  to coupling of the phase (sine and cosine terms in (1)). Figure  2c demonstrates the amplitude image (Ip(DC)) of the devices  obtained through processing the images in Fig. 2a and Fig. 2b  according to Eq. (3). The phase coupling is dramatically  reduced and the topographic amplitude image of the devices is  recovered. The reconstructed image accuracy can be improved  by performing a more precise calibration of the detectors.


Figure 3: (a) A reflectance image of DUT-1 obtained without  the interferometric mode. (b) The carrier modulation map of  DUT-1 at 12.5Mhz using the interferometric mode. (c) The  carrier modulation of DUT-1 at 12.5Mhz without the  interferometric mode. The length of the scale bar is 5 microns.


Figure 3a shows a reflectance DC image of the three elements  of the inverter chains in the same test chip. Each element  contains four inverters built using different unit cells. At first, we studied the free-carrier modulation activity on the inverter  chains using our interferometric method. Figure 3b shows the  intensity map for the free-carrier modulation activity at


12.5Mhz i.e. the frequency of the input signal fed into the  inverter chains. The strong contrast of the modulation map  helped us to identify the location of the charge density  modulations accurately on the DUT-1.

Figure 4: (a) A reflectance image of DUT-2 obtained without  the interferometric mode. (b) The carrier modulation map of  the DUT-2 at 12.5Mhz (c) The phase map of DUT-2 at

In order to determine the improvement in detection sensitivity, we performed the measurement with the conventional  approach for which we simply blocked the reference arm and  one of the inputs of the balanced detector and adjusted the  laser power so that the comparison was performed at the same  probe beam power level. Figure 3c shows the amplitude image  of the modulation map obtained with non-interferometric  method. Although the signal was observable for relatively  larger structures, the SNR was not adequate to reach to a  conclusion about the proper operation of DUT-1. We note that  the interferometric mixing provided an SNR about an order of  magnitude better for this specific test-chip.


We then investigated a different test-chip of 32nm SOI  technology (DUT-2). Figure 4a shows a reflectance DC image  of the end section of the inverter chains labeled from “I1” to “I5”. The size of the transistors is identical in each chain and it  increases from “I1” to “I5”. As opposed to DUT-1, this chip  contains an insulating oxide layer underneath the active layer  therefore the total internal reflection and the aberrations due to  the silicon-silicondioxide interface reduces the effective NA of  our aSIL objective down to ~1.5. Despite the significant  reduction of NA, we observed a considerably large SNR for  the modulation amplitude on the inverter chains at nominal  operating conditions (Vdd = 0.9V) even without the  interferometric method. Figure 4b illustrates the amplitude  map at 12.5Mhz, i.e. the frequency of the input signals fed into  the inverters, without the interferometric mixing. The SNR is  sufficiently high to confirm the normal switching behavior on  all chains except “I2” which was deactivated in advance to  have a negative control unit in DUT-2. Note that the phase  map (Fig. 4c) illustrates the in-phase and out-of-phase  switching activity of the NMOS and PMOS transistors as well  as successive inverters in the chains with respect to the signal  at the output pin of the “I3” chains which was used as the local  oscillator signal for the lock-in amplifier.


Figure 5: Modulation map images of the first inverter in “I3” chain with interferometric method in (a) and without the  interferometric method in (b). (c) DC reflectance image of the  device. (d) The CAD layout of the device showing the metal-3  filling materials in the vicinity of the transistors. The scale bar  is 1 micron.


We compared the improvement in sensitivity obtained by  using the interferometric detection. Note that the laser power  was reduced about 60% with respect to the earlier  measurement on DUT-2 (Pprobe ≈ 0.25mW after the aSIL) in  order to avoid saturation of the lock-in amplifier in the  interferometric measurements. At the nominal operating  conditions, the interferometric method consistently provided  an SNR improvement about a factor of three. The image  distortion observed on the modulation maps is believed to be  a


I2


I1


I3


I4


I5


due to the filling structures underneath the devices. The  reflectance DC image of the devices for which the CAD  layout design is shown in Fig. 5d illustrates the presence of  these filling structures in the vicinity of the device layer and  their interference with the image quality (Fig. 5c).


Under the same experimental conditions, the modulation  amplitude was observed to be significantly higher in DUT-2 in  comparison with DUT-1 when the interferometric detection  was not employed (compare Fig.3c and Fig.4b). The common  path-interferometric effect due the presence of the oxide-box  underneath the device layer is believed to play an important  role in enhancing the modulation amplitude on the reflected  probe beam in DUT-2. Further experimental measurements on  this issue needs to be performed using laser beams at different  near-infrared wavelengths which will provide insight about the  effect of the insulating oxide box on the enhanced optical  response in SOI chips.


We demonstrated a dual-phase interferometric method that  provides a significant improvement in SNR over conventional  detection configuration based on monitoring the modulation  on the reflected probe beam intensity. We applied our  technique on inverter chains in the 180nm bulk-silicon and  32nm SOI technology test chips. The future work will focus  on adapting the method for GHz frequency regime and timedomain implementation of the method. The method will find  applications in failure analysis and testing of advanced  technology IC chips for which the high sensitivity in  modulation mapping is required.

In some DC-DC-converters we found internal circuitries like  simplified drawn in fig. 8. These bipolar converters sometimes  suffer from a kind of “smooth ground” on the output side (in  the drawing, this is symbolized by the resistor drawn in


yellow), which makes the ground level shifting, dependent  from the load. Especially when the load starts to become  asymmetrical, the overall output voltage maintains while the  ground level starts to shift. Thus, for instance a +/- 15V output  may convert towards a +10/ -20V output which becomes  critically for the internal or external output circuitry. Also in  these cases, frequently broken electrolytic capacitors are in the  foreground but also (secondary) failures on supplied integrated  circuits, mostly in the back coupling loop (not drawn in fig. 8).


Cross Talk and Electromagnetic Interference (EMI)


Another block of EOS failure sources show up under the  headline “cross talk”. It is mainly observed when long cable  installations end at sensitive PCBs. Specifically, we found  related failures in data bus lines of railway vehicles, trolley  buses and wind power plants. Besides the long wiring  installations, the common issue in these applications is a close  neighborhood between power- and microelectronic  components.  Frequently, specific protection circuitry of signal  lines is limited in order to avoid restrictions in transfer speed  and loss of signal levels. Sometimes, industry electronics in  big machineries, manufacturing lines or ships suffer from  comparable problems, too. The upcoming electrical- and  hybrid traction electronics certainly will afford further  analytical surprises in this direction in the future.


Another EOS failure cause – different, but sometimes with a  certain relation to cross talk - is what we call electromagnetic  interference (EMI). In such cases, it must be distinguished, whether the RF irradiates from outside or whether it is  generated inside the circuitry. Inside sources are frequently  high-speed processors of regular RF circuitry. EMI  interferences cover a wide variety of effects; to describe them  in detail would be beyond the scope of this article. However, the basic effect behind is the caption of RF by a resonant  conductive line (which could be a non-shielded piece of cable  or a PCB strip line), thus injecting current into parts of the  circuitry where it never should appear. This RF current is  rectified at the next diode or p/n junction and thereafter acts as  a DC failure current of non-negligible order-of-magnitude.


Cross-talk-similar effects may appear when power interrupts or  switching generate pulses to the circuitry involved or  neighbored. Electrical sectioning points of railway overhead  contact wires and power instabilities of windpower plants  generate significant backlash pulses to control electronics  PCBs, as well as such effects were observed in interfaces  between power- and microelectronic circuitry during power  switching events. Household power networks may further  suffer from power inductive loads like for instance vacuum  cleaners. The pulses sent from them back into the net may be  sufficient to destroy sensitive switching power supplies being  in standby mode.


Last but not least, a specific ESD category is given by  neighborhood lightning strikes. Their travelling waves and RF  high voltage components also generate significant risks for  both power- and signal circuitry.


EOS, however, is not only an issue of active components and  short pulses. EOS of course includes also those long-term-


+12V


-12V


C1 10µF/15V


C2 10µF/15V


effects that the abbreviation describes literally. It includes  molten plug connectors operated for too long time at too high  current, molten PCB-lines in high-current-application – all  those cases where either even elementary rules of derating in  circuitry design were violated or where sportive datasheets  were made above the component’s capabilities.


Slow and fast EOS, EDD


In principle, we discussed now the different causes of three  main categories of EOS:


The first EOS category shows effects which need a certain  time-of-operation before the device fails. Most of these effects  are thermally-induced, the device becomes overheated.


The second category is caused by EMI and can cause both  long- as well as short-term failures.


The third category summarizes short-term-EOS. These failures  are caused by high voltage- or current-pulses and usually cause  rapid failures. Since the root cause of such problems are found  in most cases in (unknown) inductance responses or cross talk  effects within the system circuitry, we named these fast, transient events EDD for Electro-Dynamic-Discharge. Within the fast EOS, EDD makes by far the major part  compared to ESD-induced, usually also fast EOS.
