EOS on LEDs


A specific attention must be given on LEDs, optocouplers and  other optoelectronic components. Usually, these devices are  not silicon based but use materials like GaAs, GaP or GaN etc. In opposite to silicon, these materials usually have a larger


intrinsic defect density, making them rather sensitive against  reverse-bias voltage pulses. Useful derating strategies and  circuitry prevention measures are of utmost importance for  their lifetime and reliability. As we can see in fig. 9, LEDs  usually don’t die abruptly but reduce their light emission  within short time of operation under stress or critical operation  conditions. The failure analysis by emission microscopy could  clearly highlight the dark spot as a consequence of reverse bias  leakage (Fig. 10). In order to avoid LED-EOS conditions, the  observation of current limitations as well as to avoid reverse  bias voltage spikes are mandatory. In some circuitries, the  latter would require serial diodes, acting as a peak protector.


Fig. 9: Degradation of a dark spot within an LED, showing  reverse bias leakage (However, this image shows the LED in  regular operation, where light emission appears). The  originally very small spot (left) degrades by superimposed  reverse bias pulses or ESD. Due to local high current density, the damage location extends itself and starts to bidirectional  bypass the LED; thus, the dark spot becomes more and more  extended (center and right image)


Fig. 10: Emission microscopy of the LED of fig. 9 at 3V in  reverse bias direction, before starting reverse pulse stress. The  pointer shows the small blue emission microscopy signal, indicating the current flow location of the reverse bias  leakage. Note that in this image, the LED shows as it looks in  the optical view, while in fig. 9 the metal contact  ring appears  dark due to the shadowing of the light emission. The failure  location – opposite to the metal line to the contact ring – can  be clearly recognized.


An example for such problem circuitry is shown in fig. 11. The  current path drawn in blue allows a reverse current flow  through unaddressed LEDs, which will increase by the time  due to its high current density in the LED.


Fig. 11: Section view of a matrix control circuitry of an LED  array. The yellow path indicates a regular operation current  flow. The blue path indicates a parasitic current flow, resulting from the circled LED’s reverse bias leakage. Serial  diodes would avoid stressing LEDs with reverse bias pulses.


Looking at optocoupler IC’s and glass fiber LED transmitters, a frequent problem are inverted duty cycles. For instance, if a  locomotive with optocouplers in the control electronics is in  parking position, the LEDs in the optocoupler should be offstate. However, due to “wrongly” inverted signals, these LEDs  are continuously in on-state, which may reduce their emission  within unexpectedly short time frames. As a consequence of  long-term EOS of optocoupler-LEDs, we also found the  internal transparent soft gel filling between LED and optoreceiver becoming “blind” – it becomes white and looses  lucency.


Sometimes, photoelectric barriers shall operate under bright  conditions and shall use small LEDs. The day light produces a  high level of noise background. The problem has been tried to  cope with pushing short high-current-pulses through the LED  instead of a continuous DC operation. It is a popular fallacy  that the question, whether the LED is overloaded or not, depends just from the thermal equilibrium. But again: since  intrinsic impurities are inherent to LEDs, these will be  widened due to the local high current density and finally the  LED will suffer a significant but unpredictable reduction in  lifetime.


Considering LEDs in general, last but not least it should be  mentioned at this place that they are very sensitive even


against low levels of ESD, which also promotes early aging  but no immediate failure behavior [4, 5].


EOS test approaches


Testing – Stressing – Damaging? A basic Consideration


With the upcoming switching power supplies in continuous  standby mode, the problems of disturbing spikes, superimposed to the electric power net came up already years  ago and lead to the development of useful standardized burst  and surge tests according to IEC 61000-4-4 and -4-5. However, these tests cannot be assigned to tests of PCBs, devices and other subsystems. As we have seen in the  description of EOS failure causes, the conditions are very  different and sometimes consist even of a superimposition of  different stress factors. Thus, the variety of EOS root causes  will never be covered by any standardized test procedure – even not on system level.


Keeping in mind figure 3, we have to accept, that any test  which would try to test a device on withstandability against  EOS, MUST work at the right end of the distribution and, thus, will cause the device under test (DUT) to fail under  circumstances, which would be far beyond any accepted  specification!


Therefore, the discussion on EOS testing appears somehow  like the cat hunting its tail. However, if we come back to  failure analysis, the goal of EOS testing starts to become  useful: If we observe a failure texture resulting from failure  analysis, and, if thereafter we are able to reproduce the same  texture and failure location on a known good device by means  of EOS testing, we are at least on the right way to understand  the electrical mechanism behind the failure. In most cases, EOS tests are related to EDD-type stress – which means in  principle that the device is operated (normally within a system) under normal operation conditions but exposed to  superimposed EDD pulses. Of course, also other EOS types  can be used for provocation, if EDD cannot reproduce the  failure texture. Since, however, EOS- and especially EDDexperiments must leave by definition the absolute maximum  ratings  of a device or a system, it would be better to talk about  EOS or EDD stress experiments instead of testing (since  testing usually is associated to a specification). The goal of  such stress experiments never can be “pass” or “fail” of  the DUT but it can be “field failure texture successfully  reproduced” (on a healthy device) or “failure texture could  not be reproduced” by the experiment. It is very important  to keep in mind that the conclusion of such experiments  usually also cannot be “the device is not robust enough” (since EDD is often above robustness limits) but it could be  e.g. “the device suffered EDD beyond robustness limits due  to EDD pulses being introduced through a non-protected  long conductive line on the PCB, acting as antenna”.


EDD-type stress experiments


We made experiments using a Ruhmkorff induction coil, a  Tesla coil, a Marx generator and a TLP (Transmission Line  Pulse) setup. All of these approaches have in common that no  galvanic coupling exists between the DUT and the disturbing


voltage. In case of the Ruhmkorff induction coil, the generated  pulse voltage (about 25kV) is exposed to a spark gap; in series  to this, a high-voltage-cable makes a loop around the DUT (fig. 12). The coupling of the disturbance can widely be varied  by the placing of the DUT within the loop. The DUT is under  normal operational conditions. The setup allows to provoke (or  re-cover) known failures from field returns with a suspicion  towards EDD – in some cases even several times.


Figure 11: LVI mapping on data signal at 1V Pattern: PASS


On the figure 9, when the pattern is passing, the LVI mapping  demonstrates that the data activity is identical on all flip flops  of the region of interest (blue circle). Now we will decrease  the power supply value in order to map the data signal when  the pattern is failing.


Figure 12: LVI mapping on data at 0.9V Pattern: FAIL


On the figure 12, we performed the same acquisition in a  FAIL condition. We can observe that after the FF123, data  signal is not present anymore. That means that the data signal  is not going properly through FF123 in that failing condition. On the figure 13, we focus on the failing area after the FF123.


Figure 13: LVI mapping on data at 0.9V (Zoom on the failing  area):  Frequency: 5MHz; Pattern: FAIL


On the figure 13, we are in failing mode (0.9V) we can see  that the data signal is stopped at FF123. We observe that data  is captured properly by the FF123 (LVI signature on the right  side: Input (blue circle)), but no LVI signature is visible at the  output of FF123 (left side of FF). So, at this step we can be  sure the failure is located inside the FF123. The data signal is  stopped when we decrease the power supply under 920 mV.


Now, the goal is to demonstrate that the failure is a timing  degradation. To make this study we will use the 2nd harmonic  mapping in order to map the degradation. The results are  presented on the figure 14.


Figure 14: LVI 2nd harmonic mapping on data signal at


0.96V:  Pattern: limit PASS/FAIL


On the figure 14, the pattern is in limit pass/fail (0.96V) and   the frequency of the data signal is decreased in order to   distinguish  the second harmonic (5MHz) to the clock signal (10MHz) We note that a single 2nd harmonic LVI spot is  present inside FF 123. This spot highlighted the timing  degradation on the NMOS of the NOR gate inside the FF123. Now, we will use the LVP technique to check the signal  integrity on the NMOS and PMOS. The results are presented  on the figure 15.


Figure 15: LVP probing on NMOS and PMOS


To confirm the timing degradation, on the figure 15 we have  measured the PMOS and NMOS transistors from 0.92V (fail) to 1.2V (pass) at a frequency of 10MHz. The 0.92V  measurement is compared with a good flip flop (FF122). Thanks to the LVP and 2nd harmonic techniques we can  localize the failure at PMOS or NMOS level of the NOR. Now, we can go to the physical analysis with this localization.


Figure 16: Physical analysis on the suspected transistors


On the figure 16, the TEM analysis is presented, no  morphological defect has been observed in area of interest  but  a partially  missing TiN has been observed near (in a poly line  with no active below in this area).


In this paper we have demonstrated that it was possible to  localize timing defects using the LVI mapping. Thanks to the  combination of the LVI mapping and 2nd harmonic mapping  on the 28 nm  case study, we have been capable of identifying


a timing defect on the degradation of the duty cycle or a  timing degradation on a transistor. Then we used the LVP  technique in order to confirm the fail hypothesis. This new  methodology is very effective and efficient for isolating  certain timing violation.

Improving the DLS Workflow


Dan Bodoh and Kent Erington


Freescale Semiconductor, Austin Texas, USA


In this paper, we describe improved hardware to connect a  semiconductor tester or applications board to a laser scanning  microscope (LSM) for performing dynamic laser stimulation (DLS).  The hardware, called DXGlue, simplifies the DLS  workflow and enables new applications.  We describe its  precise monitoring of the fail rate and fail mode, its use for  time resolved DLS and the enabling of long test loops with  short laser dwell times.


The typical setup for Dynamic Laser Stimulation (DLS) uses a  simple latch to sample and hold a fail signal from the tester for  the subsequent test loop or for the dwell time of a pixel [1-4].   Other methods of creating a fail status for a test loop have  been reported in the literature.  In [5], the authors use the  change in the duty cycle of a digital output as fail signal for  the LSM.  In [6], additional hardware is used to resolve a fail  signal from a fast complex test for high speed DRAMs.  In all  cases, the generated fail signal is presented to the LSM  acquisition board to display as either a white or black pixel  indicating a failing test or a passing test as the laser  illuminated the die area indicated by the pixel.


We have designed and built an enhanced fail latch, DXGlue, and associated software, to replace the simple DLS latch and  improve the DLS workflow.  This paper will discuss the  functionality of DXGlue and the DLS applications that it  enables.


DXGlue Hardware


As shown in Figure 1, DXGlue interfaces to the tester, to the  laser scanning microscope (LSM) PC, and to the LSM itself.  The tester, which can be a production tester or an applications  board, creates an ephemeral rawfail signal indicating that a  failure has occurred.  The tester also indicates the beginning of  each test loop by asserting trigger.       DXGlue processes both  trigger and rawfail to create its outputs.


Two of the DXGlue outputs are sent to the LSM PC:  the  failflag is held for an entire test loop, and a timeout, explained  below.    DXGlue also controls the laser of the LSM with the  laser control signal as described below.


Figure 1.  This schematic details the connections that DXGlue  makes to the components of a DLS setup.


DXGlue connects bidirectionally to an application on the PC  with USB.  It transfers one loop status packet to the PC for  each iteration of the test loop, and receives commands from  the DXGlue application.  The loop status packet contains the  following statistics measured from the previous test loop:


1. An 8-bit packet type identifier


2. An 8-bit fail count containing of the number of  assertions of rawfail,.


3. An 8-bit fail signature describing the fail mode,


4. A 24-bit loop length containing the number of  DXGlue 84 MHz clock ticks between assertions of  trigger,


5. An optional 24-bit laserFiredAt field for time  resolved DLS


The loop status packet is transferred over the USB emulating a  serial COM port set at 921600 baud., so one packet without  the laserFiredAt field transfers in 52µs.  If the test loop length  is shorter than 52µs, some loop results will not be reported to  the application.


Due to the buffering and latency in the USB connection, the  loop status packet is not synchronous to failflag that is used  for imaging.  The loop status packet is used to gather  statistical data, rather than DLS image data.


The DXGlue logic is implemented with an inexpensive  Complex Programmable Logic Device (CPLD) development  board, specifically the Lattice MachXO2 Pico Development  Kit.  The firmware that runs on the CPLD is built with Verilog  RTL.  Custom versions of the firmware can be built and  uploaded to interface a variety of testers and applications  boards to various LSMs with no physical modifications to the  hardware necessary.


DXGlue synchronizes rawfail and trigger to an internal 84  MHz clock which controls the rest of the logic.  The first two  clock cycles after the trigger assertion start the transfer of the  loop status packet over USB, update the control signals for the  LSM, and clear the registers to prepare for gathering statistics  and controlling the laser during the next test loop.  Subsequently, loop status statistics are accumulated until the  next trigger assertion.


One of the goals of this project was to simplify the setup of a  fail flag latch.  Many latch designs require a strobe  synchronized to the expected time of the fail signal from the  tester.  Timing the strobe signal can be complicated with  applications board setups or with testers that send out a fail  signal an indeterminate time after the actual failing vector.  DXGlue, instead, interprets any rising edge of rawfail while  trigger is low as an indication that at least one fail has  occurred during the loop.   This timing model can be used  even with a strobed fail signal, by strobing a low value when  the fail is expected. When this flexible timing model doesn’t  work, a custom DXGlue firmware can be created to fit the  constraints of the test setup.


DXGlue Software


The DXGlue software which runs on the LSM PC can either  be integrated into custom LSM imaging software or be a  standalone application running alongside commercial LSM  imaging software.  The standalone application, shown in  Figure 2 does not support all the features of the integrated  application but still offers significant improvement in DLS  usage.


The software reads the loop status packets from the USB, accumulating and displaying the data as described below.  The  integrated software can use DXGlue to improve DLS imaging  and control the laser for time resolved DLS.


DXGlue Applications


Basic Fail Flag


DXGlue generates the fail flag that is routed to the LSM  acquisition hardware to create the DLS image.    When a test  cycle fails, rawfail pulses and DXGlue will then present a high  on failflag starting at the next trigger.  The failflag stays high  for the entire test loop.  Failflag is presented to our  customized LSM software as a one bit digital signal (where  logic high value indicates a fail).  Many LSM systems simply  digitize the voltage level of the DLS input, which creates a


grey pixel level in the image that is dependent on the high  logic level of the fail flag.  The custom software on our LSM  displays a high on the fail input as absolute white (maximum  brightness), and a low as absolute black (minimum brightness) creating maximum contrast regardless of the voltage levels of  the input signal.


Fail Flag Timeout


The LSM used in our lab does not offer an external pixel clock  driver.  Its longest dwell time of 420µs is too short for many  test loops.  Figure 3 illustrates the result:  DLS spots become  stretched.  The spots and the background smear together, reducing sensitivity and resolution.


Figure 3.  This averaged, magnified DLS image illustrates the  smearing of a DLS spot when the test loop is longer than the  pixel dwell time.  The test loop period was 4.4ms and the pixel  dwell time was 420µs, so each test loop is stretched to about  10 pixels.  This image is 65 pixels wide by 43 pixels high.  The  scan occurs in the horizontal direction.


To prevent the smearing, a second digital signal from  DXGlue, timeout, is presented to the LSM PC indicating that  the test loop has exceeded 420µs.  This signal is normally low, Figure 2.  A screenshot of the standalone DXGlue application.


The gauge on the top left shows the instantaneous fail rate, while the strip chart to the right displays the historical fail  rate.  Under the gauge are the current fail count, fail  signature and loop length.


but transitions high 420µs after trigger.  Our custom LSM  software samples both failflag and timeout at each pixel.  No  data is recorded for any pixel in which timeout is high, so  exactly one pixel of data is recorded for each test loop. Although the image may be shifted in the scan direction, it is  not smeared.   As shown in Figure 4, a sparse image is created  in the first few frames.  The image gradually fills in during  subsequent frames.  At the beginning of each new frame in an  averaged image, the LSM software evaluates the number of  hits for each pixel and may restart the scan to balance the  number of hits across all pixels in the averaged frame.


Figure 5 is an example of an image taken using the timeout  feature of DXGlue with a 3.6ms test loop length at the 420µs  dwell time.  Although no streaking is evident, the sites are  shifted in the scan direction relative to the reflected image and  the amount of shift can be used to determine the DLS  interaction time, as described in [7].


Although most LSMs available today have a driver for pixel  clock so that the laser dwell time can be synchronized with the  test loop, this timeout feature of DXGlue can be beneficial.  To perform a fast survey of the entire die when the test loop is  very long, the dwell time can be set faster than the test loop.   Setting a timeout value equal to the fast dwell time will allow  large, strong DLS spots to be identified after one or two fast  scans without smearing.


Figure 4.  This is a magnified DLS image that just started its  second scan using the timeout feature of DXGlue.  Each  square is one pixel of the DLS image.  The maximum LSM  dwell time of 420µs is much smaller than the loop length of


1.7ms, so each test loop consumes 4 pixels.  However, only  one pixel of data is actually recorded for each test loop.  A  white pixel indicates a loop completed and failed, black  indicates a loop completed and passed, and orange indicates  that a loop has not completed and no data has been recorded.  On subsequent scans, the orange pixels will be filled in and  the white and black will be averaged with new data.


Figure 5.  A magnified, averaged DLS image acquired using  the timeout feature of DXGlue.  The loop length is 3.6ms and  the pixel dwell time is 420µs.   No streaking is evident in the  image.


Fail Rate Monitoring


In [8], the authors mathematically show that choosing a point  in the shmoo of a failing device that causes the device to fail  50% of the test loops will lead to the best SNR for small laser  induced timing shifts.  DLS tools and testers generally do not  provide a method for monitoring the fail rate. Without a  method to monitor the fail rate, setting the initial optimum bias  point and maintaining it over long DLS acquisitions is  challenging.


The DXGlue application calculates the instantaneous fail rate  using the fail count sent in each loop status packet.  The fail  rate is the ratio of the number of test loops with a non-zero fail  count to the total number of test loops within ½ second.


The fail rate is displayed in two views, shown in Figure 2: as  an analog-style gauge, and as a scrolling strip chart.  The  gauge offers quick feedback while hunting for the 50% fail  rate bias condition in the shmoo.  The strip chart is especially  useful after a DLS image has been acquired.  With the laser  parked, the position of the laser can be nudged to precisely  find the spatial location and amplitude of the peak DLS  response.


CNES Laboratory, 18 avenue Edouard Belin, 31401 Toulouse, France


Antoine Reverdy


SECTOR Technologies, 2 avenue de Vignate, 38610 Gières, France


Dean Lewis


Université Bordeaux, IMS Laboratory, 351 cours de la libération, 33405 Talence, France


The Laser Voltage Imaging (LVI) technique [1], introduced in  2009, appears as a very promising approach for Failure  Analysis application which allows mapping frequencies  through the backside of integrated circuits. In this paper, we  propose a new range of application based on the study of the  LVI second harmonic for signal degradation analysis. After a  theoretical study of the impact of signal degradation on the  second harmonic, we will demonstrate the interest of this new  approach on two case studies on ultimate technology (28nm). This technique is a new approach of failure analysis that maps  timing degradation and duty cycle degradation. In order to  confirm the degradations we will use the LVP Technique. The  last part is two real case studies on which this LVI second  harmonic technique was used to find the root cause of a 28nm  process issue.


In this article, we introduce a new methodology based on the  LVI second harmonic imaging. Based on the Fourier  transform, if the signal is degraded (from a 50% duty cycle  square reference signal), the even harmonic will appear in the  Fourier spectrum. If a defect is present and induces a  degradation of the signal shape (i. e. duty cycle), this approach  will allow localizing this defect. Then, we applied this new  technique on a case study to overcome some of the limitations  of the LVI, EMMI and TRE [2] techniques. The previous  work on the 2nd harmonic is very interesting, this technique  has presented at ISTFA 2011 in order to isolate a scan chain  transition fail [3]. This paper presents the LVI harmonic  mapping as an emerging FA technique, in order to isolate  certain timing violation. Today, since no mapping technique  are able to pinpoint this kind of timing related issue, this new  application method of the LVI technique opens a new way of  detecting this type of defect where probing based technique  appears to be less powerful due to the single point acquisition  mode.


Laser Voltage Imaging


Origin of the LVI signal


The LVI technique is based on two phenomena: the FranzKeldysh effect [4] and the Free Carrier absorption [5]. Recent  publication demonstrates that the Free Carrier absorption  mechanism is the predominant one [5].


The LVI measurement can be referred to as „frequency  mapping‟. This technique shows the physical locations of  transistors that are activated at a specific frequency (figure 1). The laser beam is focused through the silicon substrate, on the  active area, and the reflected signal is analyzed in the  frequency domain.


Figure 1 : On the left: LVI acquisition; on the right: LVI  overlay acquisition


LVI mapping using Second Harmonic


LVI technique does not give any information about the timing. One solution to overcome this limitation when dealing with  square signal degradation consists of analyzing the discrete  spectrum peaks, and more specifically the second harmonic  amplitude.


Figure 2 shows a typical 50% duty cycle square signal in the  time domain. This signal is defined by its amplitude (A), its  period (T) and its duty cycle (k/T). If the signal is properly  propagating in the device the duty cycle should remain the  same (50%). Consequently, if we transpose this temporal  signal into the frequency domain (figure 2 bottom) we notice  that the amplitude of the second (and each even) harmonic is  zero.


Figure 2: Up: Square signal with 50% duty cycle, Down: Square signal in the frequency domain


So in order to check the integrity of a square signal, we will  use the property of discrete spectrum peaks. In the case of a  periodic function, the Fourier transform can be simplified to  the calculation of a discrete set of complex amplitudes, called  Fourier series coefficients. The Fourier series of a function  f(x) is given by


Where


With  n : the


: is the intensity of the discrete spectrum peak at  the


Now, if this signal is degraded in terms of duty cycle, this  perturbation will affect the spectral representation.


Now, we focus on the frequency spectrum of the square signal  with 30 % duty cycle.


Figure 3: Up: Square signal with 30% duty cycle, Down: Square signal in the frequency domain


In the frequency domain, a periodic square wave with 30% duty cycle (See figure 3) has discrete spectrum with even  harmonics. We will use this property of the even harmonic in  order to find a delay failure, a timing violation, and a  transition fault.


Figure 4 shows the magnitude of the second harmonic of a  square signal versus the duty cycle. If a duty cycle is increased  by x % at the input of an inverter, according to Figure 4, it will  be decreased by the same value at the output. Looking at the  amplitude of the second harmonic is therefore a very robust  approach.


Figure 4: Second Harmonic magnitude vs. duty cycle


The real interest of the second harmonic analysis is that in a  standard case, whenever we will have a look at a clock signal  this second harmonic should not exist. If the signal is degraded  the second harmonic will appear.


Second harmonic imaging


Applications on 28nm


First case: Duty cycle degradation


In this application case we focus on a timing defect on a 28  nm technology device. The problem is a timing violation on  the clock signal. Figure 5 represents the layout of the die and  the position of the clock tree transistors. The goal of this case  study is to show that it‟s possible to find a timing problem  using the LVI tuned to the second harmonic.


Figure 5: Layout and transistors localization


Now we will analyze all the transistors signatures using the  LVI technique and the LVI second harmonic technique in  order to find the root cause of the defect. Then, we confirm the  fail hypothesis using an internal probing technique (Laser  Voltage Probing (LVP)) on each transistor of the clock tree. We acquired the clock signal at 10 MHz (50% duty cycle). The spectrum analyzer resolution bandwidth (RBW) and video  bandwidth (VBW) were both set to 1 kHz, the scan speed on  the LSM is 1.7ms, the wavelength of the LSM laser used is  1330nm with a power of 1.2mW.


LVI fundamental


LVI


2nd Harmonic


PAD CLK


T1


T3


T5


T7


T10


Figure 6: LVI mapping and second harmonic mapping


We performed a frequency mapping at the clock (10MHz) and  2nd harmonic frequencies (20MHz). If the clock signal (square  signal with 50 % duty cycle) is good, we should have no  signature with the 2nd harmonic technique. But in this case, we  can see that starting from the transistor T3, a 2nd harmonic LVI  signature appears on it. This signature is abnormal and it‟s due  to a degradation of the square signal.


In this acquisition we note that the intensity of the  fundamental decreases more as we move forward on the clock  tree and the intensity of the 2nd harmonic increases.


Figure 7: Second harmonic value Vs transistors


Figure 7 represents the value of the second harmonic Vs the  transistors on the tree. If we compare this result with the  theoretical curve that was shown in figure 4, we can argue that  from pad clock to T2 the duty cycle is close to 50 per cent and  no second harmonic can be detected. From T2 to T10 the  second harmonic value increases with a similar shape  compared to figure 4, which means that each transistor  degrades the duty cycle linearly. At this step, we can say that  the clock tree isn‟t good but the defect isn‟t localized on one  transistor. From the previous figure, we expect the duty cycle  of transistor T10 to be  close to 25%. In order to confirm this  fail hypothesis we performed a LVP acquisition on each  transistor of the tree. (See figure 8)


Figure 8: Laser Voltage probing on the clock tree


Figure 8 represents the waveform of each transistor of the  clock tree. Thanks to the LVP technique we can confirm a  degradation of the duty cycle on all the transistors starting  with T3.


Figure 9: Duty cycle value Vs transistor


Figure 9 represents the duty cycle value versus the transistor  of the chain. Thanks to the LVP probing, we can confirm the  defect is a violation timing (degradation of the duty cycle). Figure 9 matches Figure 7 perfectly, with a maximum when  the duty cycle is degraded to 25%. Thanks to this study the  failure isn‟t localized on one transistor but affects all the  transistors of the clock tree.


Then, we performed the physical analysis which confirms the  duty cycle degradation. The failure is a process defect on each  PMOS transistors, all the contacts on the PMOS are not well  aligned with the metallization, so they are resistive and induce  degradation on the duty cycle.


Second case: Timing degradation


This second case study is a scan chain failure on 28 nm  technology. The fail is a soft fail at VDD min.


A soft fail at low VDD (920mV) has been flagged by test into  a scan chain using a shift test program.


The fail signature is a stuck at 0 representative of a transition  fault failure mode. A timing degradation into the scan chain is  suspected.


The first step of the analysis is to use the usual technique of  FA, the first technique used is the EMMI localization (See  figure 10) but there is no overconsumption observed, so there  isn‟t significant EMMI spot in static or dynamic acquisition (at several voltages and frequencies).


Figure 10: EMMI signature in dynamic mode


The failure isn‟t due to a leakage, so, at this step a timing  degradation is suspected. In order to confirm the fail  hypothesis we will use the LVI technique and the new  technique described in the previous part (2nd harmonic  analysis). The results are presented on the figure 11 (pattern  pass) and Figure 12 (pattern fail).


Fail Rate Spectrum


Ideally, the 50% fail rate is due to random noise sources  moving the bias point on the shmoo between pass and fail.  However, in some setups, periodic noise may dominate.  The  DXGlue application calculates the frequency spectrum of the  test loop failures, sampled at the period of the test loop.  A fast  Fourier transform is performed with the results of 8192 test  loops.  The results are continuously averaged to display the  spectrum as shown in Figure 6.


Figure 6.  This screenshot of the standalone DXGlue  application illustrates the fail rate spectrum.  This example  shows a peak at 500 Hz, indicating that the 33% fail rate is  not random.  Also shown is the fail count and fail signature  under the gauge.


Loop Length Monitoring


The pixel dwell time of the LSM must be set to be equal to or  greater than the test loop time for DLS.  DXGlue measures the  loop time by counting ticks of its 84 MHz clock between the  trigger assertions, and sends that value as part of the loop  status packet.  As shown in Figure 6, the DXGlue application  displays the loop length.  The integrated version of DXGlue  also calculates the optimum scan speed for our custom LSM.


Fail Mode Monitoring


Two fields in the loop status packet allow the DLS user to  monitor the fail mode of the device during analysis.  The first  is the fail count field, which contains the number of assertions  of rawfail during the test loop and is displayed as shown in  Figure 6.


The DXGlue hardware computes the fail signature by  combining the time values of assertions of rawfail during the  loop.  At the start of the loop, the fail signature is set to 0.  When rawfail asserts, fail signature becomes fail signature  XOR currentTime, where currentTime is a snapshot of the


loop length timer.   The DXGlue application displays the fail  signature as illustrated in Figure 6.   A change in either of  these values indicates the part may be failing differently.


Time Resolved DLS with a CW Laser


Equivalent Time Sampling


DLS is useful for design debug and for locating speed limiting  logic paths.  Having logic waveforms of the signals in the  slow path can help explain the root cause of the slow path.  But with a multi-millisecond test loop, finding the timing of  the failing signals with laser voltage probing or time resolved  emission is time consuming or impossible.


Modulating a laser to time resolve the DLS image has been  reported in [9].  In that work, the portion of the test loop that  was sensitive to laser irradiation (the interaction time) was  found by synchronizing the laser to the tester and manually  selecting the test loop time during which the laser illuminated  the device.   With DXGlue, we have automated the search for  the interaction time using an inexpensive TTL-modulated CW  laser.  The TTL input on the laser is driven from the laser  control signal of DXGlue.


In normal DLS mode, the laser control signal is held high so  the laser remains on for the entire test loop, while the laser is  scanned across the sample.   For time resolved DLS, DXGlue  is set to laser walk mode and the laser is parked at a known  DLS site.  Laser walk mode is similar to equivalent time  sampling in oscilloscopes: the laser is pulsed once per test  loop, at a sequentially later time each test loop.  The loop  status packet’s laserFiredAt field is used to send the laser  pulse time to the DXGlue application, which builds a TR-DLS “waveform” of the site using the averaged fail results at each  pulse time.  Figure 7 illustrates the resulting TR-DLS  waveform when averaged over many walks through the entire  test loop.


Interpretation of the TR-DLS Waveform


The TR-DLS waveform can be viewed as a convolution of the  laser pulse with the DLS site’s time-dependent laser


Figure 7.  This waveform is an example of time resolved DLS data taken over a 1.8ms test loop with the laser parked on a DLS pass site.  The waveform is built up by pulsing the laser at a particular time during the test loop, and recording the test result for that loop.  During the next loop, the 20µs pulse is placed 1µs later.  Over many iterations, an averaged waveform is built up that represents the  DLS response as a function of time.  The negative pulse between 1.7ms and 1.8ms indicates the interaction time of the passing DLS site.  The sample is a 45nm SOI device.


sensitivity.  Given the laser pulse shape, the time dependent  DLS response can be recovered from the TR-DLS waveform  with a resolution that is better than the laser pulse width.


For this work, we focused on laser assisted device alteration (LADA) [4], using a 1064nm laser that can be pulsed with a  TTL input.    The laser can provide 20µs pulses at a repetition  rate of tens of kilohertz.  DXGlue steps the laser pulse through  the test loop in 1µs steps.


The interaction time window the laser is limited by the rise or  fall time of the signal of interest (typically tens of  picoseconds), and the time constant of the laser’s effect on the  circuit.  Laser-induced electron-hole generation (LADA) is  must faster than the 20µs laser pulse.  Relative to the laser  pulse width, the LADA time-dependent sensitivity can be  modeled as an impulse function.


As the laser pulse steps through the “impulse function” of the  photoelectric sensitivity, the two are convolved into the  average fail rate response. The convolution of a signal with an  impulse function yields a time-reversed form of the signal.  The TR-LADA waveform is simply the time-reversed shape  of the laser pulse itself.  When parked on a passing LADA  site, the TR-LADA pulse shape is the inverted, time-reversed  laser pulse shape.


The shape of the laser pulse we used for this work is shown in  Figure 8.  The key feature of this pulse is the steep slope and  peak as the laser turns on occurring 6.93µs after the rising  edge of the TTL input.   The leading edge feature of the laser  pulse is time reversed into the trailing edge of the TR-LADA  signal, as illustrated in Figure 9.  The peak serves as a timing  reference, allowing the interaction time to be resolved to better  than the 20µs pulse width without a formal deconvolution.


Figure 8.  Laser pulse shape used for this work is shown here.  The pulse is approximately 20µs wide with a slow turn-off.   The steep slope of the peak when the laser turns on, occurring  approximately 6.93µs after the rising edge of the TTL pulse, is  used for TR-LADA.


The time axis in a TR-DLS waveform is calibrated to the  timing of the peak of the laser’s leading edge.  DXGlue  reports lasserFiredAt, the time of the rising edge of TTL input  signal.  The time on the axis is laserFiredAt + 6.93µs.  The  interaction time is read directly from the waveform by looking  for the peak on the trailing edge of the TR-DLS pulse.  Due to  the 1 µs  step size, the peak is somewhat diminished.  Although there is jitter in the laser pulse placement, the timing  resolution is dominated by the 1µs step size of the laser pulse.


Figure 9.  This TR-LADA waveform is a magnified section of  Figure 7 at the interaction time of the LADA site.    The  approximately 20µs width matches the laser pulse width, and  the trailing edge has a greater slope than the rising edge as  would be expected, reflecting the difference in edge slopes of  the laser pulse.  The timing of the peak of this waveform  indicates the actual interaction time.  The time on the x-axis is  laserFiredAt + 6.93 µs, calibrated to the timing of the leading  edge peak of the laser pulse.


Observing the Thermal Response


Although normally used to identify sites sensitive to electronhole pair injection, the 1064nm laser can also induce a thermal  effect.  Because the thermal effect often has a much longer  time constant than the photoelectric effect, it is easily  identified in the TR-LADA waveform.  As shown in Figure  10, it is even possible for a single site to show both a  photoelectric and thermal response.    DXGlue can be set to  laser sync mode to pulse the laser at one particular time each  test loop.  Figure 11 illustrates how the two responses can be  separated in the image with laser sync mode.


Figure 10.  This DLS site, from a 45nm SOI sample, has both  a thermal and a photoelectric response to the 1064nm laser in  its TR-DLS waveform.


Figure 11.  These two 1064nm DLS images were taken from  the same site as the waveform in Figure 10.  In each image, the 20µs laser pulse was synchronized to pulse at the  indicated time during the test loop.  At 9.995ms, near the peak  of the thermal response, a hazy brighter area is seen.  At


10.046ms at the time of the photoelectric response, a much  smaller dark site is seen at the same location.   The  background gray levels are different due to contrast  enhancement.


Improving the Temporal Resolution of TR-DLS


We have experimented with improving the time resolution of  TR-DLS by reducing the 1µs step size during the laser walk  and by reducing the pulse width of the laser.  Both techniques  are illustrated in Figure 12.  A step size of 11.9ns is used with  a narrow sequence of laser pulses that are created by turning  off the TTL input approximately 400ns after lasing starts.  The  first peak is used as the timing reference because it occurs


6.56µs after the rising edge of the TTL input.


The laser waveform can be improved even more by turning off  the TTL input just before the lasing starts, creating a pulse that  returns to zero power shown in Figure 13.  Using the first  pulse as a timing reference, the temporal resolution will likely  be better than 100ns, which is a reasonable time window in  which to search for the event with laser voltage probing.


Figure 12.  This plot shows the effect of turning the TTL input  off approximately 400ns after the lasing starts.  Three pulses  are created, which are reflected in the time-reversed TR-DLS  waveform.  In this waveform, the DLS data is taken with an


11.9ns step size.


Figure 13.  The laser pulse can be reduced to approximately  100ns FWHM by turning off the TTL input just before laser  turns on.  Two pulses with less intensity follow the main pulse.


We have shown that by improving the simple fail flag latch  used in DLS systems can improve the workflow and enable  new applications.  We have used DXGlue to make time  resolved DLS more practical.


The LADA work in this paper was performed by Kris Dickson  and George Lange.

Differential Polarization Imaging and Probing [DPIP]: Seeing and Probing the “Invisible”


Baohua Niu, Martin von Haartman, Patrick Pardy, Mitch Sacks


Intel Corporation, Hillsboro, OR 97124


Corresponding author: Baohua.Niu@intel.com


A novel method for obtaining diffraction limited high  resolution images, and increased signal to noise ratio (SnR), for imaging and probing silicon based complementary metal  oxide semiconductor field effect transistor (CMOS, and  MOSFET) integrated circuits (IC), is presented. The improved  imaging is based on the sub wavelength features’ asymmetric  layout, which is dictated by the lithography design rules  constrain in CMOS IC and their interactions with polarized  light. This asymmetry in layout and the inherent stress  engineering on the CMOS IC, produce both dichroism and  birefringence in silicon (Si). An elegant design enabled us to  obtain two images with orthogonal polarization detection to  take advantages of the dichroism and birefringence in Si based  CMOS IC. Differential Polarization Image (DPI) is obtained  by subtracting the two orthogonal polarization resolved  images. On infrared emission microscopes (IREM), DPI in  optical imaging mode and DPI plus probing [DPIP] in  emission mode, showed 2X or more in terms of optical  resolution (imaging mode) and 2X or more SnR (emissionprobing mode) improvements. Striking images in probing  mode, revealing previously “invisible” emission, were  demonstrated.


Infrared Emission Microscope/Microscopy (IREM) is one of the  most utilized tools in advanced Si based CMOS IC defect  localization and circuits design debug application. IREM takes full  advantage of the fact that Si has high transmission from 1.1µm to  about 1.6µm in the near infrared (NIR) region of the spectrum. This  enables imaging and probing the Si CMOS IC through the backside  of the Si. Since its early introduction in the 1980's, IREM has  become an indispensable tool in Si CMOS device manufacturing  defect localization and device debug applications.


With the continuation of the Moore's law, Si CMOS IC has  continued to shrink in physical dimension. Novell lithography  advances using 193nm wavelength laser, combining phase shift mask (PSM) and liquid immersion (193i), have enabled patterning of  advanced Si CMOS IC with feature size as small as 22nm in the  most advanced Si CMOS products from leading manufacturers like  Intel.


IREM fundamental physics limitation, due to its longer wavelength  working range, has put tremendous challenges on debug engineers to  keep pace with the continued scaling of the Si CMOS IC devices. The IR optics has evolved from high numerical aperture (NA) air


lens (with NA up to 0.85), to liquid immersion objective (LIO) [1] with NA up to 1.4, and more recently with Solid Immersion Lens (SIL) with NA up to about 3.0.  Even with the SIL NA at 3.0, the  diffraction limited resolution of an aberration free IREM system is  limited to resolving minimum features according to the following  equation at the most:


This is referred to as Sparrow criteria.  is the wavelength used for  imaging. Assuming 1200nm for today's IREM illumination source, will gives us diffraction limited, aberration free minimum resolvable  feature at 200nm full width half max (FWHM). This is almost 2X  larger than the minimum contacted gate pitch of Intel's products  offering of the 32nm node technology. Intel’s 32nm technology has  contact gate pitch at 112.5nm [2, 3], and eight times larger than the  minimum feature dimension the latest 22nm technology node.


There are additional challenges by the implementation of the SIL  technology to achieve even this kind of resolution. One key feature  to achieve the high resolution with the SIL is high magnification and  with it the small field of view (FOV) of the SIL at about 50X50 µm2. We will have to rely on the low mag air lenses (low resolution) to  navigate around the chip to find the region of interest (ROI). No antireflection coating (ARC) can be used with Si. The ARC will render  the SIL non-optimized and increase the sample preparation  complexity. The air-Si interface when using air lens, without ARC, has a surface reflectivity close to 31% based on the following  equation [4]:


For the wavelength range of interest in Si CMOS IREM application,


Si refraction index is


. The surface reflectivity is


. This large amount of surface reflected light smears out any underline  features below the surface rendering them difficult to image. This  makes the SIL technology very tedious and difficult to navigate. Fault isolation, design and debug engineers must rely on very large  features to navigate to the area of interests before they could take  advantages of the high resolution images made available by the SIL.


Differential Polarization Imaging (DPI), sometimes also called  Polarization-Difference Imaging (PDI) [5-10], has been used in  biology to take advantages of the build in asymmetry of the biologic  samples and its interactions with polarized light. It has shown  improved image resolution and signal to noise ratio through “scattering” media by common mode rejection.


In this paper, we present an innovative technique called Differential  Polarization Imaging and Probing (DPIP) for imaging and probing  Si CMOS IC.  DPIP takes advantage of the Si CMOS IC layout  asymmetry, and orientation effect. Full advantages are taken from the  inherent dichroism and birefringence from the Si CMOS IC devices  and their interaction with polarized light in order to improve the IR  image contrast and resolution. For air lens, this improvement is close  to 3X by eliminating the “surface” reflection effectively without  using ARC. For LIO and SIL lens, the improvement is close to 2~3X  as well. Due to the asymmetry inherent to the MOSFET structure, we thought that emission from active MOSFET could also be highly  non-symmetric and polarized. This has been verified by emission  images. DPIP affords us another knob that we can use to distinguish  the active MOSFET switching emission which is polarized,  from  thermal induced emission and other “non polarized” emissions. We  will show how to utilize DPIP in emission mode to resolve and  reveal features that were previously very difficult to detect, nearly “invisible” and DPIP makes them clearly visible.


The paper is organized as following. We will first present the  experimental setup and outline the asymmetric nature of the  MOSFET IC device layout as dictated by lithography  patterning and design rule constrains. We will then explain the  physics behind the interaction of light with this asymmetric  physical layout and propose a mechanism for the observed  asymmetry in the reflected and emission signal. Taking a page  from laser induced fluorescence in biological application [5-10]; we developed and implemented a very simple image  contrast and resolution enhancement technique – Differential  Polarization Imaging and Probing (DPIP). We will then show  the expected contrast improvement in the illuminated images  and emission images when we detect different polarized  images of the active device under test (DUT). Lastly, a few  real defects and debug cases on Intel's 32nm and 22nm SRAM  test chips will be presented to show clearly those previously “invisible” emission features becoming visible.


Experimental Setup and Physics Behinds  Polarization Difference Imaging and Probing


A block diagram of the tool used to enable PDIP is in Figure 1  below.


Figure 1, Block diagram illustration of DPIP enabled IREM


In the normal optical imaging mode, a light source, in this case a  1200nm light emitting diode (LED), illuminates a pellicle at 45  degree angle to the beam input. The pellicle reflects typically 5~20% of the incoming light to illuminate the DUT, while the rest gets  captured by the beam dump. The reflected light gets focused on to  the DUT through the objective lens which can be air lens, LIO or  SIL. The reflected light from the DUT gets collected by the  objective, and passes through the pellicle and gets imaged by the  high sensitivity, low noise liquid nitrogen cooled, InGaAs infrared  camera. This gives an optical (illuminated) image of the DUT region  of interests (ROI).


As well documented in the literature and illustrated in figure 2 [4], the reflected light off the pellicle is partially linearly polarized.


Figure 2, Illustration of polarization by reflection


Depending on the orientation of the features in the ROI, this  polarized light can interact with the features resulting in very  interesting orientation sensitive images that have very different image  contrast (variations), therefore different resolution [11-12]. This is  due to the well known physics of interactions of "wire grid" with  polarized or non-polarized light as illustrated in Figure 3.


Figure 3, Illustration of wire grid polarizer, the “conductive” wire  diameter is much smaller than the light wavelength


This wire grid polarizer is very effective when the "grid lines" are  small compared to the wavelength being used, and the space  approaching a few tenths of the wavelength. So for linearly polarized  light, with the polarization vector parallel to the direction of the wire  in the grid, no light will be transmitted, all will be reflected instead. The wire grid in this case acts like a mirror. However, when the  linearly (or partially) polarized light is polarized in the direction


perpendicular to the direction of the wire(s) in the grid, some portion  of the light will be transmitted through the grid and some portion will  be reflected. This creates an "image" with some depth of contrast, therefore resolves features to certain degree.


As briefly mentioned in the introduction, Si CMOS IC using 193i  and phase shit mask (PSM) technology and other design constrains  dictates that all CMOS features are oriented in one direction per  patterning layer. This is illustrated in figure 4, which highlights only  the diffusion layers.


Figure 4, Illustration of CMOS diffusion layer asymmetric layout. Green: nDiff; purple: pdiff, not to scale.


For example, for 32nm node design, the minimum diffusion width is  about 60nm and the space that separates them is on the order of  about 80nm. For the wavelength range used in IREM, all these  diffusion lines are effectively behaving like “wire” grid.


In the IREM setup, as illustrated in figure 1, the light used to  illuminate the DUT is partially polarized perpendicular to the paper  plan. Because of the diffusion "wire grid" (as shown in Fig. 4) where  the DUT is oriented such that the wires are perpendicular to this  polarization direction, as discussed above, we will get some  transmitted light and some reflected light which creates an image  with contrast. However, when the DUT is oriented such as the "wire  grid" is parallel to the polarization direction, no image with good  contrast will show up.


Figure 5 shows both the diffusion layers and the first metal layer  orientation behind (above it when looked from the backside of the  Si) it. The metal layer will act as a reflective “mirror” like wire grid, and when the polarization is oriented correctly this will enhance the  contrast of the diffusion layers when imaged from the backside of the  active Si.


Figure 5, CMOS diffusion layer and “metal” layer above. The red  double arrows indicate possible light polarization direction


In practice, we found it's much easier to detect both DUT orientation (polarized) images by putting a polarizer just before the InGaAs  camera as illustrated in figure 1. We call this optical element 1 (OE1). It is a linear polarizer which has extinction coefficient close  to 10,000:1. We could rotate the OE1 to get two images of different (orthogonal) polarization images and subtract one from another  which creates a differential polarization image (DPIP) that has much  better image resolution and contrast than either of the individual  image. The physics behind this enhancement is very simple in fact. DPIP takes a very good image (correct polarization oriented image) and subtract out a near perfect "in focus background" image (90  degree rotated polarization oriented image) and removes any residual  surface “glare” reflection from the highly reflective Si surface  without ARC-in cases of using air lenses. This differential (or  difference) image eliminates any blur and optical aberrations (systematic errors in images from the optics in the IREM system) as  well as the background scattering from the resulting image.


In emission mode, the 45 degree pellicle is moved out of the optical  path. And the OE1 can also be moved out of the optical path to start  with. Emission images can be collected with OE1 out (normal), and  OE1-in front of the InGaAs camera entrance window at 0 or 90  degree polarization orientation. Each image can be saved and  viewed, as well as analyzed separately. The tool software has built in  capabilities to get DPIP automatically as well as through MACRO  functions built in for both optical and emission mode for imaging  and probing.


Figure 6 shows one such example when using a 5X air objective in  conjunction with the OE1 imaging a 22nm technology node test  chip.


Figure 6, 5X air lens image of a 22nm test-chip ROI with DPIP (bottom two) and without DPIP (top three).


Top portion of figure 6 are individual polarization oriented images (left and right portion of the top). The middle is the typical nonpolarization resolved (sum of the left and right images) image. The  bottom shows two DPIP images with varying degree of contrast  adjustment. The image contrast and resolution are at least three times  better when compared with the images without DPIP.


Figure 7 shows DPIP using a 20X air objective on the 22nm  technology test chip.


Figure 7, 20X air lens image of a 22nm test-chip ROI. Left side  shows the best polarization resolved image , right side is the DPIP  image of the same area.


Again, the DPIP image at the right side of the figure shows  dramatically improved image resolution and contrast. Left side  image was the best possible one orientation polarized image. DPIP  brings out features that were not identifiable from the image on the  left.  DPIP image clearly showed those “hidden features” in the ROI  and removed all those "surface artifacts” from the imaging system as  well. As we have shown in the introductions before, the underline  CMOS device structures are oriented and therefore polarization  direction sensitive, while the surface and other system artifacts are  normally not oriented, therefore they will be the same in both  polarization resolved images. DPIP remove all surface and ‘system  defects’ in the result image.


Figure 8 shows similar comparative images using a 100X 0.75NA air  objective lens.


Figure 8, 100X air lens image of a 22nm test-chip ROI, left side  shows the best polarization resolved image, and right side is the  DPIP image of the same area. Notice the absence of “surface” defects from the DPIP image, and DPIP image clearly shows those “invisible” features highlighted in the ROI, as well as an overall  contrast and resolution improvements.


Figure 9 to 11 compare images with different polarization orientation  on the same ROI and final DPIP using a 350X 2.6 NA SIL on a  22nm SRAM test-chip with optical resolution target structure.


Figure 9, 2.6NA SIL images of a 22nm test-chip resolution structure. On the left is a line scan of the upper left side of the image where the  yellow dotted line. The red double arrow indicates the OE1  direction. The “active diffusion features are parallel to the  polarization direction.


Figure 10, same area image with the OE1 polarization direction  rotated 90 degree. On the left side, the same line scan on the same  feature clearly showed improved image contrast and resolution  compared with figure 9.


Figure 11, DPIP image: Figure 10 minus figure 9. The image and  line scan showed much improved image contrast and resolution.

Image contrast is calculated using the following formula


We used the average of the middle 10 min-max (features, as labeled  in the image) in the line scan of figure 11 to calculate the DPIP image  contrast and resolution FWHM.


As discussed in the introduction, the 2.6NA SIL, when illuminated  with 1200nm light, has a diffraction limited-aberration free resolution  of about 230nm FWHM. The DPIP image showed diffraction  limited-aberration free resolution to within less than 5% at 240nm  minimum resolution. Compared the X-polarized images, this  improvement is dramatic, compared with the non-polarized or the Ypolarized images, the contrast improvement is close to 4X and  resolution improvement is more than 2X. Figure 10 compared with  figure 9, clearly showed much improved image contrast and  resolution. This supports our proposed physics principle behind the  interaction of polarized light with direction oriented structures in Si  CMOS IC.


Similarly, the active CMOS emission images are highly asymmetric  as well. In our study, we found that for the device illustrated as in  figure 5 and figure 6, the predominately emission strength is in the  light that was polarized along parallel to the diffusion direction (Ex). The emission intensity ratio measured is greater than 3:1 ratio (Ex/ Ey)2.


Figure 12 shows one such example from a 32nm SRAM test chip.


Figure 12, top: emission images taken with two different orientations  of OE1. Device diffusion direction is parallel to OE1-0. Bottom  shows the line plot of the emission intensity along the dotted lines in  the emission images above


The 0 degree orientation emission is almost a factor of 10 stronger  than the 90 degree orientation emission. But, the 90 degree  orientation emission gives additional fine features that were not  visible in the 0 degree orientation emission. Additional analysis is  being carried out to give us more in depth understanding of the  interaction of the emission with the "underline" features layoutorientation.


Figure 13 below shows several 32nm SRAM test chip hot electron  emission images. Here the 0 degree polarized emission is about a  factor of 2 stronger than the 90 degree polarized emission. Again this  indicated very strong asymmetry in the emission and the emitted  photons undergo scattering which also have "polarization" –direction  asymmetry.


Figure 13, left side from top to bottom, emission images with OE1  out of the optical path, and OE1 oriented in 0 degree and 90 degree. Right side show the line scans along the dotted lines in the images.


Figure 14 below shows two images of a 22nm test chip transistor  emission, comparing a known open (failed) transistor to a normal  transistor emission images with OE1 at different orientations.


Figure 14,  left side emission images: top  is a suspected


“open” transistor with weak emission and much smaller asymmetry. Bottom is a “normal” transistor emission. On right side, intensity  cross sections of the emission images along the lines


On the right side, the top is a line plot of a transistor with a suspected "open" emission where the 0 degree emission is almost 2.5 times  stronger than the 90 degree emission. The bottom portion is a “normal” transistor emission where the 0 degree emission is almost 5  times stronger than the 90 degree emission. It clearly indicates  different emission mechanism, and different interaction of the  emitted photons with the "structures" around the emitter, which  apparently has different asymmetry-in the "defect".


Figure 15 below showcases the PDIP on a 22nm SRAM test chip  with an unknown defect.


Figure 15, 22nm sram test-chip emission image taken at 1.000V Vcc  and integrated for 64 seconds. Upper left, low mag air lens emission  image. Upper right, SIL-OE1 at 0 degree, lower left SIL-OE1 at 90  degree. Bottom right is DPIP image.


In this case, there is a very strong emission signal (upper right corner  of images without DPIP). The origin of this strong emission is still  unknown at time of writing. As can be seen even with the low mag  image (upper right side of the image on the upper left of figure 15), it  shows up on the upper right corner. However, this "unknown" background is polarization independent. We were able to take a 0  and 90 polarization resolved images and generated DPIP which is  showing at the lower right of figure 15. Clearly, the DPIP image


shows many emissions "signature" that will not manifest themselves  at all with either 0 or 90 OE1 alone. Without OE1 and DPIP, there is  no chance of seeing anything else not "shadowed" by the unknown  strong emission. DPIP in this case is a new capability that enhances  the IREM usefulness tremendously. DPIP makes those “invisible” emission features clearly visible.


We have shown that Differential Polarization Imaging and Probing (DPIP) can improve both the optical and emission images of the  IREM by a factor of 2 or more. The underlining physics are well  documented and understood. We used the patterning constraints of Si  CMOS IC where each layer is typically oriented in one direction to  our advantage. We have shown a simple implementation of the OE1  apparatus on a standard IREM tool to obtain polarization resolved  optical and emission images. This OE1 enabled DPIP on the IREM. DPIP makes it possible to see those previously “invisible” features  and emissions in Si based CMOS IC. We see continued  improvement will make the IREM capable supporting well into  future generations of CMOS based Si IC defect and debug  applications.

Advanced Physical Analysis Methodology for Yield and Reliability of 28-nm, Bulk-Si, Flip-Chip ICs Using SEM and Backside Deprocessing


Yuanjing (Jane) Li, Steven Scott and Howard Lee Marks


NVIDIA Corporation, Santa Clara, CA, USA  janli@nvidia.com


This paper presents a backside chip-level physical analysis  methodology using backside de-processing techniques in  combination with optimized Scanning Electron Microscopic (SEM) imaging technique and Focused Ion Beam (FIB) cross  sectioning to locate and analyze defects and faults in failing IC  devices. The case studies illustrate the applications of the  method for 28nm flip chip bulk Si CMOS devices and  demonstrate how it is used in providing insight into the fab  process and design for process and yield improvements. The  methods are expected to play an even more important role  during 20-nm process development and yield-ramping.


Physical analysis to identify the root-cause for device defects  in failing IC chips plays a critical role in driving corrective  actions for IC technology advancement and yield  enhancement.


For leading-edge CMOS ICs with multi-level interconnects  and flip-chip packaging, the traditional front-side analysis  techniques for chip-level defect localization and analysis have  become ineffective and even non-viable. An alternative  backside deprocessing technique for bulk-Si devices was  therefore developed [1]. As will be summarized, this method  allows defects and anomalies to be localized and analyzed  from the backside of the flip chip devices without removing  the die from the package.


In this paper, we will demonstrate that, in addition to the  physical failure analysis application of root-causing defects, the backside analysis method can help a fabless company to  gain valuable information about fab process and chip design. The failures that result in yield loss can be corrected in a timely  manner for process and/or chip design improvements.


Three case studies will be presented to illustrate a few of the  many applications of backside physical analysis method on flip  chip bulk Si CMOS devices. All are taken from 28nm IC  debug during process development and yield ramping. The  first case study shows how the physical backside analysis was  used to help isolate a yield limiting design issue in a MOS  capacitor. The second shows an application to e-fuse  programming characterization, while the third shows how the  technique was used to investigate fab Optical Proximity  Correction (OPC) issues.


Backside Analysis Method


To determine the root cause for a chip failure in a flip chip  packaged IC, test techniques such as scan based diagnostics  and/or failure analysis techniques such as backside emission  microscopy or laser modulation mapping [2] are first used to  isolate the fault. Once the suspect failure cell and/or net are  located, the IC device is de-processed from the backside to  remove the bulk silicon and diffusions for physical analysis. Details of the backside de-processing technique used have  been previously given [1]. The procedure is summarized  below.


First, mechanical grinding and polishing of the backside of the  die is performed using a milling machine to remove most of the  silicon thickness. A highly selective potassium hydroxide (KOH) chemical wet etch is then used to remove all remaining  silicon to expose the transistor level IC structures. The high  selectivity of the KOH wet etch (Si/SiO2 is >1000:1) leaves the  gate level materials and the entire interconnect metallization  intact. After de-processing, the die (<10m in thickness) remains attached to the well-preserved package, including the  underfill material, which provides structural support and  applications of electrical bias. Figure 1 illustrates cross-section  schematics (top) and optical images of a flip-chip packaged  graphics processor before and after Si removal.


Figure 1. Cross-section schematics (top) and optical images of  a flip-chip packaged graphics processor before and after Si  removal


Si Sub.


With IC structures fully exposed from transistor side of the die, unprecedented access is offered for defect isolation and  analysis at gate level.


1)  Transistor Level Analysis  Exposure of Gate Structures:


Figure 2(a) shows a high resolution SEM image of 28nm  transistor gate-level structures taken from backside of a  graphics processing unit (GPU) after silicon removal. The gate  dielectric and source-drain silicide in both N-MOS and P-MOS  active regions are free from silicon and can be clearly resolved  in the images. The tungsten plugs behind the silicide look more  visible in N-MOSFETs than in P-MOS areas, indicating a  lower etch rate of the Si-Ge (stressors) than that of the silicon. No difference in etch selectivity is observed between n-doped  regions and p-doped regions.


Figure 2.  (a) High resolution SEM image showing backside of  MOSFET gate structures in a 28nm process GPU after silicon  removal; (b-c) SE and BSE images of the gate level structures  on the same area of the device.


Using secondary electron (SE) and backscattered electron (BSE) imaging, gate level IC structures and defects on the  surface or buried underneath the dielectric surface can be  imaged, respectively. Shown in figure 2 (b) and (c) are  gate-level IC structures in a 28nm device imaged with SE  mode and BSE mode, respectively. Materials of different  atomic numbers near the surface and buried inside the  dielectric are highlighted differently in SE image and BSE  images. Once an embedded defect is located from the  described imaging method, the analysis of the defect using FIB  cross section can be performed in a SEM/FIB dual-beam  system.


Preservation of Diffusion:


If the defects are suspected to be in the silicon, the backside  silicon etch can be controlled such that the diffusion layer  remains after etching. Because the shallow trench isolation (STI) is much deeper than that of the silicon diffusion, the  silicon etching can be monitored and stopped when the bottom  of the STI is exposed. Figure 3 shows backside silicon etch  with remaining diffusion: (a) the planar view of the device  backside; and (b) a FIB cross section view of the up-side-down  device. The active regions surrounded by the STI match the  CAD diffusion layer exactly, allowing easy navigation for FIB  cross section, SEM and TEM (transmission electron  microscopy) analysis.


PMOS  NMOS


Figure 3. Backside silicon etch with remaining diffusion: (a) planar view of the device backside; (b) FIB cross sectionview  of the up-side-down device.


2)  Metallization Defect Analysis


The preservation of the entire IC metallization after backside  silicon etching opens an excellent opportunity for defect  localization in a failing net using backside Electron Beam  Absorbed Current (EBAC) technique. The defect mapping  technique allows for landing a nanoprobe on any net that  connects to source/drain or gate regions and mapping the  current flow in that net, including any other nets connected by  a defect. Shorts and electrical opens or resistive defects can be  effectively identified, if they exist anywhere along that net. Figure 4 shows a clock net mapped using backside EBAC from  a 28nm flip chip bulk Si device. A single nanoprobe needle  was landed on a source-drain silicide contact with a 10kV  e-beam acceleration voltage. The backside EBAC technique  was well described by K. Dickson et. al. on 45nm devices with  silicon-on-insulator (SOI) technology [3].


Figure 4. (a) SEM image showing backside de-processed  28nm bulk Si device with nanoprobes; (b) a clock net mapped  in a 28nm GPU chip using EBAC technique (Image courtesy of  Bob Newton at DCG Systems); and (c) A FIB cross section  analysis performed at failure site located by EBAC.


STI


Diffusion


Diff.


Die Backside


EBAC Map


FIB Cross Section


M1  M2  M3


One of the major advantages of analyzing the device from the  backside is that the layers with smaller geometry circuit  elements and structures are located at shallower depths and in  front of the layers with larger geometries. This situation allows  several layers of lower level IC structures to be imaged during  optical or electron microscopic inspection, which greatly  increases the likelihood of locating defects or anomalies that  are buried within dielectric materials.


3)  Backside Analysis for 20nm technology and beyond


Backside chip physical analysis method works well for flip  chip packaged 20nm bulk Si devices. The technique is  expected to be usable for 3D FinFET devices as well.


For 2.5D packaging with silicon chips on silicon interposer  with through-silicon via (TSV), the backside analysis method  should work fine because access is available to individual  chips from the backside. The milling can be done on any given  flip chip die within the package. The Si etching then can be  done in the same manner as describe above.


1)  MOS capacitor yield loss analysis


A MOS decoupling capacitor in a graphics processor was  suffering from an abnormally high yield loss. A photon  emission spot was found on the backside of the fail units on the  MOS capacitor. To root cause the defect, backside  deprocessing and SEM imaging were used to investigate the  emission “hot spot”.


In order to determine whether the emission originated from the  MOS capacitor’s dielectric or from the underlying metal, SEM  imaging was operated at SE and BSE imaging modes with  proper accelerating voltages. Figure 5 shows the backside of  the MOS capacitor after Si removal on a “good” area. As  shown in figure 5 (a) and (b), the MOS dielectric on the surface  and the M1 behind the dielectric in the same area can be  selectively highlighted using SE mode at 5kV e-beam voltage  and BSE mode at 10kV, respectively. Using this method on a  suspect area identified by an emission “hot spot”, it was  determined that the anomaly was located underneath the MOS  dielectric, as shown in (c). FIB cross section performed across  the damage area confirmed that the anomaly is a metal damage. The information was fed back to the designer who  implemented a design change, along with a new DFM rule, that resulted in a significant yield improvement.


Figure 5. SEM images showing substrate side of MOS  dielectric highlighted at 5kV e-beam voltage using SE imaging (a); Metallization behind the dielectric on the same area  imaged at 10kV using BSE imaging (b), BSE image of a  defective metal site (c), and backside FIB cross-section  through the damaged area (d).


2) e-Fuse Backside Characterization


Process changes for 28-nm process requires a new design for  electrically programmable fuses (e-fuses), and, consequently, a re-characterization of fuse programming (blowing) parameters. Backside de-processing provides an excellent  opportunity for examination of e-fuses.


After backside Si etch, an oxide etch is performed to thin down  the thickness of the oxide underneath the fuses to within the  information depth of BSE imaging with selected electron beam  energies. As a result of selective etching, e-fuses buried inside  the oxide under a uniform thickness can be imaged and  examined under SEM without artifacts. This method of  physical analysis of e-fuses has played an important role in  e-fuse reliability assessment and failure analysis.


The SEM images in figure 6 show details of the physical  changes that occur in 40-nm poly fuses (a) and 28-nm M2  fuses (b) after electrical programming under various  conditions. The arrows in the images point at the e-fuse blow  locations. The images were taken from the backside of the die  through a thin layer of dielectric. The ability to systematically  image the microstructural changes of the e-fuse structures was  invaluable for characterizing the electrical programming  parameters, leading to optimized programming conditions.


(a) Poly Fuses


(b) Metal Fuses


Figure 6.  Backside SEM images showing the details of  physical changes on 40nm technology poly fuses (a); and  28nm technology M2 fuses (b) blown  at various conditions.


3) OPC End Result Examination


Optical proximity effects cause features to be printed  differently than intended. Optical Proximity Correction (OPC) is increasingly being used to try to correct for these  discrepancies. However, even with the most rigorous OPC, the  weaknesses can still occur due to the dense patterning and the  tight process window.


The backside electron microscopic imaging technique allows  direct examination of the end results and exposes the weak  locations. Figure 7 shows SEM imaging of contact and M1  layer in a dense logic area after backside parallel lapping  following the KOH Si etch. OPC end results in flip chip  packaged devices can be examined using the above described  backside method. Combining SEM planar imaging and FIB  cross section analysis, the details of the process were studied


and weaknesses identified. The information was relayed back  to the fab for improvement.


Figure 7. Backside SEM images showing planar view of  contacts and M1 in a cutting edge technology device after  backside parallel lapping following the backside Si etch. (a) and (b) show low magnification and a close-up views of the  OPC end result at the contact level.


A method using backside de-processing combined with SEM  imaging for device-level physical analysis of 28-nm, flip-chip  packaged, bulk Si CMOS ICs was presented. Case studies  illustrated how the method can be used to help locate and  analyze device defects and anomalies. The invaluable design  and process information thus obtained helped contribute to  significant yield and quality improvement of product ICs.


The authors wish to thank William Lo for reviewing and  improving the paper.
