A comprehensive failure analysis method and mechanism study on   ultra-low-k film adhesion failure


Chen Shuting, Zhu Lei, Teo Han Wei, Liu Binghai, Huang Yanhua, Ong Kenny, Mo Zhiqiang,  Hua Younan, Yuan Zhaoxin, Heng Yong Seng, Li Chao Yong


GLOBALFOUNDRIES Singapore Pte Ltd


60 Woodlands Industrial Park D, Street 2, Singapore 738406   Tel: +(65) 63604370, E-mail: chenst@globalfoundries.com


The back-end-of-line (BEOL) structure of current IC devices  fabricated for advanced technologies is composed of film  stacks with multiple interfaces. The requirement of high  interfacial strength is therefore necessary between the different  layers in the BEOL stacks to ensure device reliability. To  enhance the IC performance for new technologies, inter-level  dielectric (ILD) made of SiO2 is replaced by low-k and ultra  low-k (ULK) dielectrics, which possess a low dielectric  constant but have poor mechanical strength. Therefore, the  challenge in maintaining BEOL film stack integrity and  reliability becomes even greater for advanced technologies.


In this paper, we show failure analysis results on a case study  of ULK adhesion failure during the IC manufacturing process. The symptoms of the BEOL failure are due to debris dropping  on the wafer during chemical mechanical polishing (CMP) after Cu thin film deposition and failure of focusing at wafer  extreme edge during the subsequent photolithography process. Extensive mechanical and chemical analyses were conducted  on the ULK and adjacent thin films. It was revealed that the  interface of ULK and Silicon Nitride from a suspected  problematic machine showed abnormally low adhesion energy  and high carbon composition. Troubleshooting on that  suspected machine found a clog in the foreline. Based on the  failure analysis and machine troubleshooting results, the  failure mechanism of the case was discussed.


With the rapid development of semiconductor IC devices, the  number of transistors on a chip is exponentially increasing, which is accompanied by the significantly increased levels of  interconnect wiring in the back-end-of- line (BEOL). In the  devices of 0.25um tech node and below, the performance of IC  devices is substantially limited by the BEOL signal delay  resulted from the shrinkage of the interconnect wiring. The  introduction of low dielectric constant materials as the  insulating layers enables significant decrease of BEOL delay  and thus improvement in performance of devices. However, with the replacement of SiO2 by low-k and ultra low-k (ULK) dielectrics, the complexity of the interconnect structure


increases significantly, leading to great challenges in  maintaining the high interfacial strength.


Figure 1(a) shows a schematic view of a typical ULK material  structure, which is composed of SiCOH base and uniformly  distributed nano-meter sized pores. The nano-pores  significantly reduced the dielectric constant of the ULK  material, but they substantially weakened the mechanical  properties. Since the intrinsic low cohesive strength of the  ULK layer reduced the mechanical properties of BEOL stacks, a transition layer was introduced between ULK and its  adjacent silicon nitride layer (i.e. Nblk) (Figure 1(b) and (c))


[1]. The transition layer is mainly silicon oxide based material  with graded carbon distribution, and its relatively high  mechanical strength could result in strong bond between ULK  and Nblk layers.)


Figure 1. (a) Schematic view of the structure of ULK  materials, (b) low adhesion strength of ULK/Nblk by directly  depositing ULK on Nblk, (c) the adhesion strength is improved  by the introduction of a transition layer between ULK and  Nblk


The introduction of the transition layer brings new challenges  for the fabrication process. During fabrication, the silicon  nitride (Nblk), transition layer and ULK films are deposited by


Plasma Enhanced Chemical Vapor Deposition (PECVD) in the  same chamber.


The thickness of the transition layer should be kept as thin as  possible to eliminate the effect of the dielectric constant of the  whole stack. On the other hand, the carbon distribution in the  transition layer needs to be accurately controlled by  controlling the flow of gases. It is often reported that  inappropriate process parameters during ULK deposition  resulted in low adhesion strength [2].


In this work, a failure analysis investigation on an IC  fabrication issue caused by the adhesion failure of ultra-low-k (ULK) material was discussed.  The interface properties of  Nblk\ ULK are studied and machine troubleshooting results  are presented. Based on the results, the failure mechanism is  discussed.


Symptoms of the failure


The symptoms of this case study of ULK adhesion failure  include the dropping of debris during chemical mechanical  polishing after Cu thin film deposition (Figure 2(a)) and failure  of focusing at wafer extreme edge during the subsequent  photolithography process. The final product wafer showed  abnormal color in the dies at the wafer extreme edge, as  presented in Figure 2(b). Failure analysis on the cross-section  of the devices showed that some layers were missing in the  wafer extreme edge (Figure 3).


In-line monitoring data and equipment commonality analysis  revealed that a thin film deposition chamber for the ULK layer  was suspected to be responsible for the failure. To investigate  the root cause, extensive failure analysis was conducted by  various techniques such as 4 Point Bending (4PB) for thin film  adhesion testing and Time-Of-Flight Secondary Ion Mass  Spectrometry (TOF-SIMS) for chemical composition analysis. The low-yield fully processed wafer was sent for Transmission  Electron Microscopy (TEM) and energy dispersive x-ray  spectroscopy (EDS) failure analysis.


Figure 2. (a) Wafer scanning map showing scratches and drop  of debris during chemical mechanical polishing after Cu thin  film deposition, (b) Photo of a final product wafer showing  abnormal color in the extreme edge.


Figure 3. SEM cross section images of devices from different  locations of the affected wafers: (a) from wafer center, (b) from the abnormal color region of wafer extreme edge. Comparison on the layer stacks revealed that some SiO2 and  ULK layers were missing in (b), resulting in metal layer  bridging.


Failure analysis results


To investigate the potential abnormal condition of the  suspected ULK deposition tool, short-loop wafers were  prepared from the affected chamber for failure analysis. The  short-loop wafers consist of layer-stacks of Si  substrate\Nblk\ULK, which could facilitate the analysis of  interface properties of Nblk\ULK.


The adhesion strength of the Nblk\ULK layer stack was  measured by 4 point bending technique. It was found that the  adhesion strength values of Nblk\ULK interface of wafers  fabricated from that chamber were significantly lower than the  baseline value, as shown in Figure 4. More specifically, the  adhesion strength of Nblk\ULK at the wafer edge is only 1.3  J/m2, which is much lower than the adhesion strength reported  for safe Chemical Mechanical Polishing (CMP) process (2.0  J/m2) [3]. The abnormally low adhesion strength measured  from 4PB confirmed that the suspected chamber is responsible  for the failure in this case study.


Figure 4. Adhesion strength of Nblk\ULK interface measured  by 4 point bending.


To study the cause of the observed abnormally low adhesion  strength, SIMS analysis was conducted to study the chemical  composition at the interface of Si sub\ULK. As presented in  Figure 5, from Nblk to ULK layer, the carbon profile increases  sharply in the wafer from the suspected chamber, while the


carbon profile showed gradual increase in the wafer from a  chamber in good condition.


Figure 5. SIMS composition profile of samples. The green and  brown lines show the carbon composition from suspected  chamber and good chamber, respectively.


High resolution TEM/EDS analysis on the failed devices of  actual product wafers revealed more details about the cause of  the adhesion failure. Figure 6 shows the results of STEM/EDS  line scan across the ULK and Nblk layer. As seen from the line  scan profile in Figure 6, a clear carbon peak was observed just  before the appearance of an oxygen peak (corresponding to the  transition oxide layer of around 10nm). The high carbon layer  was around 10nm thick according the line profile. The  appearance of the C-peak is accompanied by the falling of Si  and O concentration, implying that there is abnormal layer  with high carbon concentration in-between normal ULK and  the transition oxide layer. Therefore, TEM/EDS results  indicated that the ULK popping issue was closely related to  the appearance of such C-rich abnormal layer, which played a  detrimental role in affecting the adhesion between NBlk and  ULK layers even with the transition oxide layer.


Figure 6. STEM Z-contrast image and EDS line-scan across  the ULK and Nblk layers


Root cause analysis


To find the cause for the sharp increase of carbon profile in the  transition layer, extensive equipment troubleshooting has been  conducted. The schematic diagram of the PECVD deposition  of ULK is presented in Figure 7. There are three input gases: Bi-Cyclo-Hepta-Diene (BCHD, molecular formula: C7H8), methyldiethoxysilane (Mdeos) and helium. The BCHD is the  source of carbon, hydrogen and the pores of ULK, while  Mdeos provides the silicon and oxygen of ULK. Helium is  used as the inert carrier gas for the transportation of the other  two gases.


In the deposition process, the Mdeos and helium are first  introduced into the chamber to deposit the initial part of  transition oxide layer, after which BCHD is introduced into the  chamber to continue the transition oxide\ULK deposition. Before BCHD is piped into the chamber, the gas is transported  through a foreline to pumps that regulate its flow rate. The inline monitoring data revealed that the chamber pressure was  abnormally high and the flow of carrier gas was abnormally  low during the fabrication of the transition layer in the  suspected chamber. Further investigation found a clog in the  foreline (Figure 8) and failure of the temperature controller for  the heater jacket of the foreline.


Figure 7. Schematic configuration diagram of the chamber for  ULK deposition.


Figure 8. Photos of the failed foreline: (a) side view, (b) view  into its inside showing clog.


Mechanism of the ULK adhesion failure


The clog was due to a failure of a heater jacket in the foreline. During the process of ULK deposition, the foreline needs to be  heated above 100oC to effectively vaporize the BCHD  precursor which has a boiling point of 90oC. However, due to  the failure of the heater jacket, the actual temperature of the  foreline dropped to room temperature, resulting in  condensation of BCHD. When exposed to normal atmosphere  for certain durations, the liquid BCHD starts the process of  polymerization, as reported in reference [4]. During  polymerization, the BCHD molecules join to larger molecules  and formed BCHD oligomer, which is in a solid state at room  temperature. Consequently, the solid BCHD oligomer  accumulated and formed the clog in the foreline.


The clogged foreline resulted in abnormally high carbon in the  transition oxide. As stated above, the BCHD gas needs to be  transported through the foreline to pumps that regulate its flow  rate before it is introduced into chamber. However, the  pumping efficiency in the foreline was significantly reduced  due to the clog. As a result, the BCHD flow was abnormally  high when it was introduced into chamber, which is consistent  with the observed high chamber pressure and low flow of  carrier gas. Due to the excessively introduced BCHD, the  carbon composition in the transition oxide was abnormally  high.


After the failed foreline and heater jacket were replaced by  new ones, the properties of ULK layer deposited from the  suspected chamber were studied by 4PB and SIMS. It was  found that ULK/Nblk from the recovered chamber showed  high adhesion strength values, which are comparable with the  baseline and those from good equipment (Figure 9). SIMS  analysis showed the carbon profiles in the transition layer are  also comparable for the wafers from the recovered chamber  and good chambers (Figure 10).


Figure 9. Comparison of adhesion strength of Nblk\ULK  interfaces of wafers fabricated from different conditions.


Figure 10. SIMS composition profile of samples. The green  and brown lines show the carbon composition of wafers from  recovered chamber and good chamber, respectively.


In this paper, we show a comprehensive FA method and  mechanism study on ULK film adhesion failure.  A case study  of ULK adhesion failure during semiconductor manufacturing  process was discussed. Extensive mechanical and chemical  analyses were conducted on the ULK and adjacent thin films. It was revealed that the interface of ULK and Silicon Nitride  from a suspected problematic machine showed abnormally low  adhesion energy and high carbon composition. Troubleshooting on that suspected machine found a clog in a  foreline. Based on the failure analysis and machine  troubleshooting results, the failure mechanism of the case was  discussed.


The failure analysis results enabled quick root cause  identification, tool recovery and qualification for tool release. The results of this work could provide guidance for the  analysis of ULK adhesion failures in IC fabrications with the  increasing popularity of ULK materials in advanced  technology nodes.


[1] L. A. Clevenger, S. R. Chiras, T. Dalton, et al, U. S. Patent 7,102,232 B2.


[2] A. Grill, D. Edelstein, M. Lane, et al. J. Appl. Phys. Vol. 103, (2008) 054104.


[3] K. Maex, M. R. Baklanov, D. Shamiryan, et al. J. Appl. Phys. Vol. 93, No. 11 (2003) pp. 8793.


[4] J. J. F. Mcandrew, J. M. Girard, Y. Marot, et al. U. S. Patent 2010/0143580 A1.


Systematic Approach for the Gate Oxide Failure Caused by Arsenic Cross  Contamination


Lei Zhu, Huipeng Ng, Yanhua Huang, Hanwei Teo, Kenny Ong, Shuting Chen, Changqing Chen, Ghimboon Ang, Younan Hua, Zheng Li


Failure Analysis Department, QRA, GLOBALFOUNDRIES Singapore Pte Ltd  60 Woodlands Industrial Park D, Street 2, Singapore 738406


Tel: +(65) 66701537, E-mail: Lei.Zhu@Globalfoundries.com


This paper described a gate oxide failure case which affected  the electrical parameters such as Vt and Idsat of both HV N&P  MOS. A systematic problem solving approach combined with  several FA techniques was applied to find the root-cause to be  arsenic outgas cross-contamination.


Gate oxide breakdown failure could be caused by  abnormalities in gate oxide films during oxide growth, plasma  damage during etch, deposition of subsequent layers, charging  due to implantation and contamination. As the cause of the  failure is very complicated, a systematic problem solving  approach has to be applied for root cause investigation. This  paper describes a gate oxide failure case which affects the  electrical parameters such as Vt and Idsat of both HV N&P  MOS. The HV MOS device failed with lower Vt compared to  the baseline. However, the gate oxide thickness was found not  correlated to the lower Vt trend. Failure analysis by I-V curve  tracing on the ET structure of both HV PMOS and NMOS  showed early breakdown symptoms. A systematic problem  solving approach combined with several FA techniques, including electrical curve tracer, Thermally Induced Voltage  Alteration (TIVA), Wright Etch, Inductively Coupled Plasma  Mass Spectrometry (ICP-MS), Time-of-Flight-Secondary Ion  Mass Spectrometry (TOF-SIMS), Chemical Deprocessing, and  Dynamic Secondary Ion Mass Spectrometry (D-SIMS), led to  in-depth understanding of the root cause and the failure  mechanism. This outcome is a joint effort with Fab extensive  investigations. The gate oxide of the HV MOS device was  found contaminated by the As dopant which was prior  implanted in the neighboring shallow cell, out-gassed and  transferred to the HV gate oxide during the high temperature  oxidation process. The As outgas is possible due to insufficient  initial oxide to cover the exposed mask area of the As implant  caused by the gas pressure change in load lock chamber after  preventive maintenance. The cross contaminant As causes the  Vt shift in opposite direction for PMOS and NMOS  respectively.


In this study, a 130nm process wafer suffered early GOX  breakdown for both N and P type HV MOS device at wafer  center which is shown in Fig.1 with the ET full map pattern  signature. It failed for the Vt/Idsat which shifted towards  slower side compared to the baseline. However, the gate oxide  thickness was found not correlated to the lower Vt trend.


The first step in this analysis was the electrical verification by  I-V measurement to determine the extent of gate oxide  damage. Based on the I-V curve tracing on the ET failed  structure for both HV PMOS and NMOS as shown in Fig.2, it  was confirmed to be early breakdown for all analyzed failed  sites.


Fig.1 shows the ET full map suffered both P and  NMOS with center patch pattern signature.


Fig.2 I-V curve tracing on the ET failed structure.


After the initial verification, further electrical fault localization  using TIVA was performed in order to localize whether the  breakdown occurred in the bulk or at the edge of the structure. The breakdown location may reveal the possible cause of the  failure, i.e. the possibility of the failure caused by poly/spacer  etches, gate oxide thinning at the edge or at STI can be ruled  out if the breakdown at the bulk area instead of poly edge  structure.


TIVA is considered as a powerful electrical fault isolation tool  that has been widely used in conductivity related failure  analysis [1]. The TIVA analysis shown in Fig.3 was performed  with distinct TIVA spot detected in the bulk of the structure  for all analyzed failed sites. Thus the breakdown at the bulk  gate oxide was due to deterioration of the gate oxide property. Further top down SEM inspection at the TIVA spot location  performed on poly and active layer did not show any physical  evidence of the anomaly.


After narrowing down the equipment commonality for the gate  oxide furnace tools, a hypothesis was proposed that the root  cause could be due to contamination issue. Wright etch test  was engaged to check for any sign of contamination-related  defect. Wafers were de-layered to active by using 3-4 mins of  HF etching followed by acetone tape to lift off polysilicon and  metal lines. The samples were then dipped in Wright Etch for  2 mins. The SEM inspection revealed contamination-related  defects appeared only on the die area of the failed sample  located at the periphery region of the die where the HV MOS  structure located as shown in Fig.4.


These FA results pinpointed to a gross contamination in the  gate oxide of both PMOS and NMOS. Further ICP-MS  analysis for the affected diffusion chamber did not reveal any  significant metallic ions. However, a slightly enhanced As was  detected (2.5E9) compared to the baseline (1E9) which  provided clues for the possible contaminants in the chamber  environment.


Based on the inline ET data, for the negative biased PMOS  device, the lower the Vt (value) goes, the higher the Idsat (value) is. For the positive biased NMOS device, the lower the  Vt goes, the higher the Idsat is. These results are summarized  in Fig.5 and Fig.6 respectively. It helps to understand the trend  by considering a simple PMOS and NMOS structures as the  following. Assuming that there is a p-type dopant in both P  well and N well of NMOS and PMOS respectively, it could  lead to higher Vt (value) and lower Idsat. In contrast, if there is  a n-type dopant in both P well and N well of NMOS and  PMOS respectively, it could lead to lower Vt (value) and  higher Idsat. Thus, most likely in this case, the contaminant is  a n-type dopant contaminant.


From above FA analysis, the top suspect points to As  contamination. In order to find out whether the As contaminant  is truly present in the gate oxide of the device, D-SIMS


Idsat (uA)


Fig.5 Vt as a function of Idsat for PMOS


Fig.3 TIVA spot detected at bulk region


Fig.4 Wright etch test shows sign of  contamination-related defect.


Fig.6 Vt as a function of Idsat for NMOS


analysis has to be employed. For D-SIMS sample preparation, both the affected and good finish wafers have to be chemically  de-layered by HF until the poly layer. The results with direct  D-SIMS depth profiling shown in Fig.7 were not conclusive  because a background As profile extended from top poly  surface through the oxide layer and its signal is higher than  that in the oxide.


After examining the layout of the poly layer of the device  which consists of a stack of two poly sub-layers (poly1 and 2) separated by a ONO layer, it was found that a thin gap goes  through poly2 and stops at poly 1 surface, that was used to  form the poly1 contacts. Thus it is possible that impurities (including As compounds) induced during the chemical  delayering process slips into this poly2 gap, forming the high  As background in the poly layer. In order to effectively  remove the As background in poly2, a low energy O2+ ion  beam in TOF-SIMS was used to etch away the poly2 layer


completely while poly1 has to be intact to protect the gate  oxide. This is accomplished by monitoring boron profile since  B is doped only in poly2. The low energy O2+ ion etching  process stops when the boron intensity drops to about half of  its original as shown in Fig.8.


After that, the sample will be loaded into D-SIMS for  continuous depth profiling analysis down to the gate oxide.  Fig.9 shows clearly the As emerging in the gate oxide of bad  sample whereas no As is present in the good sample. Therefore  it is confirmed that As is the contaminant in GOX of the  affected device.


The effects of arsenic concentration profiles in gate oxide on  the electrical properties of MOS capacitors had been


investigated by Liao et al [2]. The arsenic in the bulk of gate  oxide degrades the electric property of MOS devices, which  could be attributed to the electron traps introduced by arsenic.


As


Based on the tool commonality, Fab found out that N2 load  lock pressure was increased after PM check with improved N2


load lock sealing. With higher BHU pressure, the process gas


will slow down to reach the bottom position of tube, which  then affect thickness of the native oxide especially at the wafer  center region. This resulted in out-diffusion of As (from prior  implant process) on the thinner native oxide region causing a  counter doping to HV structures.


The systematic approach combined with various relevant FA  techniques can be efficient in identifying the root cause of  contamination in gate oxide failures. The arsenic crosscontamination was found to be the root cause in the particular  failure case in this study. The arsenic source was originated  from the prior shallow implant doping of arsenic and crosscontaminated into the HV GOX with the subsequent thermal  process.

Failure Analysis on Integrated Power Devices


Arthur Chiang, David Le, and Huixian Wu*


Vishay Siliconix


2201 Laurelwood Road, Santa Clara, CA, USA


*Currently with Aptina, San Jose, CA


Phone: 408-970-5558, Email: Arthur.chiang@vishay.com


Abstract: Integrated power devices (IPD,


IC+MOSFETs) have gained popularity for their


excellent power management performance and


compact size. During the development, it was soon


found that exposing the multi-die device for debug


could be challenging. In the article, we will show how


the traditional decapsulation method fails to expose


the device properly for follow up analysis. We will


then present a new technique using laser ablation +


chemical/plasma etch that proves to work for IPD


products. We will then present a complete FA


example combining this decapsulation technique and


many other advanced FA techniques to solve an


elusive failure during the development phase of an


IPD product.


With power semiconductor industry moving towards


high complexity, high efficiency, high switching


frequency and low power dissipation, there is demand


for integrated power devices (IPD) as a low-cost and


high-performance solution. By putting the controller


IC and the power MOSFETs, typically one high-side


and one low-side, on the same package, the close


proximity of the dice greatly reduces parasitics


associated with wires and package, thereby lowering


the ringing in electrical signal. The diminished ringing


allows the circuit to work at higher switching


frequency, which helps to improve power transfer


efficiency and reduce the size of input/output


capacitors and inductors. Hence, the use of IPD


reduces the overall footprint of the power


management circuit by not only being more compact


itself but also requiring less bulky passive


components.


However, IPD products do present some challenges


for failure analysts [1] from the outset, namely,


decapsulation of plastic mold compound to expose the


dice.


In this paper, we will show how the traditional


decapsulation method fails to expose the device


properly for follow up analysis. We will then present a  new technique using laser ablation + chemical/plasma  etch that proves to work for IPD products. We will  then present a complete FA example combining this  decapsulation technique and many other advanced FA  techniques to solve an elusive failure during the  development phase of an IPD product.


2. Failure analysis procedure for IPD  products


The typical FA process flow for IPD products is  shown in Figure 1:


Figure 1: FA process flow on IPD products (IC + MOSFETs)


We will highlight a few key steps in this flow. First, decapsulation of a plastic co-package could be tricky  because the fabrication and the arrangement of these 3


dice could be so different a special procedure might be  needed. We will describe the use of laser ablation as  the first step of local decapsulation [2]. Backside  photoemission (PEM) is used extensively for defect  localization [3], especially in power devices with  heavily doped substrate [4]. Finally, Focused-IonBeam (FIB) will be presented as a powerful tool for  defect revelation [5].


3.  Failure Analysis for IPD Parts


3.1 Decapsulation by the traditional jet  etch method


An essential step of physical failure analysis for  plastic encapsulated devices is the decapsulation. However, traditional wet chemical jet etch method  often fails to work in decapsulating IPD products – it  tends to over-etch or under-etch the area of interest. We will illustrate this deficiency and a workable  solution in great detail below using 2 versions of  Vishay IPD products.


Figure 2a shows an x-ray photo of the bondwire (BW) version of a Vishay IPD. Figure 2b shows an x-ray  photo of the bondwireless (BWL), or clip version of a  Vishay IPD using copper clip as source connection for  both high-side (HS) and low-side (LS) MOSFETs.


Figure 2a: X-ray photo of a BW version of a Vishay  IPD product.


Figure 2b: X-ray photo of a BWL version of a Vishay  IPD product.


Using traditional jet etch, we found that it’s easy to  over-etch some part of the device to ensure all three  dice are exposed. Figure 3a shows the SEM photo of a  jet-etch decapsulated IPD. The wires connecting the  HS MOSFET to the substrate were lifted due to overetching of copper leadframe beneath while other part  of the device, such as the Controller IC, had not been  fully exposed, as detailed in Figure 3b.


Figure 3a: SEM photo of a Vishay BW IPD product  after traditional jet etch. Some wires bonded to the  copper leadframe were lifted while other part of the  device had not been fully exposed.


HS


MOSFET


LS

Controller IC


These wires were lifted  due to over-etch


Figure 3b: High magnification photo of the lifted bond  wires in Figure 3a.


For the BWL version, the challenge is even greater. Figure 4 shows an optical photo of a jet etch


decapsulated BWL version of the same Vishay IPD  product. One can see that while the HS MOSFET  was yet to be fully exposed, the copper clip of the  LS MOSFET had been partially etched away.


Figure 4: Optical photo of a BWL version of Vishay  IPD product after traditional jet etch decapsulation. The copper clip of the LS MOSFET had been partially  etched away while HS MOSFET was still partially  covered by the plastic molding compound.


3.2 An advanced decapsulation method for  IPD products


After numerous experimentations, we found that using  laser ablation to partially remove the molding  compound first and followed by the jet etch to expose  the die surface, one could achieve good decapsulation  result with relative ease. Figure 5 show the optical  images of another Vishay IPD product after the laser  ablation + jet etch decapsulation. All the dice were  clearly exposed while there is no over-etching or  lifting of bond wires.


Figure 5: Laser/chemical decapsulation of an IPD  device showing clean exposure of all dice with all  bond wires still functional.


The power of laser ablation + chemical decapsulation  method is best demonstrated when only a userspecified area of interest needs to be exposed while  the rest of the package is left intact for its mechanical  integrity. For example, for the purpose of circuit  design debug by microprobing of the controller IC, it’s desirable to expose only the IC. Figure 6 shows a  Vishay IPD product with only the IC being exposed. Such capability allows the debug of individual  MOSFET as well, as shown in Figure 7.


It should be noted that to examine the lifted wire  bond, die attach overflow, die surface contamination  et al, chemical etching might wash away crucial  evidence and hence plasma etching [6] is required  under those circumstances, even though it’s a much  slower process.


Remaining  Cu clip

Figure 7b: Selective exposure of the HS MOSFET die.


3.3 Failure analysis example


Over the years, numerous failure analysis techniques  have been developed, including those covered by this  conference. These techniques usually assume the  silicon die is accessible with few exceptions, notably  backside emission microscopy [3]. Thus, it’s fair to  say that exposing the desired die or circuit to analyze  is the most important step in any failure analysis task.


We show below an example of a complete failure  analysis, employing many tools available to most  failure analysts today.


During the development of an IPD product, we found  several units failed VIN leakage (~ 30uA) to GND. Following selective decapsulation of the IC die, as  described above, several front side defect localization  techniques, including use of microprobing, liquid  crystal, and photoemission were employed without  success. Backside photoemission analysis [3] was then  performed and an emission site was detected at the  same location on multiple units. The combination of  chemical deprocessing, optical/SEM inspection and  EDX were then performed. M2-M1 and M1-M1 metal  bridging was eventually found at the photoemission  site.


Figure 8: Backside PEM image. An emission (within  the red circle) was found.


Figure 8 shows the backside PEM localization site. Optical inspection of the PEM area revealed an  anomaly near the PEM site, shown in Figure 9. FIB  in-situ cross-section through the anomaly site  confirmed the root cause of M2 to M1 bridging  through a via, as shown in Figure 10.


Figure 9: Optical Image of the Anomaly near the  backside PEM site.


Figure 10: FIB Cross-section Image of the anomaly  site.


The information acquired by our analysis was  provided to the process engineer. After several runs of  DOE, luckily the process engineer was able to find  the root cause and the fix, which is beyond the scope  of this article, for it. This clearly demonstrated that  failure analysts can play a very important part starting  from the development phase of a new product.


4. Conclusion


In this paper, we showed how traditional  decapsulation method failed to execute the first key  step in physical failure analysis for multi-die IPD  products. We showed how laser ablation + chemical/plasma etch to enable the development of an  optimized decapsulation for each IPD product. We  also presented an example of a complete failure  analysis using all commonly available techniques, as  applied to a particular issue we encountered during the  development of an IPD product, illustrating the  importance of failure analysis, especially for IPD  products where decapsulation requires new  techniques.

Lock-in Phase Mapping of Modulated Reflectance in   Dynamically Operating Mixed-Signal IC Devices


Zhongling Qian*, Christof Brillert and Christian Burmer


Infineon Technologies AG, Munich, Germany  Email: zhongling.qian@infineon.com


The lock-in phase mapping technique with modulated  reflectance is introduced for the first time. With its help, the  modulated reflectance mechanisms using thermal laser in  operating FETs, particularly in the pinch off region, are  clarified. The free carrier absorption mechanism dominates at  a high modulation frequency, while thermo-reflectance  mechanisms at a low modulation frequency. In the pinch-off  region, thermo-reflectance mechanism cannot be neglected  due to extremely low free carrier concentration. The  modulated reflectance signal was unexpectedly observed at  passive poly/oxide/poly capacitance. The advantages of lockin phase mapping in dynamically operating mixed-signal IC  devices are shown in several case studies.


With shrinking feature size of VLSI circuits and decreasing  power supply voltage, the traditional global localization tools  in failure analysis (FA), such as photon emission microscopy (PEM) and thermal laser stimulation (TLS), have met a great  challenge [1-4]. The lock-in assisted TLS techniques, such as  lock-in TIVA, Lock-in assisted SDL and mixed frequency  etc., extend the TLS application range in failure analysis with  the improved detection sensitivity, particularly in noisy  backgrounds [5-8]. The lock-in technique has also been  successfully applied with dynamic PEM for both global  localization of failed Flip-Flops (FF) in a broken scan chain  and enhancement of weak emission spots correlated to one  specific power domain [9].


The recent application of lock-in technique showing switching  activities of operating FETs is  called laser voltage imaging (LVI), or modulated reflectance mapping (MRM), or  frequency mapping (FM) [10-15]. Modulated reflectance (MR) mapping shows unique advantages to improve the  detection sensitivity of FET switching activities at low  operating voltage and frequency.  It can be particularly applied  in IC devices with a long test pattern or working at a  frequency range below 1MHz, where laser voltage probing (LVP) or time-resolved emissions (TRE) are not suitable. The  application of modulation reflectance (MR) mapping has


covered  investigating of active nFET and pFET devices and  passive metal and polysilicon lines,  pinpointing  the position  of the failed FF in a broken scan chain and detecting the  bridge defect test structure [10-15]. In these aforementioned  applications [10-15], only the amplitude from a lock-in  amplifier or from a single frequency at a real-time spectrum  analyzer is used for mapping.


In this paper, lock-in phase mapping of modulated reflectance  is utilized in order to show its unique advantage in comparison  with amplitude mode.  The paper begins with theoretical  consideration about MR mechanism in MOSFET. With help  of lock-in phase mapping method, MR mechanisms in  MOSFET, particularly in the pinch-off region and capacitors (poly/oxide/poly) will be discussed in detail.  The case studies  in mixed-signal and power IC devices confirmed the unique  advantage of phase mode.


Origin of Modulated Reflectance


As a localization tool, MR has been successfully applied in  scan chain analysis in advance logic device. However, its  complicated mechanisms in dynamically operating IC devices  have limited its usage in other areas of failure analysis, particularly in mixed–signal and power IC devices.


The MR mechanisms include electro-optical effects, known as  the Franz-Keldysh effect, free carrier absorption, and thermoreflectance due to temperature variation induced by modulated  electrical field [10-15].  Electro-optical effects normally  happen as a result of band gap shift when a high electrical  field intensity arrives around and above 105V/cm. However, unlike a laser at 1064nm, the laser with a wavelength at  1340nm in our setup has photon energy smaller than silicon  band gap, so it makes a resonant detection at the band gap  impossible and generates also no electron-hole pair. Therefore, absorption and refraction of free carriers are regarded as the  main effect in operating MOSFETs with a detection laser at  1319nm [10]. The thermo-reflectance induced by heating due  to Joule effect in copper line resistance was observed to have a  quadratic relationship to power supply voltage and showed a  clear decrease with an increased modulation frequency [15].

Case study: Combined Dynamic Laser Stimulation and Static Emission  Microscopy techniques applied to Scan Test failure on Mixed Mode device


Magdalena Sienkiewicz, Philippe Rousseille


Freescale Semiconductor, Toulouse, France


This paper presents a case study on scan test reject in a mixed  mode IC. It focuses on the smart use of combined mature FA  techniques, such as Soft Defect Localization (SDL) and  emission microscopy (EMMI), to localize a random scan test  anomaly at the silicon bulk level.


As the complexity and integration level of microelectronic  devices increase, the localization of failures becomes more and  more difficult. The use of a single technique is seldom  sufficient. Instead, a combination of different techniques is  required to obtain successful results. To date, in order to deal  with “challenging” failure modes in mixed mode ICs, multiple  techniques were developed: dynamic emission microscopy (PICA[1], TRE[2]), Dynamic Laser Stimulation techniques (RIL[3], SDL[4], LADA[5], T-LSIM[6], xVM[7], LVP[8]…), Lock-In Thermography [9], Atomic Force Microscopy [10], Magnetic Microscopy [11]. The choice of technique is not  only based on its application field, but also on the access to the  tool, which is not always obvious. The smart combination of a  few failure localization techniques can sometimes give the  same result as the application of one dedicated technique. This  paper presents a case study on advanced mixed-mode IC with  logic scan test failure. It demonstrates how we combined two  techniques available in our lab, i.e. SDL and EMMI, to  localize silicon anomaly in a cost and cycle time effective  approach.


Case study


Device & failure description


The analyzed device was an automotive IC processed in


0.25µm technology. It represented customer field return. The  reject was at ambient temperature. Freescale Automatic Test  revealed scan test failure which was confirmed by using our  digital tester. Each of the DUT outputs was failing for at least  one vector. Additionally, the result of every scan test run was  different (Fig.1). Since too many vectors were randomly  failing, we were unable to apply our diagnostic fault  simulation software. In this case, Laser Voltage Probing (LVP) or Time Resolved Emission (TRE) would be the most suitable  techniques; however we had no direct access to the LVP tool. To comply with the cycle time, we had to use some alternative


techniques. First, we used EMMI technique while the scan test  was running in loop mode. Few abnormal photon emissions  were localized by using CCD camera with 0.8x and 5x  magnification lenses; however all of them were the  consequence of the logic block malfunction. Then, the CLK  signal sent to the device under test (DUT), as well as the  output signals level were measured correct.


Figure 1: Random results on failing device - red color points  on failing vectors and failing states.


These first steps did not help us to progress in our  investigation. Hence, we decided to verify whether the failure  was temperature dependent. In case of any changes in circuit  response induced by temperature variation, we could apply  SDL technique. First, we performed a coarse test by using a fix  temperature gun. After few seconds heating, we observed that  with the temperature increase, many but not all vectors  recovered starting from the beginning of the scan test (Fig. 2).


Figure 2: Failing device recovery when heated.


DUT OUT


Scan test: run 1


Scan test: run 2


Then, the part on its board was placed in our FA localization  tool and scanned by the “thermal” laser beam (1.3µm). At this  time we were just verifying the scan test results - no link was  established between FA localization tool and the DUT output. The same device behavior was observed - many but not all  vectors recovered at the beginning of the scan test.


SDL analysis


We used our FA tool with SDL capability to localize the  sensitive zone. A 1.3µm wavelength laser beam was scanning  the DUT while a slightly modified scan test ran in loop on the  digital tester (Fig.3). This modification was needed to extract  the DUT’s PASS/FAIL state and was done in the way to  simplify SDL setup, i.e., no synchronization between DUT and  laser moves. In this setup the circuit was already at the edge of  PASS/FAIL state, i.e. by playing mainly with the laser beam  power and pixel dwell time the PASS/FAIL signal switched  the state from FAIL to PASS.


Figure 3: Principle of SDL analysis [12].


The scan test loop ran on the first 600 vectors maximum. PASS/FAIL state was verified only for 25 vectors and for all  output signals. Four specific commands were programmed  inside the scan test loop and one OUTPUT signal was added, SDL (PASS/FAIL) on our digital tester. These commands: CTRG (vector 298), RSET (vector 300), CJMP (vector 325) and JMP (vector 600) allowed respectively to reset the fail flag  of the electrical tester, to clear the fail register, to jump to the  beginning of the scan (vector 0) if any failure was detected and  to jump to the vector 0 (Fig. 4). SDL signal equaled 0 between  vectors 0 and 325 and it equaled 1 between vectors 326 and


600. It means that if any failure was detected, the SDL signal  always equaled 0. Otherwise, the SDL signal equaled 0 for a  little more than half of the scan test period and equaled 1 for  the rest of the scan test period (Fig. 5). Note that the scan test  period was variable.  In case of fail state detection, the scan  test period took a little more than a half of pass state period. Such variation of the scan test period does not matter as long  as the laser displacement is not synchronized with the SDL  signal. This setup could be used with analog signal acquisition  mode on our failure localization tool. In fact, the SDL signal  was integrated by the SDL system for each pixel and its mean  voltage value indicated if the test was PASS or FAIL. The only  condition to respect was to ensure that the pixel dwell time  was longer than one period of the scan test. This guaranteed  the detection of any changes in the SDL signal (PASS/FAIL  state).


The SDL results are presented in figure 6. The SDL mapping  showed only one abnormal spot, corresponding to the PASS  state of the scan test. More precisely, an output transistor of a  flip flop, a metal3 line and metal3/metal2 via above this flip  flop were localized inside clksys_gen block. This block  generates a high frequency clock for the digital block from the  CLK signal applied on the external pin. An anomaly with the  clock signal could perfectly explain the failure mode – the  scan test behavior. However, the choice of the physical  analysis was not obvious: step by step deprocessing (risk of  missing via anomaly) or cross section (risk of missing  transistor anomaly in the flip flop block). Finally, we decided  to apply the EMMI technique with focus on the suspected  clksys_gen block.

test period variation.


Figure 6: SDL results: one abnormal spot, corresponding to  the PASS state of the scan test, was localized in clksys_gen


block.


EMMI analysis


The EMMI technique was applied from the front side of the  chip. The electrical setup was modified. As the anomaly was  found in the clksys_gen block, instead of complex scan test  run, only the CLK signal was sent to the DUT. Moreover, to  avoid a photon emission masking issue, a high magnification  lens was used and all strong photon emission areas were out of  the camera field of view. Abnormal photon emission was  observed from clksys_gen block after 6min acquisition time (Fig. 7). More precisely, this photon emission was captured  from a pMOS transistor in the inverter16 block, close to the  SDL spot. Just one of the four fingers of the gate on this  transistor was a source of photon emission.


Figure 7: EMMI result: photon emission from inverter16 in


clksys_gen block.


Results correlation & discussion


The SDL and EMMI results were correlated first on the layout (Fig. 8) and then on the electrical schematic (Fig.9). Metal3  line with metal3/metal2 via localized by the SDL technique  were directly connected to the input of inverter16 localized by  the EMMI technique. The heterogeneous photon emission  from pMOS transistor in inverter16 block seemed the origin of  the failure. Concerning SDL results, the heat induced along  metal3 line by the “thermal” laser beam, propagated to the  inverter16 input. It modified enough the defect properties to  cause the clock signal transient recovery (scan test PASS). Based on these findings, step by step deprocessing was  performed on pMOS transistor from inverter16 block.


Figure 8: Layout extract with SDL and EMMI results pointed  on.


Figure 9: Extract of clksys_gen block with SDL and EMMI


results pointed on.


Physical analysis


Step by step deprocessing was carefully carried out with the  special focus on EMMI localized pMOS transistor. An  anomaly in silicon bulk under the spacer was highlighted at the  exact EMMI localization (Fig.10). The local defect on the  channel of the pMOS probably disturbed the hole current flow. This soft defect on pMOS transistor induced the intermittent  switching of the clock signal, which in turn generated the scan  test failure.

Figure 10: Physical analysis results: Silicon bulk anomaly  under spacer at the EMMI localization.


This paper presents a soft failure case study on mixed mode  IC. A complementary use of SDL and EMMI techniques  allowed us to successfully localize the origin of scan test  random failure in 10 days. The SDL techniques helped us to  isolate the failing block while the EMMI technique, applied  with simplified setup, pinpointed a failing device. Physical  analysis highlighted a silicon bulk anomaly on a pMOS  transistor localized by EMMI technique.


Moreover, this paper presents an interesting SDL setup and  talks about smart use of EMMI technique. Finally, it  demonstrates how the innovative use of well established  localization techniques can be used to analyze failures.
