Anomalies in  silicon bulk  under spacer


From EBT to LVP, from 130nm to 28nm node,


internal timing characterization evolution


Thierry Parrassin, Philippe Larre, Sylvain Dudit, Michel Vallet


STMicroelectronics, 850 rue Jean Monnet, 38920 Crolles, France


In semiconductor industries, development of new technologies  and new products generally follows a phase of yield  improvement where Failure Analysis expertise is used to  locate and fix killer defects and for design debug. When  process and design reach a certain level of maturity, a second  phase of optimization, qualification and reliability is executed  in which Failure Analysis expertise is used for internal timing  characterization of integrated circuit and results are compared  with design/process simulations. In order to reduce the cost of  testing during manufacturing, circuits embed Built in Self  Timing Characterizer (BISC) for timing measurements inside  critical functional blocks. Thanks to advanced integration, the  last CMOS technologies allow high performance in terms of  speed. Arithmetic and Logical Units (ALU) are able to work at  frequencies greater than few GHz and some memories’ access  time is lower than hundreds picoseconds.


In the CMOS 40nm analysis case study presented in this  paper, a BISC measurement of memories’ access times gives  different results than what was expected from simulation. Internal probing becomes mandatory to understand this critical  timing issue. A complete comparison is done between the 3  contactless probing techniques available in our laboratory  which are the E-Beam Testing (EBT), Time Resolved  Emission (TRE) and the recent Laser Voltage Probing (LVP) to highlight strength and weakness of each probing techniques  in front of this timing related defect.


We demonstrate that the LVP is an inevitable technique to  address the nanometer-scale technologies in terms of spatial  resolution, low voltage measurements and timing  performance.


Internal probing for timing characterization of integrated  circuit needs to hold appropriate failure analysis tools to  address the new nanometer scale technologies. With the  integration of transistors, probing tools have to get over  several constraints imposed by the down-scaling of integrated  circuit. First of all, a constraint of the physical access to the  node that need to be probed, this critical parameter is related


to spatial resolution of equipments which will define the size  of probing area. Secondly, a constraint related to signal  acquisition capability with the drop down of the operational  voltage of digital circuits. Finally the timing resolution of such  debug design tools appears also as a key parameter due to the  increase of execution frequency of new integrated circuit.


In our lab, we own three techniques suitable for contactless  internal probing which are:


- The proven and experienced EBT technique


- The well known TRE technique


- The more recent LVP technique


In a first part we will describe briefly these three different  approaches and we will compare the performances between  each technique based on the capability to access and probe  signals, the spatial resolution and the timing limitations.


In a second part, we will apply each technique on a case study  related to memory access time issue in a digital CMOS 40nm  test chip. Access times (Taa) are initially measured by the  circuit itself thanks to an embedded BISC and the results are  compared to the simulations. In this case study, expected  access times are in hundred of picoseconds order of magnitude  and the mismatch is in the order of ten or so picoseconds. Signal rising/falling edge measurements will also be  mandatory to understand the failure.


Contactless probing techniques used in internal  timing characterization


E-Beam Testing (EBT)


Until three to four years ago, E-Beam Testing was the most  widely used contactless probing technique for internal timing  characterization. It is based on scanning electron microscopy. The electron beam is synchronized with the test sequence  which is emulating the Device Under Test (DUT) and  secondary electrons, which will be influenced by voltage  potential, will be classified in time bins in a way to reconstruct  waveforms of signal available on metal lines [1].


Physical access to the signal


Physical access to the node of interest is the main limitation of  EBT technique essentially due to the bad penetration depth of  the electrons (~1µm at 10KeV). Consequently, the signal of  interest must be available at the surface of the chip.


EBT is usually performed from the frontside of the DUT. This was very efficient on older technologies with few metal  layers where it was possible to probe directly the metal nodes  through the passivation layer by capacitive coupling. Dealing  with more advanced technologies, it was needed to access  embedded metal levels using ebeam probe pads thanks to a  Focused Ion Beam (FIB) as shown in figure1.


Figure 1: X-section and topview of a FIB deposited probe  point


As generation of sub-micron and nanometrics circuit appears, the number of metals increases and FIB edit for probe  deposition became more difficult. A way to avoid these  problems was to implement at design level specific E-beam  probe pads for design debugging and characterization. See  Figure 2.


Figure 2: E-Beam probe pad. Left: simple E-beam pad, Right: Improved E-beam pad.


Nowadays, new package assembly of IC such as flip-chip or  3D integration becomes more frequent and prevents access to  the frontside of device. Some work has been done to perform  EBT through the backside by thinning the silicon [2]. This  approach is suitable for debug purpose but not applicable for  fine timing characterization since the silicon removal is  shifting timings and electrical behavior of transistors.


In conclusion, EBT can still be used in advanced technology  for timing characterization but it implies circuitry accessibility  with embedded ebeam probe pads. Unfortunately, this


approach is hardly applicable due to price issue, space  limitation on silicon and signal accessibility.


Spatial resolution


EBT is a Scanning Electron Microscope based system, therefore spatial resolution for imaging and probing is linked  to the primary beam spot size which is 0.08µm. That makes  the EBT the best system for zooming and for probe placement. Signal acquisition


EBT is able to measure very low voltage. The voltage  resolution, which defines the smallest detectable variation for  a given bandwidth is 10mV for 20MHz. It can be useful for  internal Voltage drop measurement on power supplies.


Timing measurement


The timing accuracy is given for 40ps and the timing  resolution which defines the maximum frequency of  observable signals is 8.75GHz in equivalent bandwidth.


Time Resolved Emission (TRE)


TRE is an optical technique based on the photoemission which  occurs at transistor level during commutation. It uses a single  point InGaAs photodiode detector to detect photons trough the  backside of devices. Each collected photons is saved with a  time stamp thanks to the synchronization of the diode with the  test sequence. TRE allows histogram reconstruction of  photons which is an image of the dynamic current of the  commutating gate [3].


Since TRE is a near infrared optical technique, access to  transistors is not possible through frontside due to the  metallization stack. It is an exclusive backside technique  which requires a preparation of the circuit (backside thinning  at 100µm typically) in order to use the most efficient objective (Solid Immersion Lens 220x or 350x) for imaging and  probing. That is a positive aspect since no physical access  limitation will be faced by TRE.


The resolution is limited by optical performance of the lens for  imaging, which is about 310nm for the 220x SIL (Numerical  Aperture = 2.45). Concerning the probe placement, there is a  limitation due to the dimension of the optical fiber diameter  used for probing. This optical fiber diameter can be reduced  using blades in the optical path. By the way, for advanced  technologies (<40nm), it is not possible to probe individual  transistors into a high density area since multiple transistors  signals will be collected at the same time. The TRE technique  is limited to “gate” level probing and the measurement  includes a cross talk of all transistors seen inside the probe  area.


Signal acquisition


TRE acquires photons during gate commutations; the  histogram result doesn’t give any information about digital  levels inside the measured gate or about rising/falling edge  timing. Only rising/falling edge placement information is  accessible and some rising/falling edge information can be  interpreted by the width of the emission peak.


One of the main limitations of TRE when dealing with  advanced technologies is the exponential dependency of the  number of photons emitted versus the voltage power supply. Consequently, TRE is not efficient enough to address  advanced technologies which are working at low voltage (limit at 0.8V on recent technologies) [4]. A second main  limitation of this photon based probing method is that since  PMOS are emitting much less than NMOS transistors, it is  very challenging to access PMOS switching information.


However, one benefit of TRE is that this is the only technique  able to detect dynamic leakages during switching.


The timing accuracy is given for 25ps, the bandwidth of the  system is 7GHz.


Laser Voltage Probing (LVP)


The LVP system is an optical microscope using a 1064nm  laser probe to measure signals on active regions of an IC and  acquiring voltage waveforms by analyzing the phase and the  amplitude of the reflected laser beam. This reflected laser  beam will be influenced by the state of the active region due to  the varying charge density in the space-charge region and the  Franz-Keldysh effect [5]. The last generation of LVP system (Phase Differential Probing, PDP) analyses amplitude  modulation, phase modulation and polarization difference of  the reflected beam versus the incident in order to acquire  quickly voltage/logic waveforms with very good SNR[6].


Figure 3: diagram of the Laser Voltage Probing PDP  detection scheme.


Since LVP is also based on a near infrared optical system, it  will have similar characteristics as TRE. Thus, physical access  to transistors is limited to the backside.


The optical resolution is driven by the performance of the  optical system for imaging. Since LVP is using a 1064nm  laser source, resolution is better than TRE (about 275nm). The  spatial resolution for probing application is defined by the  probe placement accuracy. Laser Scanning Microscope (LSM) approach allows reducing drastically the equivalent pixel size  by using LSM zoom IN option. Figure 4 demonstrates that the  probing spatial resolution is even better than optical  resolution. On this acquisition, two transistors 180nm away  from each other were probed without any crosstalk effect.


Figure 4: LVP results on a 45nm D flip flop layout (left). CLK  and CLKN signals are probed without any crosstalk effect (right). These two signals are separated by only 180nm.


This excellent probing resolution (better than 180 nm) combined with backside access allows LVP technique to  probe any individual transistors down to our current most  aggressive technology node which is 28nm.


The main motivation for the LVP technique development was  to address advanced technologies. As we discussed earlier, photon based technique efficiency decreases exponentially  with the voltage supply. LVP signal is also affected by the  voltage drops but in this case we have a linear dependency  which makes possible to acquire waveforms at very low  voltage (600mV experimented) on different type of device. Moreover, NMOS and PMOS transistors can be probed  without any differences in term of signal strength due to  different physical effect used which is equivalent on these two  types of transistors. Let’s add that forward and reverse diodes, antenna diodes or MOS capacitances can also be probed  without any limitation.


One limitation of LVP could be the invasiveness of the  1064nm laser which can alter the functionality of the probed  transistor if laser power is too high. But since LVP gives  access to clear signals with very low incident laser power (<1mW), this impact is still negligible up to now. One way to


overcome this limitation if it starts to be more noticeable on  future technology would be to use antenna diode or specific  probe pad integrated at design level.


The timing accuracy is given for 10ps and a bandwidth of  23GHz. LVP is adequate for high frequency timing  measurement and rising/falling edge measurement of signal  with a very good resolution.


Obviously, contactless internal probing techniques follow the  technology nodes. EBT is more dedicated for “large” technologies (>130nm) in frontside access, and need FIB or  integrated internal probe pads if we want to measure signals  on technologies below. With the arrival of backside packaging  and the reduction of the technology node, the TRE is useful  for backside access measurement until the 40nm technology  node. But it becomes inoperative on new high-K metal gates  technologies (since 32nm) due to the spatial resolution and the  voltage limitation. Nowadays the LVP is able to cover the  technology nodes below 32nm at low voltage with a good  timing resolution (table1 and chart1).


Table 1: Summary table of the three techniques


Chart 1: limitations of the probing techniques in terms of  voltage and timing resolution.


Application case for timing characterization


Since ICs and especially memories are running faster and  faster in advanced technologies (access time reduced from few  nanoseconds to hundred picoseconds), it becomes challenging  to characterize memories timing directly with Automated Test  Equipment (ATE).


In our qualification test chip, in order to reduce testing time  and eliminate ATE dependency, memories are self  characterized with an embedded on-chip Built in self  Characterizer (BISC) block. Access time, setup time and  maximum operating frequency measurements are executed by  this block with a pretty good accuracy (access time  characterizer resolution is about 20ps @vdd=1V).


The BISC module is composed of:


 A pulse generator (PG). It is picking the clock input of  the memory and some data outputs of memory, usually  the fastest and the slowest output data signals. The  delay between the clock and the output is the access  time of the memory. A pulse is generated by the PG  where the width is proportional to the access time. Then this pulse is sent to the Access Time  Characterizer (ATC).


 The ATC which analyzes the pulse width sent by the  PG delivers a digital code which gives the value of the  access time of the memory for the corresponding  output data signal.


Figure 5: Schematic of the Built In Self Characterizer.


The issue on this CMOS40nm case study is related to a  mismatch of access time values measured by the BISC versus  the expected value from SPICE simulations. Silicon is  measured too fast. Measurement on worst case can show 30% mismatch. A failure analysis is requested for internal timing  characterization in order to check integrities of delays and for  signal falling/rising edge measurement.


The three techniques EBT, TRE and LVP have been used for  the timing characterization.


E-Beam results (EBT)


EBT was applicable on this specific case because of the  integration of ebeam probe pads on critical nets and since this  device was accessible from the frontside in SBGA package  type (no sample preparation).


Figure 6 describes EBT measurement on accessible EBEAM  probe pads at 0.9V. This operating low voltage is chosen  because the timing mismatch is amplified at lower voltage. Since EBT is able to probe at low voltage, we use this method  to maximize the timing issue to fit the timing performance of  the EBT technique.


Figure 6:  EBT waveforms. Left: taa measured on memories @0.9V for read operation (ck to Qn) =735ps. Right: pulse  measured at input of ATC = 485ps. 250ps lost in the


propagation path.


Taa measured on memory probes is 735ps which is matching  the expected value from SPICE simulation. Due to EBEAM  probe pads implementation it was not possible to check the  pulse width at the output of the PG. The only accessible  measurement area was located at the input of the ATC; after  the signal of interest went through ten logic cells (see figure  7). The pulse width measured at the input of ATC is 485ps  which is the digital code that is going out from the BISC  module.


Considering both contributions from free carrier absorption  and thermo-reflectance to the MR signal under a thermal laser  detection,  the MR signal


∆


can be expressed in the first order  linear approximation as follows:


∆ ∆ ∆ ∆


(1) where ∆R is the variation of the reflectivity R at the interface,


are periodic variations of free carriers (hole or  electron) and local temperature due to electrical field  modulation,   and  are MR coefficients due to free carrier  absorption and thermo-reflectance, correspondingly.


Both modulated  reflectance  coefficients are on  the  order   of   10-4 and 10-5, and depend on optical and thermal material  properties. For silicon, they may have different signs.


For	silicon,


=1.5x10-4/K and in the FET inversion channel


<0 as a result of the strong absorption due to high free  carrier concentration [10]. According to Eq. (1), the  contribution to the MR signal from free carrier absorption can  counteract the effect from thermal-reflectance.


In a layered system like MOSFET, the reflectivity also  depends on the thickness of oxide, metal, buried layer and  other layers due to using coherent laser detection. Normally  MR mapping is applied from die backside, so the die thickness  must be also taken into account. The reflectance signal can be  enhanced or cancelled out due to the interference effect of  reflected coherent lights from different layers. The layer  thickness can experience a variation as a result of thermal  expansion due to local temperature periodic change, which  makes also a contribution to the MR signal.


There are several in-depth investigations of MR mechanisms  in active MOSFET devices [10,12-13]. The pinch-off region (POR) is a very critical region in the saturation state of a FET.  In the channel pinch off region, a lateral and transverse  electrical field intensity up to 105V/cm is within easy reach. The photoemission of hot carriers was observed during  switching events in the pinch-off region, which is the basic  principle for PICA imaging and TRE probing [2]. The pinchoff region in nFET was believed to have been observed using  MR mapping at a low modulation frequency 7.33 KHz, since  there exist both an electric field and heat dissipation profile  peaks [13].    However, in Ref. [10], a drop of MR signal  instead of a MR peak was observed in the pinch-off region  with higher modulation frequencies of 11MHz. This  discrepancy may be due to different dominant mechanisms at  low and high modulation frequencies according to Eq. (1).


Lock-in Phase Detection


The lock-in technique can provide not only amplitude  information but also the relative phase shift to a reference  signal. In lock-in thermography [15], the imaging lock-in  algorithm was used to improve thermal sensitivity to below


100μK.  Particularly the lock-in phase imaging can overcome  artifacts due to a large difference of thermal emissivity in  materials in an IC die [16].


The advantage of lock-in phase mapping is its independency  of the intensity of detection laser, which is very apparent from  the formula below:


θ= arctan(Y/X)


where θ is the phase of MR signal, and correspondingly, X


and Y are the in-phase and out-of-phase components.


Both X and Y components of the MR signal are proportional  to the reflection intensity of a detection laser. On one side, the  intensity/phase fluctuation of such a detection laser  deteriorates its intensity stability. It can be solved by using a  very stable laser source. On the other hand, the DC reflectivity  R depends on materials (Cu, Al, Si, Poly and so on), layer  thickness and doping dose in an IC device, the real MR signal  can be obtained only by using ∆R/R, where ∆R is the  reflectivity variation.  A typical case is shown in Figure 1. The  large reflectivity difference in well, S/D regions and metal  lines are much more apparent in the surface plot of laser  scanning reflectance image (the lower image in Fig. 1).


Figure 1: Laser scanning reflectance image of a test structure  from backside (upper); The corresponding surface plot of the  reflectance image shows large reflectivity difference from  different materials/regions (lower).


However, the phase mapping by dividing of both in-phase and  out-of-phase components will greatly remove the influence  from laser and reflectivity large variation and obtain phase


information of real MR signal ∆R/R, which is to be  demonstrated later. It must be pointed out that the phase  mapping of MR signal is based on a sufficient S/N of its  amplitude.  The lower the S/N, the longer the corresponding  integration time, i.e. the longer the frame time.


Setup and Test


The setup is based on a Hamamatsu Phemos system in  combination with a high frequency lock-in amplifier, and an  external InGaAs APD detector (up to 8GHz). The thermal  laser (1340nm) is used as an optical source with a maximum  output power of 500mW.  The best focus can be realized with  an ULWD 50x lens (NA=0.76) from backside. A typical  image acquisition frame time is 36 seconds.  The output of the  reference frequency source from the lock-in amplifier serves  as a driver source for MR mapping. The amplitude and phase  outputs from the lock-in amplifier are sent to the analog input  of this system for mapping.


Test 1:  10umx10um pMOSFET


In order to properly compare both the amplitude and phase  mapping techniques of modulated reflectance a large test  pFET structure (10umx10um) was used, shown in the upper  side of Figure 1.


In the test, the source and well were connected to 0V, and the  gate was set to -3V, so that this pFET stayed in an on state and  a p type inversion layer was formed below gate oxide in nwell region. The drain was modulated between 0V and -5V  with a modulation frequency of 10MHz and 50% duty cycle. With the drain at -5V, the condition of pinch-off appearance  was met. The initial result is shown in Figure 2.  The MR  amplitude along source/channel/drain regions can be  explained very well by using the concentration profile of free  holes in the inversion channel, as in Ref [10].  The line  profiles of amplitude shows a clear laser intensity fluctuation.  The comparison between amplitude and phase profiles of  mapping show clearly that the phase mapping has a better  SNR across the whole range.


The dotted red line shows clearly a maximum in MR signal  near the source region. At the position of the dot-dashed red  line, the phase mapping profile showed a sudden drop, which  indicates a boundary, i.e. pinch off region, from active channel  to drain region.  However, in the amplitude mapping there is  no drop observed. The phase in the active channel regions  show a gradual decrease from source to drain region, which  indicates that there are at least two factors contributing to the  MR mechanism of a pFET at operating state.  One of them is  clearly modulated free holes absorption and refraction. However, in the pinch-off depletion region, the free hole  concentration becomes extremely low. It makes other factors, such as thermo-reflectance due to Joule heating in pinch-off  depletion region, dominant. The Joule heating can happen also  in the channel region, but its contribution to MR signal is  relatively small due to a high free carrier concentration.  According to Eq. (1), the contributions from two effects have


different signs. Therefore their contribution to MR signal  cancels out each other, which makes a gradual change of MR  phase along the gate region.


Figure 2: The amplitude and phase mappings of modulation  reflectance of a pFET operating activity (Left: overlay with  optical pattern image; Right: line profile and grey image of  modulated reflectance). A dotted red line and a dot/dash line  are marked for comparison. Two brown lines in grey image  show the direction of line profile.


Test 2:  MR Dependence on Modulation Frequency


Figure 3: The MR amplitude comparison in the channel  region at different modulation frequencies.   The insets are  corresponding MR mapping images of the gate region. The  red dot-lined arrows in insets indicate the line scan direction  from drain to source region.


In order to study the influence of modulation frequency on the  MR signal, different modulation frequencies were used in the  same test sample as in test1above.  The corresponding result is  shown in Figure 3. To obtain a good S/N, the line scan results  are obtained by integration of MR signal along gate width.


With the modulation frequency decreased from 1MHz to 25  KHz, the MR signal in the POR region at the drain side is  observed to increase significantly. The noise of MR mapping  at 25 KHz is stronger than that at 1MHz due to 1/f noise. This  experiment clearly shows that some mechanism at the low  frequency is dominant. It is different from the dominant  mechanism of free carrier absorption and refraction at a higher  modulation frequency.  Thermo-reflectance and thermal  expansion are two possible candidates responsible for  contributing to MR signal at a low modulation frequency. Since the inversion layer normally is in the range of 10nm, which is much smaller than the detection laser wavelength  1340nm. So the thermal expansion of inversion layer can be  neglected.


Test 3:  Bypass Chain


In order to check whether the setup is also suitable for logic IC  devices, one test device fabricated at 110nm was used in the  bypass mode. The bypass chain consists of numerous serially  connected inverters.


Figure 4: Comparison of dynamic photon emission at 50MHz (integration time 60s) and MR mapping at two different  operating frequencies 1MHz and 10MHz (time 36s/frame).


The input of one of bypass chains is connected to a 50MHz  signal for backside inspection using dynamic photon emission. The photon emission image induced by hot  carriers  in  pinchoff regions is shown in the upper side of Figure 4.  It was  observed that the dynamic PEM intensity decreases with the  magnification from x0.8 to x50 lens.   On the contrary, the MR  signal intensity increases with the magnification. With higher  magnification the radius of the laser focus point becomes  smaller. Most of the reflected laser brings MR information  from FETs at the operating state. At a low magnification the


laser focus point (~2um) is much larger than channels in nand pFETs. Therefore, for global localization using MRM at a  lower magnification, the detection laser power must be  increased.


Figure 5: Modulation reflectance mapping of the output driver  region in a driver IC (15V domain).  Upper: MR amplitude  mapping; Middle: Overlay of MRM image with the optical  pattern image; Lower: MR phase mapping.


With this setup the MR detection can be realized from 50MHz  down to 1MHz for a laser scan frame time as slow as 36s, typically shown in the middle (10MHz) and right images (1MHz) in Figure 4. The higher the modulation frequency, the  smaller the localization spots (red spot in Figure 4). The direct  use of the reference signal output from lock-in amplifier as the  input signal to the chain is believed to give a better correlation  between the MR signal and reference signal.  In this case, note  the MR signal is strongly dependent on the focus plane, so a  fine focus tuning is a must.


Applications in Mixed-Signal IC Devices


Poly/Oxide/Poly Capacitor


The MRM has been typically used to pinpoint the failing flip  flops of open scan chains in advanced modern digital ICs [14]. However, there is no report on its application on mixed-signal  or power IC devices.


The test device is a power driver with 8 output channels and  one of them was activated by using a 1MHz TTL input signal.  The MR mapping from backside was done at first in the output  driver region in the 15V power domain.


Figure 6: Probing results at two capacitors.  An apparent  180°C phase shift between the two capacitors was observed


To our surprise, a very strong MR signal was observed on  both poly/oxide/poly capacitors CAP 1 and 2 (Figure 5). The  signal in capacitor areas is very homogenous not only in the  amplitude mapping but also in the phase mapping. The  homogenous phase indicates a single mechanism responsible  for the origin of MR signal in such a capacitor. This capacitor  is a passive element, which is different from the varactor (poly/oxide/well) in MOSFET with drain/source connected to  the well [10]. Its polysilicon plates are highly doped and  silicided for a high electrical conductance, so no inversion  layer is formed at one of polysilicon plates.  Therefore, the  mechanism of free carrier absorption can be excluded. The  MR signal can be generated by thermo-reflectance due to  Joule heating of a nonideal capacitor in an AC electrical field. The further confirmation of this MR mechanism in the passive  capacitor needs further experiments on a capacitor test  structure with varying different parameters, such as the  modulation frequency and voltage applied on the capacitors, like in Ref. [15].


In addition, a strong contrast between CAP1 and CAP2 was  observed in the MR phase mapping (lower in Figure 5).  Such  a white-black contrast indicates a large phase shift. To confirm  it, MR probing was tried at both passive capacitors and  unexpectedly the waveforms at both capacitors were obtained (Figure 6). The waveforms confirmed a 180° phase shift.  It  indicates that MR phase mapping can provide not only normal  MR spots but also additional phase shift or time delay  information.


LDMOS


Further probing the pre-driver consisting of multi-finger drainextended LDMOS FETs, a relatively weak signal was only  observed in the MR phase mapping, shown in the red block in  Figure 5. Due to the stronger signals from both capacitors in  the MR amplitude mapping (upper in Figure 5), the signal


from the pre-driver is nearly hidden in the noise. In contrast to  the MR amplitude mapping, the lock-in phase MR mapping at  the predriver shows a better S/N in the lower image of Figure


5.


Figure 7: Overlay of MR mapping image with the layout. In  the extended drain region, no MR signal was observed.


The detailed MR Mapping of a LDMOS FET is shown in  Figure 7. There is no MR signal to be observed in the  extended drain region.  The stronger MRM signal was  observed under the multi-finger gates.


Bipolar Transistor


The NPN bipolar transistor (a) in Fig. 8) in a mixed-signal IC  device serves as a protection diode to VDD at one input pin. The transistor’s base is shorted to its collector.   Electrical  measurements showed an early reverse breakdown voltage at


4.5V between emitter and collector. However, the basecollector and base-emitter showed a typical PN junction  characteristic.  PEM inspection showed spots covering the  entire emitter area.  MR mapping using a 10MHz input signal  was used to map the variation of free carrier concentration due  to a local reversed-biased electrical field, shown in b and c in  Figure 8.  The comparison clearly showed that the phase  mapping of MR is more sensitive at this operating frequency.  As a reference device, Image d in Figure 6 showed a  homogenous electrical field in the emitter region in the MR  phase mapping. However, the emitter of the failing device (c  in Figure 8) showed an inhomogeous electric field at a reverse  bias.  The SCM inspection of the cross sections through  emitter/base/collector showed that the emitter junction of a  failed device is deeper than that of a reference device, which  makes the corresponding base region thin enough to cause an  early breakthrough.


Figure 8: The comparison shows the phase MR imaging is  more sensitive to local electrical field at 10MHz (b and c). The  failed device showed an inhomogeous emitter region (c and  d).


Lock-in phase mapping of modulated reflectance was for the  first time introduced as an enhancement to the normal  amplitude mode. It showed a greatly improved SNR than the  amplitude mode, particularly in situations with a large MR  amplitude contrast. The MR mechanisms including free  carries absorption, thermo-reflectance and thermal expansion  in MOSFET devices, particularly in the pinch off region, are  clarified with the assistance of phase MR mapping. At a high  modulation frequency the mechanism of free carrier  absorption dominates in channel region, while at a low  modulation frequency thermo-reflectance can be dominant. In  pinch-off region the contribution due to thermo-reflectance  cannot be neglected due to low free carrier concentration. The  applications to map in bipolar transistors, LDMOS and  capacitors in mixed-signal and power devices are also shown. The MR amplitude and phase mapping at capacitors was for  first time observed and explained due to the thermoreflectance induced by Joule heating in an AC electrical field.   The probing at passive elements extends the usage range of  the MR probing method.

Advanced Laser Probe Techniques Applied to FA of RF  Integrated Circuits - A Case Study


Mark Kimball


Maxim Integrated Products, Inc.


Christopher Nemirow


DCG Systems


Failure analysis of RFIC’s can be a challenging problem, particularly as frequencies ascend into the medium to high  GHz region.  As frequency goes up, active probes become less  and less accurate due to capacitive loading of circuit nodes, and capacitive coupling of stray signals into the probe from  nearby circuit traces.  We have found that Laser Voltage  Imaging (LVi) offers an alternative measurement technique  that can avoid these problems.  But our work also showed that  there are unusual failure signatures which appear as signal  frequencies go up.  A combination of LVI and RF-SDL was  found to yield the best result.


An RF transceiver operating at approximately 5.8GHz was  experiencing high failure rates in reliability testing and also in  elevated customer return rates.  The failure signature was  unusual in that the units were only failing one test parameter:  transmitter power output.  Power supply currents were OK, no  unusual emission sites were found, and no deviant pin to pin  signature was observed, yet the devices had low power output. For purposes of clarity, a simplified block diagram of the  transmitter section of the device is shown in Figure 1.  The  circuit consists of a mixer, which accepts low-frequency data  signals and modulates an on-chip 5.8GHz local oscillator.  The  output of the mixer is fed to a variable-gain amplifier and then  to two power amplifier stages.  All of the stages employ SiGe  bipolar junction transistors (BJT’s) for signal processing.


Active probing of internal circuit nodes with a passive probe (a 500 ohm resistor connected to a 50-ohm semi-rigid coaxial  cable) initially exonerated the power amplifier chain.  However, attempts to measure low-level signals in the variable  gain amplifier (VGA) and mixer were stymied due to parasitic  signals being coupled from the high-level power amplifier  circuitry.  In an attempt to address this problem, the power  amplifiers were disabled by FIB cuts which severed the DC  bias connections to the amplifiers.  The idea was to prevent


the high level RF signals from masking the low-level signals  of interest.  Even with this approach, parasitic signals coupling  from the local oscillator swamped out the signals in the VGA  and mixer.  We determined this to be the case by simply  hovering the probe a few microns away from the probe point, measuring the signal amplitude, and then dropping the probe  onto the circuit node.  No difference in the signal levels was  observed.


Laser Voltage Imaging Description


There are a number of electro-optical effects that can be used  to monitor signals in silicon-based integrated circuits.  These  include electric field-induced electroabsorption and  electrorefraction, as well as carrier-based absorption and  refraction [1].  At a laser probe wavelength of 1.34um, electric  field effects are much smaller than carrier-based effects.


Free-carriers in the active device modulate the laser probe  beam.  The modulation frequency of the reflected laser  matches that of the device.  A system consisting of a high  speed photodetector and spectrum analyzer monitors the  strength of this modulation and maps it onto a laser scanning  microscope (LSM) image.   The resultant laser voltage image  highlights areas of the device that are operating at one or more  specific frequencies, as well as the relative strength of the  modulation.  See Figure 2 for a block diagram of the system  used for this work.


For the device described in this paper, the interaction is on a  very local scale.  As such, laser voltage imaging is a potential  alternative to active probing.  LVI is particularly attractive in  this case because it avoids the capacitive coupling and loading  effects that affect physical


probes.


Several vendors offer tools to perform laser voltage  probing/imaging.  However, most work to date has been done  on digital CMOS devices [2,3,4]. Relatively little has been  done on bipolar transistors at GHz frequencies, although the  measurement bandwidth of the LVI system we used can be as  high as 12GHz.  In addition, the RF signal levels on our


circuits were expected to be much lower than those found on  CMOS digital devices.  Thus, we had two open questions that  needed to be addressed:  (1) would LVi work on BJT’s; and


(2) if so, does it have good enough sensitivity to make useful  measurements?  The latter question was of particular interest  because we expected some of the signal levels to be in the -40  to -60dBm range.


Laser Voltage Imaging Setup & Results


We prepared two devices to evaluate the usefulness of LVi on  our SiGe BJT designs.  A failing unit and a known good  device (KGU) were prepared for backside analysis by milling  a hole in the back of each package.  This product is packaged  with an exposed paddle to act as a heat sink; it also serves as a  high frequency ground plane.  As such, the exposed paddle  had to remain electrically intact because a number of circuit  grounds are down-bonded to the exposed paddle.  This step of  the sample preparation process was done with our ASAP-1  milling tool.  In addition to preparing the devices, two circuit  boards were modified by milling a hole in the board, beneath  the mounting point of the device.  The units were mounted on  the boards and tested to verify functionality.  Sample  preparation prohibited the use of a SIL lens.  A 100X air-gap  objective was used for all imaging.


Laser Voltage Imaging: Results


The laser voltage images were obtained  using a laser probe  wavelength of 1.32um.  The slightly different wavelength  produces a better signal-to-noise ratio compared to the more  widely employed 1.34um.  A spectrum analyzer monitored the  amplitude of the 5.8GHz signal component in the light  reflected from the circuit devices.  Initial scans performed on a  KGU demonstrated that LVi data was easily obtained from the  RF signals in BJT’s.  In addition, the sensitivity was high  enough to image signals down in the -50dBm range.  See  Figure 3.


Scans performed on the failing device revealed substantial  differences in the on-chip signal levels, compared to the KGU.  See Figures 4 and 5.  The major difference appears to arise in  the first RF power amplifier in the power amplifier block, where the LVI signal decreases by over 50% compared with  the KGU.  This marked signal reduction is present in the  second RF power amplifier as well, suggesting that the  problem lies in the first RF amplifier circuit.  However, extensive probing on other failures failed to identify a defect  in that portion of the circuit.  This was a mystery that took  another type of laser imaging scheme to resolve.


RF SDL Results


Due to the unusual result obtained by the LVi work, we began  working on a way to extend Soft Defect Localization into the  RF regime.  One hypothesis for the low power output was that  one or more of the passive RF matching components were  defective.  This would have an indirect impact on the power  output by increasing internal signal losses.  Using an IR laser  to heat the passive components, while monitoring the RF  output power, might produce additional insights into the  nature of the failures.


Therefore, the output of a wideband power detector was  monitored (and mapped) while the device was being scanned  by our in-house SLM.  The incident laser beam wavelength  was 1.34um so only heating effects occurred.  Scans  performed on a KGU and a failing device revealed an unusual  response in a “peaking” capacitor connected across the output  transformer.  The transformer is driven by the second  amplifier in the power amplifier chain.  See Figures 6 and 7  for examples of our RF SDL results.  Small variations in  capacitance due to heating effects might be expected, but, since the KGU did not exhibit a significant response, this  seemed very suspicious.


It also should be noted that the RF power variations were quite  small, so it was necessary to use very slow scans (8ms/pixel).  The LVi results helped reduce the time needed for RF SDL, by quickly identifying regions suitable for the more timeconsuming SDL work.


Isolating the capacitor using FIB cuts confirmed our  suspicions:  the capacitor was shorted.  The short had not been  detected with our pin to pin testing, because the capacitor was  shunted by the on-chip transformer, which “looks” like a short  at low frequencies.


Our evaluation of LVi showed that it can image RF signals in  BJT designs down to at least the -40 to -50dBm range.  The  image results did point to the general area of failure but did  not pinpoint the exact location, which was eventually  identified by using RF-SDL.


There are several possible explanations for the discrepancy:


1.  The LVi signal is mostly the result of free-carrier  modulation effects.  A short on the output of an amplifier  won’t necessarily alter the number of free carriers present in  the affected transistor, so variations in the LVi signal may not  be present.


2.  Due to the short, there may be high RF currents circulating  in the device grounds, causing unusual interactions between  active circuitry and this spurious ground loop signal.  The  signals could either add or subtract, causing variations in the  effective drive to an amplifier:  and this would appear as a  variation in the LVi signal.  The variations would not  necessarily show up in the circuitry directly connected to the  shorted device.


3.  Due to circuit mismatch effects, the effective input  impedance of the affected circuit may have been degraded, loading the preceding stage.


Work is ongoing to better identify which of these hypotheses  is correct.


One obvious result from this work was that active probing  completely failed to identify the failing circuit block.  This  result highlights the limitations of active probing at high  frequencies.


However, the combination of LVi -- to help identify the  affected circuit block -- and RF SDL -- to find the specific  failing device -- proved to be a time-efficient solution to our  difficult RF FA problem.


[1] Kindereit et al., “Quantitative Investigation of Laser Beam  Modulation in Electrically Active Devices as Used in Laser  Voltage Probing”, IEEE Transactions on Device and  Materials Reliability, Vol. 7, No.  1, March 2007


[2] Kasapi et al., “Advanced Scan Chain Failure Analysis  Using Laser Modulation Mapping and Continuous Wave  Probing”, Proc 37th Int’l Symp for Testing and Failure  Analysis, November 2011 pp. 12-17.


[3] Ng et al., “Laser Voltage Imaging:  A New Perspective of  Laser Voltage Probing”, Proc 36th Int’l Symp for Testing and  Failure Analysis, November 2010 pp. 5-13.


[4] Koh et al., “Laser Timing Probe with Frequency Mapping  for Locating Signal Maxima”, Proc 35th Int’l Symp for Testing  and Failure Analysis, November 2009 Proceedings, pp. 33-37


As a result, we can conclude that the pulse signal has been  reduced by 30% between the PG and the input of the ATC. At  this step, we can argue that memory Taa is correct (735ps) and  ATC is working properly and is measuring the correct pulse  width value. A strong suspicion is on slopes degradation of the  pulse signal through the logic path (code match ACT pulse).  Many logic cells are present in this propagation path, they are  used for the pulse propagation buffering and for multiplexing  all signals coming from different memory blocks on the same  ATC.


However, since EBT technique is not able to probe inside the  logic because of physical access limitation already discussed, EBT was not the right technique to solve this issue.


Figure 7: Detail of propagation logic path for the measured  memory: 4 inverters, 3 NOR2x, 3 NOR4x and one MUX.


Time Resolved Emission results.


TRE allows following the pulse at gate level into the  propagation logic. Unfortunately it is not possible to measure  the slope of signal with this technique.


Another issue is that the taa mismatch is less important at high  voltage (1.2V). Only measurement at low voltage 0.9V allows  measuring the degradation of taa with a resolution compatible  with TRE performances. However, TRE acquisition was not  working for low voltage. At 0.9V the emission strength of the  generated photons from switching transistors is too low to be  detected by the photodiode of the equipment.


Figure 8 :  Left: TRE results@1.20V, pulse width degradation  not visible with a good accuracy (voltage too high). Right: degradation becomes visible @1.1V


At 1.1V we start to observe a slight degradation of the pulse  width (110ps). These measurements at the limit of timing  resolution are good enough to deduct that the PG block  generates the correct pulse which is proportional to the  memory taa and that the ATC bloc generates a correct code  proportional to the pulse with of signal. Conclusion is that the  pulse width degradation of signal occurs into the propagation  path between the PG block and the ATC block. So TRE gives  us enough information to conclude that the problem is located  in the propagation path. Unfortunately, we are still not able to  pinpoint a specific defect location in this propagation path. We  will demonstrate in the next part that LVP measurement  allows accessing this critical information.


Laser Voltage Probing results


The LVP technique has enough probe placement resolution to  measure individually NMOS and PMOS of each gate and  enough timing accuracy to measure slopes and delay of each  signal as shown in figure 9.


Pulse width generated by the PG block is corresponding to the  taa measured on memory.


Figure 9: LVP results @1.10V, IN and OUT signals of PG  block. Raw and Fitted data. Yellow NMOS, Pink PMOS. Measured delays and slopes (<10ps of accuracy). Pulse width  corresponds to the access time measured directly on memory.


Thank to LVP measurements on the full path, we don’t  observe any issue of slope between NMOS and PMOS as  suspected during TRE measurement. However we observe that  the pulse width degradation occurs on the three last gates due  to a timing imbalance of few picoseconds of PMOS versus  NMOS as seen on figure10.


The cumulated timing shift measured on the three last gates is  corresponding to the timing mismatch observed on access time  between memory and ATC.


According to the LVP results, the issue can be explained by a  layout-induced variation of transistor characteristics, especially on the threshold voltage of transistors. The design  analysis shows that these gates are located in a low density  area which is not the case for the gates located at the  beginning of the path. Due to this configuration, threshold  voltages (Vt) adjustment of transistors are sensitive to layout  induced variation during post metallization laser annealing for  implanted dopants activation. In this particular case, PMOS at  the end of the path were slightly too fast and NMOS slightly  too slow.  The design has been correct with a placement of the  ATC block near the memory blocks in order to reduce  cumulative error through gates. The layout has been corrected  in term of density of dummies.


Figure 10: LVP results (fitted curves) @1.10V measured on  the full path. Yellow NMOS, Pink PMOS. A noticeable  imbalance between PMOS and NMOS are observed on the  three last gates.


The results presented in this paper demonstrate the gap of  performances of the LVP technique vs EBT/TRE for internal  timing characterization.


Regarding the three different techniques, we have a superior  level of the interpretation with the LVP versus the others  which ease to find the root cause.


We illustrated the gap between these techniques regarding  probing access, timing accuracy, low voltage probing as well  as spatial probing resolution.


On this case study, LVP was the unique technique that gives  access to enough information to converge to a conclusive  result. The timing characterization given by the LVP  technique allows us to pinpoint the process step that needed to  be tuned to fix the issue.

Lock-in Thermography-Based Local Efficiency Analysis of Solar Cells


Otwin Breitenstein


Max Planck Institute of Microstructure Physics, Halle, Germany


breiten@mpi-halle.mpg.de, phone: +49-345-5582740, fax: +49-345-5511223


The electronic properties of solar cells, particularly   multicrystalline silicon-based ones, are distributed spatially  inhomogeneous, where regions of poor quality may degrade  the performance of the whole cell. These inhomogeneities  mostly affect the dark current-voltage (I-V) characteristic, which decisively affects the efficiency. Since the grid  distributes the local voltage homogeneously across the cell and  leads to lateral balancing currents, local light beam-induced  current methods alone cannot be used to image local cell  efficiency parameters. Lock-in thermography (LIT) is the  method of choice for imaging inhomogeneities of the dark I-V  characteristic. This contribution introduces a novel method for  evaluating a number of LIT images taken at different applied  biases. By pixel-wise fitting the data to a two diode model and  taking into account local series resistance and short circuit  current density data, realistically simulated images of the other  cell efficiency parameters (open circuit voltage, fill factor, and  efficiency) are obtained. Moreover, simulated local and global  dark and illuminated I-V characteristics are obtained, also for  various illumination intensities. These local efficiency data are  expectation values, which would hold if a homogeneous solar  cell had the properties of the selected region of the  inhomogeneous cell. Alternatively, also local efficiency data  holding for the cell working at its own maximum power point  may be generated. The amount of degradation of different cell  efficiency parameters in some local defect positions is an  indication how dangerous these defects are for degrading this  parameter of the whole cell. The method allows to virtually 'cut out' certain defects for checking their influence on the  global characteristics.
 Thus, by applying this method, a  detailed local efficiency analysis of locally inhomogeneous  solar cells is possible. It can be reliably predicted how a cell  would improve if certain defects could be avoided. This  method is implemented in a software code, which is available.


All industrial solar cells, even monocrystalline ones, are  locally inhomogeneous. This holds especially for their dark  current-voltage (I-V) characteristic. This characteristic strongly  influences the energy conversion efficiency of solar cells, since  in operation solar cells are forward-biased. All dark currents  flowing at the working point of the cell (lying between +0.5  and +0.55 V for crystalline silicon cells) are internal loss


currents, which degrade the fill factor FF (a measure of the  degree of rectangular shape of the illuminated I-V  characteristic) and/or the open circuit voltage Voc of the cell. These two parameters directly influence the efficiency η of the  cell. Local sites of increased forward current are traditionally  called "shunts", even if they are not short-circuits in the  electro-technical sense and do not necessarily show a linear (ohmic) I-V characteristic. In a solar cell, owing to the top grid  metallization, all local regions are electrically connected with  each other. Therefore, some local shunts may seriously  increase the dark current of the whole device and thus degrade  the efficiency of the whole cell. Methods for imaging local  values of solar cell parameters, like the open circuit voltage, the fill factor, or the efficiency, are highly desirable, but were  not available until now. Only the short circuit current density  Jsc could be mapped by light beam-induced current (LBIC) imaging, but this technique is not able to image e.g. the local  open circuit voltage and other efficiency parameters [1]. The  imaging of solar cell parameters is particularly necessary for  characterizing multicrystalline solar cells, which are by nature  more inhomogeneous than monocrystalline cells.


Lock-in thermography [2, 3] is the technique of choice for  imaging local inhomogeneities of the dark current of solar  cells. Based on this imaging technique, a procedure was  developed and implemented into a commercial software code  called "Local I-V 2", which for the first time enables the  realistic imaging of cell parameters like Voc, FF, and η on  complete solar cells with a good spatial resolution of about 2  mm [4]. This procedure is a further development of an earlier  published procedure [5], which, was not able to image realistic  solar cell parameters. In this contribution the operation  principles of the "Local I-V 2" procedure are explained and  some results on a typical multicrystalline solar cell are  presented.


Method


"Local I-V 2" is based on the evaluation of four dark lock-in  thermography (DLIT) images taken at four different biases V1, V2, V3, and Vrev, from which three are forward biases and one a  reverse bias. Moreover, an image of the local series resistance  Rs is necessary. This image may be obtained by evaluating  photoluminescence (PL) or electroluminescence (EL) images  of this solar cell [6, 7]. Alternatively, an image of the local  voltage across the p-n junction may be obtained by evaluating  EL images [7], and the "Local I-V 2" software converts it into


an Rs image by applying the so-called RESI procedure [8]. The  local short current density Jsc may either be assumed to be  homogeneous being that of the whole cell (which often is a  good approximation) or a corresponding image may be loaded  separately. The whole procedure is based on the validity of  two well-known and (at least for crystalline silicon solar cells) generally accepted models, which are the superposition  principle and the two-diode model [9]. The superposition  principle says that the illuminated cell characteristic equals the  dark characteristic shifted into the negative by the short circuit  current. This principle holds only for the local diode, hence  without regarding the voltage drop at Rs, which may be  regarded separately. For thin film solar cells this principle  often only poorly holds, therefore for such cells the procedure  described here is less appropriate. The two-diode model says  that the local diode consists of two diodes, one described by  the saturation current density J01 and having an ideality factor  of unity, describing the so-called diffusion current, and one  described by the saturation current density J02 and having an  ideality factor n2 of two and above, describing the depletion  region recombination current. Moreover, an ohmic shunt  contribution is regarded, described by the parallel resistance Rp  or its inverse, the parallel ohmic conductance Gp. If all these  four local diode parameters J01, J02, n2, and Gp are determined, the cell is completely locally described. Based on these local  diode parameters and the Rs image, the local cell parameters  Voc, FF, and η may be calculated for any position or region, if  the local short circuit current density Jsc is known.


After all input images are loaded into the "Local I-V 2" software, it calculates the local diode parameters by applying  an iteration procedure described in [5]. The Jsc image, which is  also necessary for calculating local cell parameters, may be  externally obtained e.g. by LBIC imaging. Alternatively, Jsc  may be assumed to be homogeneous equal to the average Jsc of  the cell, which is quite a good approximation for high-quality  solar cells, even for multicrystalline ones. The "Local I-V 2" code generates, among others, images of the local cell  parameters Voc, FF, and η. Note that all these cell parameter  images are only hypothetical cell parameters. They would hold  only for a homogeneous cell having exactly the parameters of  the local diode in this position. Since all these parameters are  referred to the maximum power point of the local cell, and  since in an inhomogeneous cell these maximum power points  deviate from each other, it is not allowed to average any of  these cell parameters across a larger area. However, qualitatively the amount of degradation of a certain efficiency  parameter in the position of certain defects is an indication, how dangerous this defect is for the selected cell efficiency  parameter. The software is able to consider a selected region  as one isolated cell, to simulate dark and illuminated  characteristics of this region (with and without regarding Rs), and thus to calculate the cell parameters for this region. Since  this region may also be the whole cell, "Local I-V 2" may  simulate the parameters of the complete cell, based on the  local cell analysis.


Fig. 1 shows a typical set of input images used for "Local I-V  2". The DLIT images taken at different forward biases differ in  their ratio of local shunt currents to the homogeneously  flowing current.


Figure 1: Set of input images used for the local efficiency  analysis. Position D marks a weak ohmic shunt. The color bar  holds for all images in this paper.


The lower the voltage, the stronger the relative influence of the  shunt currents. This is due to the fact that the homogeneous  current is due to the diffusion current (first diode current), which shows the strongest increase with increasing bias and  dominates at high forward bias, whereas most of the shunts are  due to the depletion region recombination current, which  shows a weaker increase with increasing bias and dominates at  low forward bias. Under reverse bias voltage only ohmic  current contributions are visible in DLIT. In Fig. 1, for Rs a  RESI-based Rs image was used. In this image the positions of  local shunts (bright spots in the forward bias DLIT images) shows local minima of Rs. This is not an artifact but reality, since the local value of Rs is defined here as the voltage drop  across the series resistance divided by the local current density

[6-8]. While for a homogeneously flowing diode current the  horizontal current in the emitter and in the grid lines flows  only in one direction (towards the grid lines and bus bars), in  the positions of local shunts the current flows horizontally  from all four sides, leading to the lower effective Rs-values. The bright vertical stripes in the Rs image are due to some grid  line interruptions.


Figure 2: Set of output images describing the dark current  properties of the cell. J01 is the saturation current density of  the diffusion current, which depends on the bulk lifetime. Jrec  is the depletion region recombination current flowing at 0.6 V. It is described by its saturation current density J02 (here  logarithmically displayed) and its ideality factor n2.


Fig. 2 shows a typical set of output images regarding the local  dark characteristics. The data show that the diffusion current  and the recombination current are distributed very differently. This is due to different physical processes, the diffusion  current is due to recombination in the bulk, and the  recombination current is due to recombination in the depletion  region. The recombination current flows only in certain local  shunt positions and its homogeneous contribution is negligible. The spatial extension of the shunts is mostly due to inevitable  lateral heat diffusion. Hence, the real spatial extension of the  defects leading to the shunts is much smaller than the bright  spots visible in this investigation. While Shockley-Read-Hall  theory predicts an ideality factor of two for the recombination  current, the ideality factors measured here at some shunts are  up to ten and above. Such large ideality factors may be  described by multi level recombination, since the defects being  responsible for this current are extended defects crossing the  p-n junction, which show a high local density of gap states [10]. Position A marks a typical recombination current shunt, position B marks a diffusion current shunt, and position C  marks a defect-free region close to the bus bar, where also Rs


is low. In Fig. 1 position D was already flagged as a weak  ohmic shunt.


In Fig. 3 simulated local dark and illuminated I-V  characteristics of positions A, B, C, and D are shown. The  dark characteristics are split into their constituents, i.e. the  diffusion, recombination, and the shunt current. It is nicely  visible that in position A the recombination current dominates, whereas in positions B and C the diffusion current dominates, which represents the ideal diode current. Only in position D  the ohmic shunt current plays a major role. The dark spots on  the dark sum currents mark the points where the dark current  was measured by DLIT. We see a perfect fit of these data  points by the simulations. Note that the illuminated  characteristic equals the inverted dark characteristic, shifted by  the short circuit current density. Therefore, according to the  different dark characteristics in the different positions, also the  illuminated characteristics vary considerably.


Figure 3: Simulated dark and illuminated characteristics of  regions A, B, C, and D


Fig. 4 shows images of local cell efficiency parameters. Here a  homogeneous Jsc = 31.8 mA/cm2 was assumed, which equals  Jsc measured for the whole cell. While for the whole cell the  measured illuminated characteristic lead to Voc = 624 mV, FF = 77.6 %, and η = 15.4 %, the simulation of the whole cell  lead to Voc = 625 mV, FF = 78.1 %, and η = 15.5 %, giving  confidence with local simulation results of this cell. In Fig. 4  the open circuit voltage was simulated both for full  illumination intensity (1 sun) and reduced intensity (0.2 sun). The comparison of Figs. 1 and 2 with Fig. 4 shows that Voc is  mainly influenced by diffusion current shunts dominating at


0.6 V, and that the FF is mainly influenced by recombination  current shunts dominating at 0.5 V. This is due to the fact that  Voc is larger than the voltage of the maximum power point, where the FF is defined. Therefore Voc is more influenced by  the diffusion current, which dominates at high voltage, and  vice versa. At reduced intensity Voc is influenced more by the  recombination current shunts than at full intensity, since here

Voc reduces. Regions of high series resistance (bright regions  in the Rs image of Fig. 1), which are due to broken grid  fingers, influence FF and the efficiency, but not Voc. This is  due to the fact that Voc is defined under zero current condition  where Rs does not play a role. In this cell, ohmic shunts (see  the DLIT data of -1 V in Fig. 1) generally play only a minor  role. Nevertheless, the weak ohmic shunt in position D  considerably degrades the fill factor and Voc at 0.2 sun, but not  Voc at 1 sun. In position C, which is the best region of this cell, local cell parameters of Voc = 632 mV, FF =  81.5 %, and η =


16.4 % are simulated. This is an improvement of 1 % (absolute), compared to the whole cell, which marks the  efficiency gain which could be obtained by this cell technology  and this material, if all defects causing the locally reduced  efficiency parameters were avoided. Note that the local  efficiency parameters shown in Fig. 4, as well as the local  characteristics in Fig. 3, are "efficiency potential" data. They  are calculated by assuming that each pixel region is electrically  isolated from the rest of the cell. Thus, these data refer to the  individual maximum power point (mpp) voltages in the  considered position. Only an extended cell having the  properties of this position would show these efficiency  parameters. If the whole solar cell is operated at its own  maximum power point, the locally generated power in each  position is different to that shown in Fig. 4, since now all  regions are at the same voltage. "Local I-V 2" may also  generate images of the locally generated power and other  expected data under this condition, which is not shown here.


Figure 4: Images of the local open circuit voltage (Voc) at one  sun (full illumination intensity) and 0.2 sun (reduced  illumination intensity, the fill factor (FF) and the cell  efficiency, both simulated at one sun.


In Fig. 5 a) the measured global and simulated dark  characteristics are compared, and in Fig. 5 b) the same is done  with the illuminated characteristics, these figures show great


correlation again giving confidence to the correlation of  simulation vs. actual. The dark spots in a) are the points where  the DLIT images have been measured, and the spots in b) mark the measured and simulated maximum power points. The  simulated curve shows a somewhat larger fill factor (higher  current) than the measured one; an indication that there are  some minor departures from the superposition principle, which  are well understood [11]. Finally, Fig. 5 c) shows the  simulated efficiency of regions A, B, C, and D, and of the  whole cell, as a function of the illumination intensity. We see  that the degradation of the efficiency with reducing intensity is  strongest for the ohmic shunt position (D), still strong for the  recombination shunt position (A), and weakest for positions B  and C, which are dominated by the diffusion current. The  degradation of the whole cell is somewhat stronger than that of  regions B and C.

It has been shown that the local analysis of solar cells based on  the "Local I-V 2" procedure, which is available [12], is a  valuable tool for analyzing local regions responsible for  degraded cell parameters and evaluating the efficiency  potential of solar cell technologies and materials. Of course, this tool only enables the localization and evaluation of local  defects leading to degraded cell efficiency. Once the most  dangerous defects are identified, their physical nature has to be  investigated to improve manufacturing and reducing/ eliminating their presence.
