Challenges for parametric analysis of the solar cells using failure analysis  technique developed for the microelectronics


M. Boostandoost, X. Ycaza, R. Leihkauf, U. Kerst, C. Boit


mahb@mikro.ee.tu-berlin.de, Tel.: +49-30-314-29947


In this study, the challenges to transfer the microelectronics  failure analysis techniques to the photovoltaic industry have  been discussed. The main focus of this study was the  PHEMOS as a tool with strong technological research capacity  developed for microelectronics failure analysis, and OBIC (Optical Beam Induced Current) as a non-destructive  technique for detecting and localizing various defects in  semiconductor devices. This failure analysis tool was a high  resolution optical infrared photon emission microscope used  mainly in microelectronics for qualitative analysis and  localization of semiconductor defects. Such failure analysis  equipment was designed to meet requirements for modern  microelectronic devices. Characterization of current  photovoltaic device often requires quantitative analysis and  should provide information about the electrical and material  properties of the solar cell. Therefore, in addition to the  demand for further data processing of the obtained results we  had to study the corresponding operating regime of solar cells  to allow for a correct interpretation of measurement results. In  this paper, some of the related problems we faced during this  study, e.g. large amount of data processing, the spatial  misalignment of the images obtained as EL (Electroluminescence) and IR-LBIC (Infrared Light Beam  Induced Current), the implemented laser wavelength, its  profile and power density for IR-LBIC measurement. These  topics have been discussed in detailed to facilitate a reliable  transfer of these techniques from microelectronics to the  photovoltaic world.


1) Introduction


Photovoltaic (PV) power generation is an important type of the  solar energy technology that has been strengthening its  position among others due to its variety of benefits: flexibility, simplicity, the possibility of installing almost everywhere that  sun shines and easy maintenance. Current PV technology has  been developed so well that solar cell producers guarantee the  reliable operation of their high efficiency solar systems for  over 25 years. But to compete with other types of the green  energy sources, further development and research is still


needed to improve efficiency and reliability developing new  materials and processing techniques and adapting them to suit  the industrial requirements of PV is critical for success. For  this purpose, significant progress is still required to improve  the conversion efficiency of the solar cells, which are to a large  extent limited by microscopic defects (e.g. at the grain  boundaries in the poly-silicon based solar cells). Photovoltaic  characterization techniques have been developed well in the  last decades by leveraging the methods used for  microelectronics. The expensive tools designed for  microelectronics failure analysis have made their way into the  PV industry by adapting technically and financially to the  requirements of this technology. To fulfill this task there has  always been a need to build a bridge between the requirements  and demands of the PV and the development and strength of  microelectronics technology.


In this study, we implemented different measurements such as  IR-LBIC using PHEMOS (Photon Emission Microscope  System) as a core of our setup. PHEMOS is an optical  microscope equipped with a camera to perform PEM (Photon  Emission Microscopy) and two lasers for OBIC (Optical Beam  Induced Current) and OBIRCH (Optical Beam Induced  Resistive Change). In this paper we just focus on the  challenges we face making OBIC measurements, which is  called LBIC (Light Beam Induced Current) in PV. The  PHEMOS 1000 is equipped with two laser wavelengths: 1064  and 1300 nm. The first one is used for performing the OBIC  measurements on semiconductor devices.


OBIC is an efficient and non-invasive optical analysis  technique in microelectronics which can be used to detect and  localize defects like ESD (Electrostatic Discharge) or EOS (Electrical Overstress) damage, leakage current, defective  junctions, and open and shorts in ICs. In OBIC, a laser beam is  scanned over the device and the current induced by the laser  beam is monitored simultaneously. The changes in the amount  of the induced current produce the contrast in an OBIC image. The variations in the current collection are correlated with the  position of the laser leading to a current map (OBIC image). The result can be either an indication of the buried diffusion  on the sample or defects associated with the junction [1].


Due to the increasing complexity of modern microelectronic  systems, it is almost impossible to perform optical interaction  techniques like OBIC from the front side of the sample. Therefore, devices must be investigated from the backside


where there are no metal layers preventing the laser from  reaching the active layers on the front side (Backside OBIC). To realize this technique, the measurements should be  performed using an optimal laser wavelength. On the one  hand, the laser wavelength should be low enough to allow  generating electron/hole pairs, and on the other hand should be  high enough to be absorbed as few as possible in the substrate. To fulfill these requirements, normally a laser with the  wavelength of 1064 nm (1.165 eV) in the IR range is used. This wavelength has the energy slightly above the silicon  bandgap energy (1.12 eV) and can be used to perform OBIC  from the polished backside of the IC die. Regarding the laser  wavelength of 1064 nm which is in the IR range, this technique  is called IR-LBIC in PV [2].


The PHEMOS 1000 is a tool to localize failures in  microelectronic devices and is not oriented for the quantitative  measurement on the semiconductor devices like solar cells. Therefore, one can have difficulty when performing  quantitative analysis, where several measurements should be  performed on a same area under different electrical or optical  conditions. The IR-LBIC measurement can be performed of a  function of the temperature, bias voltage and laser power to  extract some electrical and material parameters and to  determine the interaction mechanisms [3 - 5]. For this purpose, several IR-LBIC measurements should be performed one after  each other on the same area of the sample. The first challenge  doing such measurements is managing and processing a huge  amount of the data, which has to be done manually by the user. Another challenge we faced is the instability of the system. Despite the high resolution and high accuracy of the results, the obtained images are not spatially aligned, making the  results unreliable. Misalignment is highly probable because of  the instability of the stage and dark room of the setup due to  the temperature change. This problem leads to a smooth  displacement of the images obtained as EL or IR-LBIC results. The displacement is generally just several pixels between  consecutive images, which are worse using higher  magnification objectives and longer measurement time. But  even this small displacement can mislead the data process and  results. The correction of this displacement is very crucial  while developing the data processing program.


Some failure analysis tools such as PHEMOS output images in  TIF32 format, which is one of the subformats within TIFF  image. TIF32 is characterized by having 32 bits per sample. Therefore, they are useful for representing CMYK (Cyan  Magenta Yellow Black) or RGB (Red Green Blue) images  with an extra alpha channel for enhanced contrast. Both  formats require four different values per pixel. TIF32 stores  one byte for each of these values.  Not all failure analysis  equipment and image processing software handles this image  format, which makes the data processing difficult. After an  attempt to find the find the appropriate software to handle this  task, we chose MATLAB (matrix laboratory) for further  processing of the results.


This alignment process is realized by locating image features  between each pair of images, and then aligning the rest. In  figure 6 the whole alignment process is illustrated on two  images obtained by IR-LBIC measurements on the thin-film  poly-Si solar cell. Two images are obtained by applying  different laser power. The images are not well aligned and  there is a displacement in x and y direction, which should be  corrected before further processing. Afterward, the data, which  are saved as a matrix of integer values, are ready for further  processing. We can then apply different algorithms, e.g. IRLBIC(V), IR-LBIC(T), IR-LBIC(LP) on the whole or an  arbitrary area of the final matched images and  obtained a  reliable results in a reasonably short time [3 - 5].


Fig 6: The two images obtained by performing IR-LBIC


measurement on a poly-Si thin-film solar cell by applying


different laser power on a same area. The left image is the one  obtained by applying 50% of the maximum laser power using  100X objective and the right one with 5% (a). As a first step  the template has been selected and then rotated by 2×90° (b), then the displacement between two consecutive pictures has  been defined (c), and with further processing (cutting) (d), two  images were aligned. This process will be performed further  until all images are aligned.


We applied a mathematical tool called correlation, which is  also known in this context of two dimensional matrixes or  images as template matching to align the images in MATLAB. Template matching is a technique in digital image processing  for detecting small parts of an image (left image from figure


6.a) which match the template image (right image from figure


6.a). In this method, Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) based  convolution technique is used to perform the correlations, which then will be used to locate features within an image.


Convolution is equivalent to correlation if the convolution  kernel be rotated by 180° (2×90°):


Correlated Image = real (IFFT (FFT (Image).* FFT (rot2×90 (Template))));


It represents a convolution of the rotated template image (red  square at the center of the image 6.a (right)) with the first  image (right image from figure 6.a), which is equivalent to the  correlation process. FFT function returns the discrete Fourier  transform (DFT) of a vector, computed with a fast Fourier  transform (FFT) algorithm. IFFT function returns the inverse  fast Fourier transform of a vector.


The result of this mathematical procedure is an image or  matrix as big as the first image. The maximum value of the  obtained matrix represents the match position of the bottom  right corner of the template (small rotated picture) with the  first image (figure 6.c). Knowing the coordinates of that  maximum correlation value, it is possible to find out the spatial  displacement between two consecutive images. Afterward by  cutting the edges of both images, as is showed in figure 6.d, we  obtain aligned images results having the same size. By further  similar procedure, it is possible to align rest of the images.


Still there are some problems which can not be resolved using  this alignment procedure. The most important unsolved issue  is the displacement in z direction (focus). The focus of the  camera changes over the time and the only solution for this  challenge is refocusing manually once in a while during the  measurement, although even the refocusing is not a guarantee  of equity of the images. First, because it is performed by the  human eye, which is very subjective to this level of precision; second, because the setup itself is not optimized for such a  purposes. Therefore, in a series of measurements, some of the  images will be rejected by the program because they are not  adjustable to rest of the images.


We have discussed some possible challenges for transferring  the well developed failure analysis techniques in  microelectronics to the photovoltaic industry. We focused on  some issues that are more relevant for the parametric  characterization of the photovoltaic devices but are not well  considered in such tools.


The first challenge was the laser power density applied to the  sample. It has been shown that special care has to be taken  when selecting the laser power density and the laser  wavelength to determine if the device is operating in the high  or low injection regimes. Otherwise the measurement results  can be misinterpreted. We covered this topic using three  different approaches: an analytic investigation, a one  dimensional device simulation using the PC1D program and  finally an additional experimental setup (PVD).


Another important issue is the possibility for quantitative  analysis and therefore the extraction of the electrical and  material parameters of the solar cell. We have developed a  program in MATLAB to process the huge amount of data  produced by measurements. Additionally, the problem with the


misalignment of the images has been discussed. Before  applying any data processing algorithm, the images must be  aligned spatially. The misalignment is with high probability  due to the instability of the stage and the dark room, which  become worse while using high magnification objectives.


Great care has to be taken to ensure the correct interpretation  of the data when employing failure analysis techniques and  instruments developed for microelectronics to photovoltaics. These issues are not due to imperfections of the tools or  techniques, but rather the different operating conditions of  devices and requirements of these two worlds.

Filtering and emission area identification in the Time Resolved Imaging data


S. CHEF, S. JACQUIR and S.BINCZAK


LE2I, UMR CNRS 6306, University of Burgundy, 9 Avenue Alain Savary, 21000 Dijon, France


K.SANCHEZ and P.PERDU


CNES, French Space Agency, 18 Avenue Edouard Belin, 31401 Toulouse, France


Time Resolved Imaging (TRI) acquisitions allow precise  timing analysis of emission spots. Up to date technologies  deeply challenge their isolation by hiding the weak ones, under sizing or over sizing visually detectable emission spots  and finally by jeopardizing timing resolution. We report on an  algorithm based on 1 and 2D signal processing tools which  automates the identification of emission sites and optimizes  separation between noise and useful signal, even for weak  spots surrounding strong emission areas. The application of  the algorithm on several sets of data from different types of  devices and their results are also discussed.


Transistor shrinkage at each technology node improves  integrated circuits (IC) performance in many ways by  allowing, among other properties, the integration of a bigger  number of transistors on the same die dimension. One  drawback is that devices became sensitive to defect that would  not have any impact on yesterday’s technology. Another  challenge for the failure analysis expert is that there are more   circuit nodes to investigate while searching for defects. The  final techniques which enable the expert to unveil them rely  on some of the most accurate microscope technologies like  Transmission Electron Microscope (TEM) or Atomic Force  Microscope (AFM) which can operate on the nanometer scale. To perform such precise analysis, the circuit needs to be  physically delayed or cross-sectioned. Because of the  destructive nature of sample preparation, techniques that can  give a first but yet precise candidate localization of the defect  are required.


Optical localization techniques like Picosecond Imaging  Circuit Analysis (PICA) [1], also known as Time Resolved  Imaging (TRI) or Emission Microscopy (EMMI) have been  developed for that purpose. Thanks to photoemission induced  by switching states at transistor’s input, it is possible to locate  an area with abnormal electrical activity. Moreover, it is  possible to probe the electrical activity of transistors with the  precise timing analysis of the localized emission.


TRI analysis relies on several parameters. First, there is a link  between the IC's technology and its bias voltage in terms of  photoemission [2]. Its ability to work on, at least, 45 nm  transistors, has been shown [3] but there are significant  differences in quantities of photons collected from one IC to  another, according to its technology and its architecture. For  weak signals, the data acquired through this process are  corrupted by noise. In photon counting, there are mainly two  type of noise, thermal noise and shot noise, also known as  Poisson noise. For a photomultiplier tubes sensor (PMT) used  at a constant low temperature level, the main noise is shot  noise [4]. This type of noise is difficult to determine as it is  correlated to the number of photons. In photon counting  mode, [4] defines the Signal over Noise Ratio (SNR) from the  number of signal photons, noted NPH. A large NPH will lead to  large SNR. So, if the structure emits only a few photons, it  will be hard to distinguish signal photons from noise. The  regions of interest identification will be compromised, as well  as the temporal study and electrical activity recovery.


Usually, the region of interest where the temporal study is  performed is drawn manually. Its common shape is a square  box. This way of proceeding could include some pixels  corresponding to noise information. In addition, weak spots  can be completely missed by this approach. Some techniques  have been developed to isolate signal photons from noise ones  like Spatial and Temporal Photon Correlation (STPC) [5], Positive Photon Discrimination (PPD) [6] or CAD Autochanneling [7]. Nevertheless, none of these techniques are  fully automated and some of them require layout information  which is sometimes not available for the failure analysis  laboratory.


The purpose of this paper is to introduce an algorithm that can  extract useful signal from photon counting database. This  algorithm can also qualify the information retrieved. It is  based on thresholding and mathematical morphology  operations to identify region of interest (ROI) without any  design information. The second section introduces the  experimental setup and the set of data. In the third section, the  processing algorithm allowing the ROI identification is  described. Then, in the fourth section, some examples of  application and their results are introduced. In the fifth  section, some algorithm limitations are explained and  extensions to tackle them are discussed.


0 100 200 300 400 500 600 700 800 900


Figure 1 : An example of spatial display of emitted photons. The colorbar gives information about pixel value. The studied  circuit is 90-nm technology memory.


Acquisition Setup


Physical background


TRI finds its origin in hot carrier luminescence in silicon. Transistor gate is in the required conditions so that such  emissions happen only when the transistor switches from low (or high) to high (or low) state [8]. Emitted photons can be  collected by photodetector like PMTs, Single Photon  Avalanche Diode (SPAD) or Charge Coupled Device (CCD) sensor. The acquisition setup records events and techniques  like Pattern Matching [9] can be used to perform IC's analysis.


TRI dataset


All the datasets used in the paper have been recorded using  Hamamatsu's TRIPhemos equipped with the PMT sensor. Each detected photon is characterized by two spatial  coordinates (x,y) and a temporal value (t). A usual way of data  processing is to project them on a two dimensions (spatial) plane, to define manually the ROI and finally to perform a  temporal study of the defined region. In the spatial display, data forms an image. Each pixel is weighted by its number of  occurrence in the TRI database, as illustrated in Fig. 1.


Algorithm presentation


The algorithm is composed of a succession of image  processing operations. As illustrated in Fig. 2, the algorithm  includes four steps. The first one is the transformation of TRI  database to an image. An example of result has previously  been given in Fig. 1.


Iterative Thresholding


Once the image has been computed, image processing aims to  extract each component of the original image. Thresholding is  one of the most used image processing for image  segmentation [10], [11]. Moreover, for a long enough  acquisition time, even for a weak emission region, the


matching pixel value will be above background pixel value, which is mainly composed of noise or no signal at all. Contrary to previous one, the chosen method is fully  automated. Thresholding method used is Tsai's Moment  Preserving thresholding [12]. To sum up, in the binarization  case, threshold is determined so that the first four statistical  moments of the original image and the binarized one are the  same. This technique can also be use for multilevel  thresholding.


Image Computation from


TRI database


Image decomposition by iterative  bilevel thresholding


Residual outlines suppresion


Labeled ROI displaying


Figure 2 : Algorithm block diagram


This method is very efficient to give us the optimum threshold  but does not solve the weak points localization challenge. To  do this, we have to iterate this operation on resulting  databases. It means that a first threshold is performed on the  original image, the one with the raw data. The result is named  the first order thresholding. The image composed of the under  threshold object from the first computation operation is the  first order resulting image. Then, a new thresholding operation  is performed on this first order resulting image. The new  objects identified by this second order thresholding are then  removed from the reduced original image to form the second  order resulting image. The operation is repeated until there is  nothing left in the thresholded image.


Another way of proceeding is to perform a multilevel  thresholding. A justification of the iterative choice can be  found from the moment equation (eq. 1)

where mk is the kth order moment, g the considered gray-level, p(g) its probability computed from image histogram and G the  highest gray level. If a n-level thresholding operation is  performed, the moment of the image after computation can be  formulated as written in eq.2:

where tn is the nth threshold.


The aim of the whole process is to extract all of the emission  spots, even the weak ones. If the number n is not big enough, there will always be a chance that a really weak emission area  regarding the other spots will be associated to the first class, the one from g = 0 to g = t1-1. This class also contains noise or  background pixels. In an automatic processing scheme, this  class will be discarded and therefore the hypothetical area. So, if the number of level is not well defined, there will be a nonzeros probability of missing one weak emission. On the other  hand, the iterative way allows decomposing of the under  threshold image. So in some sense, this approach fits better for  this particular case of application.


Residual outlines suppression sub-algorithm


Unfortunately, this iterative process cannot be used directly on  resulting images because of the residual outlines. An example  of residual outlines is shown in Fig. 3. It appears on higher  order threshold than its core regions because its pixels are  above background pixel.


These boundaries are not interesting because in many case, they are the result of different core region contribution. So the  outline is only a mixture of the core region contribution. In  addition, as the pixels composing the boundaries are further  from true emission origin than the pixel from the core region, their SNR shall be worse than core region SNR.


Figure 3 : An example of residual outlines. It is marked by a  red rectangle.


In an automatic processing scheme, where the purpose is to  perform temporal study of each spotted region, boundaries can  be a problem and an algorithm based on a succession of  morphological operations have been developed to discard  them. A block diagram describing it can be found in Fig. 4.


Mathematical morphology : opening


Edge detection by


Laplacian of Gaussian filter


Flood-Fill


Labeling by connexity


Intersection between the previous  order image and the considered one


Figure 4 : Residual outline suppression algorithm block  diagram.


An image opening operation is performed in order to delete  single pixel isolated from bigger regions. It is completed by a  gradient operation by Laplacian of Gaussian filter to remove  the remaining single pixel.


The general idea of the algorithm is to fill the holes inside the  boundaries, label all of the regions and then perform the  intersection between this image and the one resulting from the  previous order. After the intersection, the remaining pixels  give information about which labeled regions match with  outlines. Details about each step can be found in literature. The used flood-fill algorithm, also known as morphological  reconstruction, is described in [13] and the region labeling by  connected component is portrayed in [14].


Application and Results


Some examples of application of the algorithm are presented  in this section.


The Flip-Flop case


The first application is made on a 45 nm-technology flip flop  circuit (Fig. 5). In this case, the signal region identification is  made easier by a stronger emission than the rest of the  acquired area. In a CADless context, it is possible to wonder if  there is no other signal areas left in the background, apart  from those already observed. Moreover, the algorithm may  draw precise outline of these regions.


The thresholding results are given in Fig. 6. After iterative  thresholding, the residual outlines suppression sub-algorithm  is computed as shown in Fig. 7 with arrows pointing at  remaining regions.


0 20 40 60 80 100 120


Figure 5 : Flip-Flop raw data visualization


Figure 6 : Results of iterative moment preserving thresholding  on flip-flop data. (a) is the first thresholding operation result  and (f) the last one.


Except for the first image, there is no large enough coherent  object detected in the other thresholded images. This first  analysis will be confirmed by the application of residual  outlines suppression sub-algorithm. The combination of the  mathematical morphology operations removes most  components of the image (b) to (e) ((f) is an empty image). It  means that all these images were mostly composed of residual


boundaries (image (b)) or single pixel components (which  could be identified as noise pixel).


Figure 7 : Labeling for each thresholded image. Only the first  four results are given. Arrows point at remaining regions after  the application of the residual outlines suppression algorithm.


The Memory case


The studied circuit in this section is a 90 nm-technology  memory. The previous case could be qualified as a simple one  as every emission sites could be identified after the first  threshold. The memory case study is more complex (Fig. 8). There is a really strong emission spot where the activity can  reach more than nine hundred emitted photons during the  acquisition time. An emission timing analysis study, associated with a design study, informs that the true region of  interest is in the white rectangle in Fig. 8. If local electrical  activity follows the input pattern, emission should only  happen on switching transistors. So this constant emission  region is not interesting and the only potential one is marked  by a white rectangle. Therefore, a simple visualization of the  projected raw data cannot permit an identification of the real  region of interest in this particular case.


The algorithm is performed on the raw data (see Fig. 9). The  result shows a more complex decomposition. Eight  thresholdings were required. The first one enables to identify  the core of the permanent emission area. We can see that it is  composed of three sites. The second threshold makes other  small regions appear but they are mainly constituted of the  residual edges of the previously identified cores of the  permanent emission region. The third threshold unveils a  coherent object in the region defined by the white rectangle in  Fig. 8. At least three thresholding operations are necessary to  find the true region of interest. The other images contain  mainly single pixels and residual outlines.


Figure 8 : Memory raw data visualization. The true ROI is  spotted by a white rectangle.


(g)


(h)


Figure 9 : Result of iterative thresholding on memory data. The letters matches with the number of the thresholding.


After applying the residual outlines removing sub-algorithm (see Fig. 10), images have been simplified. Many  unnecessarily objects have been removed and the true region  of interest can easily be identified in the third order  decomposition. It has been marked with a red rectangle in Fig.


10. Once again, all of the identified region must be analyzed  more precisely with other tools than just a spatial display. However, the whole algorithm allows unveiling the true  region of interest in this example.


Algorithm limitation and requirement for candidate  regions classification


Previous section showed the applicability of the algorithm on  some emission databases. As it can be seen on Fig. 10, even  after the application of the sub-algorithm based on  mathematical morphology operations, there remain many  potential coherent regions. The question now is: Are they all  interesting? Indeed the algorithm operates on image attributes  and mathematical morphology can sometimes create artifact if  the structuring element is not well chosen. Furthermore, the  first part of the Laplacian of Gaussian filter acts like a low  pass filter which means it introduces a blur effect in the  processed image.


Figure 10 : Labeling for each thresholded image. Only the  first four results are given.


The proposed image processing can be applied in a CADless  context as it operates only on the emission data. Nevertheless, in order to classify identified region as linked to input pattern  activity or not, layout information can be used.  In this precise  case, the algorithm becomes an improvement or a preprocessing of CAD-Autochanneling. Thanks to design data,


classification of regions resulting from processing can be  achieved in an automated way.


When CAD information is not available, automatic  discrimination between remaining regions at the end of the  process might be done by exploiting the last dimension of the  data set: time. To each region of each resulting image can be  associated a temporal signal composed of the sampled number  of photon emitted in that region during the electrical  stimulation. Due to the fact that region edges are drawn with  respect to the region emission profile, signal over noise ratio  shall be good enough so that emission events are  distinguishable from background noise in temporal displaying. The main advantages of working with time series is that many  tools like cross correlation or auto correlation have been  developed to qualify them. In addition, from time domain, they can be expressed in frequency domain. It allows  utilization of many other tools to characterize the signal like  Power Spectral Density (PSD). The study can be expanded by  using time-frequency method like wavelet transform, scalogram, etc.


In the next section, we propose an example of post-image  processing tool that can be used to automatically separate  candidate regions linked to input activity from the non-linked  one. Frequency spectrum is studied with a model on the noise  spectral properties.


Frequency spectrum characterization


Noises like Gaussian white noise or pink noise have well  known spectral properties. For example, pink noise exhibits  equalized Power Spectral Density inside each octave. Using  these statements, noise regions can be automatically  discarded.

0.06


LBIC in PV industry is used mainly for profiling solar cell  performance and is usually employed under the condition of  short circuit, which is not influenced by the dark IV  characteristics of the device. In photovoltaics, LBIC is  generally performed at a wavelength where the photon energy  is clearly above the bandgap energy of the absorber layer to  ensure a high cell efficiency regime. The IR-LBIC  measurement, benefiting from its unusual wavelength, can  provide information about the solar cell spectral response for  photons close to the silicon bandgap energy, which is  especially interesting in this context because of its low  absorption rate and can be used for many applications to  extract some material and electrical parameters of thin-film  solar cells [2 - 5].


2) Experimental Setup


The PHEMOS 1000 from Hamamatsu is a standard highresolution optical infrared photon emission microscope. It is  equipped with a cooled (up to -55 °C) silicon CCD camera as  a detector for acquisition of extremely faint emission in the  range of visible and infrared wavelength with a spectral range  from 400 up to 1100 nm. Additionally, a 1064 nm laser is  installed for the laser stimulation mode and provides a map of  the induced current which can be superimposed on the  reflected micrograph picture [6]. The main components of this  setup include a Laser Scanning Microscope (LSM), a DC  power supply, and a current / voltage monitoring unit followed  by an amplification stage, a laser light source, an image  processor and a display [6]. The laser scan controller of the  system enabled not only control over the scanning time but  also control over scanning direction, scanning area as well as  using point scan mode.


The PHEMOS 1000 is mainly designed and used for failure  analysis and qualitative measurement in the microelectronic  arena, and is used to localize failures in an integrated circuit (IC) with high resolution (~2 μm) by performing variety of  different optical interaction failure analysis techniques such as  PEM, OBIC, LVI (Laser Voltage Imaging) and OBIRCH. The  results can be processed further to understand the physical  origins of the failures, although sometimes additional  measurements are still required.


Despite many advantages like high accuracy and resolution, some problems have to be overcome to effectively use such


tools and techniques for parametric analysis of solar cells. In  this paper we address some of these issues, including the laser  power density, the stability of the system for quantitative  analysis and the data processing. The applied laser wavelength (1064 nm) and its pros and cons have been discussed by the  author in previous publications and will not be addressed here [2 - 5].


We implement additionally the PVD (Photovoltage Decay) technique using the PHEMOS setup to evaluate the applied  laser power and determine the changeover condition from low  to the high injection regime. In this setup the laser is pulsed  externally using the pulse generator (Keithley 3390) with 100  Hz frequency while the laser is illuminating in the point scan  mode on the device. The open circuit voltage (VOC) is then  measured directly using a high frequency oscilloscope (6 GHz  Lecroy digital oscilloscope). The oscilloscope has a high input  impedance (1 M ) to keep the device in the open circuit  condition. The open circuit voltage amplitude and its delay are  then measured by applying different laser power.


3) Changeover Condition


As already mentioned, the PHEMOS is equipped with a 1064  nm laser wavelength for performing OBIC measurements on  microelectronic devices. Modern microelectronic systems can  be up to 9 metal layers in the front side that prevent the laser  from reaching the active layer. To overcome this problem, measurements are performed from the polished backside and  for this the laser power should be enough high to stimulate the  active layers through several hundred micrometer silicon. An  another important issue is that the doping determined free (majority) carrier concentration is relatively high in the active  layer of the microelectronic semiconductor devices ranging  from 1017 to 1018 cm-3. Therefore, there is a large margin for  the laser power to be increased before the laser induced excess  carrier concentration reaches the high injection operation  regime. In some cases even the operation regime of the device  plays no significant role in the failure analysis of the  microelectronic systems.


By applying the same technique to the solar cells, especially  ones based on thin-film technology, the situation is more  complicated. First of all, the measurement is normally not  performed in the backside mode and therefore the laser should  not travel a long way to reach the p-n junction, and secondly  the equilibrium free (majority) carrier concentration is lower  compared to the transistor devices ranging from 1015 up to 1016 cm-3. Thus, the IR-laser induced operating regime of the solar  cell has to be studied more carefully because the transition  from low to the high injection regime is crucial for  characterization of the solar cell. The measurement result is  strongly dependent on the regime the solar cell is operating. In  some cases, such as concentrated solar cell, device operation  under the condition of high injection is relevant. Normally, though, solar cells are investigated the condition of low  injection. In the low injection regime (the laser induced excess  carrier concentration is well below the equilibrium free (majority) carrier concentration) the solar cell’s behavior is  described by the minority carrier diffusion and drift current,


resulting from electron/hole generation in the neutral and the  space charge region (SCR), respectively. In the silicon based  solar cell under low injection, the recombination/generation  processes are mainly due to recombination and generation via  recombination centers. Under this assumption the electrical  and material parameters of the solar cell are then extracted.


In the high injection regime, the operation of the solar cell is  more complicated. In this case, the influence of the majority  carriers on the current transport mechanism must be  considered in addition to the minority carrier current flow. Moreover, Auger recombination can dominate the  recombination process, which can lead to an extracted lifetime  which appears to be very low and does not correspond to the  lifetime that is important for the conventional operation of a  solar cell.  The Dember effect can also influence the results  due to the increased difference of the electron and hole  mobilities in the high injection regime. Furthermore, the  plasma frequency of the carriers can reach the frequency of  radiation and therefore reflect the incident beam. Another  possible effect is activation of additional recombination  centers in the illuminated surface and in the bulk that can  degrade the device’s performance [7, 8]. Finally, the injected  electron/hole pairs can create a so-called Space Charge  Limited Current (SCLC) that affects the carrier mobility [9]. Therefore, to understand the experimental results of such  measurements, it is crucial important to determine if the solar  cell is operating in either the low or the high injection regimes. In the following, a corresponding changeover condition from  low to the high injection regime in terms of an IR-laser power  density limit is discussed based on three different approaches: the PVD (Photovoltage Decay) measurement technique, an  analytic investigation, and finally a 1D simulation using the  PC1D program [10]. Before attempting to interpret the  interaction of the applied laser with the device, we discuss the  beam profile of the applied laser, especially its spot size and  power density. Using this knowledge we can then interpret and  discuss the PVD experimental results and associating  theoretical approaches.


3.1) Laser Intensity Profile


Considering the theoretical TEM00, the laser beam propagation  can be modeled by an ideal Gaussian intensity profile. The  definition of the laser beam diameter posing a Gaussian profile  is one of its most important properties, which influence  directly the resolution, the laser power intensity and therefore  the generation rate in the device.


In the PHEMOS setup the laser beam is transmitted through  different optical elements, including fibers, mirrors and the  objective lens that are designed to provide a specific laser  beam intensity and spot size on the device. The intensity  profile in the optical path and at the device surface keeps its  Gaussian profile. The laser profile on the device can be  defined theoretically by knowing the property of the laser and  optical elements in the laser path or the applied laser power  and its power loss in the optical path, and experimentally by  using high resolution optical beam profiler [11]. Due to the  lack of information about the optical elements, e.g. the fiber  and the mirrors, in the following we will define theoretically


the possible range for the laser beam spot size and therefore its  power density on the device surface.


There are five different definitions for the laser spot size  posing a Gaussian profile. We are using the definition that the  beam diameter is determined by position where the intensity  has fallen to 1/e2 of its peak value. The laser spot size on the  surface is dependent on the laser wavelength, the ratio of the  Gaussian laser spot size to the limiting aperture of the  objective  so called Truncation ratio (T), and the position of  the objective relative to the laser beam waist [12]. In such a  complex optical system a so called k-factor can be used to  define the laser beam spot size on the surface of the device. The laser spot size (diameter of image spot) can be defined  then by knowing the k-factor using the following equation


[12]:

d××= λ,


where N.A. is the numerical aperture of the objective,  the  laser wavelength and K is the k-factor, which is defined by  knowing the Truncation ratio (T) of the optical system. The  choice of the Truncation ratio is the compromise between the  laser power loss and the spot size. A higher T results in higher  power loss but smaller spot size. By varying T from infinity to


0.5 the k-factor varies between 1.61 and 2.51, respectively. In  this range the laser beam profile changes from an Airy disc to  a Gaussian profile, respectively. The laser spot size can then  be defined numerically by the optics of the scanning laser  microscope, considering the numerical aperture (N.A.) of the  optical system, the laser wavelength (1064 nm) and the  maximum and minimum value of the k-factor.

The minimum and  maximum laser spot size (d) and its corresponding average  laser power intensity (IAvg) by applying the maximum laser  power (100%) are calculated from equation 1.


We measured the laser power using a laser power meter for  each objective in the optimal distance from the focused level  specified by the manufacturer. The tool integration software  allows varying the laser power from 1% up to 100% of the  maximum laser power while keeping the same spot size. Table  1 shows the maximum measured laser power (100%), the


minimum and maximum spot size of the laser (1064 nm) and  the average laser power density for each objective.


The maximum laser power intensity of a Gaussian laser beam  is defined as two times the total maximum power divided by  the illuminated area. For our calculation we considered the  average laser power intensity that is half of the maximum  intensity.


4) PVD Measurement


The OCVD (Open Circuit Voltage Decay) is a well known  technique used to determine the carrier lifetime in a  semiconductor device. In this method, the device is biased in  the forward direction for a long enough time to attain a steady  state. The excess charge carriers (ECC) are then injected  electrically or optically in the p-n junction.  In the case of  optical excitation, this technique is called PVD. Then the  excitation switches off and the excess charge carriers decay to  zero, while keeping the device under the open circuit  condition. In the ideal case the high and low injection lifetimes  are then evaluated from two distinct regions of the voltage  transient [13]. Therefore, one could be able to determine the  laser power associating with the changeover condition. In this  study we implemented the PVD measurement based on the  PHEMOS setup to investigate experimentally the changeover  condition.


Fig 1: The results of the PVD measurement performed on a  thin-film poly-Si solar cell. The laser power intensity varied  from 2×103 up to 8×104 W/cm2.


The result of the PVD measurement performed on a thin-film  poly-Si solar cell is shown in figure 1 for different applied  laser power intensities using 5× objective lens. The averaged  power density is calculated by considering the maximum laser  spot size defined in the table 1. The laser power varied from  2% up to 80% of its maximum, equivalent to 2×103 and 8×104 W/cm2, respectively. The solar cell active area is about 7.64  mm2. The sample, its structure and production process were  explained in detail previously by the author [2 - 5].


As it is seen from figure 1, there is no clear linear region in the  VOC decay curve and therefore no changeover point from high  to low injection is obviously indicated. The result shows an  exponential decay behavior that is almost independent of the


applied laser power. The reason is that in real devices  including solar cells, not just the recombination in the bulk but  also many other parameters, such as the recombination in  the  space charge region (SCR) and emitter, capacitive effects, finite base width and shunt resistance influence the decay  behavior of the device under the condition of open circuit [13]. Each of the above parameters affects the decay mechanism  differently and makes the interpretation of the result harder  and more complicated. The result of the VOC decay measured  here seems to be governed by the junction capacitance, which  can be due to the low minority carrier lifetime in the absorber  layer [13, 14]. If one tries to extract the carrier lifetime from a  small time interval very near to the initial VOC decay, the  obtained value will be much higher than the expected value. It  is also obviously not possible to determine the changeover  condition based on these results. As an attempt, we have  considered another approach to determine the device operating  injection regime by using the illumination intensity  dependence of the VOC.


Fig 2: The measured VOC of the thin-film poly-Si solar cell  depends on  the applied laser power density in the rage of  3×103 up to 8.5×104 W/cm2. The result is depicted versus


log(IAvg). The red line shows the logarithmic fit to the


measured data.


The open circuit voltage of an abrupt asymmetric p-n junction  under low injection condition is a logarithmic function of the  short circuit current (ISC) and therefore the light intensity [15]. But in the very high injection regime, where the excess carrier  density is higher the equilibrium free (majority) carrier  concentration, this voltage saturates [16]. The saturated  voltage should be equal to the diffusion potential plus the  Dember potential. But in some cases there is a deviation from  the expected value. This deviation is due to the complexity of  the device operating mechanism in the high injection regime, which can be influenced by the difference of the diffusion  coefficient of electron and holes at high injection, Auger  recombination, and introducing new recombination centers on  the surface and bulk of the cell [8]. Despite the complexity  associated with the saturated open circuit voltage at the high  injection level, we expect to see a logarithmic dependency of  the VOC under low injection condition and the presence of a


saturation tendency of the voltage under high injection  condition.


Figure 2 shows the VOC measured at the thin-film poly-Si solar  cell versus the log(IAvg), where IAvg is the averaged laser power  density. The measurement was performed using the objective  with 5× magnification and the laser power density varying  from 3×103 up to 8.5×104 W/cm2. The red line shows the  logarithmic fit of the measured data. The diagram shows the  deviation of the measured data from the fit and the tendency to  saturate from about 8×103 W/cm2. Therefore, we can conclude  that the solar cell for this power density is not operating in the  low injection regime, so that the excess carrier concentration is  equal or higher than equilibrium free (majority) carrier  concentration in the absorber layer but still less than the one in  the emitter.


In addition, we performed PVD measurements under same  condition on a semiconductor n+p crystalline Si diode based on  650 nm siemens technology. The result provided us a direct  comparison of the condition in a standard microelectronic  device with a complex solar cell structure. The emitter window  was formed by n+ diffusion in the p-well and had an active area  of 10000 μm2. Due to the lower absorption coefficient in the  crystalline Si compared to the poly-Si and also higher doping  concentration in the investigated diode compared to the solar  cell, the changeover condition was expected to be shifted to  the higher laser power density. The result of the measurement  is depicted in the figure 3 and shows, in contrast to the solar  cell (figure 2), a clear logarithmic dependency in the applied  laser power density range. We can then conclude that in this  laser power range, the device is performing in the low  injection regime.


Fig 3: The measured VOC of the n+p crystalline Si based diode


depends on  the applied laser power density in the rage of  3×103 up to 8×104 W/cm2. The Voc is depicted versus log(IAvg)


and the red line shows the logarithmic fit to the measured


data.


5) Simulation of the changeover condition


In this section, the corresponding changeover condition in  terms of an IR-laser power limit is discussed based on two  different theoretical approaches: an analytic investigation and


a simulation using the PC1D program. Finally, the acquired  knowledge will be used to interpret and discuss the  experimental PVD result.


5.1) Analytic Investigation


To evaluate the changeover condition using an analytic  investigation, a heterojunction silicon based n+p junction with  a highly n-doped amorphous thin emitter (a-Si) and p-doped  polysilicon (p-Si) thin absorber layer is assumed. The sample  has similar electrical and optical parameters to the investigated  thin-film solar cell presented in the section 4. Because of the  longer wavelength of the laser in relation to the thickness of  the emitter layer and also higher bandgab energy of the a-Si  compared to the wavelength of applied laser, the activity, which is observed in the IR-LBIC measurement, provides  mainly information on the p-doped poly-Si absorber layer. Therefore, for the analytic investigation, we suppose a p-type  poly-Si as an absorber layer which is illuminated with a  coherent light source and there is no recombination at its  surface and backside. The transition from low to high injection (changeover condition) in the absorber layer happens when n comes to p0, where n and p0 are the excess charge carrier and  equilibrium hole concentration due to the doping, respectively. The optical generation rate under a homogeneous illumination  is expressed as [7]:

where, A is the recombination rate constant depending on the  recombination mechanism, n and p are the electron and hole  density concentration, respectively, and ni is the intrinsic  carrier concentration. The changeover condition is defined by  n = p0. This expression can be simplified as:

Considering the definition of the minority carrier lifetime  under low injection, this equation can then finally be formed as


[7]:


ατ


where n is the minority carrier lifetime. Multiplying Changeover by (hc/ ) produces the corresponding laser power density, where h is the plank constant, c is the light velocity in vacuum  and  is the laser wavelength. Using this simple equation, we  calculated the changeover condition.


It is obvious from equation 6 the changeover condition is  strongly dependent on the absorption coefficient and therefore  the wavelength of the applied laser and also to the minority  carrier lifetime. The higher the wavelength, the smaller the  absorption coefficient is. Therefore, by applying the LBIC  measurements using higher wavelength the changeover  condition increases and higher laser power can be applied to  the sample while the solar cell is still operating in the low  injection regime. The absorption coefficient of the 1064 nm  laser in the crystalline silicon is ~10 cm-1, and in polysilicon is  213 cm-1, which is approximately 20 times higher [17, 18]. The absorption coefficient in this wavelength (1064 nm) is  several orders of magnitude smaller than the usual wavelength  used in the photovoltaic (500 - 600 nm) [2]. Assuming a  silicon based thin-film solar cell with the thin poly-Si absorber  layer (~2 μm) with doping concentration of 1016 cm-3 and  relatively low minority carrier lifetime of 2.5 ns, the calculated  changeover condition based on the later equation is equal to


3.75×1026 m-2s-1, which is about 7×103 W/cm2.


In the case of samples with higher carrier lifetimes, the  situation becomes even worse. The maximum laser power  density that can be applied to the device using different  objective has been illustrated in table 1. The laser power can  be varied from 1% to 100% of the maximum laser power  keeping the same laser spot size. Comparison of the minimum  possible laser power density with the calculated value of the  changeover condition shows that the laser power density is at  the limit of the changeover condition. Although, by  considering the surface and backside recombination, the  existence of recombination centre, e.g. grain boundary, within  the device, and influence of the SCR, the margin for the laser  power and therefore the changeover condition is increased. Therefore, despite the high wavelength of the applied laser, the  transition from low to high injection is possible and it is a  critical issue to be determined based on the solar cell type; otherwise the extracted parameters can be misinterpreted.


5.2) Simulation Results


For further investigation of the changeover condition, we  simulated additionally the investigated thin-film solar cell  using the PC1D program under different illumination  conditions to study the changeover condition and the  parameters that may influence it. Combining the result of the  analytic approach with the simulation helped us understand  and interpret the PVD experimental results.


The PC1D software is a commercially available solar cell  modeling program that solves the fully coupled nonlinear  equations for the quasi-one-dimensional transport of electrons  and holes in a solar cell.


A similar structure as for analytic investigation has been used  for the simulation. The device consists of a very thin (10 nm) emitter layer of highly n-doped a-Si (1.7×1017 cm-3), a thin (2 μm) absorber layer of p-doped poly-Si (1×1016 cm-3) and


finally a thin layer (200 nm) of highly p-doped poly-Si as BSF (Back Surface Field) layer (1018 cm-3). The minority carrier  lifetime in the absorber layer was set to 2.5 ns. The emitter is  covered with 80 nm thick antireflection layer (ZnO) and was  illuminated through this layer. The absorption coefficient in  the absorber and seed layers was set to 213 cm-1, corresponding to the illuminated laser with the wavelength of  1064 nm.


The device structure starts with the emitter layer (x = 0) and  end with the BSF layer (x = 2.21 μm). The result of the  simulation is depicted in figure 4. The minority and majority  carrier density are monitored when the device is in the  darkness and also when it is illuminated from the front side  through emitter layer with the monochromatic light (  = 1064  nm) and power density of 8.43 up to 8.43×106 W/cm2. The  whole device is illuminated and there is no electrical  stimulation.


The antireflection layer (ZnO) is about 80 nm thick to reduce  the reflection from the surface. In the IR the reflection of the  applied laser is higher than the visible range, so that almost  17% of the illumination at the wavelength of 1064 nm is  reflected. The PC1D considered the power loss due to the  reflection. For example by illuminating the device with the  laser power intensity of 8.43 W/cm2, the power intensity at the  device is about 7 W/cm2.


Fig 4: The minority and majority carrier density in n- and pregion as result of the device simulation using the PC1D


program. By increasing the power density, the minority


carrier density increases, so that by applying 8.43×105 W/cm2 power density device is at the changeover condition.


The minority and majority carrier density in n- and p-region  are depicted the figure 4 as result of the simulation under  different illumination intensity. The result is illustrated in the  equilibrium condition when there was no electrical and optical  stimulation and also under optical stimulation with different  illumination intensity. By applying the laser power density of


8.43 W/cm2, the minority carrier density (green curve) in the  absorber layer is much lower than equilibrium free (majority) carrier concentration and the device is in the low injection  regime. By increasing the laser power intensity the minority  carrier density reaches to the same level as the equilibrium  majority carrier concentration (cyan curve) at the power  intensity 8.43×103 W/cm2. By considering the 17% reflection


at the surface, the power density is equal to ~7×103 W/cm2. At  this point the device is at the transition from low to the high  injection regime (changeover condition). This is in good  agreement with that one from analytical investigation. By  applying higher power density of 8.43×106 W/cm2 the minority  carrier density increases further and stays obviously above the  equilibrium majority carrier density in the absorber layer, where the device is operating in the high injection regime.


Fig 5: The VOC dependent to the log of the applied laser power  intensity obtained from the device simulation using the PC1D  program. The result shows a non logarithmic behavior from


~8×103 W/cm2 and voltage tends to saturate at very high


power density.


We investigated further the dependency of the open circuit  voltage to the illumination intensity using PC1D to interpret  the experimental results from the section 4. VOC is extracted  from the light IV curve under different laser power density  from 8.5 up to 8.5×106 W/cm2 with consideration of the 17% reflection at the surface of the device. Figure 5 shows the  result of the simulation, where VOC is depicted versus log of  laser power density. The voltage tends to saturate at very high  density about 2×105 W/cm2. We observe the similar behavior  as obtained for the investigated solar cell at figure 2 and nonlogarithmic behavior from ~ 8×103 W/cm2. As can be seen, the  applied laser power in the experimental setup was not high  enough to reach a really saturated level of VOC. It should also  be mentioned that the reflection is not considered in the  experimental measurement and thus the power density is lower  at the device.


One another phenomena observed is that the measured VOC is  lower than the one from the simulation result. One reason for  this difference can be the size of the illumination area in  comparison to the whole device. In the experimental result we  illuminate an area of about 100 μm2 compared to the whole  area that is about 7.68 mm2, while in the simulation the whole  device is illuminated. The influence of the dark area  accompanying with the high series resistance of the device can  lead to a reduction of the obtained experimental VOC [19]. The  comparison of the VOC obtained for the solar cell and the  semiconductor diode confirms also this hypothesis.  The VOC increases up to 0.7 V in the later case. The ratio of the  illuminated area to the whole device in the diode (100 μm2 to  10000 μm2) is much higher than the solar cell. Therefore, the


effect of the dark area and the VOC drop is smaller in  comparison with the solar cell.


6) Setup Instability for Quantitative Analysis


Frequency (Hz)


Figure 11 : Fourier transform of the temporal signal from  green region in Fig. 10 (a). The constant component at 0 Hz  has been removed for readability.


Fig. 11 and Fig. 12 are two examples of characterization. Fig. 11 is the frequency spectrum of the green object, marked by a  small black rectangle, from Fig. 10 (a). The spectrum is  almost flat from 0 to 100 MHz. It means that each frequency


component of the region signal is equivalent. This is typical of  a white noise. In a design analysis perspective, this result can  be explained by the fact that this region is a permanent  emission spot without any link to input pattern activity.


On the other hand, Fig. 12 is the frequency spectrum of the  region marked by a red rectangle in Fig. 10 (c). Spectrum  exhibits harmonic decomposition and significant differences  between each frequency component. So, it is possible to  conclude that this region is really related to the input pattern  activity and that it can be ranked as a true signal region or a  true emission spot.


Figure 12 : Fourier transform of the region marked by a red  rectangle in Fig. 10 (c). The 0 Hz component has been  removed.


These two examples extracted from the 90 nm-technology  memory case study show that frequency spectrum is different  in what can be called a false detection from true signal region. They show that such properties can be used to fully automate  the emission region extraction and classification process, without human intervention.


Processing order


In this paper, the suggested processing scheme is to first  exploit spatial dimensions (x,y) to extract all of the potential  emission spots and remove background noise (which is  composed of pixels of low value) and then use the remaining  dimension, i.e. time, to qualify a potential region as related to  electrical activity or not and finally perform timing analysis of  the whole studied ROI. We suggest this way of proceeding as  a logical succession of automated operation because it is close  to what used to be done in manual exploitation of TRI data. The Introduction section already points out drawbacks of the  manual approach and why it can be interesting to use an  automated and less subjective as possible process.  Nevertheless, the case where time dimension is used before  spatial dimensions (x, y) shall be considered.


Indeed, in time display, with a good sensor and a long enough  acquisition time, emission event linked to electrical activity  can be spotted easily. So why not first perform temporal


discrimination, which means removing photon uncorrelated to  emission event (or spike), and then use the proposed image  processing algorithm? Expected result would be an  improvement in emission region identification and spatial  filtering. Unfortunately, this suggestion cannot really be more  efficient than what has been previously discussed for several  reasons.


Actually, when performing a temporal discrimination in an  automated processing scheme, the whole ROI might be  considered. There is no a priori knowledge of emission region. It can be solved by layout information but in this case, it is  equivalent to perform a spatial filtering prior to temporal  discrimination.  Two types of ROI might be considered. There  is the homogeneous ROI, where all the emission spots follow  the same pattern or, at least are synchronous, and the  inhomogeneous where different activities are involved. The  first ones are generally met at strong magnification whereas  the others are more encountered at low or medium  magnification.


For inhomogeneous ROI, the identification of events can be  very complex. Fig. 13 is an example of such signals. It is the  Time Resolved Emission of the whole area from Fig. 8. Because there is only one small region related to input pattern  among other strong emission spots, events cannot be extracted  from time display in a simple way, e.g. thresholding.

12. So spectral properties of signal linked to input pattern  cannot be exploited form that kind of ROI. This example  shows that the exploitation of time information before spatial  information for inhomogeneous ROI is a complex process.

To investigate the benefit of exploiting time before spatial  information, a simple thresholding has been performed, even  if it is not the best way of processing as under threshold parts  of spikes are removed. The threshold has been set to 30  photons. The difference in pixel values between the two  images to process is given in Fig. 16. As expected, two types  of photons where removed. Some background one that result  from thermal noise and some in the middle of the picture  which were in the side of the temporal spike that were under  the threshold. A part of the information has been removed.


0 1 2 3 4 5 6 7


Figure 16 : Removed pixel value after temporal thresholding  on the 45 nm Flip-Flop case.


Fig. 17 is a comparison between the first order image after  image processing of the before and after temporal  thresholding images. Results are not so different. There is a  slight improvement in region labeling in the image (b) as the  yellow region from (a) has been separed in two in (b).  So, for  this example, the use of the parameter time (t) before the  spatial parameters (x,y) bring a slightly better detection.


Figure 17 : Comparative between unthresholded (a) and  thresholded in time dimension (b) for the first order result  from 45 nm Flip-Flop case.


Yet, on another case study, an area that have been spotted on  the (x, y) then (t) process has completely disappeared. So for  homogeneous ROI, the best processing order depends on  ROI’s properties.


To conclude this subsection, the processing order suggested in  this paper ((x, y) then (t)) can be applied to any case whereas  the opposite one cannot be applied to every ROI and even if it  can, cautions shall be taken. In addition, techniques based on  temporal thresholding operations, like PPD, remove a part of  the information.


Spatial Resolution


By spatial resolution, we mean the ability of the algorithm to  separate two or more emission spots that partly overlap. In the


suggested algorithm, three steps shall be considered for spatial  resolution. These three steps are thresholding operations, edge  detection and connected component labeling.


The connected component labeling uses several assertions to  detect if a pixel is a part of a region or not. Two close  emission spots can be labeled as the same region. It depends  on how their pixels are promiscuous. Furthermore, if they  partly overlap, differentiation is impossible. Connected  component labeling results depends on success of the two  others operations i.e. thresholding and edge detection.


Fig. 17 shows 3D projection of emission raw data from the 45  nm Flip-Flop case that can be seen in Fig. 5. Data has been  smoothed with a 3x3 mean filter. From Fig. 18, it is possible  to formulate the hypothesis that every emission spot can be  approximated by a 2D Gaussian function.


Figure 18 : 3D projection of the 45 nm Flip-Flop case study. To z-axis matches the number of photons detected in the pixel.


When two 2D Gaussian intersect, there exists a projection  plane P  which is orthogonal to the (Oxy) plane and where the


intersection area between the two planes is maximized.  If the  computed threshold in image processing has not a bigger  value than the maximum of intersection in P, the two


Gaussians cannot be resolved in the (Oxy) plane. To illustrate  this, Fig. 19 shows two Gaussian functions projected in the P


plane. M is the maxima of intersection. It is quite obvious that  if the threshold T is not strictly superior to M, it is impossible  to separate the two functions in the (Oxy) plane.


Unfortunately, T depends on the statistical properties of pixel  value of the whole image. It means that size of the image  processed influences the threshold, so as all other Gaussian  parameters, mainly their standard deviation, that are parts of


the image. So there is only a chance that two close emission  spots cannot be properly resolved by thresholding. One  solution consists in an increase of acquisition duration as it  might change the Gaussian properties of the emission profile (higher value per pixel).


−2 −1.5 −1 −0.5 0 0.5 1 1.5 2 2.5 0


M


T


Figure 19: Schematic of the projection of the two Gaussian  functions in the P plane. M is the maximum of intersection


and T is the threshold computed from moment preserving  method. Hatching highlight the maximized intersection area.


The Laplacian of Gaussian edge detector used in the residual  outlines suppression sub-algorithm is a zero-crossing method. Edges are unveiled by identification of zeros after the  convolution of the raw image with a Laplacian of Gaussian  filter. If two Gaussians intersect like in Fig. 19, this detector is  not able to resolve them.


A new algorithm which operates on a spatial level on TRI data  has been introduced. It automates the identification of  emission sites and can be applied in a CADless context. It is  more precise than previous methods, attributing the optimal  size for spotted regions, even for those which are very weak  and hidden by strong emission. The study of properties like  frequency spectrum completes the characterization of the  areas. Furthermore it can be used for both static and dynamic  emission dataset. In terms of perspectives and future works, the research of new discrimination methods to develop an  even better detection is on our roadmap.

Advanced Methods and Software for Enhancing Analytical Tools Capabilities  during Chip Diagnostic and Characterization


Franco Stellari and Peilin Song


IBM T.J. Watson Research Center, 1101 Kitchawan Rd, Yorktown Heights, NY - stellari@us.ibm.com, 914-945-3223


In this paper, we present a comprehensive overview of several  advanced methods and software solutions that we have  developed during the years in support of chip diagnostic and  characterization in our lab. These techniques apply to the  analytical tools in our lab, such as time-integrated and timeresolved emission microscopes, and are devised to help  improve productivity and ease of use. In particular we discuss  emission-based auto-focusing for detecting very faint signals, processing large sets of images for high-resolution mapping of  very large fiend of views, careful registration of emission  images to circuit layout shapes, and advanced processing of  the data for extracting the valuable underlying information. In  the past, these techniques have been extensively used in  support of many diagnostic and characterization applications, and they have been effective enablers for the development of  innovative methods and tools.


The use of hot-carrier photon emission from VLSI circuits for  localizing and identifying failures has been widely used for  several decades. First, with the introduction of emission  microscopy [1] it soon became an essential instrument for  physical failure analysis by localizing hot-spot emission, shorts, non-uniform quiescent/stand-by current of the chip (IDDQ) [2-4], etc. In 1995, Time-Resolved Emission (TRE) measurements, also known as Picosecond Imaging for Circuit  Analysis (PICA) [5-7], introduced a new set of techniques for  non-invasive circuit testing and diagnostic through the  backside of the chip. In particular, it became possible for the  first time to precisely probe the timing performance of VLSI  chips at the transistor level and measure delays and skews  with picosecond precision. During the past decade, the use of  time-integrated and time-resolved emission has been broadly  extended to adjacent areas of applications, such as logic  diagnostics and chip performance characterization. Most of  these new techniques rely on the detection of Light Emission  due to Off-State Leakage Current (LEOSLC) [8-11]. Important examples are circuit logic states mapping for  monitoring the internal logic state of a circuit in a static or  dynamic mode [12], power supply droop calculation for  estimating the effect of different exercisers onto the chip  power distribution grid [3,10,13], circuit internal temperature  and gate self heating measurements [14], process variability  mapping for creating higher resolution images of the across  chip process related effects [15], chip alterations and  malicious circuit detection for trusted IC [16], etc.


However, all these techniques require advanced methods and  software solutions on top of existing tools for detecting very  faint signals, processing large sets of images, carefully  registering emission images to circuit layout shapes, and  advanced processing of the data for extracting the valuable  underlying information. For these reasons, during the years, we have developed methods and software packages that  leverage the basic capability offered by most emission and  analytical tools in general, to enhance the data analysis and  interpretation for advanced emission-based applications such  as the one described above.


In the following sections, we will present few representative  examples of such methods and software that were developed  and are available in our lab. Currently, most of these  capabilities are external software packages that run on top of  the emission tools software and layout browser but they could  be easily integrated either in the analytical tool software to  enhance their offering or as an additional analysis package.


Method overview


Fig. 1 shows a simplified overview of the different  methods/software packages and their relative dependences and  interactions. At one end there are different tools common in  our labs such as analytical tools, instrumentation to bias and  control the chips under test, and layout and schematic  browsers. A key component of our effort was to create generic  software interfaces to these tools so that key functionalities of  the tools, such as stage movement, instrument condition setup, and data collection, could be operated in a programmatic way  from other dedicated software packages or from different tools  involved in the experiment (see Fig. 2). These interfaces are  generic, do not require software API from the tool vendor, and, although coded with one particular programming  language, can be easily addressed using other software and  programming languages. In addition, network connectivity is

and mapping
