Fig. 1: Proposed methods overview and dependences.


easily allowed so that the overall acquisition system may be  run over the network, thus permitting the interaction among  tools that are not located in the same place or connected to the  same computer or controller in a virtual lab environment such  as the one discussed in [17]. This is of particular interest for  users, designers, or consultants that may not be physically  present in the lab or to enable interaction between tools with  closed software interfaces. It should also be understood that, although these interfaces need to be custom designed, they  rely significantly on the underlying tool software in order to  interact correctly with the hardware so that most of the  complex operation, as well as protections and checking, can  be easily implemented. Leveraging the custom software  interfaces, an automation software layer was created to receive  user input and commands, experiment requirements and  objectives, experiment conditions, and use this information to  act on several methodologies that were developed to acquire  data and interpret it. In the following section we will describe  several of the methods present in this automation software  layer. For the implementation discussed in this paper, the  automation software layer and custom interfaces are coded  with a scripting language and do not rely on proprietary API  interfaces from tool vendors. In fact, the existing control  software that is used by a user to operate the tool will be  commanded by the proposed software layer.


Emission-based auto-focusing


In general, an optical tool for measuring the emission from a  VLSI circuit is composed by the elements shown in Fig. 3. The Device Under Test (DUT) is positioned at the focal plane  of the collection optics of a microscope so that the  spontaneous or stimulated emission from the DUT can be  efficiently collected and refocused on different types of  cameras. The relative position of the microscope and DUT can


be altered by means of certain types of translation that could  move in X, Y, Z. The X,Y movement of the stage allows to  position different portions of the sample in the field of view of  different types of collection optics so that different part of the  sample can be measured using the camera. Additionally, Z  movement is necessary so that different samples of different  thickness and materials can be positioned at the correct focal  plane for obtaining the sharpest emission images. Inadequate  focusing of a tool may significantly reduce its spatial  resolution, image contrast, sensitivity, and Signal-to-NoiseRatio (SNR) thus making measurements not effective.


Identifying the correct/best focus for a measurement is often  very challenging. In most cases some kind of reflected light  images of the sample (e.g., by illuminating the sample and  measuring the reflected light intensity, or by raster scanning a  laser in an Laser Scanning Microscope (LSM) and measuring  the reflected light intensity) is used by the user to identify the  best contrast possible and therefore estimating the best Z  focus position for subsequent emission measurements. This  method worked well for older technologies where feature  sizes were relatively large, easily recognizable in pattern  images, and did not require very high magnification for the  measurements, such as Solid Immersion Lens (SIL).


The magnification required to spatially separate emission  sources in today’s ICs is very high; and, as a consequence, it  causes the depth of focus of the optical system to be very  shallow, so that very fine changes in Z position cause  significant focus changes. Additionally, the circuit features  that are observed in the reflected light pattern image are  getting so small that a precise and unique interpretation of the  best focus is often challenging and requires a lot of experience  from the tool operator. It should also be understood that the  reflected light image focusing may be dominated by reflective  elements such as metal levels (e.g., metal 1 level as  schematically depicted in Fig. 4), while the spontaneous  emission that one wants to measure is generated by the active  area of the DUT, where diffusion may offer little reflectivity  and contrast. Therefore, there is a physical reason why the  emission best focus may differ from the reflected light pattern  best focus. Tool vendors try to compensate for this problem  by introducing an offset between the reflected light pattern  image and the spontaneous emission measurement. However, variations in sample thickness, doping, materials, technology, thermal variations, etc. may cause a relative shift of the best  emission focus compared to the best pattern image focus. Therefore a fixed offset compensation does not yield the best  results for most cases, thus leading to less than optimal  emission images. In addition, often times the pattern image

In our approach we propose to use pattern images just for  coarse chip navigation and focusing and subsequently use  directly emission images for fine focus adjustment leading to  optimal image quality. Our method requires acquiring several  emission images/waveforms at different Z positions, for each  image/waveform a Figure Of Merit (FOM) is calculated to  measure the quality of the focus for that image relative to the  others. Then the FOM is plotted (or examined numerically) as  a function of the Z positions and the value corresponding to  maximum of the FOM is identified as best focus condition for  the emission measurements. From this qualitative description, it is easy to find a strong reliance on the tool automation.


In one implementation of this method, the best focus position  is sought starting from the best pattern image focus. Fig. 5  illustrates the basic steps of our method: the Z stage of the  optical tool is moved to the Zi position and an emission  measurement is acquired. It should be noted, that a delay  between the movement and the emission acquisition may be  necessary to allow for the stage movement to complete, vibrations to subside, thermal conditions to stabilize, etc. Additionally it should be noted that the conditions/settings of  the camera may be different from the one used during a  regular emission measurement: shorter acquisition times may  be used along with a reduced number of pixels or a limited  region of interest. Each acquired image/waveform may  undergo post processing such as filtering, cropping, and other  transformations in preparation for the FOM calculation. For  example filtering may help remove noise as well as reduce the  effect of sporadic emission sources such as cosmic rays. Cropping the image may allow to remove areas that are not  sources of emission so that the variation of the FOM with the  Z position is increased and a maximum can be easily  identified. Subsequently the FOM of the image with regards to  the focus is calculated. The same process is repeated for all  the Zi positions in the set. Finally the FOM function is plotted  and the maximum is located. The Zm position corresponding  to the maximum is then used to move the stage for subsequent  measurements.


Fig. 6 shows a graphical representation of the results of the  described method as implemented for a 2D static camera. The  top row of images shows the emission images acquired at  different values of Z focus (indicated by the numerical value  above each figure). In the chart below, the horizontal axis  indicates the Z position, similarly to the acquired images. The  circles/symbols indicate the calculated FOM for the image at  the specified Z position. For Z values below -6875 (or above -6860), the FOM is almost constant with only small variations  caused by noise. On the contrary, towards the center of the  range, the FOM is higher with a peak. The red line is a fitting  of the FOM as implemented by the software. The fitting may  be used to better identify the peak of the FOM, compared to  just using the highest FOM value. For example the Z  corresponding to the highest FOM value is -6868 while the  fitting makes it easy to understand that the best focal position  is at ~-6869. As a reference, the pattern image corresponding  to the best pattern focus is shown in an inset of Fig. 6 and  correspond to Z = -6864, about 5 micron away from the best  emission focus calculated above. The location of the best  focus may be further refined by repeating the measurements at  smaller Z steps for a smaller range.


Although the previous example refers mainly to 2D static  images, the same method with different types of FOM may be  applied to auto-focus single point Time-Resolved Emission (TRE) and 2D PICA as well as laser-based localization tools. In general one has to notice that in most cases the emission  image is composed of small localized emission sources (switching or leaky transistors), many of which can be  combined to create more complicated structures in the image. Given these premises, it is easy to understand that a higher (in  term of intensity compared to the background emission) and  smaller (in term of X and Y spatial size) emission peaks is a  good indication of good Z focusing of the image. When the Z  is moved out of the best focus the emission amplitude of each  spot is reduced and divided among neighboring pixels of the  camera, thus blurring the entire image. Additionally, looking  at the TRE waveform, a better defined emission peak in time  is an indication of a good focal position.

methods. In general emission images are composed of small  localized emission sources. Therefore achieving brighter (in  term of intensity) and smaller (in term of lateral size) emission  peaks is a good indication of a good Z focusing. For this  reason, a function similar to the gradient of the image is often  used as the FOM.


Considering the case of emission waveforms, one can  calculate the FOM as the absolute value of the derivative of  the measured waveform. Sharper emission peaks, corresponding to best focus condition, lead to steeper changes  in emission and therefore large values of the derivative. Alternative methods are also possible.


High resolution mapping of large field of views


For many types of applications such as the one described in


[15] and [16], images of the entire chip may be necessary for  extracting important information such as across chip process  variations maps. However, the aggressive scaling of transistor  dimensions as well as the increase in chip area pose a  challenge to these types of emission acquisition since cameras  have a limited number of pixels and therefore the spatial  resolution is inversely proportional to the field of view of the  optical system. In specific, if the magnification of the optical  system is increased to obtain a suitable spatial resolution, the  field of view is inevitably reduced as a side effect. In  performing diagnostics of high end products, we have reached  the point that the emission from the entire chip cannot be  acquired with a single acquisition even at the lowest possible  magnification offered by most optical microscopes [15].


To solve the previous issue, we have developed a method for  automatically acquiring large sets of images from different  positions of a chip and then mathematically stitching them  together to create a high-resolution image with a very large  field of view. Our proposed method is summarized in Fig. 7. For a given Region Of Interest (ROI), a set of overlapping  images are acquired following, for example, a raster pattern as  shown in Fig. 8. Adjacent images (in the X or Y direction) are  acquired so that a partial overlap is achieved. Obviously the  overlap size needs to be carefully selected to be large enough  to guarantee accurate registration of the images across the


entire ROI but small enough to reduce overhead. For this  reason, adaptive methodologies have also been considered  where the overlap size is adjusted during the image  acquisition process to take into account the content of current  emission image (i-th) before the stage is moved to the next ((i+1)th) partial overlapping image.


Once all the images have been acquired, or during the  measurement process, individual frames are stitched together  to form the final image. Fig. 9 shows a real-life example of  image stitching process. Two neighboring images may be first  process to remove aberrations on the overlapping edges, as  well as to enhance features that may be used for the fine  relative registration of the two images. The overlapping  amount between the i-th and (i+1)th image is coarsely  estimated based on the knowledge of the stage movement and  size of the FOV as indicated by the red boxes in Fig. 9. The  corresponding overlapping areas are then selected in both  images for analysis. This step is necessary because the  movement of the translating stage between two images is only  approximately known due to mechanical limitations, backslash in the stage, thermal drifts, etc. Consequently a  method is necessary for finely adjusting the relative position  of the two images. In this example, a cross correlation  function of the two overlapping area is calculated as shown in  the curve inset of Fig. 9. The maximum of the cross

The stitching process may take place by  stitching together all the images in each row of images and  then subsequently stitching together the larger images  corresponding to the rows as shown in Fig. 10-11. The  process is repeated for all the neighboring images in both  directions, until a single large image at high resolution is  obtained.


Compared to other methods that rely uniquely on the precise  movement of the stage, our method leads to a more precise  alignment of neighboring images. Moreover, the overlapping  regions may be used to better adjust relative intensity changes  that may take place during long acquisitions. It should also be  noted that this method may be effectively combined with the  auto-focusing algorithm in our virtual lab environment to  guarantee that the entire large FOV remains in focus even  when the sample is tilted compared to the stage movement or  when the thermal and mechanical drift take place during long  acquisitions.


Accurate and automated layout registration


Time-integrated (static) images of both switching and leakage  emission are a key tool for testing and characterizing modern  VLSI circuits. For example, in [15] LEOSLC is used to map  systematic and random variability across chips, thus allowing  the identification problematic process steps. In [2], LEOSLC  is used to monitor gates at different operating conditions so  that their logic state can be inferred for debugging circuit  behavior and fix fails. In [18], the combined use of multiple  static emission images acquired for different set of stimuli is  used to characterize the performance of VLSI circuits. In [16], the static and dynamic emission from chips is correlated to  their predicted values based on layout so that intentional or  malicious modifications could be easily detected.


Common to all techniques is the necessity of precisely  correlating the emission pattern back (and forth) with the  layout of the DUT. In this way, every (or particular) emission


spot can be associated with a certain transistor, gate, subcircuit and properly interpreted, for example to reconstruct the  logic state of the gate. Achieving this precise registration with  the required sub-micron prevision is not trivial and, in the  past, required a lot of custom manual work, often prone to  error and user interpretation. Therefore, a better way to  automate this process and guarantee reproducible and accurate  results is needed to make all previous techniques work in an  effective way.


In some other cases, for example during failure analysis, emission images of particularly suspicious areas of the chip  are acquired often without much knowledge of the layout by  simply following areas of suspicious emission, such as  anomalously bright emission spots. However, in order to  identify the root problem, the area suspected in the emission  image needs to be traced back to the correct location in the  chip layout with very precise registration. Again such a task of  tracing back a small portion of the chip emission acquired at  high magnification to its unknown location in the chip layout  is not trivial and very time consuming when done manually by  an experienced tool operator.


For navigation purpose, this problem is approached in some  tools by using three (or more) point alignment. In particular, some specific locations in the layout and in the light reflected  pattern image of the chip are selected and correlated so that  the entire layout can be transformed using linear  transformations and drawn over the reflected light pattern  image. Although, this allows for simple navigation of the chip, a precise alignment at the local level is never achieved. In  other cases, the reflected light pattern image is correlated with  the layout while the tool is calibrated in a way that should  guarantee that the emission image is perfectly aligned with the  reflected light image. Unfortunately the latest assumption is  rarely realized due to sample thickness variations, temperature  changes, material differences, etc. so that even if the layout is  perfectly aligned with the pattern image, it is often not in a  very good alignment with the emission image. Additionally, in  modern technology, the reflected light images is insufficient  for a precise sub-micron alignment with the layout.

For all these reasons, in this paper we propose a novel method  for registering the emission image to the layout shapes, and  vice versa. Referring to the schematic block diagram in  Fig. 12, the key idea is to process the layout layers and shape  to identify the ones that contribute to the particular emission  pattern that one is creating. For example, gates connected to  the clock, if switching activity is measured through the  emission measurement. Once the layout shapes are properly  scaled to match the magnification used during the emission  measurement, the polygons are processed to create a “mask  image” predicting some feature highlighted in the emission  measurement. 2D cross correlation technique can then be used  to precisely align the emission and layout mask image. Directly using the emission image for the alignment allows to  avoid several passes prone to errors, focusing on the result of  interest. Additionally, the emission image is intrinsically more  selective because it is related only to active area of transistors  and gates.


As a first step, the layout may be processed to select only a  specific layer in a region of interest. In addition, the layout  shape may be filtered based on their connection to particular  signal lines or power grids. In some cases, layers may be  intersected or combined to create new polygons better  representing the expected source of emission: e.g., the layer  corresponding to the transistor poly may be intersected with  the layer defining the transistor channel to create a new set of  polygons corresponding to the active areas of the circuit  causing the emission. In a second step, the resulting parsed  layout is further manipulated through transformations such as


scaling, rotation, flipping to match the expected magnification  and orientation of the chip based on the conditions during the  emission image acquisition. Subsequently, a “mask” image is  created from the processed layout. This step consists in  constructing an “artificial” image with the same pixel size of  the emission image and enough pixels to cover the entire  layout after previous processing and transformation steps. Then the layout shapes are analyzed and corresponding pixels  of the “mask” image are be set to a value different from 0. In  some case, all values would be the same (for example 1), in  other case different numerical values could be assigned to  weight for example expected brighter emission areas, or key  feature that needs a prominent consideration during the  registration process. In one possible embodiment, all the  pixels of the “mask” image that fall inside the perimeter of a  layout shape corresponding to an emission source are  modified to a value > 0. In another implementation, the center  of a layout shape is calculated first and only the pixel of the “mask” image corresponding to such location is changed to a  value >0. In some cases, such pixel may be enlarged to cover  adjacent pixels, thus making a bigger target. During the final  step, the “mask” and measured image are correlated, for  example using 2D cross correlation or correlation algorithms  that allows to identify in a statistical way the best X and Y  translation that guarantees the best matching of the layout and  emission image. In practice, if the location of the emission  region is roughly known, a smaller portion of the layout  maybe selected (to speed up the process and reduce resource  consumption) in order to achieve a very fine alignment. Alternatively, if the location of the emission is not known in  relation to the layout, the entire layout may be processed so  that the correlation step identifies the best area of the layout  matching the measured emission image. It should also be  noted that in most cases the emission image may undergo  some kind of post processing before the registration step. For  example low pass filtering may be used to improve the signal  to noise ratio and remove spurious peaks due to cosmic rays  and alpha particles. Finally, the optimal transformation  function is applied directly to the original (or often the post  processed layout) so that it can be matched to the original (or  post processed) emission image.
